PREFACE 

One valid criticism of much of the teaching of science is that it is indefinite and meager; that pupils learn details - definitions, facts, and experiments - but do not gain a proper perspective of the field of science. This text in physics was written in the belief that it is possible to organize the subject matter usually included in an elementary course in physics so that the pupils studying it may readily master the fundamentals of the science. The material in this text has been thoroughly tested in the class room and the results have been most satisfactory. The pupils have gained an understanding of the theories underlying modern physics as well as a grasp of the essential physical laws. 

The number of mechanical devices about us today has so multiplied that there is a tendency for the pupil who is studying Physics to be diverted from the science to its attractive applications. In the course here offered every effort has been made to stress the essential scientific principles and then to illustrate them and to make them vital by describing a number of their practical applications. The "Introduction" furnishes the beginner with a perspective and gives him a framework upon which to build his knowledge of the science.

One major cause of the difficulties pupils have with the study of physics is that they do not have clear concepts of many of the most fundamental terms. We have given much space to the developments of these concepts, and they are placed in a prominent position with respect to the other material of the text. 

The reader will observe that we have given more than the usual amount of space to certain definitions; notably to those of the dyne, the erg, and the electrostatic units. Not only is it an observation of college teachers that a hazy concept of definitions is one of the great handicaps under which their pupils labor, but it is doubtless true that this defect applies to the general public, whether it reads popular articles for pleasure or more technical matter in connection with its occupations. Needless to say, all definitions should be thoroughly understood, not merely learned by rote. iii

iv 

PREFACE 

Another major source of the difficulties which pupils encounter is a lack of ability to apply simple mathematical operations to physical problems. To facilitate problem solving there is presented a review of those mathematical processes which our experience has shown us to be most needed and a series of carefully selected exercises in Mental Arithmetic which we have found to be most effective in fixing principles and formulas. 

The most important topics are introduced early in the course and are reviewed frequently. Notice the nature of the problems at the end of Chapter II. We have found that an early presentation of these problems and frequent repetition of the principles involved in them insures a mastery not obtainable in any other way. 

Men of Science have in general agreed upon a standard system of symbols. We believe the beginner should learn the accepted system. This involves the introduction of five Greek letters. Our pupils have had no difficulty with these. There are certain decided advantages in using for density and for flux, just as there is an advantage in using for 3.14. We have tried to restrict the course to such topics as a normal class can cover in one year. The material is so arranged that it may be used satisfactorily with pupils of varying abilities. As an aid to this certain sections which are less essential and somewhat more difficult are placed in smaller type.

INTRODUCTION SECTION 1. THE PHYSICAL WORLD 

In glacial times. Let us first think of our far-away forefather who lived towards the close of the Great Ice Age. 

The long years of winter are over and the great ice sheet, slowly melting, moves back towards Polar seas. The earth is entering upon a wonderful era wherein man is destined to learn many of nature's secrets and to harness her forces to do his will.

We picture to ourselves a typical man of the period as he rests by the shore of a new-formed lake. The air grows chill as daylight fades and the moon rises over the mountains. The stars appear above the pines. A great eagle soars above, and a rock, crashing down the mountain side, disturbs the quiet of evening. To him these are unrelated experiences. He thinks of the moon, stars, trees, and flying eagle, each as having a conscious spirit like himself. he may worship as gods. How little does he realize that many of the actions about him are governed by a few simple laws. For example, the path of the moon and the trail of the crashing rock are both determined by the force of gravity and the laws of motion. Some

As age after age passes by, his descendants will gradually gain a greater and greater knowledge of these laws, and thereby be enabled to utilize more and more effectively the forces of nature. Then they will be protected from the savage beasts. Cold and hunger will have no terrors, and they will attain that place of prominence on the earth for which they are destined. 1

In the present day. -The great advancements in the physical welfare of the race since the time when fire was first used by man have been due, then, to a better understanding of the laws of nature. 

Each discovery has been a milestone along the path over which man has labored. It has been a long journey from the tools which he made of chipped flint and sharpened bone to the mechanical marvels of today, and each year brings these man-made implements and inventions to higher stages of development.

In the science of physics we learn of laws by which we can explain our varied experiences with inanimate objects. Let us enter upon this study with zest, and then see nature with the eyes of modern man, rather than with the dim vision of our forefather of Glacial times. If parts of the lesson seem dull and uninteresting, remember that the rules themselves of even the most fascinating game are not exciting. An understanding of the laws of physical science will necessitate consistent and alert effort, but it will give a life long satisfaction.

The physical world. The physical world includes all things that can be measured. It does not include that which pertains to mind or consciousness. One cannot measure love in pounds or fear in feet per second. These things do not come within the range of the science of physics-yet they exist. 

The three fundamental realities of the physical world are matter, space, and motion. This is because, in terms of these, we can describe any happening that comes within our experience. But, if we watch carefully, we must grant even empty space the capacity, under certain conditions, to produce motion of matter, as when the earth attracts a meteor. 

This is contrary to our concept of absolute nothingness, and leads to belief in the existence of the "ether," a something that fills all space. 

Matter, space, and motion.

These three realities form the bases of the physical world which we are to study. We

INTRODUCTION 

3 cannot think of one of them without considering the other two. Space without matter has little meaning, because, in so far as we know, if there were no matter there would be no 

FIG. 1.- THE GREAT NEBULA OF ANDROMEDA 

This is the first spiral nebula whose distance from us was measured. It is not a great cloud of gas or dust, as was once thought, but another "universe" beyond the Milky Way which latter is the great star mass to which our solar system belongs. It is 

930,000 light years distant and consists of about a billion suns. way of detecting the difference between the width of a hair and a million miles. Space is measured by means of both matter and motion as when a man measures the length of a wall by means of a yard-stick. He must have a unit, a piece

of matter, with which to compare the length of the wall and he must also move it when measuring. 

The young infant forms his ideas of space through movements. At first his mother's face above him, the clock on the wall, and the moon in the sky appear equally near. It is through motions, the waving of arms, crawling, and finally walking, that he learns something of the space about him. 

All matter occupies some space. We could not think of it as existing if it did not. The three dimensions of space are length, breadth, and thickness. Extension in one dimension is called length, in two dimensions area, and in three dimensions volume. The outer boundary of every bit of matter is an area; for instance the surface of a ball. Every particle of matter has some volume. A certain volume of space may contain no matter, as when the air is exhausted from a glass bulb. Such a space is called a vacuum. 

The lengths, areas, and volumes of objects vary with conditions such as pressure and temperature. Thus, an iron rod becomes longer when heated, and a block of wood smaller when compressed. 

Space. By space we do not always mean empty space. 

We mean extension in three dimensions and this may or may not include matter. There is a certain amount of space within the surface of a solid lead ball. The measure of this space is called the volume of the ball. A bushel, a quart, and a cubic foot measure definite amounts of space, and are called units of volume. 

As the atmosphere extends upwards for but a few hundred miles at most, it follows that upward from the surface of the air for inconceivable distances is empty space, except at those places where are the stars and other heavenly bodies. 

We can form but inadequate ideas of the great distances in space that have been measured by the astronomers. The diameter of the sun is about one hundred times the diameter of the earth, and the distance from the earth to the sun about one hundred times the diameter of the sun. Let us draw a

INTRODUCTION 

5 LO circle of one inch radius to represent the path of the earth around the sun. The sun is now a speck of about half the diameter of the period at the end of this sentence and the earth like a grain of fine dust seen only when light shines upon it when floating in a darkened room. To locate the nearest fixed star on a map, using this scale, would require piece of paper between three and four miles long. To make a map of the "Milky Way," the system of stars to which our sun belongs, using this scale, would require a sheet of paper between 20,000 and 200,000 miles square. Beyond the Milky Way are the spiral nebulae, thousands of which have been photographed. The Great Nebula in Andromeda, probably the nearest of these, on our scale, would be a million miles away, while the fainter ones are very likely one hundred or more times as far. 

Matter. Matter is often defined as "that which occupies space" or as or as "that which has weight." These definitions include things not regarded as matter, though due to the presence of matter. The field of gravity which surrounds the earth and causes bodies to fall occupies or exists in space, but it is not matter. The same may be said of the magnetic field about a magnet or the electric field about a charge of electricity. Light also occupies space. Astronomers tell us that a ray of light passing by the sun is attracted as though it had weight, being pulled towards the sun as it passes by. 

Here is a case where the second definition fails. 

Matter may be defined exactly by naming its four chief characteristics, which are:

It occupies a space of three dimensions. 

It has the property of motion in this space. 

It has mass. (See below.)

It is built up of small particles. 

Chemistry teaches us that there are 92 kinds of matter which cannot be subdivided, called the chemical elements. 

The smallest particles of the elements are called atoms, hence

there are 92 varieties of atoms. These unite in many ways to form molecules, which make up the objects of the physical world. For example, each molecule of water contains two atoms of hydrogen and one of oxygen, and each molecule of common salt contains an atom of sodium, a metal, and one of chlorine, a gas.

Atoms in turn contain smaller particles, charges of positive and of negative electricity. The negative units are called electrons and the positive are named protons. Today it is generally believed that matter is made up of electricity as there is no evidence to indicate that it contains anything else. Combinations of electrons and protons form atoms, atoms unite in many combinations to form the types of molecules which make up the many different substances which we know as matter. In atoms the positive and the negative charges are intimately associated. It is when these are separated that we have electrical phenomena, such as the lightning and electric power.

The ether. We know that matter can act on other matter across empty space. The earth draws bodies to itself, causing them to fall. This occurs in a vacuum and beyond the limits of the atmosphere as is evidenced by the meteors that are drawn to the earth. Again, light and radio waves pass through a vacuum. There are reasons for believing that these are forms of wave motion. But there cannot be waves in absolutely nothing. Here we have reasons for believing that empty space contains a something which has been named the "ether" from the Greek word aither meaning the blue sky of heaven. (Do not confuse this with the liquid of the same name which is used by surgeons to produce temporary unconsciousness.) 66 99 It may be that the ether that accounts for light is not the same as the one that accounts for gravitation, but until we have a reason for believing otherwise we shall speak of only one ether.

The field of gravity surrounds the earth and grows weaker

INTRODUCTION 

7 with respect to the earth as we pass out into space. The field of gravity of the sun is much stronger than the field of gravity of the earth and all bodies within the solar system come under its influence. Far out in space, at a great distance from all stars, there is practically no field of gravity. If we could place a stone there, it would remain motionless until some star approached near enough to attract it. 

Notice the action of the field of gravity of the earth. 

When we lift a ball above the table we realize that there is some force pulling it down. The higher we lift the ball, the greater will be its speed as it falls to the table when released. 

Our experiences have taught us that this force reaches out from the earth in all directions and extends throughout all space surrounding the earth. We call the range of this force about the earth, the earth's "field of gravity" and the force itself the "force of gravity." The field of gravity exists everywhere throughout a space such as a room, but the force of gravity exists at those points only where there is some object upon which the field of gravity can act. 

Mass. That property of every bit of matter that enables it to be acted upon by the field of gravity is called mass. Hence, we say: "Mass is the amount of matter in a body as determined by its weight." Mass and weight are not the same. Weight is the measure of the pull of the field of gravity on a body, a force, while mass is the matter within the body upon which the field of gravity acts. Far out in space a body has the same mass as it would have upon the earth, but it has no weight whatever.

The field of gravity on the surface of the sun is nearly thirty times as strong as that at the surface of the earth. 

A boy who weighs 120 lb. here would weigh thirty times as much there or about one and one-half tons. He would be unable to lift himself if it were possible for him to live there which, of course, is not the case. If he could go to the asteroids, small planets between Mars and Jupiter, he would find the fields of gravity there very weak, and because of

his small weight would be able to jump hundreds of feet into the air. There bodies would fall as we see them fall in the "slow motion pictures.' 99 The field of gravity of the earth is stronger at the poles than at the equator. Delicate spring scales would show an increase in weight of any object there, but beam balances would not. Does the reader see why this is so? The increase in weight is about one pound in two-hundred. 

The attraction of gravity furnishes the simplest and the most accurate means of determining the mass of a body. 

There is one other way of doing this. It is through the property of inertia. If we kick a small, empty box it moves across the room swiftly, but if it is filled with stones the effect is quite different. Masses may be compared and measured through the effects produced when forces act upon them. 

This is more fully explained on page 37. Here we note that there are two, and only two, means known by which mass may be determined through the attraction of gravity and through the property of inertia. Every bit of matter has some mass, and hence may have weight. A balloon of hydrogen rises and appears to have no weight. This is because it is raised by the air which is heavier and forces it up. In the same way a block of pine tends to rise when held beneath the water. The mass and weight of the balloon and of the pine may be determined when due allowance is made for the lifting effect of the mediums surrounding them. 

Mass is not affected by physical and chemical changes, at least not enough to be detected by the most delicate instruments. One pound of hydrogen and eight pounds of oxygen unite and form nine pounds of water, and nine pounds of water may be broken up into one pound of hydrogen and eight pounds of oxygen. We can take any particles of matter whatever, combine them in any way we please, subject them to heat, cold, pressure, electric discharges, or any other agency at our command, yet we cannot change

INTRODUCTION 

9 

the mass of the particles. The " Law of the Conservation of 

Matter states that matter can be neither created nor destroyed. What is meant here is that the amount of mass of matter remains unchanged. Whenever a body shows less mass than it did before, we know that some particles have been removed from it.

It is now believed that there are two exceptions to the above law, one here on earth and one in the interior of the stars. The first has to do with radium and other radioelements. Radium changes to other elements and gives off enough heat to melt its own weight of ice every hour. There are reasons for believing that this change is accompanied by the disappearance of a small amount of mass, but too slight to be detected by weighing. Again, it is believed that the sun has been giving out light and heat for possibly millions of millions of years. This cannot be accounted for by any theory other than the theory that the mass of the sun is changing into energy. 

There are several good reasons for believing this to be so. These are explained in recent books on astronomy, astro-physics, and relativity. 

We shall learn that light, heat, and all forms of energy may be converted into motion. If matter changes into energy, then it looks as though matter itself is some form of motion of a primary substance. In this connection we note that late experiments indicate that atoms are the seat of some kind of waves.

Motion and space only. We may think of motion without considering the amount of mass moved. The comparison of motions in this way gives us the concept of speed and velocity. These two words mean about the same except that we think of velocity as being positive or negative depending on the direction of the motion. Two velocities are equal only when the directions as well as the speeds are the same. 

The motion of one body can be detected only when its positions are compared with the position of some other body that is thought of as being at rest. The much discussed

"Theory of Relativity" arose from the fact that, though the most delicate experiments had been carefully performed, yet excepting rotary motion about an axis, motion through space itself is impossible to detect. All motion must be considered as relative to some other bit of matter. 

For most common purposes we measure motions relative to the earth, which is considered as being at rest. But the earth, of course, is not at rest. Its rotation causes bodies at the equator to have a speed of over one thousand miles per hour. It also revolves around the sun with the speed of 18.5 miles per second. Moreover, it follows the sun in its course among the stars and this speed is about 11 miles per second. Each day we are a million miles away from that point in space where we were the day before. 

If motion through space cannot be detected, how do we know that the sun moves? The answer is that it is very unlikely that, on the average, the speeds of the stars on one side of the solar system should be away from it and that of those on the other side towards it. Our relative speed with respect to any one star may be in any direction, and with respect to some stars it is one or more hundred miles per second.

It is hard for us to realize that motion through space cannot be detected. This is because ordinary movements make themselves felt through irregular jars. If the track is smooth and the motion steady, children may play marbles on the floor of a Pullman car even though it be moving at a high speed. We should think of the earth as a great car moving along a path of absolute smoothness. 

Time. Time is a convenient common denominator,. as one might say, with which to compare speeds. To be sure, time would continue if all clocks stopped and even though the earth ceased to turn. But there would be no time if all motion ceased. This would mean, not only that our hearts would stop beating, but that no chemical changes would occur within or about us.

INTRODUCTION 

11 The earth turning on its axis moves with unvarying speed. 

Whenever we use time we are in reality comparing the speed of certain changes with that of the revolving earth. 

Velocity and acceleration. There are indeed few cases where a body maintains the same speed or the same velocity for any length of time. (Note the difference between speed and velocity as explained above.) When the speed of a body is increasing we say it has a positive acceleration, when decreasing, a negative acceleration. If negative acceleration continues the body will come to rest and then begin to move in the opposite direction. As long as the acceleration is not zero, there must be a continuous change in speed.

Positive and negative velocities and accelerations are illustrated by a weight. bobbing up and down at the end of a spring. Let up be the positive direction and down the negative. When the weight is at the middle point and moving up, the velocity is positive and at its maximum, that is, the velocity is at its greatest. The acceleration is zero. After it passes the mid-point negative acceleration begins to act to slow down the motion. Negative acceleration is at its maximum at the highest point to which the weight moves, and at this point the velocity is zero. The velocity becomes negative as the weight begins to move down and the negative acceleration becomes less and less until at the mid-point it becomes zero at which point the negative velocity is at its maximum. 

Below the mid-point as the ball continues to fall, the acceleration is positive, reaching a maximum at the lowest point to which the ball descends. The velocity is negative when going down and positive when returning. Notice that when 

FIG. 2. WEIGHT ON SPRING

either acceleration or velocity changes from positive to negative it must pass through the value zero. 

One speaks of velocity as so many feet per second but of acceleration as so many feet per second per second. The reason for this can be seen from the following. Suppose the acceleration of a train starting from rest is 22 feet per second per minute. At the end of the first minute its speed is 22 feet per second, at the end of the second minute it is 44 feet per second and at the end of the third minute the speed is 66 feet per second. At the end of the fourth minute the speed is 88 feet per second which is the same as 60 miles per hour. (It is convenient to remember this number.) 

The acceleration is 22 feet per second per minute and in four minutes the speed acquired is 60 miles per hour. Now 

22 divided by 60, the number of seconds in a minute, is .3666 and an acceleration of 22 feet per second per minute is the same as an acceleration of .3666 feet per second per second. This is written .3666 ft./sec.2 in order to save space. 

In like manner a velocity of 100 feet per second is written 100 ft./sec. 

Acceleration may be constant or it may be variable. 

That is, it may remain the same all the time or it may change. 

A stone falling a few hundred feet is said to have constant acceleration as the gain in speed is uniform. A train starting from rest has variable acceleration. The gain in speed becomes less and less until there is no acceleration whatever and the train moves at a uniform speed. If such were not the case the train would soon be moving inconceivably fast. 

If the acceleration were constant and 2 ft./sec.² the speed would be that of a rifle ball in twenty minutes. 

Mass and motion. When we consider both the mass of a moving object and also its speed we arrive at the concept of ENERGY. We do not think of motion as something that can be measured. We see a ship steaming into a harbor and a gull circling about its masts. We do not think of the ship as possessing more motion than the bird, although in a sense

INTRODUCTION 

13 this is the case. If the motion of the ship were used in winding up some giant spring, as of an ancient catapult, then upon release the spring could give a speed equal to that of the gull to many stones each weighing as much as the bird. When motion is thought of as a measurable thing that can pass from one object to another, we call it energy.

When this motion is actually 

FIG. 3. GULL AND SHIP 

taking place, we call it kinetic energy. 

The kinetic energy of the moving ship is enormously greater than that of the flying gull.

Whenever energy passes from one moving object to some other body almost always some of it is converted into heat. 

But this apparently lost energy still exists as heat which also is a form of motion. If a block is slid along the floor it soon comes to rest through friction. Friction produces heat as can be demonstrated by rubbing the hands together. 

The kinetic energy of the block is transformed into heat. 

In some cases the percentage of energy converted into heat is readily found. Suppose we drop a tennis ball to the floor through the distance of five feet and it bounces back to the height of four feet. The speed of rebound carries it back to but four-fifths of the height through which it fell. 

As we shall prove later, the resistance of the air and the loss at impact has used up twenty per cent of the energy present at the beginning.

In a later chapter we shall prove that kinetic energy can be measured by the product of the mass and the square of its velocity. That is, if the product of one mass and the square of its velocity is twice as great as the product of some other mass and the square of the velocity of the second mass, then the kinetic energy of the first mass is twice as great as that of the second. One pound with the speed of 100

ft./sec. has the same kinetic energy as 100 pounds having the speed of 10 ft./sec. If the speeds are the same, the kinetic energies of two bodies are proportional to their masses; if the masses are the same, the kinetic energies are proportional to the squares of the speeds. In all cases the kinetic energies are proportional to the product of the masses and the squares of their respective speeds.

The simplest case of energy passing from one body to another is when two elastic balls strike; as with billiard balls, or with tennis balls colliding in the air. Here the amount of energy lost as heat is small, and if we neglect this we can say that the amount of energy lost by one ball is the same as that gained by the other. Let M₁ and M₂ be the masses of the two balls and let v₁ and v₂ be their respective velocities before striking and u₁ and us the velocities after striking. Suppose M₁ loses energy and M₂ gains it. 

Then we can write:

occurs in the first term on the left of the equality sign, u must occur in the first term on the right of that sign; otherwise we would have a positive quantity equal to a negative one. Notice that all velocities are squared. 

Kinetic energy increases with the square of the velocity. If the velocity is doubled, the amount of energy is increased four times; if it is tripled the energy becomes nine times as great; and so on. Notice that the subscripts simply tell which

Potential energy. Sometimes a moving body comes to rest without giving its motion to some other body and 

Courtesy Utah Power and Light Company

FIG. 5. -POWER PLANT 

A power plant for the conversion of the potential energy of water into electrical energy. 

This potential energy is due to the position of the water in the gravitational field of the earth. without transferring it to heat. As an example of this, consider a ball tossed up on a roof where it remains. The motion has disappeared, but if the ball is pushed off the roof it again appears. In all such cases we know that, under proper conditions, the motion may be caused to reappear. 

We say that the energy of motion has hidden itself, as it were, in the space within or about the body, and it is now called potential energy. Other examples of potential energy

are a coiled spring, a load of gunpowder, a gallon of gasoline, and a charge of electricity.

The potential energy of a rock on the edge of a cliff can be thought of as energy stored in the space between the earth and the rock. It is due to the pull that the earth has on the rock, that is, to the field of gravity about the earth. 

In the case of the coiled spring the energy is probably due to some sort of pull between the molecules of the steel or to a strain between the atoms in the molecules. If this is true, we again have energy localized in space as was the case with the rock on the cliff. We shall later see how the potential energy of electric charges and of magnets appears to be in the space about these bodies. In many cases, perhaps in all, potential energy appears to be hidden in space, or, if we care to look upon it in another way, in the ether that fills space. 

Potential energy is sometimes called "energy of position." 

This is misleading as the potential energy of a torpedo is about the same whether carried in an airplane or a submarine. 

Radiant energy.— Light, radiant heat, X-rays, and radio waves are examples of a form of energy that is neither kinetic or potential in the ordinary sense. These are examples of radiant energy. Like potential energy they appear to be located in space or in the ether. The radiant energy from the sun is the source of nearly all the energy used on the earth. For instance it warms the air and produces the kinetic energy of the wind. It causes plants to grow and thus becomes chemical energy, due to the arrangement of atoms in wood and sap. This energy is changed into heat when wood is burned or directly to kinetic energy when the fruit of the plant is used as food by animals. As to exactly what happens when these changes take place we are, as yet, very ignorant.

The law of the conservation of energy. Energy is continually passing from one form to another, but in all cases the amount present is a measurable quantity and none is lost. Hence we have "The Law of the Conservation of

INTRODUCTION 

17 Energy " which states that energy can be neither created nor destroyed. While this law is certainly true for nearly all energy changes that take place here on earth, yet we are not at all sure that it is true throughout the universe. Today there is thought to be a relation between energy and mass and that mass is sometimes transformed into radiant energy. This transformation of mass to energy is supposed to be the source of the light and heat of the sun which is estimated to lose four million tons of mass every second. If this is true the Law of the Conservation of Matter and the Law of the Conservation of Energy should be combined into one law, and we should say: Neither mass nor energy can be created or destroyed though the one may pass into the other. 

Force. The reader should note that force and energy are not the same thing at all. A force may be defined as a "push" or "pull." It is "that which tends to change the state of rest or motion of a body." It is a set of conditions that generally exists when there is a tendency for energy to pass from one body to another. When a rock rests on the edge of a cliff, there is a tendency for the potential energy in it to change into the kinetic energy of a falling rock. 

If it weighs a ton, there is the force of one ton against the ground. This is the same whether the rock is at the top or at the bottom of the cliff, though the energies involved in the two cases are quite different. Note the use of the word "tendency" in the above. The energy change does not actually take place unless the support beneath the rock is removed and it begins to fall, but the force is there as long as there is a tendency for the falling to take place. 

The three fundamental units. All quantities in physics are measured by the use of only three fundamental units. 

These are a unit of length, of mass, and of time. We know that space is measured by a length times a length times a length, as in cubic feet. Velocity is determined by length per unit of time, or length divided by time. Acceleration is measured by length divided by time squared, and energy 

---

by mass times velocity squared. These we have already considered.

As we proceed in the study of physics, we shall see that all physical units - volts, amperes, and kilowatt-hours, for instance are expressed in terms of the three fundamental units. All other units of measurement are called derived units.

In scientific work the units most commonly used are the centimeter, the gram, and the second. These, and units derived from them, are called c.g.s. units. 

Physics. Physics is the science of the interactions between matter and energy. Astronomy, chemistry, geology and other sciences treat of matter and energy relations also, but in a more restricted sense than does physics. For instance, astronomy deals with the immense masses of the stars and their motions, while chemistry has to do with the interactions between atoms.

Note that one speaks of physics as a science. There is a great difference between knowledge and scientific knowledge. 

A man may acquire much information during the course of a long life, but his knowledge may not be scientific. Science is classified knowledge. The facts of experience are grouped according to some definite scheme, and this is so worked out that all new facts fall into their proper places in the outline. 

A study of the scheme gives one certain rules or laws. 

Through the application of these laws, one often may predict what will happen under certain given conditions. They enable one to understand the fundamental relation between various actions, and what, at first sight, may appear to be a hopeless tangle of events may become a beautiful pattern that is easily understood.

The student of physics, however, should not attempt to fit all new facts of nature into a scheme that has been worked out, but should so modify the scheme as to make allowance for such new facts as are discovered. He should try to avoid becoming" set" in his mental attitude. He should re-

INTRODUCTION 

19 99 member that the laws of physics have been radically modified at times in the past, and that facts may be brought to light that will compel us to give up some of our most cherished theories. The science of physics changes as it grows. A few years ago the "Law of the Conservation of Mass and the "Wave Theory of Light" were unquestioned. Today there is considerable doubt as to the statements in these laws. Before the time of Copernicus, it was generally believed that the sun revolved about the earth. He sought to prove that this theory was wrong, and today we believe he was right and that all the others of his day were in error. 

Even so, a new Copernicus may arise any day who will bring forward proofs that will cause us to discard others of our theories. 

IMPORTANT DEFINITIONS 

Matter Matter is that which occupies a space of three dimensions, has the property of motion in this space, possesses mass, and is made up of small particles. 

These particles are, in general, molecules. Molecules are built up of other particles called atoms. These in turn contain yet smaller divisions, electrons and charges of positive electricity. Kinetic Energy When a bit of matter possesses motion with respect to some point that is considered as being at rest, it is said to have a certain amount of kinetic energy. 

This is proportional to the product of the amount of mass and the square of its velocity. The amount of kinetic energy is definite and measurable. In a sense it is the amount of motion the mass possesses.

Potential Energy When conditions about or within a mass are such that there is a possibility of motion being imparted to it, there is said to be potential energy present. Examples of this are a rock on the edge of a cliff, a bent bow, a coiled spring, a gallon of gasoline, and a charge of electricity. The amount is definite and generally measurable.

Radiant Energy. 

Light and radio waves are forms of radiant energy. These forms are different from kinetic and potential energies in that they can exist in space at great distances from matter, perhaps having no connection with matter at times. They are like kinetic energy in that they appear to be something moving, and like potential energy in that they are intimately connected with space or the ether. Force

A force is defined as a "push" or a "pull," or as "that which can change the state of rest or motion of a body." It is a set of conditions wherein energy tends to pass from one body or one condition to another. Forces may so balance one another that no motion results. An example of this is a ball lying on a table. The force of gravity pulls it down and the force of the table against it bears it up. If the latter force is removed a certain motion results. Ether The ether is that which is supposed to fill all space, the immense distances between the stars as well as the space within and between the atoms. Its existence is assumed in order to explain how motion or energy can be transferred from one body to another when they do not touch and there is no material substance connecting them. Examples of its action are seen in the attraction of gravity which draws bodies towards the earth and holds planets in their paths; in the transfer of energy from the sun to the earth in the form of light and heat; and in certain actions of electric charges and magnets. 

To the teacher. Before assigning the next section make sure the class has mastered the matter in this introduction. One great difficulty in the teaching of physics is that pupils use words without having a clear concept of their meaning. They must understand mass and volume before they study density. We believe the best results follow when the matter in this introduction is mastered at the beginning of the course. 

The kinetic energy formula is not only to teach energy relations, but to teach the pupil to see the physical meaning of a mathematical expression. The function of subscript and exponent must be made clear. Time enough should be given to the discussion of this formula to make certain that it is understood thoroughly by the members of the class. Other formulas which occur later in the text should be treated in the same way.

CHAPTER I  SECTION 2. MEASUREMENTS 

Our space of three dimensions. Why do we not say simply, "matter occupies space," instead of specifying "a space of three dimensions "? This is because much has been written about spaces of four or more dimensions. the reader has heard of " the fourth dimension." Perhaps

While we cannot conceive of a space having more dimensions than the space in which we live, yet this does not prove that there are not other kinds of spaces. Much of the discussion of this question arises from the fact that mathematicians often use equations involving more than three dimensions. We may regard the fact that matter occupies a space of three dimensions as one of its most fundamental properties.

Matter and measurement. We consider the second fundamental property of matter as its ability to be moved about in space. From this we get the idea of measurement, whether of length, area, or volume. A unit of length is taken as the distance between marks on some piece of matter. 

This original distance is called the standard of length. 

The unit of area is a square of plane surface one unit of length along each edge. The unit of volume is the space enclosed by the surfaces of a cube that is one unit of length along each edge. In actual practice objects are not measured by applying unit areas or unit volumes to the body to be measured, as is the case in measuring length, but by utilizing the rules of geometry.

Although the unit of area is a square of plane surface, yet irregularly shaped surfaces, plane or curved, can be measured 21

in terms of plane units by imagining the surface to be cut up into a number of exceedingly small squares, and then taking the sum of the areas of these. By making these squares small enough we may determine the area of curved and irregularly shaped surfaces to any degree of accuracy desired. By using a method known in mathematics as passing to the limit," wherein little squares or cubes become infinitely small, it is proved that certain figures with curved boundaries may have their areas and volumes exactly expressed by the following formulas. The reader needs to know them thoroughly.

The law of similar figures. There is a very important law concerning the three dimensions of space. If there are two pieces of matter of exactly the same shape, but a length measured on one is twice as long as the corresponding length on the other, then any area measured on the surface of the larger body is four times as great as the corresponding area on the smaller. Also, the volume of the larger body is eight times as great as that of the smaller. The law, as proved in geometry is as follows: The areas of similar figures vary as the squares and the volumes of similar solids vary as the cubes of their corresponding linear dimensions. 

We illustrate this law by some examples. Suppose it takes seven square feet of tin to cover a box, then to cover one of the same shape but three times as high would require 7 × 3² = 63 square feet. Again, if a ball of a certain material weighs 5 pounds, then one of the same material and twice the diameter will be 23 = 8 times as large and will weigh 40 pounds. 

A boy is 5 ft. 2 in. tall and weighs 110 pounds. Another boy is of exactly the same "build" but is 6 ft. tall. The second boy is 72 inches divided by 62 inches or 1.161 times as tall as the first. The cube of 1.161 is 1.565 which shows that the volume, and hence the weight of the second boy is 1.565 times that of the first and he weighs 172.1 pounds.

Suppose we have several objects made of the same materials and of the same shape but of increasing sizes. It is seen that areas increase faster than lengths and volumes faster than areas. This is why zeppelins are made so large. 

Suppose two air ships are of the same shape, but one is three times as long as the other. While the larger offers nine times the area to the resistance of the air, it has twenty-seven 

FIG. 8. 

ZEPPELIN AND AIRPLANE 

Notice the comparative size. times the volume and, therefore, it has twenty-seven times the lifting power and can carry relatively much more powerful motors for propelling it through the air. 

Do pieces of lead ever float in the air like feathers? Yes, if they are small enough. Fine dust particles of metals and rocks settle very slowly. As we consider smaller and yet smaller pieces of a substance, the amount of the external area of the body becomes larger in proportion to its volume and weight. It is the amount of the external area of a body that determines the resistance of the air to it as it falls. 

When the external area of a body is large in comparison to its weight the object falls slowly as do feathers, paper and parachutes. It is known that comets' tails always point away from the sun. It is believed that the particles composing them are very small, and their areas so large in comparison to the mass, that the particles are

forced away from the sun by the pressure of the light falling on them.

There is another interesting fact in connection with the law of similar figures. Small objects are stronger in proportion to their weight than large ones. The strength of a bridge or a house depends on areas, the areas of cross sections of its beams, rods, and wires. The strength of an animal depends on areas, the areas of cross sections of its bones and of its muscles. If the Brooklyn bridge were ten times as 

FIG. 9.

BROOKLYN BRIDGE 

large as it is, it might fall of its own weight. If an elephant were to grow as large as a whale he would be helpless. The great dinosaurs supported their weight to a large extent by floating in water. Small animals are quicker or more nimble 

An ant can carry several times his own weight but a horse cannot. than large ones.

The law of similar figures explains an old story. A mouse, a cat and a man fall down a mine shaft. The mouse runs away unhurt, the cat limps in pain, while the man is killed. 

It also explains why inventors sometimes find that the large machine does not perform as well as does the small model.

UNITS 

The metric system. This system was worked out during the stirring times of the French Revolution. It was intended to provide one simple and accurate set of weights and measures to take the place of the many confusing systems then in use in France. The valuable feature of this system, as is also true of the United States system of coinage, is that ten of each unit make one of the next higher. This saves the user much calculating. 

For scientific purposes the standard of length is the centimeter. This is one-hundredth of the distance, at the temperature of melting ice, between the centers of two lines engraved on a platinum bar. This bar is carefully preserved by the 

French government and is known as the "Standard Meter." 

Accurate copies of this standard are in the possession of the governments of all civilized countries. 

When it was adopted the meter was supposed to be one ten-millionth of the distance from the equator to the north pole along the meridian of Paris. Later surveys have shown that this distance is about 10,002,100 of the standard meters. 

However, the length of the standard is not changed to conform with more accurate measurements of the earth.

The only metric units of length much used are the millimeter, centimeter, meter, and the kilometer. Note that 

1,000 mm equal one m; 100 cm equal one m, and that there are 1,000 m in one Km. Note also that the abbreviations of

all units larger than a meter begin with capital letters. 

It is not necessary to place a period after abbreviation of metric units, but one should use it with the English units.

The Latin words for thousand, for hundred, and for ten are respectively millia, centum, and decem. These words were used when naming the divisions smaller than the meter, as is seen at once. The Greek words for thousand, for hundred, and for ten are kilioi, hekaton, and deka, while the word for ten thousand is myrioi. These words were used in naming the divisions larger than the meter. 

Bear in mind that the diameter of a pin is about a millimeter. The centimeter is about the width of the end of a little finger. The width of a small hand is about one decimeter. The meter is a little longer than the yard and the kilometer is slightly more than six-tenths of a mile.

There are other standards of length, but the only one of importance is the yard, from which are derived feet and inches. The yard is the distance between two lines on a platinum bar prepared for the English government and kept in London.

Units of volume. 

In the metric system the unit of volume is the liter. It is one cubic decimeter or one thousand cubic centimeters.

Units of mass and of force.

Metric weights are derived from the standard kilogram, which is a platinum weight kept with the standard meter. It was intended that this weight should have the same mass as that of one liter of distilled water at four degrees Centigrade, the temperature of its greatest density. It

is about one part in thirty thousand heavier than was intended.

For most common purposes we may assume the weight of one liter of tap water, one thousand cubic centimeters, to be one kilogram. As a kilogram is a thousand grams we assume one cubic centimeter of water to weigh one gram. These values are not exact. At boiling, water is lighter by four per cent, and at room temperature by only about one-fifth of one per cent as compared to the mass of an equal volume at its freezing temperature. The errors caused by salts, gases, and other materials dissolved in the water are in general, very small.

A mosquito weighs from one to five milligrams, a house fly a few centigrams, a piece of chalk a centimeter in diameter and a centimeter in length about one gram, and a nickel five grams.

The metric ton is about 2,200 pounds. 

The kilogram is about 2.2 pounds, more exactly, 2.2046 pounds.

The unit of time. For scientific purposes the unit of time is the second. There are 365.2422 days in the year but the earth turns on its axis 366.2422 times in that period of

time. The lost day is caused by the fact that the sun appears to drop back a little each day due to the revolution of the earth about it. The speed of the earth in its orbit is slightly greater in winter when it is closer to the sun. Then the sun appears to drop back more each day than is the case in summer and it takes longer for the sun to appear to pass around the earth. Hence all days have not the same length. 

The length of the average day throughout the year is called a "mean solar day." The second is 6 X 6 X 24 of a mean solar day.

Measurements and significant figures. Physics differs from finance and pure mathematics in that almost every number it employs is derived from a measurement. Such numbers are never exact. They may be correct to within one per cent, or to within one-tenth of one per cent, or to within one-thousandth of one per cent; but they are never exact.

When we say there are 365 days in the year, we use a number of three significant figures, and it is correct to within of one per cent. If we say there are 365.24 days in a year, we use a number of five significant figures and it is correct to within one three-hundredth of one per cent. We have this number to seven significant figures, 365.2422, correct to one part in three million.

Do not confuse significant figures with decimal places. 

There are 36524.22 days in a century. This has two decimal places yet the degree of accuracy is the same as that given above for the length of a year. The accuracy of a measurement cannot be increased by reducing the result to other units. 

It depends on the nature of the measuring instruments used and the skill and care of the observer. Many numbers in physics are known to only two or three significant figures. 

The reader will find that his laboratory exercises can seldom be depended on to more than three significant figures. A few numbers in physics are known to seven or eight significant figures, due to the great accuracy with which certain measure-

ments have been made. Thus a kilogram is 2.2046223 pounds. Other figures follow the 3, but we do not know what they are.

Numbers in pure mathematics may be determined to a high degree of accuracy. The value of π has been calculated to over seven hundred places. We give a few of them here.

T = 3.14 159 265 358 979 323 846 264 338 327 950 288 419 716 939 937 510 582 097 497 459 230 781 640 628 620 899 862 803 482 534 211 · · 

FIG. 14. - CALIPERS 

The reader will seldom make a measurement correct to more than four figures; often it will be correct to three figures only. Hence he should guard against the following mistake as it involves much useless and senseless labor. 

Suppose he measures the radius of a sphere, using the micrometer calipers, and finds it to be .5071 cm, a number possibly correct to four figures. That is, we feel sure the true radius lies between .50705 and .50715 cm. Suppose he now calculates the volume by using the formula, v = πr³. 

Upon cubing the radius he gets the number, .13039097291.

He has carried more figures than he should. The original measurement contained only four figures and no figure in his calculations should contain more than five figures. For the radius cubed he should use .1304 cm³. For this reason problems in physics should be worked with decimal fractions, discarding all figures beyond a certain number. In most problems in this book the results were obtained by carrying four significant figures throughout the calculations. 

Very large and very small numbers. In chapter three we shall learn how the mass of the earth is determined. It is given as 

5,980,000,000,000,000,000,000,000,000. grams or 5.98X1027 grams

or 5,870,000,000,000,000,000,000. tons or 5.87 × 1021 tons. 

Notice the shorter way of writing these numbers. We read the first number as five, point nine eight, times ten to the twenty-seventh power grams. The 1027 means that there are twenty-seven places between the five and the decimal point. In other words, the point has been moved twenty-seven places to the left. In the other number it has been moved twenty-one places. 

The mass of the earth is here given to three significant figures. The degree of accuracy is better than of one per cent, in fact it is nearly of one per cent, a result more accurate than most determinations in school laboratories. 

In any measurement the amount of error is not the important thing, but the ratio of this to the total quantity measured. 

A million tons is nothing in comparison to the mass of the earth, a few mountain ranges more or less are a mere trifle, and even a continent or two would make no great difference. 

Again, consider a pound of strychnine bought by a druggist. 

A few grains more or less in this package makes no difference. 

But an error of only of a grain in a pill would be a serious one. 

The lightest thing known is the electron, the unit of negative electricity. There are several independent ways of determining this mass, one of which is briefly described in the last chapter of this book. One method gives the mass of this particle to five significant figures, .00 000 000 000 000 000 000 000 000 089 991 grams. This is written as 8.9991 X 10-28 grams. (Read, eight, point, nine, nine, nine, one, times ten to the minus twenty-eight power grams.) It is sufficiently correct to write this 9 X 10-28 

The minus sign shows that the decimal point has been moved to the right, in this case 28 places. This way of writing numbers is very convenient, and is often used in scientific works. It is simple enough if one remembers to count the places by which the point is moved.

If we multiply the mass of the earth and that of an electron together and then take the square root we get 2.33 grams. 

This is the mean proportional between the mass of the earth and that of an electron. The mass of the earth is as many times greater than 23 g as this mass is greater than that of an electron.

SECTION 3. DENSITY, INERTIA, AND RELATED TOPICS 

Density. Density is defined as the amount of mass per unit of volume. The density of a brick is greater than that of a pine block. This is evident at once because, if both have the same dimensions, the brick has more mass, and hence more mass for each unit of volume. The density of granite is about 170 pounds per cubic foot or about 2.6 grams per cubic centimeter.

This is more briefly written 2.6 g/cm³. 

The density of lead is about 700 lb./ft.3 or 11.37 g/cm³. 

FIG. 15.- IRON AND MERCURY 

It is customary, in scientific works, to use the Greek letter rho, written p, to represent density. If M is the mass of a substance and v its volume then, from the definition of density we have, p = We see by the formula that the mass of a body may be obtained if we multiply the density by the volume. 

Notice that the numerical value of the density of a substance depends on the units used. These may be pounds per cubic foot, grams per cubic centimeter, pounds per cubic yard, tons per cubic mile, and so on. Densities are generally given in g/cm³.

Specific gravity. The specific gravity of a substance is the ratio of its mass to that of an equal volume of water. 

We may think of it as the number of times it is heavier than the same volume of water. As one cubic centimeter of water weighs one gram we see that the density of a substance in g/cm³ is numerically equal to its specific gravity. Specific gravity is a "pure number" while density is given in terms of certain units. Thus the specific gravity of mercury is 13.6 and its density is 13.6 g/cm³. Note this distinction. Whatever the units used in measuring, the value of the specific gravity is not affected. The weight of a cubic foot of lead divided by the weight of a cubic foot of water gives 11.4, the same as if grams and cubic centimeters were used. 

A cubic foot of water weighs 62.43 pounds. To find the mass of any object in pounds we multiply its volume in cubic feet by 62.43 to get the mass of an equal volume of water and then multiply this number by the specific gravity of the material of the object. 

Archimedes' principle. The reader has probably observed that objects appear to weigh less when under water. 

This is especially noticeable when taking a heavy stone out of a pond or other shallow water. A law regarding this apparent loss of weight was announced by Archimedes of Syracuse, a Greek philosopher and one of the very greatest scientists of all time. He was murdered when Syracuse was captured by the Romans in 212 B.C.

Although this principle can be proved through the laws of liquid pressure, yet, at present we shall take it as a fact of experiment. It is as follows: The apparent loss of weight of an object immersed in a liquid is equal to the weight of the displaced liquid.

If we have a vessel completely full of a liquid, and then catch and weigh such of the liquid as runs out when some body is immersed in the liquid, we shall find that the weight of the displaced liquid is the same as the apparent loss of the weight of the object.

The volume of the liquid displaced is the same as the volume of the body immersed. Suppose the liquid is water. 

Then the weight of the displaced water in grams is the same as the volume of the object in cubic centimeters, because each cubic centimeter of water weighs one gram. 

Hence, the apparent loss of weight of the body in grams is the same as its volume in cubic centimeters. 

To find density we divide mass by volume. Hence, we can find density by dividing the mass of an object by its apparent loss of weight in water. This method will give the specific gravity of the body no matter what unit is used, since we divide the mass of the object by FIG. 16. 

the mass of an equal volume of water.

FINDING THE DENSITY OF A SUBSTANCE THAT FLOATS 

The apparent loss of weight as the paraffine passes beneath the water, in grams, is equal to the volume of the paraffine in cubic centimeters. This follows from Archimedes' principle, and because one cubic centimeter of water weighs

A floating object is held up by the buoyancy of the water, hence it appears to lose all of its weight. 

Therefore, a floating object displaces its own weight of the liquid. An object that sinks displaces less than its own weight. The heavier the cargo placed in a one gram.

boat, the lower the boat sinks in the water as it must displace an amount of liquid equal in weight to that of the boat and the cargo.

To find the density of a floating object, such as a block of paraffine, we must use a sinker. We first weigh the paraffine in air, then in air with a sinker attached which is immersed in water, then we determine the weight of the paraffine and the sinker when both are immersed in water. The difference between the last two weights is the apparent loss of weight of the paraffine when immersed in water. If this difference is in grams, we have the volume of the block in cubic centimeters.

A simple method of finding the density of liquids is to weigh an empty bottle, then weigh the bottle filled with water, and then the bottle filled with the liquid whose density is to be determined. 

The weight of the water gives the volume of liquid in the bottle, which is the same each time. Another method is to weigh some dense insoluble object in air, in water, and in the liquid. The loss of weight in water gives the volume of the solid. The apparent loss of weight in the liquid gives the weight of the same volume of this liquid. Knowing the mass and the volume of the liquid displaced by the solid, the density of the liquid can be determined.

FIG. 17. HYDROMETER 

The densities of liquids are quickly found by hydrometers. These are floating bodies with narrow stems that stand upright because of a weight at the bottom. They sink until they displace their own weight of liquid. The lighter the liquid, the deeper they sink. The density is read directly from the slender stem. Why is the stem slender?

Inertia. 

The easiest and also the most accurate means of finding the mass of a body is by weighing it. When we find the mass of a body by calculation from its volume and a table of densities we depend on weighing which was done in forming the density table. But there is another means of finding the mass of a body. This is through its inertia. 

Inertia is sometimes called a fundamental property of matter. 

This word is used to designate the fact that a body at rest tends to remain at rest and one in motion tends to continue in motion in a straight line. This follows from the Law of the Conservation of Energy. A moving body cannot change its speed unless the amount of kinetic energy present is changed. This cannot happen unless some force acts on the body to add or subtract energy from it. Hence, the property of inertia shows itself whenever a force appears. 

The two ways by which mass can be determined are through the attraction of gravity and the property of inertia. 

If a small mass is attached to the balance wheel of a watch it will cause the time-piece to run slower because of the greater inertia which the spring must work against. If a piece of wax is attached to a prong of a tuning fork it will vibrate slower as can be detected by the change in pitch. The rates of vibration of the reeds of harmonicas, clarinets, and of piano wires are affected by the amount of mass of the moving parts. If a known and an unknown mass are separately hung on a coiled spring and caused to bob up and down, the value of the unknown mass may be calculated from that of the known mass and the numbers of vibrations per second. 

In this case gravity pulls the mass down. However, another spring could be used to do this. Then mass could be found without using the attraction of gravity at all. 

To the teacher. It is expected that the pupils will make several determinations of density in their laboratory work, either following or accompanying this discussion. Some classes are required to attain a much greater proficiency in the solving of numerical problems than others. A number of these follow. The teacher will select such as

suit the requirements of his course and the mathematical proficiency of the pupils. These problems may be worked all at one time or they may be taken a few each day, as the class studies the section on the structure of matter. 

The following lecture room experiment is worth performing here. 

It is not essential, but it creates a lively interest, helps in understanding mass, and furnishes a good introduction to a type of proportion much used in physics.

It is based on the fact that the period of vibration of a weight attached to a spring is proportional to the square root of the mass, onethird of the weight of the spring itself being included. 

One needs a spiral spring, and support, a Jolly balance does nicely, a standard weight of several hundred grams, a stone or a piece of metal of about the same mass, a seconds' pendulum or stop-watch, and scales and weights. The calculations should be performed by the class, or on the blackboard by the teacher, as the experiment progresses. 

Weigh the spring. Attach the known mass and find the time of 100 or more vibrations. Find the period. Call this P1. Call known mass plus weight of spring M₁. Now attach unknown mass and repeat, finding P2. Find M₂ from the proportion: 

P₁: P₂ :: √M₁: √M2 

2 Subtract of weight of spring to find value of the unknown mass. 

Weigh the unknown mass on the balances, find the error and the per cent of error. After a little practice, it is easy to keep this within one per cent. This mathematical review is extremely valuable.

SECTION 4. THE STRUCTURE OF MATTER 

Molecules. All matter is made up of small particles called atoms. In many cases these are grouped together to form small units called molecules. It is easy to show that all matter is made up of particles, but it is not so easy to show that atoms and molecules have definite sizes and masses, depending on the kind of matter. When a gas is admitted into a room it quickly distributes itself throughout this space. This indicates that we are dealing with very small particles in very rapid motion. As all substances are reduced to gases or vapors if the temperature is made high enough, we know that all substances are composed of particles.

That each kind of molecule and atom has a definite mass and size was first suspected from the way in which chemicals
