FIG. 18. -PERIODIC TABLE 

In the chart the names of the more familiar elements only are listed. The number above and to the left of the name is the atomic number, the number to the right the atomic weight. Elements 57 to 72 inclusive, the "Rare Earths," do not fit into this table. The two where the question mark is placed have not yet been discovered. 

Elements in the same column have similar chemical properties. The atomic number gives the number of free positive charges on the nucleus, and also the number of electrons in the various shells of layers when it is electrically neutral. 

The mass of the oxygen atom is taken as 16 and all others are compared with it.

combine. One pound of hydrogen and eight pounds of oxygen unite to form nine pounds of water, and only this much water can be formed from one pound of hydrogen, no matter how much oxygen is present. This sort of thing happens in all chemical reactions and is expressed as the "Law of Definite Proportions." The explanation is that each molecule of water must contain just so much hydrogen and so much oxygen. The same thing is true for other elements and for all chemical compounds. The simple explanation is that each molecule is like all other molecules of its kind in being a union of the same number of the same kinds of atoms, and so all like molecules have the same mass.

Today we have means of determining the masses of individual atoms directly, and also the distances between their centers when they form solid bodies. A brief description of how this is done is given in the last chapter of this book. 

Molecules and atoms are very small and are invisible by means of the most powerful microscopes. As a rough means of comparison we may say that a small drop of water is as large when compared to a water molecule as the whole earth is to a human being. While we know the mass and something of the size of many kinds of molecules, we know little as to their shape and nothing as to what they would look like if they could be magnified until made visible. 

The diameters of the simpler molecules are approximately 

10-8 cm. 

Atoms. The smallest particles that show characteristic properties of the different chemical elements are called atoms. 

An element is a substance that cannot be broken up into two or more kinds of matter. Substances which are not elements are compounds. The molecule is the smallest particle of a compound that shows its characteristic properties.

In the study of chemistry we learn how to determine the numbers of and the kinds of atoms that make up the mol-

ecules of all substances. An element can be thought of as a substance made up of only one kind of atom. 

The molecule of water contains three atoms, two of hydrogen and one of oxygen. The metal sodium is light and silvery but sputters about and sometimes catches on fire when placed on water. The element chlorine is a greenish yellow gas which disintegrates flesh and is poisonous. Yet one atom of sodium and one of chlorine unite to form a molecule of common salt, a harmless substance necessary in our food. The properties of a compound are often very different indeed from those of the elements of which it is composed. 

The number of atoms which unite to form a molecule of an element varies with conditions. Molecules of hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, and chlorine contain two atoms. Helium and mercury vapor contain but one. Sulphur molecules contain from eight to one atom, depending on the temperature. In many solids the atoms are not grouped into definite molecules. In some ways a crystal is like a large molecule. In common salt crystals the sodium atoms are not paired off with particular chlorine atoms, but the two form a regular pattern throughout the crystal.

The three states of matter. - The three states of matter are solid, liquid, and gas.

A given mass of solid re- FIG. 19. 

tains the same volume and the same shape; a given mass of a liquid retains the same volume but assumes 

THE ARRANGEMENT OF ATOMS IN ROCK SALT 

The light circles represent sodium atoms and the dark chlorine.

The distance between atoms is 

2.81 x 10-8 cm. the shape of the containing vessel; while a gas has no definite size or shape but fills any vessel in which it is placed.

The molecules of liquids can move about with respect to each other with great freedom. This is not true of solids. 

Here each molecule is held more or less tightly in its proper

place. The softer the solid, the less firmly it is held. The molecules of gases are in rapid motion. Though there is some attraction between these, yet their kinetic energy overcomes this, and they fly past each other, travelling back and forth throughout the extent of the vessel that contains them. A certain number of molecules escape from some liquids by evaporation, and even from some solids. An evidence of this is that the odor of gasoline or camphor quickly spreads throughout a room. A certain amount of energy must be used to separate molecules as they are held together in a substance. 

Physical and chemical changes. A chemical change is said to take place whenever the arrangement of the atoms in molecules is changed. Other changes are said to be merely physical. The electric current is used to separate water into its elements, hydrogen and oxygen. This is a chemical change, but when water freezes to ice or passes into steam, only physical changes occur. The molecules of ice and of steam contain the same kinds and arrangement of atoms as those of water, but the molecules of oxygen and hydrogen do not. 

Atomic weights. Much labor has been devoted to the finding of the relative masses of atoms. The results of this work are given in tables of atomic weights. The unit of mass here is one-sixteenth of that of the oxygen atom. 

This unit causes the masses of many types of atoms to come out in whole numbers. That of hydrogen is 1.008 instead of unity. The heaviest atom is that of the metal uranium with a mass of 238.2.

It is now known that, in many cases where the atomic weight is not a whole number, the element is composed of two or more kinds of atoms of slightly different masses. 

The atomic weight of chlorine is 35.46. It is composed of atoms having masses of 35 and 37, but always mixed in such proportion that the average mass of a chlorine atom is 35.46. 

These two kinds of atoms are called the two isotopes of

chlorine. Silver has two isotopes, zinc four, and mercury at least six.

We not only know the relative masses of atoms but also their actual masses. That of the hydrogen atom is 1.66 X 10-24 grams. 

Electrons and positive nuclei. — The atom in turn is built up of parts. We are sure that it contains at least two things, positive and negative electricity. The positive charge seems to be, in a way, the heart of the atom, and it cannot be separated from it. Some of the negative electricity can be removed from the atom. It is found in small units, all of the same mass and electric charge, called electrons. An atom consists of a central part containing positive electricity called the "nucleus." About this electrons are arranged, forming, as it were, various layers or shells to the atom. 

A normal atom contains an equal amount of positive and of negative electricity. If some of the electrons are removed from the atoms of a body, an excess of positive electricity remains and the body has a positive charge. When a body has an excess of electrons it has a negative charge. An electric current consists of a flow of electrons. not absolutely sure that this is true, yet, all the evidence we have today seems to show that matter is made up of positive and negative electricity only. 

While we are

Finding the size of molecules. It is difficult to imagine the smallness of the size of molecules. Suppose we had 

100,000,000 watches, each ticking four times a second. If these watches were to run for 15,000 years, then the total number of ticks of all the watches would approximate the number of molecules in a teaspoonful of water.

A simple but very inaccurate method of estimating the size of molecules is by allowing oil to spread over water. 

The water is first dusted with a light powder, and a drop of oil dissolved in benzene is then placed on it. From the percentage of oil in the mixture and the weight of the drop the mass of the oil can be determined. From the mass and

the density the volume is found. The volume divided by the area of water covered gives the thickness of the film, which can not be less than one molecule. In one experiment .0001 g of oil spread over 900 square centimeters. The density was .89, and hence the film was 1.2 X 10-7 or .00000012 cm thick. From this we get some idea as to the size of molecules. There are about a dozen other entirely independent ways of doing this, the more accurate methods giving results that agree within a few per cent. Two of the most accurate are described in Chapter VI, page 247, and in Chapter VIII, page 414. 

Inside the atom. The lightest atom is that of hydrogen. 

It consists of one positive charge, a proton, and one negative charge, an electron. There is much evidence that points to the fact that the electron revolves about the proton somewhat as the earth does about the sun. It is believed that the other atoms have more than one proton united to form the nucleus. Passing down the periodic table we find helium with two electrons revolving about a nucleus of four protons and two electrons. This gives two positive charges on the nucleus and the same number of free electrons outside the nucleus. Lithium has three free electrons, and, as we pass down the table, each element has one more free electron until we come to the last element, uranium, which has 92. 

Nearly all the mass of an atom is in its nucleus. A proton weighs 1846 times as much as an electron. Hence the helium atom is about four times as heavy as a hydrogen atom. The uranium atom is over 238 times as heavy as one of hydrogen, and hence we believe its nucleus contains about this number of protons. As it has only 93 electrons outside the nucleus, and as it is electrically neutral, we believe it has a number of electrons inside the nucleus.

The hydrogen atom, with its single electron, has a diameter between 2 and 3 × 10-8 cm. The nucleus is supposed to be very much smaller than this, smaller by some hundreds of thousands of times. It is known that an electron will,

under certain conditions, shoot through a half-million atoms before it comes near enough to a nucleus or to another electron to be thrown off its course. It must have a great velocity, because, ordinarily, electrons do not penetrate atoms nor do atoms penetrate each other. They are kept apart by powerful electrical forces of repulsion. Hence we speak of the impenetrability of matter, that is, two things cannot occupy the same space at the same time. But this does not mean that there is not much empty space inside the atoms. 

It may be that there is proportionally more empty space there than we think of as being inside the solar system. 

These electrical forces, when attracting, hold the parts of atoms together, hold atoms in groups to form molecules, and also bind the molecules of liquids and solids together. 

The electron. There are several independent methods of determining the mass of an electron and also the amount of negative electricity it represents. These values check each other so accurately that we are quite confident when speaking of them. But we are uncertain as to what an electron really is. We are not sure as to its shape, or what one would look like if it were possible to make it visible. 

There are two experiments, based on different principles, that indicate that electrons are some sort of waves, or at least are accompanied by waves. This is further supported by later theories. These theories, based on the supposition that electrons are waves, explain the facts of experiment even better than those which supposed the atom to be a sort of minute solar system. It has long been held that light is waves which travel with great speed. It may be that matter is made of stationary waves, and that thus both matter and radiant energy are fundamentally related. As to these theories, there remains much to be discovered. 

The atomic numbers. In the periodic table the elements in the same columns have similar chemical properties. The number of the place which an element occupies in the periodic table is called its atomic number. In all cases except three

the atomic weight increases with the atomic number. The exceptions are argon, cobalt, and tellurium. These are placed before atoms of less mass than themselves. 

Long after Mendeleef first wrote the periodic table, a method was discovered by which the atomic number of any element can be determined directly by the use of X-rays. (Moseley's Law.) All the evidence points to the fact that the atomic number of an element is determined by the number of free positive charges on the nucleus of its atom. 

Hence it is the electrical charge on the nucleus, and not the mass of an atom, which determines its physical and chemical properties. 

Molecular forces. - It is well known that like kinds of electricity repel and unlike kinds attract each other. This fact, together with the various groupings of the electrons in the outer shells of atoms, probably accounts for those forces that bind atoms together to form molecules and that hold the molecules of solids and liquids in place. 

The force that binds atoms to form molecules is called chemical attraction, that which binds like molecules together is called cohesion, and that which binds together the molecules of two unlike substances is called adhesion. Cohesion holds together the molecules of a drop of water or a steel rod. 

Adhesion holds water to a wet stick and paint to a wall. 

There is probably little, if any, difference in the nature of these two forces. All three are probably the result of electrical charges.

Each of these forces indicates energy relations. The energy set free in an explosion, whether of powder, nitroglycerine, or gasoline vapor is due to the force of attraction between atoms. Energy is changed into the potential form when these atoms are then separated. Heat tends to weaken the force of cohesion and sometimes it overcomes the force of chemical attraction. It requires much force to break a piece of steel, yet at the temperature of 2450° Centigrade it begins to boil and becomes a vapor.

Cohesion acts only when molecules are very close together. 

If the parts of a broken solid are pressed in place, as a rule it does not mend. Due to the irregularities of the surface, it is probable that but few of the molecules come close enough to others for cohesion to act. It may be that the molecules of the broken surfaces are affected by the air or some other agency. If the two surfaces are ground very smooth and then placed together, there is a tendency for them to cohere. Pieces of softer materials are more likely to cohere when pressed together with considerable force than pieces of harder materials. For example, if two pieces of lead are pressed together with considerable force they will cling to each other. Being soft, a large number of molecules can be forced close to the molecules of the opposing surface. 

The cohesion of liquids is quite strong. If a piece of flat glass is placed on water, a considerable force is necessary to tear it away and when this is done, drops of water adhere to the glass. The force was used in separating water molecules. 

Here adhesion is stronger than cohesion. This does not happen if the liquid is mercury, in which case adhesion is very weak. 

Special properties of matter. The following properties are due to the difference in the force of cohesion when different kinds of molecules and atoms are considered. This force in turn is determined by the number and the arrangement of the electrons in the outer parts of the atoms and molecules. 

Hardness and softness are properties defining the ease with which a solid may be scratched.

Brittleness is the property which determines the ease with which the material can be shattered by a blow. Taffy may be both soft and brittle.

Toughness results when the force of cohesion is strong and there is an absence of brittleness.

Ductility is the property that permits the material to be drawn out into a wire. Iron and hot glass are ductile. Lead and zinc are not.

Malleability is the property which permits the substance to be hammered into thin sheets. It is the opposite of brittleness. Gold is the most malleable of metals. It has been hammered to such thinness that one cubic inch would cover a surface four feet wide and five hundred feet long. 

Elasticity has to do with the force with which a substance tends to return to its former size or shape after stretching, bending, twisting, or compressing. One material is said to have a greater elasticity than another when, comparing pieces of the same size and shape that have received the same deformation, the one exerts a greater force in tending to return to its original condition than does the other. The limit of perfect elasticity is that point to which a body may be deformed and yet return to its original condition. If deformed beyond that point, it receives a permanent "set," that is, it does not return perfectly to its former condition. 

Adhesion. That force which binds the molecules of unlike substances together is called adhesion. A good example of this is the binding of pieces of wood together by means of glue. 

The phenomenon of solution has to do with the adhesive force of liquid molecules. Whether a solid will dissolve in a liquid or not depends on the nature of both the solvent and the solute. Shellac dissolves in alcohol, but not in water or kerosene. Paraffine dissolves in the latter but not in either of the first two mentioned. Sugar is very soluble in water but not in alcohol or kerosene. If the adhesive force between a solvent and a solute is great the solute readily dissolves.

The attraction of the molecules of one liquid for those of another is sometimes very great. Sulphuric acid holds tightly to water that is added to it. In fact, air is dried by bubbling it through concentrated sulphuric acid. The acid takes up the water and retains it. 

Liquids may hold in solution many times their own volume of some gases. Aqua ammonia is a gas dissolved in

The flotation of ores is an example of selective adhesion. adheres to the ore particles but not to the valueless ones. 

The ore is ground in water and an oil or chemical is added which Another oil is sometimes now added which helps to form bubbles as compressed air is passed through the mixture. The ore particles adhere to the bubbles but the gangue does not and they are separated from the gangue because they float away with the bubbles.

water. One pint of cold water may hold one thousand pints of ammonia gas. Soda water may be caused to hold 

1.8 times its volume of carbon dioxide gas. 

Gases also adhere to the surfaces of solids. In exhausting a vacuum tube the glass is heated to drive out gases in the glass. The metal copper adsorbs hydrogen gas, as this action is called. Note that the word is different from absorption which is applied to solids and liquids. 

A rag is used to absorb water.

WATER ON 

GLASS has been absorbed by the water.

The use of the gas mask depends on the property possessed by charcoal to adsorb gases. 

Charcoal is very porous so that much surface is available for the adherence of particles of the gas. Heat- 

The air on the glass ing drives out adsorbed gases, while cold increases the ability of the solid to hold them. High vacua are obtained by attaching a tube of charcoal to the exhausted vessel and immersing it in liquid air. The charcoal then takes up nearly all of the remaining air or other gases.

The phenomena of capillarity and surface tension depend on adhesion and cohesion. These will be taken up in 

Chapter III. 

To the teacher. If the discussion of the electrical nature of matter is left until after the topic of electricity has been taken up, there is not enough time remaining for the pupils to get well acquainted with this theory. If begun early in the course, it helps greatly in forming a true perspective of physical theory. Experience shows that to take it up here gives superior results. This information is now so common that most pupils are more or less familiar with it. One purpose of this course is to evaluate and to organize such knowledge as the pupil has already acquired. Pupils read many articles in magazines that give them false ideas as to the accepted modern theories. One function of the physics teacher is to sift that which the scientific world accepts from that which is fanciful speculation.

CHAPTER II  SECTION 5. MEASURING POTENTIAL AND KINETIC ENERGY 

We shall now learn how energy is measured. That one cannot judge its magnitude accurately by means of the senses was brought to the writer's attention one August day as he toiled with a party of climbers towards the summit of a mountain in Colorado. After hours of hard climbing they reached timberline and sat beside a snow-bank to rest. Each felt that he had expended a prodigious amount of energy, but a short calculation showed that no member of the party had expended more energy than could have been bought from a power company for about one penny. Today electric power alone does an amount of work which is far in excess of that which could be done by the hands of all the people living in the world. This application of physics to manufacturing enables each of us to enjoy possessions that even the kings of the ancient world could not obtain. 

Nearly all the energy we use comes from the sun as radiant energy. The few exceptions are, the heat from the interior of the earth as found in hot springs and volcanoes, the energy of radium and other radio-elements, that of the tides, and the minute amount of heat that comes from the stars. The energy of the tides comes from the kinetic energy of the rotating earth, as they act as a brake to slow up the rotation. 

Thus the length of the day is increased, but by such a small amount that no clock can detect it.

Energy changes. Let us trace the transfer of energy. 

A certain amount of energy comes from the sun in the form of light and heat and falls upon the green leaf of a wheat 1 59

stalk. The plant uses this energy to build certain atoms into molecules of starch, which are stored in the wheat kernel. 

The energy is in the potential form due to certain groupings of atoms, and may become free again when these atoms combine with certain others.

The wheat is harvested and ground into flour. An archer eats the bread into which it is made and in the muscles of the man the energy acts to draw back his bow. It is now potential and in the bent wood. As the arrow flies the energy passes into the kinetic form. The shaft strikes a tree. What becomes of the energy? It passes into heat and produces an increase in the speed of the motion of the molecules of the arrow and the tree which it has struck. Then, as heat waves or radiant energy, it passes back into the ether from which it came. What finally becomes of it is a great mystery. Does it pass on and on forever? As there seems to be a relation between mass and energy, mass changing into radiant energy, it has been suggested that somewhere in the depths of the universe the radiant energy is reconverted into matter. We have no evidence, however, that this is true. 

Much of the energy received from the sun passes back into space without being put to any use whatever, and the ratio of the total amount sent out by the sun to that which falls upon the earth is about the same as twenty million dollars is to one penny.

Measuring potential energy. So far as the measuring of it is concerned, the simplest form of energy is the potential energy of a lifted weight. Experiments with levers and pulleys show that one pound in falling vertically five feet will lift five pounds one foot, ten pounds one-half foot, and Hence this form of energy can be measured in footpounds, gram-centimeters, or any other similar units. 

Here we use the force-pound, not the mass-pound. We have in mind the force used in lifting the mass and the distance through which the force acts. To lift one pound vertically one foot at the surface of the sun would require so on.

about thirty of our earth foot-pounds, though the mass lifted would be only one pound.

When a spring is compressed, a bow bent, or a rubber band stretched, the amount of work performed is equal to the product of the force employed and the distance through which it overcomes opposition. Moreover, one foot-pound of work stored in a spring will do just one foot-pound of work in lifting a weight. Thus all these forms of potential energy can be measured in foot-pounds, or any other force-distance unit.

FIG. 21. MAN LIFTING BARREL 

Work is often measured by the through which it acts. 

It is easy to see that when one rolls a barrel weighing one hundred pounds up an inclined plank into a wagon 4 ft. high, one must product of a force by the distance perform 400 ft.-lb. of work, because 100 lb. have been lifted 4 feet. But there are many other simple cases besides the lifting of weights. If a boy pulls on a sled with the force of 4 lb. and drags it the distance of 100 ft., he also does 400 ft.-lb. of work. Or he may turn the crank or handle of a grindstone, a churn, or some other simple machine and do work. If he pulls on the handle with the force of 10 lb. and makes 100 turns, the circumference of the arc made by the handle being 5 ft., then he does 5,000 ft.-lb. of work. In each case we multiply the force by the distance through which it acts. This is sometimes written as the formula: W = FD, where W is work, F the force, and D the distance. 

Measuring kinetic energy. Before we can go further in this subject it is necessary to learn something of the behavior of masses when forces act upon them and they are free to move without friction. An example of this sort is a freely falling body. The friction in such a case is so small that it may be neglected in our calculations. If a mass of

one pound is dropped, it is pulled downward with the force of one pound and moves faster and faster with a definite acceleration. If a two pound mass is falling, the force acting 

​on it is also two pounds and we have the same acceleration as before. If we neglect parachutes, feathers, dust, and other things that offer surfaces to the resistance of the air, which are relatively large in proportion to their weight, we find that all bodies fall with the same acceleration. This was first proved by Galileo, "father of modern scientific experiment," at the 

Leaning Tower of Pisa. Balls

LLL of different weights, when dropped together from the tower, fell with the same gain in speed and struck the ground at the same time. 

FIG. 22. THE LEANING TOWER OF PISA 

We know that all bodies would fall with the same gain in speed if it were not for the resistance of the air, because a feather and a coin do fall together in a tube from which the air has been exhausted. This leads.us to an interesting example of the application of the "Law of Similar Figures." 

When will a sphere of solid lead float in the air like a feather? 

The answer: When it is small enough. As a sphere becomes smaller, the surface decreases as the square of the radius decreases and the volume decreases as the cube of the radius decreases. A point is then reached when the sphere has as much surface in proportion to its weight as has a feather. We know that very fine dust floats in the air, even when composed of the densest materials. 

Experiments prove that, when a force acts upon a mass

free to move without friction, the acceleration is directly proportional to the force applied and inversely proportional to the mass acted upon. α = KF In this formula a is the acceleration, F the force, M the mass, and K a constant. We know that when we double the mass of a falling body the weight is doubled also, and hence the acceleration remains the same, and is 32.16 feet per second per second or 980 centimeters per second per second. This acceleration becomes the K in the above formula when it is applied to falling bodies because F and M are

FIG. 23. CANNON SHOOTING LEVEL 

If a gun is pointing horizontally the ball drops to the ground during its flight in the same equal and cancel. If we time it would fall vertically from the muzzle. could take a mass to the surface of the sun the mass would remain the same but the weight would be almost thirty times as great. Hence the acceleration would be over nine hundred feet per second per second.

While we cannot carry a mass to some other planet or to the sun, yet we can devise numerous experiments wherein various forces act on different masses and in this way the formula given above has been established. For instance, the expanding gases due to the explosion of gunpowder exert an enormous force on the bullet as it passes along the barrel of a gun, the acceleration is large, and the bullet leaves the gun with a great velocity.

Falling bodies. From the definition of acceleration, the rate of gain in velocity, we see that the velocity of a freely falling body at the end of the first second is 32.16 ft./sec.,

at the end of the second second it is twice as great, at the end of the third second three times as great, and so on. 

Thus we have, v = gt. 

Here g is 32.16 ft./sec.2 or 980 cm/sec.2, t is the time in seconds, and v is the velocity acquired at the end of this time. The value of the acceleration of gravity is not exactly the same at all points on the surface of the earth, but as the difference is never more than one-half of one per cent, we need not concern ourselves with it here. 

When t seconds have passed, the falling body does not pass over the distance v in the next second. This is the distance it would have covered if it had continued with the same speed it had at the end of t seconds. In fact, due to the continued gain in speed, it passes over a greater distance than v in the next second.

If the mass acted on by a force is not a falling body but is some such body as a bullet in motion as it is fired from a gun, V=9t S=9 

FIG. 24. ROCKS FALLING 

then the acceleration must be determined by the conditions of the problem. In this case we do not use g to represent the acceleration but the letter a. 

Hence we write, v = at. 

Here the acceleration, a, may have any value, and must be determined from the conditions of the problem.

We now wish to find how far a body falls in a certain length of time, say five seconds. The velocity at the end of this time is 32.16 × 5 or 160.8 ft./sec. The body starts from rest and the gain in speed is uniform throughout the

five seconds. Hence the average speed is one-half the final speed or 80.4 ft./sec. This average speed multiplied by the time, five seconds, gives 402. ft., the distance the body falls. 

It is customary to let S be the distance through which a body falls, or the space covered. The velocity at the end of any number of seconds, t, is gt ft./sec., and the average velocity throughout this time is gt/2 ft./sec. The distance the body falls is the average velocity multiplied by the time, or (gt/2)t, or gt2/2. gt2 S = 912 2 

Kinetic energy. 

We are now prepared to calculate the amount of kinetic energy of a moving mass. This may be a ball thrown from the hand, a bullet shot from a gun, stone falling from a cliff, or any other body in motion. It is evident that the amount of energy depends only on the mass and the veloc- 

FIG. 25. - CANNON SHOOTING UP 

ity and not on the direction in which it is moving. A bullet leaving a gun has the same kinetic energy whether it be shot towards the north or the south, up towards the sky or down to the ground. Suppose we have a cannon that shoots a ball weighing one pound which leaves the muzzle with a velocity of 964.8 ft./sec. Let us shoot the

ball straight upward. The ball rises higher and higher losing its velocity until it comes to rest. Then it immediately begins to fall and the speed increases until, as it reaches the ground, it has the same velocity as when it left the gun. At the top of the path all the kinetic energy of the ball has become potential, and upon the return to earth it has all become kinetic again, none being lost if we do not take into consideration the resistance of the air. We wish to find how far a ball must fall to acquire a velocity of 964.8 ft./sec. We first use the formula v = gt to determine how long it takes for a body to acquire this speed and we find it to be 30 sec. (t v/g964.8/32.16 = 

30. sec.) We now use the formula S = gt2/2 to find how far a ball falls in 30 sec. The cannon would shoot one pound to the height of 14,472 ft. and the kinetic energy of the cannon ball is 14,472 ft.-lb.

We shall now work the problem in more general terms. 

Suppose we have any mass of M pounds moving with the FS-M 29 2 

FIG. 26. -BALL FALLING 

velocity of v ft./sec., or of M grams moving v cm/sec. From the equation v gt we know it will take the mass v/g seconds to acquire this speed by falling, g being the acceleration of gravity at the place we have in mind. To find how far it must fall to acquire this speed we substitute v/g for t in the formula S gt2/2 and have Sv²/2g. This is the height to which the mass must be raised so that in falling it will acquire the velocity v. The work required to lift the mass to this height is FS footpounds or gram-centimeters, F being the force with which the mass is attracted to the earth in pounds or grams, and

the unit of work being the energy required to lift a unit mass through one unit of distance at a place where the acceleration of gravity is whatever value we give to g in the formula. 

But F is numerically equal to M, the mass of the body, and v2/2g. Hence FS = Mv2/2g. We now have a formula, Mv2 2g for finding the amount of energy represented by any mass, M, moving with the velocity v, no matter how the latter is acquired. 

The dyne and the erg. In the discussion above we took our unit of energy as the amount of work required to lift a unit mass a unit distance at a place where the acceleration of gravity has the value we give to g in the formulas. Then, and then only, can we let F = M. But the field of gravity varies as much as one-half of one per cent at various places on the surface of the earth. A foot-pound or a gramcentimeter at Boston is not exactly the same amount of work as at Denver, and so on. In accurate calculations it is simpler to suppose that somewhere there is a planet where the acceleration of a falling body is but one centimeter per second per second. There bodies fall so slowly that in twenty seconds they drop only a little over six feet. The amount of energy required to lift a mass of one gram vertically one centimeter on this planet is called an erg, from the Greek work ergon meaning work. The word "energy means in ergon or in work. The force with which the mass of one gram is attracted to this planet is called a dyne from the Greek work "dynamis" meaning force. The English words dynamite and dynamic come from this root. 66 66 

99 Whether or not such a planet as we have imagined exists is immaterial. We define a dyne accurately as follows. 

The dyne is such a force that, when acting on a mass of one gram, free to move without friction, the acceleration is one centimeter per second per second. In like manner we have: An erg is the work performed when the force of one

dyne acts through the distance of one centimeter. It is a very small unit. When a mosquito crawls up the wall only one centimeter he does several ergs of work. Hence it is necessary to have the larger unit, the joule, which is 10,000,000 ergs. A joule is not quite three-quarters of a foot-pound.

If we hold up the mass of one gram and let it fall, it has the acceleration of about 980 cm/sec. The force acting on this mass is one gram. The force of a dyne would give it the acceleration of only one centimeter per second per second. 

But for equal masses, the acceleration is proportional to the force acting. Hence the force of one gram is about 980 dynes.

When finding the amount of kinetic energy of a body in ergs, the value of the acceleration of gravity is taken as unity. Hence, if E represents the energy in ergs we have the formula: E = Mv²/2.

Power. Power is the rate per unit of time at which work is performed. The watt is a unit of power, and represents one joule per second. Work divided by time gives power; power multiplied by time gives work. Thus, 40 watts for ten seconds is 400 watt-seconds or 400 joules.

A common unit is the horse power which is 33,000 footpounds per minute, or 550 foot-pounds per second. One horse power is 746 watts. A kilowatt is one thousand watts.

Hence a horse power is about three-fourths of a kilowatt. 

Work does not take time into consideration while power does. Consider a ton of sand at the foot of a hill one thousand feet high. A good truck, by expending several horse power, can carry this to the top in a short time. We can also suppose it to be carried up, grain by grain, in the jaws of an ant. 

It might take the ant a million years to do it, but when finished the same amount of work would have been performed. 

The power expended in the two cases is, indeed, very different. (1) A kilowatt-hour is the work performed by one kilowatt of power in one hour of time.

(2) A kilowatt is one thousand watts. (3) A watt is the power of one joule per second. (4) A joule is ten-million ergs. (5) An erg is the work performed by the force of one dyne acting through the distance of one centimeter. (6) A dyne is such a force that, when acting on the mass of one gram free to move without friction, the acceleration is one centimeter per second per second.

Water power.

The cruder types of water wheels have been in use for many centuries. An example is the river wheels made of bamboo which are yet used by the Chinese. 

The three types used in the early days of our country were the overshot, the breast, and the undershot.

When a small stream was utilized, the water was conducted to the top of the wheel through a trough or pipe. The weight of the water turned the wheel. This is the overshot type, and requires several feet of fall or "head." When a larger stream was used the wheel often stood in the stream, the water striking the lower part of the wheel, thus causing it to revolve. It was turned by the kinetic energy of the flowing water. This is the undershot type. The breast wheel

is a combination of these two.

The water, passing through 

FIG. 28. 

With

A COMMON TYPE OF TURBINE 

This form is very efficient at full load. 

The reaction turbine is preferred where large quantities of water are available, and has been used with as small a head of water as 3 feet and as high as 850 or more feet. 

The Pelton or impulse wheel is used where there is a comparatively small volume of water under a very great head or fall. In several cases this is over 

2,000 feet, and, naturally, enormous pressures are involved. The principle upon which it operates can be seen from the figure. a trough, strikes the wheel about the middle, and both the weight and the kinetic energy act in producing power.

The older forms of water wheels have been replaced by the reaction turbine and by the Pelton wheel. 

These wheels utilize both the pressure and the kinetic energy of the water.

Their action is similar to that of a wind mill, which is the reverse of that of an electric fan or a ship's propeller.

FIG. 29. A TYPE OF MODERN TURBINE 

The angle at which the water strikes these blades is changed by turning the blades. This operates automatically as the gates open more or less, the purpose being to insure efficiency whether the flow of water is large or small. 

The amount of flow is determined by the amount of power used.

These modern types are from 85 per cent to 93 per cent efficient, that is, they convert this percentage of the energy of the water into power. 

Impulse wheels of 25,000 horse power are no longer considered unusual, and a few are about twice as powerful. Large power stations often have several wheels. For example, one of the plants at Niagara Falls has three reaction turbines of 70,000 horse power each. We can get some idea of the immense amount of power developed here when we remember FIG. 30. -A PELTON WHEEL 

that 70,000 horses, working four abreast, would extend along forty or fifty miles of road.

SECTION 6. DETERMINING THE ACCELERATION OF GRAVITY 

The values of g. - One might suppose that the acceleration of gravity at any place is found by dropping a weight and timing the fall by means of a stop-watch. While this method gives an approximate value of the acceleration, yet

it is very inaccurate when compared to the value obtained by means of pendulums. 66 If we make a pendulum by tying a weight to a string, we see that the period, that is, the time of swing, is not influenced by the mass of the weight nor by the materials of which it is made, but depends upon the length of the pendulum. 

By a simple pendulum" we mean one composed of a spherical bob that is very heavy, indeed, in comparison to the weight of the string or wire that supports it. The "length" of such a pendulum is the distance from the point of support to the center of the bob. The period of such a pendulum can be determined from purely mathematical considerations, and is given by the formula: 

P = 2π

Here P is the period of a complete vibration, the time required for the bob to swing in one direction and back again. L is the length and g the acceleration of gravity. If L is measured in feet, then g must be in feet per second per second, and if L is in centimeters, then g must be measured in metric units also. This formula is not exactly true, but if the arc of swing is small the error due to the formula will be less than that due to measuring L or P. 

If the arc of swing is less than five degrees, the formula gives results correct to within of one per cent. To find the acceleration of gravity, we tie a heavy ball on a long, fine wire, measure L and P and calculate g by the above formula. 

FIG. 31. KATER'S PENDULUM 

The value of the acceleration of gravity is found much more accurately by means of a compound or Kater's pen-

dulum (see the figure). This consists of two heavy spheres attached to a rod. Between the spheres are two knifeedges. The positions of the spheres are adjusted until the period is almost the same when the pendulum is swung from either knife-edge. The formula used is too complicated to give here. By this instrument the value of g can be found to within one part in a million. Some results are as follows: 

Washington D. C.

Coast Survey......980.112 

New York. ..980.267 

Bureau of Standards 980.097

San Francisco. 979.965 Madras. 978.281 Oslo..... ..981.927

Laws of the pendulum. Suppose we have at the same place two pendulums of lengths Li and L, respectively. Then, from the formula, we have:

P₁ = 2π and P2 = 2π

L2 Dividing one equation by the other and cancelling out the 2 and the g, we have: 

Pi 2 

or P₁: P₂ :: VL₁: VL₂ 

L₂ P2 Law I. The period of a pendulum varies as the square root of its length. To double the period of vibration of a pendulum it must be made four times as long, to triple the period the length must be made nine times as long, etc. Suppose the same pendulum is used at places where the acceleration of gravity is different. Then by a method similar to that above we have, P₁ : P₂ :: Vg2: √g₁. 

The period of a pendulum varies inversely as the square root of the acceleration of gravity. On Mercury the acceleration of gravity is slightly more than one-fourth what it is on the earth, hence, a given pendulum would require about twice as long to make a vibration there as it would on the earth. 

Law II. The period of a pendulum is independent of the material of which it is made and of its mass.

Law III. As long as the arcs through which it vibrates are small, changes in length of arc do not affect the period sufficiently to be detected. (These last two laws have been discussed above.) α = Mass, force, and acceleration. We have already mentioned the fact that, when a mass is free to move without friction, the acceleration imparted to that mass by a force is directly proportional to the force and inversely proportional to the mass. This is expressed as a formula by 

KF/M. Here a is the acceleration, F the force, M the mass, and K a constant depending on the units used. 

This law can be verified by experiments in the laboratory. 

The reader will find them described in advanced texts in physics, but we shall not discuss them here. The best proof of the law is in the motions of the heavenly bodies. 

Astronomers have equations by means of which they are able to foretell the positions of the moon, planets, comets, and stars. These equations are derived from this law. 

When we remember how accurately the time of an eclipse can be calculated, we realize how true are the assumptions made in the calculations. 

It is often convenient to have this equation solved for F, we then have,

Ma If we hold up the mass of one pound, the force acting on it is one pound, and upon dropping it, the acceleration is 32.16 ft./sec². Upon placing these values in the above equation we see at once that K is 32.16. If we hold up the mass of one gram it is acted upon by the force of one gram and the acceleration produced is 980 cm/sec². Upon placing these values in the equation we see that K is now 980. Again, if F is one dyne and M one gram, a is one centimeter per second per second. Hence, when dynes and grams are used, K is unity. When solving problems by means of this formula,

be sure to use the proper value of K to correspond with the units employed.

We call the reader's attention to the constant, K, in the above formula. Such numbers are most common in the equations of physics and other sciences, and several equations containing them will be studied in the next chapter of this book.

These constants are necessary when we pass from a proportion to an equation. Let us take a simple example. 

Suppose the price of coal is $9.00 a ton. The number of tons of coal bought is proportional to the number of dollars spent, but the number of tons is not equal to the number of dollars. Suppose we let $ be the number of dollars and T the number of tons of coal bought. Then we have the equation, $ = KT. 

Here K is the number 9 and is a constant in this equation, that is, it is always 9, although $ and T may have any number of pairs of values.

The value of the constant depends on the units used. 

If the weight of the coal is given in pounds, then K is a much smaller number. If we keep the weight in tons, but want the money to be expressed in cents, then in this formula 

K is 900.

If we could take an object to the surface of the sun, its mass, M, would remain unchanged, but there the field of gravity is nearly 30 times as strong as it is here on the earth, and the force, F, would be 30 times what it is here on the earth. But the K of the formula would remain 32.16, and we find the acceleration would be over 900 feet per second per second.

Suppose a wagon weighs 20 lb. and a boy pulls it with the force of 10 lb. From the equation we find the acceleration to be 16.08 ft./sec². The velocity continually increases according to the equation v = at, and in less than two min-

utes it is moving at a greater speed than 1,900 ft./sec. We know such a result never occurs. The boy cannot run so 

FIG. 32. 

MAN AND PARACHUTE 

It was found that when a "dummy" was dropped from an airplane and had fallen about 1,600 feet and gained a velocity of less than 200 miles an hour there was then no further acceleration. fast. In general, we use this equation for the first few seconds after starting only. 

A train weighs 32,160 lb., and the engine pulls with the force of one ton more than that required to overcome friction. The acceleration is then 2 ft./sec². As the train gains speed the resistance of friction becomes greater and greater. After a time all of the pull is used in overcoming friction and none is left to produce acceleration. 

Though the acceleration is zero, the velocity may be 40 or 60 miles per hour. The train is simply not gaining in speed. 

We note here that when objects are dropped from airplanes at great heights, they do not strike the ground any harder than when the fall is a much shorter distance. After the object has acquired a certain velocity, the resistance of the air is so great that all the force of gravity is used up in overcoming friction and there is no further acceleration. This friction uses energy by changing it into heat. Hence, the potential energy of the gravitational field, due to the lifted weight, is transformed, part into the kinetic energy of the moving object, and part into heat.

CHAPTER III  SECTION 7. NEWTON'S LAWS AND HOOKE'S LAW 

We shall first discuss some laws formulated by Sir Isaac Newton. He is one of the greatest scientific men of all time. 

This Englishman was born on Christmas day 1642, the year civil war between Charles I and Parliament began. His important work, "The Principia," was published when he was forty-three years of age, in the first year of the reign of James II. He died at the age of eighty-four in the year 

George II became king. Many important political changes occurred during the years of his life. In the world of science, however, there were many developments of equal importance, a large proportion of which were due to his own discoveries. He developed that part of mathematics known as "calculus," which has proved of very great value to mankind. 

By means of the laws mentioned below and of the mathematics which he developed, Newton was able to determine the paths of the heavenly bodies and to foretell their positions. That the motions of the planets, comets, and satellites are in accordance with these laws is the outstanding proof of their correctness and a positive testimony to his great genius.

For convenience in their study we shall first list Newton's four laws, then later discuss them in detail. 

The law of universal gravitation. An attraction exists between every two particles of matter in the universe. This force is directly proportional to the product of the two masses, and is inversely proportional to the square of the distance between them.

KM₁M2 d2 82

GALILEO GALILEI (1564-1642) 

Father of modern experimental research; founder of the science of mechanics; discoverer of the laws of the pendulum and of falling bodies; the one who made the first thermometer and the first astronomical observations with a telescope; professor of mathematics at the Universities of Pisa and of Padua; in prison for many years because of his teachings. 

SIR ISAAC NEWTON (1642-1727) 

"Prince of Philosophers"; greatest of mathematicians; discoverer of the binomial theorem and principal inventor of the calculus; the one who explained the causes of the motions of heavenly bodies; investigator in many branches of physics; professor of mathematics in Cambridge University and later Director of the Mint of England. 

BLAISE PASCAL (1623-1662) 

French mathematician, physicist, and philosopher; discoverer of fundamental facts in hydraulics and pneumatics; author of an important theorem in the geometry of the conics when only sixteen years of age.

JAMES WATT (1736-1819) 

Perfector of the practical steam engine; instrument maker at the University of Glasgow.

Newton's first law of motion. Every body continues in its state of rest or of uniform motion in a straight line unless compelled by some external force to change that state. 

Newton's second law of motion. 

The acceleration of a mass is directly proportional to the force acting, is inversely proportional to the mass acted upon, and takes place in the direction in which the force acts. 

Newton's third law of motion. an equal and opposite reaction.

The attraction of gravity. The motion of the moon about the earth furnishes a good illustration of the law of gravitational attraction. First consider a stone tied to a string and whirled about in a circle. According to the first law of motion, the stone tends to move in a straight line, but this it cannot do on account of the fixed length of the string. The pull of the string which causes the stone to take a circular path is called "centripetal force." The force of the stone pulling on the string is called "centrifugal force." It is due to the inertia of the body tending to move it in a straight line. It is proved by calculus that, when a mass M moves with the velocity v about a circle of radius r, the centrifugal force is given by the equation: Mv2 rg If the units used are grams, centimeters, and seconds, F will be in grams when g is 980 and F will be in dynes when g is unity. If pounds and feet are the units, then F will be in pounds if g is made 32.16.

The moon revolves about the earth in approximately 28 days and, as its distance from the center of the earth is known, it is possible to find its velocity and the force of its attraction to the earth which is necessary to prevent its

flying from its path. It is not necessary to know the mass of the moon. We can calculate the force required to retain a unit mass, say one pound, in the orbit of the moon with the velocity of the latter. If this then is equal to the attraction of the earth for a mass of one pound at the distance of the moon, we shall know why the moon stays in its orbit. Newton was the first to prove that these two forces are equal, and thus to explain the moon's motion. It was six years from the time he made the first calculations on this problem until he was satisfied with the results. It is supposed that his greatest difficulty lay in proving the following statement.

Each particle of one spherical body, the moon, is attracted to each particle of the other, the earth, according to the law of universal gravitation. Investigation shows that the resulting attraction is exactly the same as it would be were all the mass of each sphere concentrated at its center. This rule also applies to spherical shells, no matter how thin, and to the attraction and repulsion of electrical charges spread out over spherical surfaces.

Having established this rule, it was easy for Newton to calculate the weight of any mass at any required distance above the surface of the earth. All the mass of the earth can be regarded as concentrated at a point 4,000 miles below the surface, the center of the planet. 1 1 Suppose a mass M₁ has the weight F₁ at the surface of the earth, and we wish to find its weight at the distance da from the center. Let M₂ be the mass of the earth and dı the distance from the surface to the center or 4,000 miles. 

From the law of gravitation we have the two equations: 2 F1 F₁ = KM₁M2 d12 and F2 

KM₁M2 d22 Upon dividing one of these equations by the other the K and the M's cancel out, and we have the proportion: F1: F2: d22 : d₁²

This result may be stated as follows: The attraction of the earth for a body varies inversely as the square of the distance from that body to the center of the earth. This rule does not apply if the body is inside the earth. A mass at the center of the earth has no weight as it is attracted equally in all directions by the surrounding earth. If the mass M₁ is small as compared to the size of the earth, it is not necessary that it be spherical in shape. This is because the distances from each of the particles of this mass to the center of the earth are practically all equal. 1 In the above proportion d₁ and d₂ may be in any unit of length, provided the same unit is used in each case. Suppose we wish to find the force with which a mass of one kilogram is attracted when it is at the distance of the moon, 240,000 miles from the center of the earth. Here the most convenient unit of length is the radius of the earth, di being unity, and d2 60. As the force of attraction between the earth and a mass of one kilogram at its surface is one kilogram of force, we have:

Causes of variations in gravity. We now see why the force of gravity varies over the surface of the earth. At high altitudes the attraction of gravity is less than at places nearer the center of the earth. But altitude is not the only condition affecting the attraction of gravity. There is the effect of latitude. Bodies on or near the equator weigh less for two reasons. First, the centrifugal action of the spinning earth tends to throw them off. Second, this same spinning

has caused the earth to bulge out along the equator to such an extent that points on the surface near the equator are about thirteen miles further from the center of the earth than are the poles. The attraction of gravity at any place is also affected by the average density of the rocks forming the crust of the earth at that point. Denser rocks, having more mass within the same space, tend to increase the amount of attraction.

The constant of gravitation. When two masses are brought near to each other no attracting force can be detected by means of the senses. This is because the amount of attraction is very minute. It can be shown by astronomical instruments that the plumb line does not hang truly vertical in the vicinity of large mountains. The bob is attracted by the mass of the mountains. By estimating the mass and the average distance of particles composing mountains, and by noting the position of the plumb line, attempts were made to determine the constant of gravitational attraction. 

This is the value of K in the formula given, and represents the attraction between two unit masses when they are one unit of distance apart. Notice that in the formula, F and K must be equal when the other quantities are unity. Later a much better method of determining this constant was devised by the Rev. 

John Mitchell. The experiment using his plan was first performed after his death by Henry Cavendish.

FIG. 33. 

MOUNTAIN AND PLUMB LINE 

The Cavendish experiment. Two lead balls, about the size of door-knobs, were placed at the ends of a light wooden rod some six feet long. This apparatus was suspended in a horizontal position by a fine copper wire. Other lead balls about a foot in diameter were used to attract the smaller balls. 

A movable support was so arranged that these were placed near the smaller balls, first in such a position that the attraction turned the rod in one direction, then in another position which

caused the rod to turn in the opposite direction. The entire apparatus was enclosed in a case, and the amount of rotation was read by means of telescopes. The force required to turn the rod any amount can be found from its dimensions and from the time it takes to make a vibration in a horizontal plane. Knowing the force acting on the rod, the masses of the lead spheres and the distance between their centers, it was possible to calculate the constant K, as all other terms in the gravitational formula were known. 

When the metric system is used, K is generally given in dynes, and represents the force of attraction between two spheres, each weighing one gram, when the distance between their centers is one centimeter.

One can never tell to what practical uses the discoveries of the scientist may be put. 

Who would have thought that the work of Henry Cavendish in investigating gravitation would lead to discoveries that have, today, made the price of gasoline cheaper to you and me? Yet such is the case.

A number of oil deposits have been located by means of the "torsion balance," which consists of two gold knobs at the ends of a light aluminum rod which is suspended by a very fine platinum wire, as shown in the figure. Suppose a large mass of

granite rises up from the interior of the earth, being covered by perhaps thousands of feet of rock of less density. The attraction of the granite is slightly different on the two gold knobs and this tends to hold the aluminum rod in a certain position. By setting up the balance at a number of places a map is made of the field of gravity, and from this the shape and position of the granite mass can be determined. The geologists then know where it is most probable that oil will be found. 

We know that a mass of one gram The mass of the earth. is attracted to the earth with a force of 980 dynes and that the distance to the center of the earth is approximately 6.36 × 

10 centimeters. If these values are substituted in the gravitational formula we have: 980 = 

6.658 × 10-8 × 1 × M2 (6.36 × 108)2

As M2, the mass of the earth, is the only unknown in this equation, its value may be determined. It is 5.98 × 1027 grams. 

If the mass of the earth, as found above, is substituted in the formula for centrifugal force, the radius being the distance from the earth to the sun, the force of attraction between the earth and the sun may be calculated. Knowing this force and the mass of the earth, we can from the gravitational formula, find the mass of the sun. It is about 392,400 times the mass of the earth. Thus, not only can we weigh" the earth, but the sun also. 66 From the size and mass of the earth its average density is found to be 5.53 grams per cubic centimeter. As the average density of the rocks of the surface is only 2.65, it is seen that the interior of the globe contains much material more dense than rock. There are reasons for thinking that the earth contains large quantities of iron. It is not unlikely that there is a layer of this metal within the earth two or three thousand miles in thickness. Our planet is quite a solid ball after all. 

It is indeed a very strong arm which holds the earth in its path about the sun. The centrifugal force generated would tear apart a steel rod of a diameter comparable to that of the earth. One of the reasons for believing that there is an ether is to account for some means through which this great force can

act. As to what gravitation really is, how it is connected with mass, or how it acts through space, we know almost nothing. 

About all we can say at the present time is that gravitational attraction is a fundamental property of mass. 

The three laws of motion. A proof of the correctness of the laws of motion is seen in the actions of the heavenly bodies. We also arrive at the first law by noticing the effect of friction on moving objects. A car, when pushed along a level road, will move a few feet and then come to rest if the force is discontinued. If the bearings are oiled, the push will send it farther. If ball bearings are used, it will go an even greater distance. We have every reason to believe that, if all retarding forces were removed, it would then pass on in a straight line forever. We have already said much regarding Newton's first law when discussing inertia, as a property of mass. We have also considered the second law when studying the formula F = Ma. We must now take up the third law, "To every action there is an equal and opposite reaction."

This means that when a force acts between two bodies, A and B, the force acting on A is equal to that acting on B. 

A weight lying on a table presses down with a certain force. 

The table pushes up on the weight with the same force. 

The wagon pulls on the horse with the same force that the horse exerts on the vehicle. The wagon follows the horse because it does not offer as much resistance in the form of friction as does the horse. When a person jumps from a small boat the vessel moves backward as his body goes forward. Moreover, the force acting on the boat is exactly the same as that which sends his body forward. A result is that one cannot jump as far from a small boat as he can from something solid. Hence inexperienced persons sometimes unwittingly jump into the water. 

Momentum. - Momentum is the product of a mass times its velocity. When a force acts between two masses, each free to move, the momentum acquired by one is the same as

that acquired by the other. This follows from the laws of motion. Here two masses are acted on by equal forces and hence the accelerations are inversely as the masses. The time these forces act is the same. Then, at each instant their velocities are inversely proportional to their masses. 

If one body has half the mass of the other it moves twice as fast, and so on. Therefore, the momentum acquired by one mass equals that acquired by the other, or M101 M2v2. 

But this does not mean that the kinetic energies of the two masses are equal. Suppose the smaller mass is one onehundredth of the larger. Its velocity is one hundred times as great and the velocity squared is ten thousand times as great as the square of the velocity of the heavier mass. 

We see it has one hundred times as much kinetic energy. 

The amounts of energy acquired are inversely proportional to the respective masses. 

It is sometimes remarked that a gun kicks back as hard as the bullet strikes. What really is meant is that the momentum acquired by the bullet is equal to that received by the gun. The bullet, however, takes up much more kinetic energy than does the gun. 

If two lead balls of the same mass move towards each other with the same speed and strike, they are both brought to rest, all the kinetic energy being transferred into heat as the lead is mashed. The sum of the momenta is zero. 

But this sum was zero before they struck, because one had a negative velocity with respect to the other and hence the momentum of one was positive and that of the other was negative.

If one lead ball has more momentum than the other, after striking they both will move in the direction of the ball having the greater momentum, the momentum of the two taken together being the arithmetical difference or the algebraic sum of the momenta before striking. This prin-

ciple holds for the striking of any number of bodies, whether they are elastic or not, and is spoken of as "the conservation of momentum." There is an interesting relation between the momentum acquired by a mass free to move without friction and the force and the time consumed. If we combine the equations F ma/g and v at by eliminating a, we have Ftg = mv. is measured in dynes and the other units correspond Ft then the momentum is equal to the force multiplied by the time. -- - If F = mv, Hooke's law. Within the limits of perfect elasticity all changes of shape due to compressions, stretchings, bendings, or twistings, are directly proportional to the force producing them.

More briefly we say, "The stress is proportional to the strain." By "stress" is meant the force, in pounds, grams, or other similar measure, applied to the body, and by strain is meant the amount of the distortion of the body. This latter is measured in inches, centimeters, or other length units. This law is named in honor of Robert Hooke, an 

English scientist and a contemporary of Sir Isaac Newton. 

As an example of stretching, take a rubber band. If one pound lengthens it an inch, then two pounds will extend it two inches, three pounds three inches, and so on. A spring-board for diving furnishes a good example of bending. 

An example of twisting stress, which is called torque, occurs in the twisting of a steel rod. An example of compression is seen in the distortion of a piece of rubber by a weight placed thereon. In all these cases Hooke's law holds if the object is not so distorted that it receives a permanent "set" and does not return exactly to its original condition. There are many schemes for verifying this law in the laboratory. 

Sometimes this is done by noting the amount of stretching in a wire when various weights are suspended therefrom, in which case the amounts of strain are small and ingenious schemes are resorted to in order to measure them accurately.

The linear relation. Experiments on Hooke's law give a good example of what is known as the linear relation between two quantities, in this case the stress and the strain. 

For each stress there is a corresponding strain, and if these pairs are plotted the resulting graph is a straight line. 

If each stress is divided by the corresponding strain, it will be noted that the quotients are all near the same value. 

Hence we may write, F/S = K or F KS, where K is the average of these quotients. It is called the constant of Hooke's law. It depends upon the shape, size, and material of the object and also on the units used. 

The linear relation is a common one. Any equation in the form of x = ay, where a is fixed and x and y are allowed to take on any pairs of values which satisfy the equation, gives a straight-line graph. Thus the relation between v and t, where a is constant, as shown by v = at, is a linear one. If the body of a substance is of the same density throughout, the equation, M/v or = ρυ indicates a linear relationship and shows that the amount of mass varies directly with the volume considered. 

The equation for the space passed over by a falling body does not show a linear relation. It is 

S = 16.08 ť², and plots into the curve called a parabola. A linear relation results whenever each term of the equation is of the first degree.

SECTION 8. 

HYDRAULICS AND PNEUMATICS 

Pressure in fluids. - We shall now consider some laws which result from the manner in which the molecules of fluids react upon each other and upon the surfaces with which they are in contact. The word fluid, from the Latin fluere, to flow, is applied to both liquids and gases. The molecules are so loosely attached to each other that they can move about freely, hence the material can flow. That part of physics which deals with the laws of liquids is called hydraulics, from the Greek word hydor, water. That part which deals with the laws of gases is called pneumatics, from the Greek pneuma, breath or spirit.

Force and pressure. Pressure is defined as the force per unit of area. If a boiler has a pressure of 100 lb. per sq. in. there is a force pressing against each square inch of the interior which could balance 100 pounds. Thus, if the total area of the interior of the boiler mentioned above were one million square inches, then the total force against the sides would be 100,000,000 pounds. If P is the pressure, A the total area considered, and F the force acting on that area, then, by definition, we have:

= PA. 

The laws of liquid pressure. Law I. At any point beneath the surface of a liquid the pressure is the same in all directions.

This must be true if the molecules of liquids are free to slide about each other. If the pressure on any particles were greater in some one direction than in some other, these particles would at once begin to move in the direction of reduced pressure. It is known that even the smallest force applied to a liquid produces a movement of the particles within the body of the liquid; also, a liquid remains at rest within its container when nothing disturbs it; hence, the above law. It may be verified by means of any type of pressure gauge. At any given depth this registers the same in whatever direction it is turned.

Law II. The pressure at any point beneath the surface of a liquid in an open vessel is directly proportional to the depth and to the density of the liquid, and is independent of the size and the shape of the containing vessel. 

If the pressure increases with the depth, how can it be the same in all directions? It would seem that the force acting down on any particle is less than the force from below acting upward. It is, but the difference is equal to the weight of the particle, and this weight, added to the force from above, exactly balances the force from below. Let us consider one cubic foot of water, which weighs 62.43 lb. The eleventh

cubic foot of water below the surface has ten resting upon it. 

The force on the bottom of the tenth cube is 624.3 lb. because each cubic foot weighs 62.43 lb.

The force acting downward on the eleventh cube, plus its own weight, is the force from the bottom acting upward, or 686.7 lb.

As the pressures on the sides, also, balance, it is readily seen that there is no tendency for any cube to move in any direction.

This applies to all liquids. 

The individual molecules of a liquid are continuously moving and striking each 10 "1 

WATER other. We illustrate this by placing a drop FIG. 36.— CUBES OF 

of a powerful dye in a large vessel of liquid. 

It is quickly distributed throughout the liquid by the moving molecules. 

When a particle in a liquid is large in comparison to the liquid molecules, the molecular blows which tend to drive it in any direction are exactly balanced by the ones that tend to drive it in the opposite direction. But if a particle has a size comparable to a molecule, the blows acting in opposite directions do not balance, and it moves along a zig-zag path. With particles suitably illuminated this motion may be observed by means of a microscope. It is called the Brownian movement, and can be seen where small particles are floating in quiet liquids or gases. The word pressure" is not used in connection with such small objects, but is applicable only when the surface against which it acts is very large in comparison to molecular dimensions. 66 The pressure at any point beneath the surface of a liquid is in no way affected by the size or the shape of the containing vessel. A diver can feel the pressure increase as he sinks beneath the surface, yet he can dive as deep into a large lake as into a small pool.

This law is further illustrated by "Pascal's Vases." 

Each of the four containers shown here has the same depth,

and the pressure at the bottom is the same in each when filled with the same liquid or with liquids of the same density. However, the total force on the bottom of B and D is greater than that on A and C because the areas are larger. 

This extra force is balanced by those acting upward at x and y and at z and w. As B and 

C have the same volume they weigh the same when filled, though the force on the bottom of B is much larger. -B LA

From the above we have the formulas:

Pressure beneath the surface of a liquid would not be proportional to the depth were it not that each unit cube of the liquid, from the surface down, has the same weight. 

This is because, for all practical purposes, liquids are incompressible. A cubic foot of water hundreds of feet below the surface weighs almost the same as a cubic foot of water near the surface. Five miles below the surface, in the deep holes of the sea, the pressure is about one ton per square centimeter. On an area ten centimeters square, the pressure at this depth would be one hundred tons. Yet this great pressure increases the density of water but four or five per cent. In other words, a quart of water at this depth has increased in weight but forty or fifty grams. For practical purposes we regard the relation between pressure and depth in liquids as a linear one. A volume of water one inch square and one foot long weighs 

62.43 144 .434 pound. Hence, for each foot of depth the pressure in water increases .434 pounds per square inch. 

This also applies to water standing in pipes, such as those of a city water system. To find the pressure at a hydrant, one multiplies the vertical distance in feet from this point

to the level of the water in the reservoir that supplies the hydrant by .434, and the result is in pounds per square inch. 

Open faucets relieve the pressure somewhat, depending on the size of the opening compared to that of the pipes. If the water is flowing freely and the hydrant is suddenly closed, the inertia of the water produces a very large momentary pressure, called a "water hammer." This may be severe enough to injure the plumbing, and is sometimes guarded against by the use of an air chamber that acts as a cushion. 

As one cubic centimeter of water weighs one gram, we see that each square centimeter of a surface under water one centi- 

FIG. 38. 

WATER SEEKS ITS LEVEL 

Two centimeters meter deep sustains a pressure of one gram. deep the pressure is twice as much, three centimeters deep it is three times as much, and so on. Hence, Hence, the pressure due to water, in grams per square centimeter, is the same as the depth in centimeters. As mercury is 13.6 times as dense as water, pressure due to mercury is 13.6 grams per square centimeter for each centimeter of depth. 

This law of liquid pressure explains why "water seeks its level." Consider the apparatus shown in the figure. No matter what the shape of the attached chambers, water stands at the same height in each. If this were not so, there would be points where the pressure is greater in one direction than in some other. The liquid would then flow until the pressure at each point is the same in all directions. This situation can exist only when the water stands at the same level in all parts of the containing vessel. 

The manometer. This is a simple instrument used to measure pressures. It consists of a U-shaped glass tube containing mercury or other liquid. The pressure forces this liquid higher in one leg of the tube than in the other. 

To measure the pressure we determine the difference between

the heights in the two tubes. If this is measured in feet, then this distance multiplied by .434 and the specific gravity of the liquid gives the pressure in pounds per square inch.

Archimedes' principle. The laws of liquid pressure explain Archimedes' 

principle. Suppose we have a cube of some solid immersed in a liquid. 

It is evident that the force on the bottom of the cube acting upward, which is called the buoyant force, is greater than that on top acting downward by an amount equal to the weight of the displaced liquid.

FIG. 39. THE MANOMETER Law III. Pascal's law of liquid pressure. - Pressure applied to any part of a confined liquid is transmitted equally in all directions, and adds an equal force to all equal areas that form a part of the boundary of the liquid.

This law applies to confined liquids only, and has to do with a pressure additional to that due to the weight of the liquid. 

Most illustrations of this law have to do with a machine which இர் 

FIG. 40. -THE HYDRAULIC PRESS 

has a piston working in a cylinder. Pressure applied to the liquid by means of the piston is transmitted to the entire surface which encloses the liquid. This follows from the fact that the molecules of liquids are free to move about each other.

The hydraulic press. This machine furnishes an illustration of the third law of liquid pressure. Suppose the diameter of the small piston, A, is one-half an inch, and that of the larger piston, B, is ten inches. The cylinders are connected by a pipe and all the enclosed space is filled with the liquid. One piston has twenty times the diameter of the other and, by the 

Law of Similar Figures, the area of the large piston is 

400 times that of the smaller. Whatever pressure is applied to the smaller piston, is applied to 400 times as much surface on the larger piston, and hence exerts an upward force 400 times as great as the force acting downward on the smaller piston.

FIG. 41. 

- CAR ON LIFT 

This principle is made use of in those large presses that are used to reduce the size of cotton bales, to push car wheels on their axles, and to press cider from apples. It is, also, used in dentists' chairs, hydraulic jacks, and in some elevators.
