While the hydraulic press greatly magnifies force there is no increase in the amount of energy or work. The volume of liquid flowing out from below the smaller piston is the same as that entering below the larger. The smaller piston must make 400 strokes, each one inch in

FIG. 42. HYDROSTATIC length, in order to raise the larger but one inch. In the practical press, valves are placed as in a pump so that the liquid does not return to the smaller cylinder as the smaller piston makes an upstroke.

To prevent rusting and to aid lubrication, the liquid used is often an oil. 

The hydrostatic bellows. The construction of this instrument is illustrated by the figure. Suppose the platform is two feet square. It

Many pounds of water need be poured into the tube to raise the platform but one inch. 

Pneumatics. All the laws of liquid pressure apply to the air and to other gases with this exception. Pressure of the air is not proportional to the depth from the top of the atmosphere. This is because gases, unlike liquids, are very compressible. A cubic foot of air at sea level weighs about twice as much as that at the tops of very high mountains. 

But first we shall see how the atmospheric pressure is measured.

Torricelli's experiment. This famous experiment was first described by Torricelli, a pupil of Galileo. If we fill

a glass tube, thirty inches or more in length and closed at one end, with mercury and invert the tube with the open end beneath the surface of mercury in a dish, the liquid will stand at about thirty inches in the tube. The upper part of the tube is a vacuum. The mercury is forced up by the pressure of the air. The mercury held up in the tube weighs the same as a section of the air from the surface of the earth to the outer limits of the atmosphere and having the same cross section as does the mass of the mercury. 

If we place a scale beside a

Torricelli tube so the height of the mercury can be accurately read, we have have a barometer. (From the Greek, 

"baros 99 

FIG. 44. - MERCURY COLUMNS 

The height to which a liquid rises in a Torricelli tube is independent of the size and shape of the tube, and is about 

76 cm at sea level. weight, or pressure, and 99 metron a measure.) To make an accurate barometer one must be certain that the tube is well sealed at the top, and that the mercury has been heated to the boiling point so as to remove all bubbles of air. It should be heated by a gas flame, a few inches of its length at a time, while in the tube before it is inverted. 

Pascal was the first to see that a barometer should read lower on a mountain top than at its base. He verified this by having his brother take an instrument to the top of a mountain in southern France. The elevations of mountains are now often calculated from barometer readings. As the height of the barometer changes with the weather, a number of readings must be taken at the same time both on the summit of the mountain and at a place of known elevation near its base.

The relation between the drop of the barometer and the rise in altitude is not a linear one as would be the case were

the air incompressible. At sea level the barometer drops .9 cm for a rise of 100 m; at 1,000 m elevation the drop is .8 cm for a rise of 100 m; while at 2,000 m elevation the drop is only .7 cm for a rise of the same amount. 

Standard pressure.

The pressure of the air at sea level is about fifteen pounds per square inch, the barometer standing at thirty inches or seventy-six centimeters. Pressure is measured in force per unit of area or in the height of the mercury in a Torricelli tube. Thus, the pressure of a gas may be "ten centimeters of mercury."

The "standard pressure" at which gases are measured is 76 cm of mercury. For accurate work this is taken with gravity 980.616, the value at sea level at 45 degrees latitude.

A cubic centimeter of dry air at standard pressure and at zero degrees centigrade, the temperature at which water freezes, weighs .001293 g. Hence, a liter weighs 1.293 g.

For rough calculations we allow twelve cubic feet of air to the pound.

The aneroid barometer. This instrument contains no liquid and can be carried about like a watch. It consists of a metallic chamber from which the air has been partially exhausted.

The top of this chamber is a thin sheet of elastic metal. The "spring" of the metal works against the pressure of the air.

As this varies the elastic top moves in and out.

This motion of the top

MOUNTAINS AND AIR 

Half the air is below the level of peaks. of the chamber is communicated to a hand which passes along a scale somewhat as does the hand of a clock. The scale can be designed to measure either pressure or altitude. 

The best instruments detect changes of less than ten feet of altitude.

The pressure of the atmosphere at the altitude of our high peaks is about half that at sea level. This indicates that half of the air is below the level of these peaks. But the air extends upwards many times the height of any mountains. There is no method of detecting the exact height of the air. Men have gone up in balloons for more than seven miles. Small balloons with self-registering instruments have ascended for twenty miles. The height at which meteors begin to burn, and the aurora borealis, give data that lead to estimations of the height of the atmosphere at from one hundred to five hundred miles.

Isotherms, dotted lines drawn for every 10°; and isobars, unbroken lines drawn for every tenth of an inch. weather. Moist air, contrary to general belief, is lighter than dry air. This results from the fact that a molecule of water is lighter than one of oxygen or nitrogen. The

damper the air, other factors being the same, the lighter it is. 

This is one reason why the barometer generally drops upon the approach of rain.

From the telegraphic reports of weather conditions at a number of places, including the barometer readings, the temperatures, the direction and velocity of the wind, and other similar data, the Weather Bureau constructs daily weather maps. Lines are drawn on these connecting points having the same barometer reading, correction having been made for the different altitudes. These lines are called " isobars," meaning equal weight, as they pass through those places having the same atmospheric pressure. They tend to form closed curves about the "lows and the "highs." A low covers that part of the map where the air pressure is the least, and a high where it is the greatest. 66 Low 99 

CLONE 

99 The surrounding air tends to flow inward towards a low pressure area and outward from a high pressure area. Inertia and the rotation of the earth cause the

FIG. 47. - CYCLONE AND ANTICY- air to flow in great circular eddies about these areas. In the Northern Hemisphere the direction of the air as it moves in toward a "low" is contrary to the direction of the movement of the hands of a clock, while the movement of the air about a high is with the clock hands. We think of the clock as face upward on the map. The reason for this can be seen from the figure. Air moving to the low from A, because of inertia, is moving eastward faster than the earth beneath it. When air from B arrives at the low it is not moving eastward as fast as the earth beneath it. The result is a circular eddy about the "low." As air moves outward from a high the rotation is then in the opposite direction. 

These areas of high and low pressure move across the

САНННШНННННЫЕ country in fairly well defined paths. Knowing their position and direction, the weather at any point can be foretold with fair accuracy several hours in advance. This is especially true of such important changes as heavy frosts and great storms. 

Pneumatic experiments. The pressure of the air operates many useful devices and explains a number of interesting phenomena.

FIG. 48. 

SUCTION PUMP FIG. 49. THE FORCE PUMP 

The suction pump. As the piston is raised the weight of the air and the water above the valve A keep the latter closed. At the same time the pressure of the air forces water up through the valve B. As the piston descends, the weight of the water closes B, and the entrapped water is forced through A into the chamber above the piston. As

the piston rises again the water is lifted and flows out through the spout. If the pump cylinder is too high above the water, the air pressure cannot raise water to it. At sea level the height to which air pressure can force water is about 34 feet or 13.6 times the height of the mercury in the barometer. 

The force pump. The valve A opens into the compartment C. Above this is the air chamber, D. As water is forced into C the compressed air causes it to flow from the nozzle in a steady stream. 

The air pump.

If we reverse the direction in which the valves act in the force pump we see that air will be pumped రరరర 

FIG. 50. 

HY-VAC PUMP 

The eccentric fits tightly in the case and is turned about the shaft. This causes the air to flow as shown by the arrows. The exhaust is at E. The slider, S, fits tight against the rotating part, being held in place by a spring. out of the chamber D. The first air pumps were constructed on this principle. Today there are a number of types of air pumps. Some consist of accurately fitting wheels revolving in metal casings. The highest vacua are obtained by the Langmuir mercury blast. After another form of air pump has removed a large portion of the air, a very high vacuum is obtained by a blast of mercury vapor from the boiling metal. This blast carries away the air molecules.

The mercury is then condensed and flows back to be used again. 

Magdeburg hemispheres. - Otto von Guericke, inventor of the air pump, exhausted the air from between the closefitting halves of a hollow sphere. The pressure of the air

held these together so firmly that sixteen horses were unable to pull them apart. The sphere which he used was somewhat over one foot in diameter. 

The siphon. The action of the siphon is familiar to everyone. The net pressure acting upward on the liquid at the bottom of the longer leg of the siphon is that of the atmosphere minus the pressure due to the weight of the liquid in this leg. The net pressure acting upward on the liquid at the end of the short leg is that of the atmosphere minus that due to the weight of the liquid in this leg. As this latter pressure is greater than the first, the liquid is forced out through the tube.

The Bacchus experiment. - 

The bottle A is tightly corked and is only partly filled with liquid. When air is exhausted from the receiver, the liquid exhausted. flows from A to B, due to the expanding of the air in A when the atmospheric pressure is removed. When air is readmitted to the receiver, the liquid flows back from B into A. 

Inverting a glass of water. If a piece of cardboard is laid upon a glass filled with water, it may be inverted and the liquid will not run out. The water is held up in the glass by air pressure in the same way as is the mercury in a Torricelli tube. Again, when an inverted glass is pushed down beneath the surface of the water in another container,

the water cannot enter the glass. The pressure of the air in the glass holds back the liquid. This is the principle of the diving bell sometimes used in under-water operations. Fresh air is supplied the workmen by a pump, and the excess air escapes from the lower edge of the bell. The pressure of the air in the bell is the same as that of the water at its base. As this pressure increases, it becomes injurious to workmen and so the bell can only be used advantageously in shallow water, the limit being from sixty to eighty feet. 

FIG. 55. 

INVERTED GLASS Boyle's law. The temperature remaining the same, the volume occupied by a confined gas is inversely proportional to the pressure to which it is subjected. 

If the pressure on a volume of gas is doubled, the volume will be reduced to one-half of its former volume; if the pressure is trebled, the volume will be reduced to one-third of its former volume; and so on. 1 This law was first discovered by the Irish physicist, Robert 

Boyle, in 1661. It is expressed mathematically as follows.

Let V₁ be the volume occupied by a certain mass of a gas when at the pressure P. Let the gas be now compressed or allowed to expand, V₂ being the second volume and P₂ the corresponding pressure. It is understood that the temperature remains the same. Then by Boyle's law, we have, 2 

V₁: V2: P2: P₁ or 

P1 1 

V₁P₁ = V₂P2. 

If we subject the same amount of a gas to a number of different pressures, and measure the corresponding volumes, we have,

PIV1 P2V2 P3V3 K. 

The constant, K, depends on the number of molecules

present and on the temperature. It does not depend on the size or the mass of the molecules. This is explained by the law of Avogadro: Equal volumes of all gases, when at the same pressure and temperature, contain the same number of molecules. Use is also made of the fact that the pressure of a gas is due to the kinetic energy of its molecules, and this energy is the same in amount per molecule for all gases at the same temperature.

The explanation of Boyle's law is as follows. Gas pressure is caused by the pounding of molecules against the walls of the container. When a given amount of gas is compressed to one-half its former volume, the molecules are twice as numerous in a given space and hence the number of blows delivered by these molecules against any section of the enclosing wall in a given time is doubled. If the temperature remains the same, the average kinetic energy of the molecules does not change, and when the number of blows in a given time is doubled, the pressure of course is doubled also. 

Boyle's law is not absolutely true, but for the gases of the air, and for hydrogen, helium, and others hard to liquefy, the law holds to within

FIG. 56. - BOYLE'S LAW 

(B+ M₁)h₁ phat M3 (B+ M2) h₂ = (B + M3)h3 .. a few tenths of one per cent for all ordinary temperatures and pressures. 

We can verify Boyle's law as follows. Use a 

J-shaped glass tube closed at the shorter end. 

The illustration represents the same tube with the same air entrapped in the shorter end. In the second figure more merthus increasing the prescury has been poured in the tube, sure and decreasing the volume of the air in the shorter arm.

Let A be the area of cross section of the tube in square centimeters. This does not change. 

Let h1, h2, h3. . . etc. be the respective heights of the volumes of air entrapped in the shorter arm. 

Then, V₁ = Ah₁, V₂ = Ah2, V3 Ahs, and so on.

V2 Let B be the reading of the barometer in centimeters. The pressure of the air, 13.6 B g/cm², must be added to the pressure due to the mercury to obtain the real pressure on the air in the tube.

Let M be the height of the mercury in the longer arm above the level of the top of the liquid in the shorter arm. Then the pressure on the enclosed air, due to the weight of the mercury, is respectively, 13.6 M1, 13.6 M2, 13.6 M3, and so on, in g/cm².

The total pressure on the enclosed air is the pressure due to the air added to that due to the mercury. Hence, P₁ = 

13.6(B+ M₁), P₂ = 13.6(B + M₂), P3 = 13.6(BM3), and so on.

We substitute these values for the P's and V's in the equation for Boyle's law, and cancel out A and 13.6 and have: (B+ M₁)h₁ = (B + M₂)h₂ (B+ M3)h3 = K. 

As B and the M's and the h's may be measured the law can be verified.

If a tube several meters long is used, fair results may be obtained when the liquid used is water. In this case the depth of the water in centimeters gives the pressure in g/cm² due to the weight of the water. The vapors of liquids do not obey Boyle's law, hence the water vapor in the air introduces a small error.

SECTION 9. 

MOLECULAR FORCES 

Gas Porous

The velocity of molecules. The average speed of the molecules of a gas is determined by their mass and by the temperature. At any given temperature the hydrogen molecules have the greatest speed because they are the lightest. 

At the point of the freezing of water, the velocity of a hydrogen molecule is more than twice the velocity of a projectile from a powerful cannon.

The speed of a water molecule in the form of vapor, under the same conditions, is only about one-third as fast, on account of its greater mass. The average distance a molecule of a gas moves between collisions with its neighbors is very small indeed. Gas→

A molecule of air, at freezing temperature, strikes about a thousand of its neighbors in passing along a distance equivalent to the width of a hair. But its speed is so great that it makes bubbles millions of impacts with other molecules every second. 

Diffusion. An interesting ex- Air

Cup Funnel 

Glass tube Water

FIG. 57.- DIFFUSION 

periment that shows the relative speeds of the molecules of different gases is as follows. A tube is attached to a porous

cup as in the figure, with its lower end beneath the surface of water. When a jar is inverted over the cup and hydrogen, or even common illuminating gas, is allowed to flow into the jar, bubbles of air are seen coming out of the tube. When the jar is removed from the cup, water ascends the tube. After a time, whether gas is kept in the jar or not, the water returns to its former level. When the gas surrounds the cup its molecules find their way inside through the pores, passing along tortuous passes which have numbers of " blind alleys."

As the average speed of hydrogen or illuminating gas molecules is greater than the speed of molecules of air, they find their way inside the porous jar faster than an equal number of air molecules work their way out. This produces a pressure inside the cup as indicated by the bubbles. After a time, as the air finds its way out, the pressure is equalized. Osmosis. - There are cases in which liquids enter an enclosure in somewhat the same manner as the gas does in the experiment mentioned above. The pressure thus produced is called "osmotic pressure." is necessary that neither the molecules already inside the enclosure, nor those entering, return through the walls fast enough to allow the pressure to fall. The following experiment illustrates such a condition. A bag made from animal membranes is tied about the lower end of a long glass tube and filled with a sugar solution. The tube is placed upright with the bag immersed in a vessel of fresh water. After some hours it is seen that water has entered through the bag and is rising in the tube. This shows that water molecules have entered

through the pores of the bag faster than the molecules of the sugar solution have been able to pass out. The sugar molecules cannot pass through the membrane readily and when water molecules enter they become attached to the sugar molecules and hence do not pass back through the porous membrane as rapidly as other water molecules come in. 

Instead of this bag a carrot or other vegetable, somewhat hollowed out, may be substituted. Osmosis and diffusion play a very important part in the growth and nourishment of both animal and vegetable tissues. 

Surface tension phenomena. The surface of a liquid acts like a stretched elastic membrane. The tension, or pull, of this surface is called surface tension. 

When a greased needle is laid carefully on water it floats, but if it is caused to break through the surface it sinks to the bottom. This is explained as follows. A molecule on the surface of a liquid is not acted upon by exactly the same forces as one in the interior. This latter FIG. 59.- NEEDLE ON WATER 

molecule is attracted equally in all directions by the other molecules about it, and hence the slightest force will cause it to move in any direction. But the molecule on the surface is not entirely surrounded by its neighbors, and the result is that it holds more tightly to those molecules on the surface beside it. The lateral attraction of these molecules at the surface produces a sort of elastic film on the surface of the liquid. The needle is not heavy enough to break through this surface layer but, once through, it easily pushes apart those molecules in the interior of the liquid and falls to the bottom.

Surface tension tends to make the surface of a liquid as small as possible, and to apply this to the explanation of certain common phenomena one needs to bear in mind the

following theorems from geometry. Of all solid figures having equal volumes, the sphere has the smallest boundary or area. Also, of all plane figures having equal areas, the circle has the shortest boundary or perimeter. One thousand cubic centimeters of a substance will have less surface exposed to 99 

FIG. 60. 

- SOAP FILM 

When the soap film inside the loop of thread is broken the thread forms a circle. Why does the loop enclose the largest area possible? the air if it is in the form of a sphere than if it has any other shape. It will also require less fence to enclose a field of one hundred acres if it is circular than if the field has any other shape. 

Small drops of water, when sprinkled on a greasy or dusty surface, take the form of spheres. The surfaces of the drops draw up and become as small as possible, and the smallest surface any given drop can have is when it is in the form of a sphere. Shot is made by having drops of molten lead cool as they fall from a tower. 

The shape is due to surface tension. 

When liquids are in large masses the force of gravity is stronger than that of surface tension and they flatten out, but if they can be so placed that the effect of gravity is counteracted they take the spherical shape. Alcohol and water can be mixed in such proportions as to give a liquid with the same density as olive oil. If this oil is placed in such a mixture it collects in a sphere. Being surrounded by another liquid of the same density the force of gravity does not flatten out the oil, and the shape assumed is due to surface tension.

Pure water at ordinary room temperature has a surface tension of about gram per centimeter. If the needle mentioned above weighs more than this amount per centi-

meter, it cannot be made to float. The surface tensions of most ordinary liquids, except salt solutions, are less than that of pure water. That of mercury and of molten metals is from five to ten times as great. 

Surface tension explains the shapes taken by bubbles and by soap films when stretched over frames. 

The sur faces tend to become as small as possible. It also explains some of the motions of oil globules in soup and of bits of camphor when thrown on water. 

Capillarity. If a number of small glass tubes having different diameters be held vertically with their lower ends beneath the surface of water, the water is seen to rise in them. The height to which the water rises is inversely proportional to the internal diameter of the tube. Every liquid which wets glass rises in the tubes, while those which do not wet it, of which mercury is the 

FIG. 61.- CAPILLARITY 

only common example, are forced down in the tubes below the level of the liquid in the containing vessel. 

The adhesion between water and glass is stronger than the cohesion between water molecules. This force of adhesion pulls a ring of molecules of water up the side of the tube. The surface membrane attached to this ring draws other molecules and causes the tube to fill nearly to the height of the ring. Because of adhesion, the water continues to work up the tube until the weight of the suspended liquid is as much as the ring of molecules can support.

In the case of mercury the liquid does not wet the tube because the force of cohesion is stronger than that of adhesion. The liquid is pulled away from the glass. The

concave side of the curved surface at the top of the liquid column is downward. It acts like a stretched elastic membrane and produces a pressure down on the liquid in the tube. This causes the liquid to stand lower in the tube than it does in the containing vessel. 

Capillary action takes place between any surfaces if they are close together. It explains the absorption of water by a rag, a sponge, or by the soil. 

SECTION 10. FLUIDS IN MOTION 

Fluids in motion. It may seem unnecessary to discuss this topic, because such actions as that of the wind blowing the door open or of the tide moving a ship are extremely simple. That all the effects of moving fluids are not so easily explained is illustrated by this interesting experiment.

We stick a pin through a card about two inches square, place the pin through the opening of a common spool, and blow hard through the spool. With a little practice we are able to cause the card to stick to the spool by blowing harder, and the harder we blow the more tightly is the card held against the spool.

FIG. 62. 

SPOOL AND CARD 

Consider the air at the point A in the diagram. Because of the pressure there exists a certain amount of energy per unit of volume. But when this same air arrives at B much of this energy has become kinetic energy of molecules moving parallel to the surface of the card. Hence less energy is available to produce pressure at right angles to the line of flow. 

The pressure of the stream against the card becomes less than that of the atmosphere, and the card is held to the spool. 

This experiment is one illustration of Bernoulli's principle, which states that, in such cases, the gain in kinetic energy of the flowing stream is made at the expense of the energy which exhibited itself as pressure.

If two steamships, moving parallel, are close to each other, a great force pushes them together. Here the pressure of the more rapidly moving water between the vessels is less than that on the outer sides. Even the tide moving out between ships moored close may cause them to strike together.

The action of a curving baseball is similar. The ball is rotating rapidly as it moves in a forward direction. Hence the air is moving past the ball with a greater velocity on one side than on the other. This produces an excess of pressure on that side where the air velocity is the less, and this force is at right angles to the direction in which the ball is moving, and causes it to curve." 66 Bernoulli's principle explains the action of several types of pumps, such as the simple arrangement used by surgeons to remove blood during an operation, and of certain gauges used in the measuring of the flow of liquids. 

When a liquid or a gas flows through a piece of smaller pipe placed between sections of a larger one, the velocity of the fluid in the smaller pipe must be greater, and the pressure against the walls of the pipe is then less. This is not true if the fluid is at rest. 

The illustration shows a 'Venturi meter." From the difference in pressure in the larger and smaller pipes it is possible to calculate the velocity of the fluid, and hence the flow, it may be in gallons per minute. 66 

FIG. 63. 

A VENTURI METER 

Another interesting application of Bernoulli's principle is in the airplane. The wings are so shaped, that is "cambered," that a cross section of a wing is as shown in Figure 64. As air passes from A to B, that which goes along the upper side of the wing has a longer path than that which passes along the bottom. Because they arrive at B at the same time, the air on top has a greater velocity, and hence less pressure. The excess of pressure upward, due to this cause, supports about

three-fourths of the weight of the plane in normal flying. The other one-fourth is due to the upward effect (component) of the force of the wind against the bottom. See Chapter IV, page 130. 

We speak of the " drag " and of the "lift" of a plane. The drag is the resistance due to friction against the air. This varies 

FIG. 64. 

CROSS SECTION OF A CAMBERED WING 

as the square of the velocity and is designated by D. See 

Chapter IV, page 132. The "lift" is the upward force on the wings and is designated by L. Both of these forces are Lift 

Pull Drag

FIG. 65. AIRPLANE 

LIFT AND DRAG 

When the pull equals the drag and the lift the weight, the plane flies horizontally with a steady speed. proportional to the area of the wings, A, and the square of the velocity, and change with the angle the wings make to the line Hence we have these two equations, of flight.

D = KxAv2 and L KyAv².

Here K and Ky are constants only so long as the angle of the wings is unchanged. That is, for each angle we have a different constant. The plane rises when L is greater than its weight, and drops when it is less. When D is less than the pull of the propeller, the plane flying horizontally, there is a gain in speed. 

This causes D to increase with the square of the velocity. When D equals the pull of the propeller, the plane flies at a steady speed. 

We can get a greater speed without increasing the horse

FIG. 66. 

STREAMLINING 

power of the engine if we can make the drag less. This is done by "streamlining" the parts, which makes the coefficient K smaller.

When an object moves through a fluid, much of the resistance is due to the eddies formed at the rear of the object. Not only 

FIG. 67. -FISH 

is it necessary to part the liquid or gas at the front of the object, but these eddies at the rear decrease the pressure there and, consequently, increase the net pressure ahead to be overcome by the object in its progress.

In a fast airplane even the wires exposed to the air flow are streamlined. Their cross section is not circular but elongated with the more pointed edge towards the rear. More care must be given to the overcoming of eddies in the rear than to the separating of the fluid at the front. 

Long before man knew of them, Nature took advantage of these principles which we have been discussing. The wings of birds are cambered as are the wings of airplanes, and the bodies of whales, seals, and fishes are streamlined.

CHAPTER IV , AND EFFICIENCY SECTION 11. FORCES 

Force.

A force is that which tends to change the con- 

This is a common defi- A force is a set of condition of rest or of motion of a body. nition. To be more exact we say: ditions wherein there is a tendency for energy to pass from one body or one condition to another. It is not necessary that any change actually take place, but only that a tendency for the change exist. One force may so act against another that no motion results. One may push on a car with much force, yet not be able to move it. Some writers use the word "stress" when speaking of a force that does not produce a change in motion. There are many examples of stresses, such as the stress applied to a beam which supports a roof or a bridge. 

Rest. - All bodies at rest with respect to the earth are acted upon by at least two forces, there may be more. 

Consider a steel ball upon a smooth, level marble surface. 

One force is the weight of the ball which draws it towards the center of the earth. This causes a minute dent in the marble and a slight flattening of the ball where the surfaces touch. The elasticity of the marble produces a force, which acting upward counteracts the weight of the ball. 

The weight is a force due to the tendency of the potential energy of the ball, because of its position in the gravitational field of the earth, to pass into the kinetic energy of a falling body. The balancing force is due to the tendency of the spring of the marble to throw the ball into the air. This would take place if it were possible to remove the force of 127

gravity. Here again potential energy would pass into the kinetic form.

While these two forces are equal, the amounts of energy tending to transform each to another form of energy in the two cases are very different. The dent in the marble is too slight to be seen, and the elastic force due to the displacement of the marble could give the ball a very small velocity compared to that acquired if it were allowed to fall any considerable distance. Should the ball fall through a distance equal only to the depth of the dent in the marble, the velocity acquired also would be very small. 

Energy is measured by force multiplied by distance. Conversely, energy divided by distance gives force. Force is the amount of energy change per unit of distance. 

Every object on the earth is acted upon by all those forces which act on the earth as a whole and cause it to take its path in space. As none of these tend to produce motion of a body with respect to the earth we will not consider them here.

When studying the effect of any force applied to a body, there are three things to be kept in mind: the magnitude of the force, the direction in which it acts, and the point of application of the force to the body. The magnitude of a force is the number of pounds, grams, or dynes represented by it. 5 lb-V3+4 

FIG. 68. 

3 lb. 

PARAL- 

LELOGRAM FORCES 

Definitions. 

The resultant of two or more forces is the single force that has the same effect OF as these other forces taken together. This is illustrated by the figure. The 5 lb. is the resultant of the 3 lb. and the 4 lb. The components of a force are any two or more forces, which acting together produce the same effect as the original force. The components of the force of 5 lb. are the forces of 3 lb. and 4 lb. Any one force may be resolved into an unlimited number of pairs or sets of components. Thus, other sets of forces besides the 3 lb.

and the 4 lb. can produce a resultant force of 5 lb., but they would not be at right angles to each other as is the case in the example above. The equilibrant of a force or of several forces is the single force that just balances these. It has the same magnitude as their resultant, but is in exactly the opposite direction. 

Vectors. Forces are good examples of what are called vector quantities. These are not fully determined until their directions as well as their magnitudes are given. Velocities are also vector quantities, but speeds are not. A change in direction does not necessarily mean a change in speed, though it does imply a change in velocity. Consider forces again. Two forces of five and of seven pounds respectively will have very different effects upon a body depending on their directions. The resultant may have any value from twelve pounds down to two pounds. They give the former when acting in the same direction and the latter when in opposite directions.

The parallelogram of forces. We shall now return to the ball upon the level surface. The two forces already discussed have no influence on the motion of the ball in any plane parallel to that of the marble surface. Consider a force tending to roll the ball to the east. The acceleration is proportional to this force, as are also the velocity and the distance passed over in any assigned interval of time. This is also true of all other forces acting parallel to the surface. 

It is reasonable to suppose, and experiment bears us out in this, that the effect of any two forces acting together, after any time interval, is the same as if one of the forces had acted for this time and stopped, and then the other force had produced its effect for a like period of time. 

Suppose one of these forces could move the ball three feet to the east in one second and the other four feet to the north in the same length of time. If each force acts separately for one second, the ball first moves three feet to the east and then four feet to the north, the two paths forming the sides

of a rectangle that has a diagonal of five feet. The effect of the two forces acting together for one second is to cause the ball to pass along this diagonal. But the point reached, at the end, is the same as if it had followed the sides of the rectangle.

If the forces are not acting at right angles, but at some acute or obtuse angle, the paths form the sides of a parallelogram, and the path taken when the forces act together is the diagonal of this figure. Forces are often shown as straight lines drawn in the proper relative directions, the respective lengths of these lines being proportional to the magnitudes 

7 lb 

4 lb. 

4 lb.

FIG. 69. Parallelogram of 

FORCES 

of the forces. Any convenient scale may be used in the drawing. The magnitude of the resultant of the forces can then be determined by measuring the length of the diagonal of the parallelogram. The figure should be drawn carefully according to the rules of geometry. This is not necessary if the angle between the forces is a right angle. Here the diagonal is the hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle and its length can be found by the theorem of Pythagoras; the square of the hypotenuse is equal to the sum of the squares of the other two sides.

One can use this method with other vector quantities such as velocities. Suppose an airplane is flying north at 

100 miles/hour, and the wind is blowing it east at 30 miles/ hour. Then the actual velocity of the machine with respect to the earth is 104.4 miles/hour. The direction taken by the machine can be found by drawing the parallelogram of forces.

If the resultant of more than two forces is wanted, it is found by first combining any two of the forces, and then by combining this resultant with another force, and so on, until all are included.

The resolution of forces. Any force may be thought of as being the resultant of certain components. We will apply this to an example of Archimedes' principle. The figure represents a block of stone suspended in water. The stone displaces 12 lb. of water. Hence the upward

The second figure represents a barrel that weighs 400 lb. being rolled up an inclined plane by a force of only

The third example is a diagram representing the force of the air against the wing of an airplane. This force may be resolved into two components. One component is balanced by the pull of the propeller and is the friction against the air.

The other component is balanced by the weight of the machine and is the force that holds it in the air or the lifting force.

When we resolve a force into two components there are six quantities to be considered, the three magnitudes and the three directions. Of these six quantities any four may be given and it is then possible to find the other two by means of the parallelogram of forces. This can be constructed according to the methods of geometry, but, there are cases in which a parallelogram cannot be drawn and the problem is then impossible. Suppose we have two forces of 100 lb. and of 50 lb. respectively. No matter in what direction we have these forces act, the resultant cannot be 10 lb. 

The least it can be is 50 lb., when they pull in opposite directions, and the greatest it can be is 150 lb., which is when they pull in the same directions. Conversely, a force of 

10 lb. cannot be resolved into a force of 100 lb. and a force of

We lay off the three directions as three lines diverting from a point, having the proper angles between them. 

Using any convenient scale we now lay off ten units from the point along the middle line. The figure is finished by drawing lines parallel to the two given sides and passing through the upper end of the diagonal. A long wire will break of its own weight before it can be drawn horizontal because of the terrific strain necessary to draw it into a straight line.

LEVERS, MOMENTS, AND EQUILIBRIUM 

The lever. A rigid rod, free to turn about one point of support, the fulcrum, is called a lever. We often use levers wh 

FIG. 77.-A LEVER 

to pry things apart and to move heavy weights.

H Let a board be balanced on a fulcrum by having a large weight, W, on one end and a smaller one, w, on the other. We here neglect the weight of the board, but later we shall learn how to include it also.

When a small extra force necessary to overcome friction

and inertia is added to w the longer end of the board descends and W rises. Let the respective perpendicular distances be h and H as in the figure. 

We have, from the law of the Conservation of Energy, Wh = w H or W/wH/h. (The second equation is obtained from the first by dividing both terms by wh.)

It is seen that the two triangles with their common vertex at ƒ are similar. Hence, by geometry, H/h = L/l. Upon substituting the L's for the H's in the above equation we have,

WI = wL. 

This expresses the law of the lever: The smaller force times the longer arm is equal to the larger force times the shorter We use the L's instead of the H's because they remain the same and are much easier to measure. arm.

We here have a special case of a general law that applies to any pair of forces acting through any machine in the manner described. The product of the smaller force and the distance through which it acts is equal to the product of the larger force and the distance through which it acts. 

The machine may consist of a number of levers, wheels and axles, or inclined planes but the general law will always apply. This is a special case of the Law of the Conservation of Energy.

Moments. In the lever the two weights are on a line that passes through the center of rotation. We shall now consider cases where this is not true. The figure represents a wellbalanced wheel. Two weights, W and w, are attached to the wheel. The weights balance in only one position of the wheel. This is when Wd wD, where the D's are the distances from the center of rotation perpendicular 

FIG. 78. -MOMENTS

to the vertical line through the weights. The larger W goes with the smaller d, and vice versa. The product, LINE OF THE FORCE 

25 

LINE OF THE FORCE FIG. 79. APPARATUS TO ILLUSTRATE MOMENTS OF FORCE 

Moments that tend to turn the wheel in the direction of motion of the hands of a clock are called negative moments, those that tend to turn in the opposite direction are positive. 

Wd, measures the turning ability of W. As the wheel revolves in the direction of the hands of a clock, the weight w has less turning effect and 

W has greater. But if the wheel is turned in the opposite direction, the weight w becomes the stronger. Hence there is but one position of bal- ance. 

A moment is a measure of the ability of a force to produce turning. It is found by multiplying the magnitude of the force by the perpendicular distance from the center of rotation to the line of the force. 

The line of a force is a line drawn in the direction in which the force acts and through the point at which it is applied. 

When a force is produced by a weight the line of the force is vertically downward. But forces may be caused by springs, taut ropes, and so on, and may be in any direction. The diagram represents a number of forces acting in the directions of the arrows, and tending to turn the wheel in one direction or the other about its center.

FIG. 80. MOMENTS

A moment tending to turn an object in the direction contrary to that of the hands of a clock is a positive moment. 

One tending to turn it in the opposite direction is a negative moment. We suppose that the moments represented by the figure balance and there is no turning. Then we can write, Fidi+F2d2 F3d3+F4d4. 

There are eight quantities in the above equation, and if any seven of them are known the other can be calculated. 

The rules for moments depend upon the Law of the Conservation of Energy. It can be shown that, when there is a very slight amount of turning, the distances through which the forces act are proportional to the perpendicular distances designated by d in the figure. 

Energy is measured by the product of a force and a distance. Returning to the wheel with the two weights we see that the energy expended in lowering one weight is equal to the energy used in raising the other. The energies are equal, and proportional to the product of the force and the respective perpendicular distance, hence Wd = wD.

Center of gravity. Each particle of a solid is attracted 

towards the center of the earth by the force of gravity. If the solid is supported at one point, One all these small forces form moments about this point of rotation. point can be found, in general within the solid, about which these moments always balance. This point is the center of gravity of the body. The center of gravity of a body is a point at which it may be supported and be balanced in all positions. In problems involving moments the entire mass of a body may be thought of as concentrated at its center of gravity.

FIG. 83. FINDING THE CENTER OF GRAVITY 

Suppose the beam in the problem mentioned above weighs 100 lb. and its center of gravity is at the point 10 ft. from B. This will produce a positive

moment of 100 x 10 = 1,000. Add this to the other positive moments, solve the equation again, and the weight supported at A is seen to be 1,276 lb.

The center of gravity of an irregularly shaped board or other object of uniform thickness and density can be found as follows. Suspend the board from any point, A, so that it is free to swing. It will come to rest with the center of gravity directly below A because it acts as though all the mass were concentrated here. Using a plumb-line draw a perpendicular through A. The center of gravity is somewhere along this line. Now suspend the board from another point, B; let it come to rest; draw a perpendicular line through B. The center of gravity is somewhere along this second line. As it is on both lines it must be at the point where the two lines cross. As a check, repeat, suspending the board from a third point, C. The three lines should pass through a point. The center of gravity is at this point and half way through the board. — - Equilibrium. Let us consider a cone balanced upon its pointed end or vertex. The center of gravity is evidently somewhere along the line passing through the vertex and the center of the base. If the cone is tipped in any direction, the center of gravity will be lowered. The body acts as though all its mass were concentrated at the center of gravity. Hence, when the cone is tipped, though some Get

FIG. 84. - EQUILIBRIA 

of the mass is raised and some is lowered, the net effect is a lowering of mass and a loss of potential energy due to the position of the cone. The cone will continue to tip until it falls upon its side. In all such cases the tendency is for the potential energy to become as small as possible. The least tip given the body will cause it to fall. Such a condition is called unstable equilibrium. 

A cone resting on its base cannot be tipped over unless the

center of gravity is first raised. Work must be expended upon the body as it is tipped for the effect is a raising of the whole mass of the body. Such a condition is called stable equilibrium.

Consider a cone lying upon its side. As it is rolled about the center of gravity is neither raised nor lowered. When the cone is slightly displaced, there is little tendency for it to continue moving nor does it roll back to its former position. 

It remains in whatever position it is rolled. A body in this condition is in neutral equilibrium. A sphere on a level surface is another example of this. 

The higher the center of gravity of a body must be raised to tip it over, the more stable the equilibrium. A brick resting on its flat side is more stable than one on its edge or one standing on end. An automobile with a low center of gravity is less apt to overturn than one that stands higher. 

SECTION 12. SIMPLE MACHINES 

The simple machines. The five simple machines are the lever, the wheel and axle, the inclined plane, the pulley, and the screw.

We have already discussed the lever. When the fulcrum is between the points where the forces act, it is called a lever of the first class. The arms of a beam balance or a teeter-totter are good examples of this. 66 A lever is said to be of the second class when the fulcrum is at one end, the force applied at the other end, and the point where the work is expended is between these. The handles of a wheel-barrow and a nut-cracker illustrate this. 

A lever is said to be of the third class when the fulcrum is at one end, the point where the work is expended at the other end, and the point where the force is applied between these. With this form of lever there is a loss of force but a gain in speed of the moving part. The forearm, joined at the elbow, forms such a lever. When the arm is raised, the hand moves much faster than the tendon at the end of the muscle that causes the motion. 

We have the same thing in driving a golf ball. The body is the

fulcrum, the force is applied through the arms, while great speed is acquired by the end of the club. 40* 

FIG. 85. FIVE SIMPLE MACHINES 

In general, complicated machines are made up of various combinations of the five simple machines. Note the great number of levers, wheels, screws, and so on that make up a printing press or an automobile. In each of the simple machines, as well as in all combinations of them, a force is applied at some one point and moves that point a certain distance, applying work to a useful purpose by causing some other point to move a certain distance. The force exerted 

FIG. 86. ELBOW AND HAMMER 

The hammer as used here is a lever of the first class, the elbow is one of the third class. at this second point may be greater or it may be less than that applied to the first point. In all cases the smaller force moves over the greater distance according to the equation, fD = Fd.

It is here understood that the loss of energy through friction may be neglected.

Dividing both sides of this equation by fd we have D/d=F/f. 

This number, the ratio of the large to the small force, or the longer to the shorter distance, is called the mechanical advantage of the machine. It tells how many times the applied force is magnified, or by how many times the speed of a moving part is increased. 

The wheel and axle. 

This machine is illustrated by a wheel on an axle, a rope passing around the wheel with a small weight, w, attached, and another rope about the axle with a larger weight, W, attached. The two weights tend to turn the wheel in opposite directions. Suppose the weights balance. Let us turn the wheel one complete revolution, thus lowering w and raising W. radius of the wheel is designated by R and of the axle by r. The 

FIG. 87. 

WHEEL AND AXLE 

2π Rw= 2πrW, or

The small weight is lowered the distance 2TR and the large weight is raised the distance 2πr. As the weights balance these two amounts of work are equal, or

Rw = rW or

R/r R/r= W/w. 

Hence, the mechanical advantage of a wheel and axle is the radius of the wheel divided by the radius of the axle or, what is the same thing, the diameter of the wheel divided by the diameter of the axle.

The above can be proved by experiment. To have w lift W, a small additional weight must be added to w to

overcome friction. 

Let us call the weight (w+x). To have w raised by W the same small weight is subtracted from w, enough to overcome friction. Call the weight (w — x).

The average of these two small weights is w, the weight that exactly balances W.

Sometimes a number of pairs of wheels on separate axles are connected together by means of gears or belts. In this case the mechanical advantage of the combination is the product, not the sum, of the separate mechanical advantages.

The inclined plane.- 

A good example of the use of the inclined plane is when a man rolls a barrel up a plank into a doorway. Suppose the board is 15 ft. long, the doorway

3 ft. high, and the barrel weighs 200 lb. When the object has been lifted into the door it is evident that 200 lb. have been raised 3 ft., and the work accomplished is 600 ft.-lb. 

Hence, by the Law of the Conservation of Energy and neglecting the loss of friction, the force pushing the barrel, x, times the length of the plane, 15 ft., must be 600 ft.-lb. of work. Then x = 600 15 40 lb. If we let F be the weight of the barrel and ƒ the force required to roll it up the plane we have,

F/f = 200/40 = 5 = 15/3 D/d. 

The mechanical advantage of an inclined plane is the length divided by the height. 

The screw. - One can consider the screw as an inclined plane wound about a rod. Common applications of the screw are in the letter-press and the screw-jack. These are used to produce great pressure and to lift heavy weights respectively. Let f be the force applied to the handle of a screw-jack and let F be the lifting force if there were no friction. Let R be the radius of the circle made as the hand turns the handle around once, or the length of the handle. As the hands move this distance the jack raises the distance between two threads of the screw, called its pitch. Let p be the pitch of the screw. Then 2πR

FIG. 90. -A SCREW JACK 

D/d 2πR/p = F/f. 

The mechanical advantage of a screw-jack is the circumference of the circle made by the end of the handle divided by the pitch of the screw.

In finding the mechanical advantage of a screw-jack the beginner is very apt to make two mistakes. As the pitch of the screw is generally given in inches, the circumference of the circle made by the handle must be in inches also.

If the pitch is four threads to the inch, the distance between the threads is one-quarter inch, and when one divides by one-quarter he multiplies by four. For example, if the handle is 2 ft. long and there are five threads to the inch, the mechanical advantage is 2.5 X 12 × 2 × TX 5=942.6.

SECTION 13. FRICTION AND EFFICIENCY FRICTION 

When two surfaces are in contact, a certain force is required to move one with respect to the other. This force is called friction. No surface can be made perfectly smooth and there is a certain amount of friction in all machines. 

Friction may be sliding or rolling. There is also friction when one or both of the bodies is a liquid or gas. 

Sliding friction is due to the roughness of the surfaces. 

If we could magnify the parts we should very likely see hills and hollows on even the smoothest of objects. When surfaces are in contact the hills of one fit more or less closely into the hollows of the other. No matter how well the hills are ground down there is a certain amount of catching between the molecules of the two surfaces. When friction occurs some molecules and groups of molecules are ground off. Others are but drawn from their normal position and then spring back. The motion is communicated to the other molecules of the body and appears to our senses as heat. As work is expended in producing this motion there is a certain loss of energy so far as its useful application is concerned. 

Sliding friction is lessened by making the surfaces smoother and by the use of lubricants such as oil, graphite, or soap. 

These probably make the surfaces more smooth by filling the hollows, and they also reduce the amount of adhesion. 

The irregularities of surfaces will, in general, fit more closely into each other if of the same material than if of different substances. Hence friction between like materials, as steel on steel, or brass on brass, is greater than when the

materials are unlike, as steel on brass. This is one reason for making the bearings of machines of bronze or of babbit metal. In like manner the jewels of watches reduce the wear of moving parts, though the extreme hardness of the jewels is also a factor. 

The coefficient of friction. - Experiments show that the amount of friction between two surfaces is proportional to the force pressing the surfaces together. The constant obtained by dividing the force necessary to move one surface over the other without acceleration by the force that holds them together is called the coefficient of friction.

It is often given as a per cent. 

If the bodies are very rough 0008

FIG. 91. COEFFICIENT OF FRICTION 

The coefficient of friction is found by dividing the force required to move one surface over the other by the force pressing them together. or of some soft substance the coefficient of friction may exceed one hundred per cent. 

Suppose a force of 200 g is required to drag a 500 g block of wood across the table. From the definition we see that the coefficient of friction is .4 or 40%.

Friction is always greater when the motion is starting. 

It requires more than 200 g to cause the above block to start to move though this force can keep it in motion. Perhaps this is because there has then been time for the irregularities of the surfaces to fit into each other. 

Within reasonable limits the amount of friction is independent of the extent of the surfaces. It takes almost the same force to drag a brick whether it lies flat, on its side, or on an end. This rule does not apply when one surface is so small that it sinks perceptibly into the other. 

Friction is proportional to the force pressing the surfaces together, is greater at starting, and is independent of the extent of surface. These three facts are sometimes called the three laws of friction.

Rolling friction. The wheels of a vehicle, as it rolls along a stretch of asphalt, sink slightly into the pavement.

The wheels continue to press down fresh portions of the pavement, and the effect is the same as though they were climbing over a rise in the road. A motor truck that makes twenty-five miles an hour on concrete may be slowed down to much less than half this speed when it strikes a piece of asphalt paving on a hot summer day. The harder the surfaces the less noticeable is this, but it is present to some extent even in the case of the metal rails beneath a train of cars. This explains the friction of rolling objects. Ball and roller bearings reduce friction greatly as here the surfaces are extremely hard. But even in this case there is some sinking into the steel surface on which the balls or rollers move and some friction remains. 

Fluid friction. Except at starting the friction between solids is independent of the speed of the moving parts. 

This is not the case when one of the substances is a liquid or gas. For low speeds the friction between a solid and a fluid is considered as varying with the square of the velocity, but as the speed becomes greater the friction increases very fast. It requires much more coal to operate a fast train over a given section of railroad than it does to operate a slow one, because of air resistance. The friction between a ship and the water is considered as varying with the cube of the speed. It requires twenty-seven times as much power to increase the speed of a boat to three times its former value. 

Hence it is expensive to run ships at high speed, and it requires a great sacrifice in guns and armor to design cruisers having a relatively high speed.

We are apt to think of friction as more or less of a nuisance and as something we should like to abolish. But it is very useful indeed. It is the friction between the wheels and the road or the track that enables an automobile or a train to move. If there were no friction whatever, we could not even walk across the floor.

Efficiency. The ratio of the useful work obtained from a machine to the total work put into it is called its efficiency.

This is often given as a percentage. As some energy is always wasted in friction the efficiency of a machine is always less than 100%. Suppose we have a machine wherein the applied force, ƒ, is smaller than the resulting force, F. If we do not neglect friction we must write fD Fd+x, where x is the energy used in overcoming friction. In this case Fd/fD is the efficiency and it is less than 100% because Fd is less than fD. 

The efficiency of the lever is, in general, close to 100% as there is here very little friction. That of the other simple machines varies much, and with the screw-jack it may be less than 25%.

The method used in problems involving efficiency is illustrated by the following. Suppose we have a screwjack with four threads to the inch operated by a handle 

4 ft. long and we know the efficiency is 18%. Suppose we wish to find how much can be lifted by a force of 20 lb. applied to the handle.

We must first find the mechanical advantage, which is the distance the end of the handle moves divided by the distance the jack raises the weight. When the handle makes one revolution the weight is lifted the distance between the threads or of an inch. The distance the end of the handle moves is 2π (4 X 12) or 96 inches. The mechanical advantage is then 96 ÷ or 288π which is equal to 904.9.

If there were no friction one pound at the handle would lift 

904.9 lb. and 20 lb. would lift 18,098 lb. But the machine is only 18% efficient, hence it can lift only 18% of this amount or 3,258 lb.

Suppose, now, we wish to find out how much force must be applied to the handle in order to lift one ton. If there were no friction one pound could lift 904.9 lb., and with friction it can lift 18% of this or 162.9 lb., and it will require 2,000 ÷ 162.9 or 12.3lb., to raise a weight of one ton,

CHAPTER V 

HEAT 

SECTION 14. THE NATURE OF HEAT AND OF TEMPERATURE 

When

Heat is energy localized in a body due to the condition of its molecules. This energy may be in either the potential or the kinetic form. The amount of the potential energy is determined by the arrangement, the relative positions and the distances that separate the molecules and atoms. the amount of this form of energy in a body is increased it does not become warmer, in other words, there is no increase in temperature. We are interested principally in heat as energy in the kinetic form. The kinetic energy of the molecules as they move about or vibrate back and forth is easily communicated to other bodies. When there is an increase in this form of heat energy there is a rise in the temperature of the object.

The temperature of a body has to do with the average amount of kinetic energy per molecule. But it requires different amounts of total energy per molecule to raise the temperature of various substances one degree. This is because, for different substances, the amounts of heat energy are divided between the kinetic and the potential forms in different proportions, and the kinetic energy may be due to different kinds of motions.

That heat is energy is proved as follows. All other forms of energy may be converted into heat, and, conversely, heat may be converted into the other forms of energy. 

If one rubs the palms of his hands together briskly the heat resulting will be quite noticeable. Here the kinetic energy of the moving hands is reduced to heat through friction. A 153

nail may be made quite hot by placing it on an anvil and striking it with a hammer. The kinetic energy of the moving hammer is changed into heat. Savages sometimes kindle a fire by rubbing sticks together. The iron shavings turned off by a machinist's lathe are at times hot enough to burn the fingers. Much of the electrical energy used by an incandescent lamp is changed into heat. Chemical energy is changed into heat when we burn anything. Another example of heat resulting from chemical energy may be observed when sulphuric acid is poured into water, which then becomes very warm. The heat of our bodies is produced in a somewhat similar way. The radiant energy of the sun warms the objects upon which it falls. These are examples of the change of other forms of energy into heat. 

A good example of the change of heat into mechanical energy is in the steam engine, as the work performed by the engine is accomplished by using the heat of a fire. Also, with the gas engine the energy of the explosion in the cylinder is due to the sudden generation of heat. Many actions of nature, such as winds, ocean currents, geysers, and even water-falls, are examples of heat energy being transformed into mechanical energy. In the case of the water-fall the action is not direct.

Potential and kinetic forms. The potential and the kinetic forms of heat energy are illustrated in the warming and melting of ice and in the formation of steam. Suppose a pan of cracked ice has been exposed for some time to a temperature of ten degrees below freezing. A thermometer placed in the ice registers ten below freezing. Suppose this pan is now placed on a hot stove. If the ice is stirred it will be found to grow warmer, but none melts until the temperature of it has risen ten degrees. Then, for a time there is a mixture of ice and water but if the mixture is kept well stirred it does not become perceptibly warmer until all of the ice has melted, although it has been absorbing much heat from the fire during this time. When the

HEAT 

155 ice is melted the temperature of the water again begins to rise and continues until the boiling point is reached. Then there is no further rise in temperature as the water is transformed into steam. If some of the steam is contained in a suitable vessel it may be made as hot as one pleases. In the Valley of Ten Thousand Smokes steam issues from the earth at such a temperature that sticks of wood placed therein catch on fire when they are removed. As ice passes into water and as water passes into steam, the absorbed energy does not impart a greater amount of kinetic energy 100° -90° +80° -70° ·60°

This is similar to the raising of a weight above the ground, thus storing potential energy. As kinetic energy appears when a weight falls, so is much heat given out to the adjacent bodies when water freezes into ice or condenses from steam.

The energy of the vibrating molecule of a solid resembles that of a swinging pendulum in that it is continually changing from the kinetic to the potential form and vice versa. 

When the pendulum bob is at its lowest point all the energy is kinetic, then it is all changed to potential again when the bob reaches its highest point. We can think of the molecules

of solids and liquids as vibrating about fixed points, held in place by the force of cohesion. Most of the molecular energy of gases is kinetic, due to the great speeds with which the molecules move about. But even here some of it is in the potential form. There is some attraction between the molecules of all gases. This attraction is not strong enough to hold the molecules together, but it does show its effect in slackening their speeds. Temperature.

In common speech temperature measures how hot or how cold an object is. It evidently has to do with the readiness with which heat passes from one body to another. If a number of objects are placed in an enclosure they will, in time, all acquire the same temperature, and no heat then passes from one to the other. 

We can not give an accurate definition of temperature without using the idea of a "perfect gas." This is an imaginary substance wherein there is no attraction between the molecules and all the energy taken up is in the kinetic form. 

It is all used in causing the molecules to fly back and forth with greater speed. No gas is "perfect" but the gases of the air, oxygen and nitrogen, as well as hydrogen, are nearly so at ordinary temperatures, and it is possible to make corrections for their departures from the performance of a perfect gas.

Temperature is a measure of the average kinetic energy per molecule of a perfect gas. The temperature of a substance is a measure of the average kinetic energy per molecule of a perfect gas when the latter has been left in contact with the substance long enough for both to have acquired the same temperature. 

Gas pressure and temperature. It is known that the pressure of an enclosed gas is due to its molecules striking the walls of the container. It can be shown from Newton's second and third laws of motion, that, when a ball strikes a wall, the average force acting against the surface of the wall while the bodies are in contact is directly proportional to

HEAT 

157 the momentum, mass times the velocity, of the ball. Hence, if a large number of balls are thrown against a surface the pressure is proportional to the average momentum of all the balls. Hence the pressure of a gas increases with the momentum of the molecules. Now, if the average speed of the molecules of a gas is doubled, each molecule not only hits twice as hard, but it moves across the vessel in half the time and hits the walls twice as often. So doubling the velocity (of the molecules of a gas) increases the pressure (on the containing vessel) four times. The pressure of a gas varies as the square of the average velocity of its molecules. This is not exactly true except in the case of a perfect gas, because each molecule that strikes the walls has had to pull itself away from its neighbors, and its average kinetic energy upon striking is not quite its average kinetic energy at other times. But correction can be made for this difference, and in the case of the air or hydrogen it may be neglected.

The kinetic energy of a molecule is given by M№2/2 ergs if M and v are measured in the proper units. But the pressure of a perfect gas varies as the average momentum times the average velocity or as Mv2. Hence, by our definition of temperature, we note that it is proportional to the pressure exerted by a perfect gas. It is known that the difference between the temperature as derived from the pressure of hydrogen and that which would be given by a perfect gas is only a few thousandths of a degree for all ordinary temperatures. 

Temperature scales. The first thermometer was made by Galileo in 1592. He measured temperatures by the amount of expansion of air in an enclosed space. From 

Boyle's law we know this expansion bears a simple relation to the pressure the air would have acquired if it had not been allowed to expand when heated. Today most thermometers consist of mercury in a glass tube and the temperature is indicated by the amount of expansion of the liquid. 

The amount of this expansion is known to be almost exactly

proportional to the true increase in temperature which would be indicated by the increased pressure of a gas. 

Three temperature scales are in common use, the centigrade, the absolute, and the Fahrenheit. The centigrade uses the temperature at which pure water freezes as zero and that at which it boils as 100, the pressure in both cases being 76 cm of mercury. One degree centigrade is then one one-hundredth of the temperature rise from the freezing to the boiling points of water. The absolute scale begins at 273° below the centigrade zero, which is theoretically, as we shall see, the coldest anything can be. One degree rise on this scale is the same as one degree on the centigrade scale, and centigrade readings may be changed to absolute simply by adding 273.

Fahrenheit took zero as the coldest weather of which he had knowledge and the temperature of the human body as 96 because it is an even multiple of twelve. On this scale water freezes at 32° and boils at 212°. It was afterwards discovered that an error had been made and that the temperature of the human body is 98.6° Fahrenheit.

Between the temperatures of freezing and of boiling water there are 100 centigrade degrees and 180 Fahrenheit degrees.

Hence the latter are 188 or § as large as the former. To reduce Fahrenheit readings to centigrade one must first subtract 32 to find how many Fahrenheit degrees this is above the centigrade zero, and then multiply by to get the centigrade reading, as five of the larger degrees equal nine of the smaller. If the original reading is below zero one must be careful that the subtraction of 32° is algebraic and takes into consideration the sign of the reading. Ten degrees below zero is written -10°.

The Hydrogen thermometer. The construction and cali bration of this instrument is as follows. A bulb of pure hydrogen, B, is attached to the glass tube, G, by means of the flexible tube F. The tube contains mercury which the pressure of the hydrogen raises to a certain height. The upper part of G is a vacuum. The scale, S, is fixed relative to the bulb, but the tube G may be moved up and down. The higher G is raised the greater the pressure the mercury exerts on the gas in B, and the amount of this pressure is measured by the height on the scale, S. 

A centigrade scale is made as follows. B is surrounded by melting ice, the air pressure being 76 cm of mercury, and G is raised until the top of the mercury in the shorter arm of the tube is at some mark as at x.

The heat increases the pressure in B and drives the mercury below x. It is brought back to this point by raising G and thus increasing the pressure and decreasing the volume of the gas in B. The mercury in G now stands at a higher point on the scale S than it did when the bulb was in ice. The point where it stood when the bulb was in ice is marked zero and the point that it reached when the bulb was surrounded by steam 100. The space between these two marks is divided into 100 equal parts. The divisions may be extended beyond the 100 mark and below the zero mark for higher and lower temperatures.

To read any temperature the volume of the gas is brought to its proper value by raising or lowering G until the top of the mercury in the short arm is at x. The temperature is then indicated by the position of the upper level of the mercury on the scale. This position is determined by the height of the mercury above x, that is, by the pressure of the hydrogen in B, which is produced by the average kinetic energy of the hydrogen molecules. Thus we observe that the average kinetic energy of the molecules determines the temperature. arm.

That is, we can start with the pressure in B at any value when we fix the zero mark on the scale. This is because the density of the gas, the number of molecules per cubic centimeter, affects the pressure as well as their average kinetic energy. Now, having determined the marks on the scale, with the pressure at zero degrees at any value whatever, it is found that a rise of one degree above zero, or a drop of one degree below zero, causes the pressure at zero to rise or fall by 1/273 of its value.

This means that a change of one degree at zero centigrade indicates a change in average kinetic energy of the molecules of 1/273. A drop of 273 degrees below zero would mean a loss of 273/273 of their kinetic energy. In other words there would be no kinetic energy at all. Hence 

273° below zero centigrade is the coldest anything can become.

Two laws. The theory given above resulted from a knowledge of two laws which are named after the Frenchmen who discovered them. They may now be stated as follows. Law of Charles:

Keeping the volume constant, the ratio of the increase in pressure to the increase in temperature is nearly the same for all gases. 

There is an increase of 1/273 of the original pressure at zero degrees for each degree centigrade rise in temperature. The law of 

Gay-Lussac: Keeping the pressure constant the ratio of the increase in volume to the increase in temperature is nearly the same for all gases. This is 1/273 of the original volume at zero centigrade for each degree rise in temperature. Either of these laws may be derived from the other through the use of Boyle's law. All three are combined into a single formula: PIV₁ T₁ P2V2 P₂V T2 P3V3 = a constant, or PV

T3 - ᎡᎢ . 

Here P represents the pressure, V the volume, T the absolute temperature, and R is a constant that depends on the number of molecules of gas present. (If one gram molecule of gas is taken, 6.06 X 1023 molecules, R is 8.32 × 107 ergs. This value of R is called the " gas constant.") 

A mercury thermometer may be calibrated by using melting ice and steam in exactly the same way as they were used in calibrating the gas thermometer. The expansion of mercury with increase of temperature is almost exactly proportional to the rise in temperature. Most of the error of a glass-mercury thermometer is due to the unequal expansion of the two substances of which the instrument is made. A good instrument reads temperatures correctly to within one-tenth of one degree. 

For very accurate work it is necessary to have the corrections for the various readings of the scale as obtained by comparison with a gas thermometer. This latter, at ordinary temperatures, will give the true temperature (as would be indicated by a perfect gas) to within a few thousandths of a degree, and even at 200° it is accurate within one-quarter of a degree. It is well to

remember that, except in the laboratory, most temperature readings are made on a Fahrenheit scale which is also used by the Weather Bureau. A comfortable temperature of 70° F. is about the same as 21° C. - It is evident that nothing can On the other hand we do not 

Extremes of temperature. be colder than absolute zero. know what is the highest temperature that exists in the universe. 

A gas thermometer can be used up to 1,550° C., while higher temperatures are determined by electrical and optical methods. 

Very low temperatures are measured by gas thermometers of helium. Solid hydrogen melts at 259° C. below zero and lead at 327° C. above. Copper melts at about 1,000° C. The melting point of iron is about 1,500° C., of platinum 1,756° C., and of tungsten about 3,300° C. The melting point of carbon, if it does not change directly to a vapor, is much higher yet. The temperature of the surface of the sun is probably around 6,000° C., while Professor Eddington, a noted English astronomer, estimates the temperatures of the interiors of stars at from four to forty million degrees centigrade. This implies a velocity of the lighter molecules of about one hundred miles per second.

THERMAL EXPANSION 

It is a matter of common experience that many substances expand on heating and contract on cooling. We have already mentioned the behavior of the mercury in a thermometer. The joints between the rails of the railroad are farther apart in cold weather than in warm. Telephone wires sing in the cold winter winds because they are drawn more taut. The steel tires of wagons and of locomotives are heated in order to place them over the wheels. The reader probably can think of other instances in which the expansion of substances due to heat is evident. 

For the same rise in temperature gases expand more in proportion to their volumes than do liquids and, in turn, liquids expand more than solids. We have learned that gases expand 1/273 of their volume at zero centigrade for

HEAT 

163 each degree rise in temperature. 

Under like conditions liquids expand from five to ten parts in ten thousand; while solids increase from three to fifty parts in one million. The linear coefficient of thermal expansion. For brevity we often call this simply the coefficient of expansion. If a copper rod one meter in length is heated 50° C., it expands about .0835 cm, as can be determined by a micrometer screw. This is .00167 cm for each degree. Then each 

FIG. 101. UNEQUAL EXPANSION 

Here we have a strip of steel and one of brass brazed together. As the coefficient of expansion of brass is greater than that of steel, when heated the rod curls with the steel on the inner side. Use is made of this principle in the thermostat, an instrument used to regulate temperature. centimeter of the rod expands .0000167 cm for each degree rise in temperature. Experiment shows that the amount of linear expansion is proportional to the length of the rod and to the rise in temperature. Hence a yard of copper expands .0000167 of a yard and a mile .0000167 of a mile for each degree rise in temperature. The coefficient of expansion is the fraction of its length a solid expands for each degree rise in temperature. Hence, to find how much a body expands for a known rise in temperature we multiply the proper coefficient by the length of the body and then by the number of degrees of rise in temperature.
