The expansion of water. Water does not expand uniformly as the temperature passes from below freezing to boiling. Beginning at zero centigrade it contracts in volume as it

grows warmer until four degrees is reached. At this point it has its maximum density. A further warming produces expansion which continues until the boiling point is reached. 

That water below four degrees C. expands upon cooling is of the greatest importance. This causes the coldest water of lakes, rivers, and other bodies of water to remain on the top and there form the first ice. If this were not so, the Sun Mon. Tues

Besides, the coating of ice on the surface helps retain the heat of the water and retards further freezing. 

The temperature of the deep ocean throughout the world is at four degrees, caused by cold water from the arctic regions. If it were made either colder or warmer than this it would rise to the top on account of the loss of density. 

SECTION 15. HEAT PASSING FROM ONE BODY OR ONE FORM TO ANOTHER HEAT UNITS 

We shall now discuss the measuring of heat which is quite a different thing from the measuring of temperature. There

HEAT 

165 are two common units of heat, the calorie and the British thermal unit. The calorie is the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of one gram of water one degree centigrade. 

The large calorie is sometimes used, in which case the mass of water considered is one kilogram. The British thermal unit is the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of one pound of water one degree Fahrenheit. The abbreviation is B.t.u. This last unit is much used in engineering. For very exact scientific work the temperature at which the calorie is taken must be specified. For example, the amount of heat in a calorie is about of one per cent more if it is taken at 15° C. than if it is taken at 20° C. 

Specific heat. The specific heat of a substance is the number of calories required to raise the temperature of one gram of that substance one degree centigrade. It may also be defined as the ratio of the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of a mass of the substance a certain number of degrees as compared to the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of an equal mass of water the same number of degrees. The specific heats of materials vary somewhat with their temperatures.

The method of mixtures for determining specific heats. - 

We shall now explain a simple method for finding the specific heats of solids and liquids. It depends on the principle that, when a hot substance is placed in cool water, the amount of heat lost by the body is equal to that gained by the water.

Suppose S grams of the substance are placed in W grams of water. If x is the specific heat of the substance then x calories of heat come out of each gram for each degree it cools. Let th be the temperature of the hot substance and tm the resulting temperature of the mixture. Then Sx times (ttm) gives the number of calories that came out of the hot substance as it cooled to the temperature of the mixture. 

Some heat is taken up by the vessel, called a calorimeter, in which the water is contained. Allowance for this is made

as follows. The mass of the vessel multiplied by the specific heat of that substance of which it is made gives the number of calories required to raise the temperature of the vessel one degree. This product is called the "water equivalent " of the calorimeter and indicates that the vessel absorbs heat to the same extent as would this mass of water. We shall designate this by Q. If there are W grams of water present it is evident that it requires (W + Q) calories to raise the temperature of both water and vessel one degree. If we represent the temperature of the cold water before the substance is placed in it by to it is evident that the number of calories entering both the water and the container is (W+Q) (tmtc). As the calories of heat leaving the substance are equal to those entering the water and the vessel we can form an equation in which all the quantities except the specific heat can be directly measured. Sx(th-tm) (W+Q) (tm− tc) The water equivalent is calculated from the specific heat of the substance composing the vessel as found in tables. If not, it can be calculated by first dropping some of the material of which the vessel is made, while hot, into the water and then the specific heat of that substance is the only unknown in the equation.

Sx(th-tm) (W + Mx) (tm — tc). where M is the mass of the vessel.

The mechanical equivalent of heat. — Having devised units with which to measure heat we are now in a position

HEAT This to show how much heat energy enters various substances and that in some cases this energy is taken up without warming the body. We are also able to determine how much mechanical energy is equivalent to one unit of heat and thus go far to establish the Law of the Conservation of Energy. 

The first man who did much to establish the theory that heat is a form of energy and not some mysterious fluid was 

Benjamin Thompson, Count Rumford, an American by birth who afterwards became a prominent official of the Austrian empire. His interest was aroused by the large amount of heat developed in the boring of cannon. was in 1798. Forty-four years afterwards, 1842, the English physicist, James Prescott Joule, began a series of famous experiments by which he determined the exact relationship between heat and other forms of energy. Joule used a known amount of mechanical energy by allowing weights to drop measured distances. These turned paddle wheels in water. The friction heated the water and the amount of heat formed was determined from the mass of the water and its rise in temperature. He found that it requires 

427 gram-meters of mechanical energy to produce one calorie of heat, a value confirmed by modern experiments. The mechanical equivalent of heat is the number of units of work that are equal to one unit of heat. A very recent and careful determination of this constant is given in ergs per calorie. 

It is well to bear this table in mind. 

41,850,000 ergs or 

42,700 gram-centimeters or 

427 gram-meters are equivalent to one calorie of heat. 

778 foot-pounds are equivalent to one British thermal unit. 

Several types of apparatus for determining the above are to be found in laboratories. With each such apparatus a certain measurable amount of work is used up in friction, and the resulting heat is absorbed by water. The amount of work expended is determined from the magnitude of the

force and the distance through which it acts. The amount of heat formed is calculated from the mass of the water, the water equivalent of the container, and the rise in temperature.

To the teacher. With a good piece of apparatus it is possible to perform the experiment and to calculate the results in one class period. 

It is advisable to do this as a demonstration experiment at the time this work is assigned for study. 

HEAT ENGINES 

The steam engine. — Although mechanical toys operated by steam pressure were known to the Greeks and Romans, yet the invention of the steam engine is properly attributed to James Watt, an instrument maker at the University of

Glasgow, who lived from 1736 to 1819. This was one of the most important inventions in the history of man as it brought about the "industrial era," which has changed so completely our methods of producing the necessities and the luxuries of life. Today machinery performs work which could not otherwise be accomplished. 

The essential parts of a steam engine are a boiler, a cylinder and piston with the necessary valves, piston rod, connecting rod, fly wheel and crank shaft, a governor, and suitable mountings for these. The reader should make himself familiar with the details of a simple engine as explained in the diagram. Steam under pressure is generated in the boiler. For small engines this is usually about 100 lb./sq. in., in locomotives it is about 300 lb./sq. in., while experiments have been made with pressures four times as great as this last.

A valve operated by an eccentric first admits steam into one side of the cylinder and then into the other, and the expanding steam causes the piston to move back and forth.

As the steam comes in on one side the exhaust or used steam is allowed to escape from the other, as shown in the figure.

Gas engines. The gas engine is called an "internal combustion engine" because the fuel is burned directly behind the piston instead of in the fire box of a boiler as is the case with the steam engine. Most gas engines are of the four-cycle type. As the piston descends the explosive mixture of air and gasoline is drawn into the cylinder. As KO 

FIG. 105. PARTS OF A GASOLINE ENGINE 

The explosive mixture is being drawn into the cylinder on the left, and the intake valve is open.

On the right the cross section is cut nearer the observer and part of the exhaust passage is shown. Notice the cam shaft which operates the valves and the springs which hold them against the valve seats. The chambers containing water for cooling are shown, also a piston, connecting rod, crank shaft, and spark plug. it ascends this mixture is compressed and, at the top of the stroke, it is ignited by an electric spark which causes the explosion. The energy thus set free drives the piston down with great force. The inertia of the moving parts carries it back again and thus expels the burnt gases through valves which open into the exhaust. The piston now descends. and draws fresh gas through the carburetor, and another cycle begins. The arrangement and method of operating

The air drawn into the cylinder is compressed so highly upon the upstroke of the piston that the temperature rises to over 500° C. The fuel oil is then sprayed in by a little pump, one for each cylinder, and the heated air ignites the oil. No electrical apparatus is used and the oil is much safer than gasoline because it does not burn readily. bo

Cylinder. The pump for injecting fuel oil is seen at the right.

A DIESEL AIRPLANE ENGINE Courtesy of the Packard Motor Car Campany.

the various valves is somewhat different in the many makes of these engines.

The advantage of the gas engine is that it furnishes the most power for its weight, hence its use in automobiles and airplanes. Also it can be started at a moment's notice and no fuel is used when it is not running. 

The Diesel engine. These engines are designed to use kerosene, crude oil, and other heavy liquid fuels, which give more energy for the same cost than does gasoline. In these engines air is drawn into the cylinder and subjected to very high pressure which causes it to become hot enough to ignite the oil which is then injected into it. The heat thus formed furnishes the energy of the power stroke. These engines are used on submarines and to some extent on ships. They are of value where the cost of fuel is an important item and where a steam engine is not suitable. 

Advancing the spark. It requires a certain though very short interval of time for the fire to spread through the exploding mixture in the cylinder of a gas engine. When the engine is running fast it is necessary for the spark to pass before the gas is fully compressed and the piston has reached its highest point. If this were not the case the piston would reach the bottom of its stroke before all the gas was ignited. 

This is called "advancing the spark," and is necessary for the smooth running of the engine. 

The steam turbine. In large power plants and in great steamships the most economic machine for converting the energy of steam into useful work is the steam turbine. It contains a number of blades mounted on a shaft. As the molecules of steam strike these the energy is communicated to the shaft. The disadvantages of the turbine are that it can not be reversed and it does not operate efficiently at low speeds. At high speeds, however, it runs more smoothly than any other type of engine and is the most economical where 1,000 or more horse power is needed.

Efficiency of heat engines. — Some heat from heat engines is lost into the air from the fire box, the boiler and the pipes.

THE TRANSFER OF HEAT 

Conduction. Heat passes from one body to another by conduction, by convection, and by radiation. In conduction it is transferred from molecule to molecule as when a copper wire is heated by placing one end in a flame. Copper is a good conductor of heat, glass and wool are poor conductors. 'One can hold an end of a glass rod in the hand and heat the

other end to the melting point, but he can not do this with copper. Sawdust, cork, wool, feathers, and fur are poor conductors of heat, and hence used for heat insulation. 

Clothing and the lining of ice-boxes illustrate the use of such materials for this purpose. In general, liquids are not good conductors of heat, while gases are very poor conductors of heat. It is the air spaces between particles of sawdust, feathers, wool, and similar materials that cause them to be valuable as heat insulators.

The rate at which heat is carried through a material is roughly proportional to the difference in temperature on the two sides of the substance. A can of hot water cools twice as fast when it is 40° above the temperature of the room as when this difference is but 20°. The rate of heat conduction is also proportional to the thickness of the material. The following table gives the number of calories conducted through each square centimeter of area each second when the material is one centimeter in thickness and the difference in temperature on the two sides is one degree centigrade.

THERMAL CONDUCTIVITIES

HEAT 

Convection. 

If a part of the liquid or gas in a vessel is heated this part expands and becomes less dense than the other parts. The cooler parts being heavier fall to the bottom, forcing up the warmer and lighter parts of the liquid or gas. Thus convection currents are formed. These carry heat away from the place where it is applied. This action is called convection. The movement of these convection currents may be observed if a vessel of water in which some light powder has been placed is heated. 

Other examples of convection currents are the wind, ocean currents, the draught in a chimney, and the circulation of hot water in a heating system. Except where fans are used it is by means of convection currents of air that we ventilate buildings. sun. 

Hot Air

Hot Air 

To Chimney

FIG. 110. HOT-AIR HEATING 

Cold Air

Radiation. The heat from the sun comes to the earth as radiant energy. Sunlight warms those bodies on which it falls. The rate of this warming may be lessened or entirely stopped when some other object intercepts the rays of the Much of this radiant heat passes through a thin sheet of glass, but not through one of copper. The warming of a room by an open fire is by radiation. The convection current of warm air passes up the chimney, and conduction has no part in it. The radiant heat from the fire cannot pass through glass as that from the sun does. This latter passes through ice also as may be determined by making a convex lens or burning glass of this material. 

The radiant heat of the sun warms the air but little as it passes through it. In the main the air receives its heat

from the earth, which has been heated by rays which have already passed through the air in reaching the earth. The air high above the earth is always cold. Why?

Light colored and polished objects reflect radiant heat in the same way as light is reflected by them. But these surfaces do not radiate heat as readily as do others. As lampblack is one of the best absorbers of heat, it is also one of the best radiators of heat. Dark cloth is also more effective in these actions than cloth of lighter shade. A dark suit of clothes is more uncomfortable in the sun on a hot day than a lighter colored one, even though the materials be alike in all other respects. More snow melts under a black rag in the sun than under a white one, other conditions being the same.

The nature of radiant energy is discussed at length in the last chapter of this book.

The Dewar flask. These containers are commonly called "thermos bottles." Materials placed in them change their temperature very slowly. They have two silvered walls separated by a space that is a most a perfect vacuum. 

Conduction can take place only through the cork or through the neck of the bottle. Heat can not escape by convection as no air comes in contact with the inner wall. The silvered surfaces reduce the amount of heat transferred by radiation. 

These vessels were invented by Dewar for use in the laboratory and their practical application to every-day life came afterwards. He devised them to retain liquid air and other liquefied gases which absorbed heat from the air and rapidly boiled away.

THE PRODUCTION OF LOW TEMPERATURES 

The reader is aware that an ice plant can manufacture its product in the hottest weather, and that the electrical refrigerator requires no ice. The artificial production of cold can be more easily understood if we first study the following analogy.

HEAT Let E represent a section of the earth's surface and Ca strong cover, say of heavy metal, while A and B are strong walls forming a complete enclosure. Suppose there are a number of elastic balls moving about within this space as do the molecules of a gas. If they are perfectly elastic they will fly back and forth continuously, rebounding from the walls. with the same speed as that with which they strike. There will be a certain average kinetic energy of all the balls.

BALLS AND WALL 

As any ball rises nearer the top of the FIG. 111. enclosure its kinetic energy becomes less because work must be done in lifting it higher above the earth.

Suppose the ceiling of this enclosure is now raised to the position C₁. Many balls immediately fly upward and become distributed in this additional space. The balls that do this evidently have less kinetic energy than they had before, and therefore, the average kinetic energy of all the balls is less. This should help us to understand what takes place when a gas expands.

The molecules of a compressed gas possess a certain average amount of kinetic energy, depending on the temperature. When the gas is allowed to expand the average speed of the molecules decreases as they separate, the kinetic energy being used up in pulling them apart. (In the above analogy, it was taken up in raising the balls from the earth.) 

As temperature depends on the average kinetic energy of molecules, we see why an expanding gas becomes colder. 

This explanation shows why a gas becomes cooler when it expands into a vacuum. When it expands against the atmospheric pressure, another cooling effect comes into play. Work must be expended in pressing back the atmosphere. This is done at the expense of the kinetic energy of the gas molecules, and hence the gas becomes cooler. 

We see the same thing in the steam engine or steam turbine.

The steam molecules impart energy to the piston or to the blades, whichever the case may be. They then have less kinetic energy, and the steam is cooler.

Refrigeration systems. In many systems of refrigeration advantage is taken of the fact that certain gases such as ammonia and sulphur dioxide become liquids when compressed even at ordinary temperatures. An electric motor operates a pump which compresses the gas into a set of coils. 

The act of compression heats the gas. Then this hot gas is 

Expansion Valve 

Heat Released Compressor 

Heat Absorbed

FIG. 112. THE ESSENTIALS OF ARTIFICIAL REFRIGERATION 

Heat is removed from the gas after compression. Heat is absorbed by the gas after expansion. cooled as it passes through the "cooling coils" and usually it condenses to a liquid. It then passes to coils in the refrigeration compartment or chamber which is to be cooled. There it evaporates and expands. As it expands it becomes cold and absorbs heat from surrounding objects. The expanded gas then passes back to the pump where it is again compressed. The household refrigerator is controlled by a simple device.

When the temperature of the gas in the refrigerating chamber drops below a certain point the reduced pressure due to the lower temperature operates a valve which opens a switch and stops the motor used to compress the gas. When the cold chamber warms beyond a fixed point the increased

HEAT 

181 pressure of the gas due to the rise in temperature operates the valve again and the motor is started. 

The manufacture of ice is on the same principle, though here the compression pump is operated by a steam or gas engine.

Liquid oxygen boils at -182.9° and nitrogen at -195.7°, hence oxygen can be secured by allowing the nitrogen to boil out of liquid air. 

These gases freeze at -219° and -210.5° respectively.

Helium is the most difficult of all gases to liquefy. Its boiling point is -268.8° and its freezing temperature is almost at absolute zero.

SECTION 16. THE BEHAVIOR OF VAPORS 

- - Evaporation. A thermometer placed in an open vessel of water registers a temperature slightly lower than that of the room, but one placed in a closed vessel of water does not. 

Alcohol, chloroform, ether, and other volatile liquids, if poured on the hand, produce a distinct sensation of cold as they evaporate. The more readily the liquid evaporates the greater the cold produced. Water may be frozen by evaporating ether beneath the receiver of an air pump as the pressure is reduced.

The explanation of the cold produced by evaporation is as follows. Certain molecules on the surface of the liquid

HEAT 

183 acquire sufficient kinetic energy from their neighbors to overcome the force of cohesion and they break away. 

Kinetic energy is changed to the potential form as the molecules separate. As these faster moving molecules first take kinetic energy from the other nearby molecules and then change it to the potential form the result is that the average kinetic energy of all the molecules is less. In other words, the liquid becomes colder. Alcohol poured on the bulb of a thermometer and then fanned will cause the mercury to fall ten or fifteen degrees.

Some liquids have very little evaporation at ordinary temperatures. Sulphuric acid is one of these. It also absorbs water vapor and holds on to it tenaciously. If a cup of strong sulphuric acid and one of water be placed under a bell-jar and left for several days, it will be found that all the water has evaporated from the one cup and entered the acid in the other cup, and the latter may have run over.

Some evaporation of water takes place at all temperatures, even from ice.

Saturated vapors. 

A dish of alcohol or of chloroform placed under a bell-jar remains for weeks, but if left in the open air it evaporates in a few minutes.

The liquid under the cover sends out molecules until there are so many in the enclosed space that as many dive back into the liquid every second as come out of it. The vapor in the bell-jar is then said to be "saturated." The higher the temperature the greater the number of molecules required to fill a given space to the point of saturation. As the temperature increases more molecules escape from the liquid in a each second as come given period of time and a denser vapor is required over the liquid to send back into the liquid as many molecules as escape from it.

FIG. 115. - SATU- RATED VAPOR 

As many molecules return to the liquid from it.

If water and kerosene are placed beneath a bell-jar the

same amount of each liquid evaporates as though the other were not present. This is because there must be the same number of each kind of molecule per cubic centimeter to have them return to the liquids as fast as they come out. 

The small amount of space actually occupied by the molecules may be neglected.

The above is true for any liquids if they are not soluble the one in the other. It is not true for water and alcohol. 

Here a mixture is soon formed in each cup that has a different rate of evaporation from that of either of the pure liquids.

Pressure applied does not affect the density of a saturated vapor. It simply sends some of the vapor back to the liquid. 

If the pressure is reduced more liquid evaporates. A change of temperature does change the rate of evaporation, and hence the density of the vapor. 

When the temperature of a saturated vapor is reduced some of it condenses into the liquid form. This is the cause of fog and rain. In such cases condensation begins about the particles of dust in the air. If these are filtered out a much greater cooling is necessary to produce condensation. 

Vapor pressure. If a few drops of ether or other volatile liquid are inserted into a Torricelli tube the mercury is seen to drop, it may be a number of inches. Only a certain amount of liquid evaporates no matter how many drops are placed in the tube. This is the amount necessary to saturate the space above the mercury. The distance the mercury column falls depends on the kind of liquid and the temperature. The vapor exerts a pressure against the pressure of the air that raises the mercury. This pressure is called the "vapor pressure of a liquid. We see at once that the fall of the mercury in centimeters multiplied by 13.6 gives the vapor pressure in grams per square centimeter. 99

​VAPOR PRESSURE 

This shows what takes place when chloroform is inserted in a Torricelli tube, the temperature being 20° C. At this temperature the vapor pressure of chloroform is 

16 centimeters of mercury, or 16 × 13.6 = 217.6 g/cm². If the pressure in the space above the mercury is increased by lowering the tube, some vapor condenses, if it is decreased, more of the liquid evaporates. If we raise or lower the tube, the mercury remains at the same height. A rise in temperature causes a decided drop. 

Boiling. When a liquid is heated until the pressure of its saturated vapor is equal to that of the atmosphere, bubbles appear within the liquid. This is called "boiling." These bubbles always form on the sides of the container or on solid particles within the liquid. Perhaps they always begin with tiny air spaces which enlarge as vapor forms within them. 

If the pressure applied to a liquid is lessened it is evident that the boiling point is lowered. This is easily illustrated as follows. A flask half filled with water is heated until the

space above the liquid is filled with steam, and then tightly corked. As it is removed from the fire the boiling soon ceases, but it begins again if cold water is poured over the flask. The cold water condenses some steam which lowers the pressure in the flask. Boiling then begins again as the water is hot enough to produce boiling at this lower pressure. 

In industry liquids which are injured by great heat are evaporated under reduced pressure. In this way water is removed from syrup in the manufacture of sugar. 

The boiling point of water changes with altitude. It falls one degree centigrade for each drop of 2.68 cm of the barometer below standard pressure, 76 cm. At Leadville, Colorado, water boils at about 92° C. Pure water in clean vessels which has been boiled to expel bubbles, may be heated five or six degrees above the usual point before boiling begins.

In the process of boiling heat is taken up by evaporation as fast as it is received from the fire, and the temperature of the liquid can rise but little above the boiling point. If the liquid is in an enclosed vessel it may be heated to a temperature higher than its normal boiling point because of the increased pressure. This is desirable in many processes and is obtained by the use of " pressure cookers." The canning industry uses these extensively as temperatures high enough for rapid sterilization are then attained.

The heat of vaporization. This is the number of calories required to change one gram of a substance from the liquid state to a vapor without raising its temperature. It varies slightly with the atmospheric pressure, as does also the temperature at which the boiling takes place. For water it is about 540 calories. This means that it requires more than five times as much heat to convert a quart of boiling water to steam as it does to warm a quart of water from freezing temperature to boiling. The heat of vaporization of most substances is less, sometimes much less, than that of water. 

The heat of vaporization of water is determined as follows.

HEAT Steam is passed into a known mass of cold water.

The increase in mass gives the weight of the steam condensed. trap as shown in the figure, to It is necessary to use a prevent hot water that has condensed from the steam from flowing into the cold water.

This may be lessened by using a thermos bottle for a calorimeter, or by allowing the temperature to rise only a slight amount. Humidity. 

The amount of water present in the atmosphere has much to do with climate, and hence with the comfort of man and the health and growth of both plants and animals. Extremes of both dryness and dampness are injurious to man. 

Absolute humidity. By absolute humidity is meant the actual amount of water vapor in the air per unit of volume. 

It is often given in grams per cubic meter. It varies with the temperature and the amount of water available for evaporation. The humidity near the sea is, in general, greater than that far inland.

Relative humidity. By the relative humidity of the atmosphere is meant the percentage of moisture present compared to what would be there were the air saturated, the temperature remaining unchanged. We see from the above

HEAT 

189 table that when the temperature is 21° C. and the absolute humidity is 9.1 g/m³ the relative humidity is 50%. 

Much of the discomfort of hot weather is caused by high relative humidity which retards the evaporation of perspiration. Probably much of the sickness of winter is due to the low relative humidity of the heated air of houses. The cold air out-of-doors in winter is comparatively damp when it contains from four to ten grams of water per cubic meter. 

But when this same air is brought into houses and heated to a summer temperature it becomes very dry. This then absorbs moisture from the nose and throat, weakens the tissues, and the germs of respiratory diseases find there a congenial home. The humidity of rooms may be raised by placing pans of water on radiators, registers, or stoves. The percentage of relative humidity is best for man if between 

44% and 72%.

The dew point. That temperature at which moisture begins to condense from the air is called the dew point. The less the absolute humidity the lower the dew point. If small pieces of ice are added to water in a polished metal vessel, and the mixture stirred with a thermometer, it will be noticed that, at a certain temperature, dew begins to form on the exterior. With continued stirring, as the water warms, a temperature is found at which this dew begins to disappear. The average of these two temperatures may be taken as the dew point. The layer of air in contact with the metal container is just saturated when it is at this temperature. Moreover, this air has the same absolute humidity as it had before it was cooled. From the table above, using the dew point, we can find the number of grams of moisture per cubic meter actually in the air, and also, from the temperature of the room, the number of grams that would need to be present in order to saturate it. A simple division then gives that percentage which we call the relative humidity. 

The humidity of the air may be determined by other means. A known volume of air may be drawn through

chemicals which absorb moisture and the amount of the moisture removed from the air may be determined by weighing the chemicals.

The hair hygrometer consists of a hand which is moved by the tension of a stretched hair or other fiber that changes its length with the degree of dampness of the air and the relative humidity may be read directly from a scale. 

The wet and dry bulb hygrometer consists of two thermometers, the bulb of one covered by a wet cloth. The relative humidity determines the rate of evaporation from the cloth on the one instrument. This in turn determines the difference in reading between the two thermometers. 

Tables are given from which the relative humidity can be found from this difference. As this instrument is affected by air currents, it is sometimes constructed so that it may be whirled around before a reading is taken. Why?

Distillation. Fresh water may be obtained from salt water by boiling the latter and condensing the steam in suitable pipes, because the salt does not pass over with the vapor. In like manner many other solids may be separated from liquids and even liquids themselves may be separated and purified by partial distillation. The boiling points of alcohols are lower than that of water. But when these are distilled some of the water passes over also. The result is a mixture containing a higher percentage of alcohol. By distilling this mixture again and again alcohol of any degree of concentration may be secured. 

SECTION 17. SOME EFFECTS OF HEAT ON SOLIDS 

The heat of fusion. The heat of fusion of a substance is the number of calories required to change one gram of that substance from a solid to a liquid state without raising the temperature. For ice this is about 80 calories. (More exactly 79.8.) Thus we see it takes 80% as much heat to melt a pound of ice as it does to warm a pound of water from freezing temperature to boiling.

HEAT 

191 The heat of fusion of ice may be found as follows. A known mass of cracked ice is placed in warm water in a calorimeter and the temperature of the mixture is taken as the ice melts. It is well to weigh the warm water and then the mixture, the difference then being the mass of ice used. 

Also the pieces of ice should be wiped dry with a rag as they are placed in the water as the addition of cold water causes considerable error. If I is the weight of the ice and x the heat of fusion, then I calories of heat go into the ice to melt it. But the water that was ice is warmed to the temperature of the mixture. If tm is the temperature of the mixture and t, that of the ice, then I(tm — tc) calories enter the cold water 

xl 

FIG. 119. 

+ I (tm - tc) Calories that melted + Calories that went = the ice. into the cold water that was ice. (W + Q) (th - tm) 

Calories that came out of the warm water and container. that was ice. But this heat comes out of the water and the vessel containing it. If th is the temperature of the warm water and Q the water equivalent of the vessel and W the weight of the water, then, (W+Q) (th- tm) gives the number of calories that left the water and the vessel. But these are equal to the calories that melted the ice and warmed the cold water that was ice. Hence we can form an equation in which all the quantities can be directly measured except the heat of fusion, which can then be calculated. 

Crystalline and amorphous substances. Such substances as ice, sulphur, and the metals have definite melting points. As ice is warmed some of it is changed to water. 

If a mixture of ice and water is cooled some of the water freezes. Ice does not become softer and softer as it gets

warmer as do wax and tar. In fact it can not get warmer than the freezing temperature as it then passes directly into water. All crystalline substances behave as ice does and have definite melting points. Such materials as tar and wax are called "amorphous" meaning without form. They do not have definite melting points but get softer as the temperature rises.

Melting points and pressure. Those materials which expand on solidifying, such as ice and iron, have their melting points lowered slightly when pressure is applied. We may think of this as the yielding of the solid somewhat to the force because the substance has less volume when in the liquid form. The substance will solidify when the pressure is removed, because the temperature is lower than the normal melting point. There is a striking experiment wherein a wire is caused to cut through a piece of ice, the latter freezing together again after the wire has passed. It requires a pressure of about one ton per square inch to lower the melting point of ice one degree centigrade.

The melting points of substances that contract on solidifying are raised by pressure. Paraffine wax is a good example of such a substance.

Sublimation. The melting and boiling points of some substances are at the same temperature. They can not be obtained in the liquid state, but pass directly from solid to vapor. Iodine and arsenic are examples of such substances. (Pure iodine, at ordinary temperatures is a grayblack solid. When heated it becomes a purple vapor. The liquid that goes by that name is iodine dissolved in alcohol. 

Arsenic is a gray solid. The white powder, a poison that goes by that name, is an oxide of arsenic.)

Cooling by solution. Many salts when added to water produce a lowering of temperature. Some of the kinetic energy of the water molecules is used in separating the salt molecules and bringing them into solution. If one mixes several pounds of ammonium sulphate in a gallon of tap

HEAT 

193 water, using the hands, the cold is so pronounced that it is painful. 

For a similar reason a lowering of temperature follows when salt is mixed with ice. The salt causes the ice to melt, using up kinetic energy in the process. The resulting salt water has a lower freezing point than pure water. Salt is sometimes placed on pavements to melt ice, even though the temperature of the air is below freezing. The salt water formed is colder than ice, and may freeze water in pipes over which it flows. Hence this practice is unlawful in some places. The addition of concentrated sulphuric acid to water produces the opposite effect, and the mixture becomes warm. 

In one action energy is absorbed, in the other it is liberated. 

The degradation of energy. In nearly every energy change some is lost in the form of heat, as that produced by friction. This heat finally escapes from the earth into space. 

It appears that there is a tendency for the whole of creation to assume a uniform temperature. As no work can be obtained from heat unless some bodies are colder or warmer than others, as in the case of the steam engine, it seems as though the whole universe will finally become motionless, with the possible exception of the stars speeding along their paths. We can not affirm that this will happen as we do not know what finally becomes of the energy that escapes into space.

Nor do we know how matter and energy were formed in the first place. It may be that the energy that passes from the earth is made into matter somewhere else in the universe. This passing of heat into a form where it can not be used again, at least so far as we know, is called the degradation of energy.

CHAPTER VI  SECTION 18. FIRST PRINCIPLES OF ELECTRICAL SCIENCE 

Undoubtedly the lightning was a topic of conversation and a phenomenon of wonder in those early days when man was just beginning to think and to hold communion with his fellows. This powerful manifestation of energy was at once associated with the gods, and, throughout Pagan times, the lightning's flash was called the thunderbolt of Zeus.

Far back in the Glacial age, as our savage ancestor lay safely in his cave on cold, crisp, winter nights, he must have noticed the electric sparks generated as he drew his hand over a piece of soft fur. But this did not frighten him, nor did he see wherein these sparks could add to his well-being, hence he gave them little attention. Yet his descendants, beginning with a study of frictional electricity, have learned to harness the thunderbolt and to put it to doing useful work in the production of food and shelter and for communication and transportation.

In Classic times men were familiar with the fact that amber, when rubbed, draws to itself such light objects as bits of hair and straw. The discovery of this is attributed to the Greek philosopher Thales, who lived six hundred years before Christ. Also the Roman writer Lucretius mentions the fact that the lodestone possesses the power of attracting iron, but no further advance along this line was made for many centuries. 

In 1660 Sir William Gilbert, the physician of Queen

Elizabeth, published the book "De Magnete" wherein we have the first accounts of experimental investigations of 197

these subjects, and the first contribution to our knowledge of electricity or magnetism since the days of Lucretius. 

Gilbert showed that the earth is a great magnet and explained the action of the compass. He found that other substances besides amber could be electrified by friction, but progress was slow, and it was more than a century before it was discovered that some materials are conductors and some are non-conductors of electricity. 

The inventor of the air pump, Otto Von Guericke, made the first electric machine which consisted of a revolving sulphur ball. He also discovered that charges of like kinds of electricity repel each other. Cavendish, who did the pioneer work on gravitation, made the first careful electrical measurements and discovered that the attraction or repulsion acting between two electric charges is a force which varies as the product of the amounts of the two charges and inversely as the square of the distance between them. 

In 1799 Volta, an Italian, found that an electric current could be produced by the action of certain liquids upon plates of two metals. This was the beginning of electric batteries and since that time progress in the scientific study of electricity has been more rapid. In 1819 Oersted, a professor in the University of 

Copenhagen, found that the electric current affects the direction of a compass placed near the conducting wire.

Two year later Faraday made the discovery that electricity can be generated by the use of magnets. This was the beginning of the electric generator and of the use of electricity for industrial purposes. 

FIG. 120. - - OERSTED'S EXPERIMENT 

Before much advancement could be made it was necessary that these simple electrical devices be put to some practical Morse built the first telegraph in 1844, Bell invented use.

the telephone in 1876, and Edison made the first incandescent light in 1879. The first power house was constructed and the first electric street car was built in 1881. 

Since this time the application of electricity to industry has extended rapidly, and today billions of dollars are invested in electrical enterprises.

A very important advance along theoretical lines was made in 1898 when Sir J. J. Thompson, by studying the discharge of electricity through vacuum tubes, was able to determine the mass of the electron and the amount of negative electricity it represents. Since then much has been learned concerning the nature of electricity and the structure of atoms. We have not mentioned all the great names in electricity. Ampère, Coulomb, Ohm, and Maxwell are noted for their theoretical researches. Three Americans deserve to be mentioned in the list of those who contributed much to the early development of the knowledge of electricity, Benjamin Franklin, Joseph Henry, and George

Westinghouse. The last-named saw the possibilities of alternating currents and established factories for the manufacture of machinery to produce them. 

At the present day it is hardly correct to say that we do not know what electricity is. The smallest piece of ordinary matter is much more complicated than electricity as its atoms are built up of positive charges and electrons. It has been well said that we know more about electricity than we do about anything else because it is simpler than anything else. An electric charge is indeed a simple thing when compared to a grain of sand. While the ability of electric charges to attract or repel other charges is mysterious, yet the ability of matter to attract other matter, gravitation, is just as great a mystery. As a city is made up of houses which are built of bricks, so our physical world is made up of matter which is built up of electricity. At least this is the theory generally accepted by physicists today.

The fundamental law of static electricity. There are two kinds of electric charges called positive and negative, like kinds repelling each other and unlike kinds attracting. This fact is established by such experiments as are described below. In these investigations the most striking phenomena are observed when the air is cold and dry. For instance, in high altitudes, say 9,000 feet or more, one may, by merely drawing a woolen blanket off a bed, create a shower of sparks, but this is never observed at lower elevations. If the air is excessively damp, electrostatic experiments may be impossible. 

Striking experiments can be performed by electrifying two of the small rubber balloons so much used as toys for children. Let these be inflated and suspended from a point near the center of the room by cords a meter or more in length. They may be charged by rubbing with fur, a clothes-brush, or a piece of silk. They now repel each other and stand quite a distance apart. If the hand or any uncharged body is brought near them they are attracted to it. When other objects are rubbed with the fur or cloth and then brought near the balloons some attract them while others repel them. If rods of ebonite, (hard rubber), and glass are rubbed with silk, the first repels the balloons and the second attracts them. That kind of electrical charge formed on ebonite when rubbed with silk is called negative, that on the glass positive. From a large number of similar experiments we arrive at the law given above. 

Whether fur, a brush, or silk is used to rub the toy balloons the charge on the balloons will be negative. It is well to bear this in mind.

The silk with which the rods are rubbed also becomes charged. It is a positive charge when the silk has been rubbed on ebonite and a negative one when it has been rubbed on glass. The sign of a charge formed by friction depends on the substances used. Thus, glass is positively electrified when rubbed with silk and negatively electrified when rubbed with cat's fur.

The electrostatic series. Every solid has its place in the following table, though only a few are mentioned here. 

When any two of these substances are rubbed together that one which appears first on the list has a positive charge and the other a negative charge.

METAL ROD AND ELECTROSCOPE 

The metal rod is negatively electrified when rubbed with cat's fur. It is insulated by the glass bottle, as otherwise the charge would pass off through the hand as fast as formed. 

If a metal rod is rubbed with fur no electrical charge can be detected unless the rod is held by a handle of glass, hard rubber, or some other similar substance. Any charge acquired by the metal rod when held in the hand passes into the hand as fast as it is formed, but it can not pass through the handle. Substances are classed as conductors and nonconductors, or insulators, depending on whether they conduct electrical charges readily or not. This variation in the conducting properties of different materials was first noticed by Stephen Gray in 1729.

THE BEST CONDUCTORS ARE METALS 

The electron theory. It is now known that all atoms consist of at least two things, a charge of positive electricity at the center called the nucleus, with particles of negative electricity, electrons, arranged about it. Under normal conditions the amounts of positive and negative electricity are equal to each other and no electrical effects are observed. 

Most of the mass of the atom is due to its positive nucleus. 

Thus the mass of the hydrogen atom is some 1,846 times as great as that of its single electron. All electrons have the same mass and carry the same amount of negative electricity. Review the paragraphs on the structure of matter in Chapter I. 

When two substances are rubbed together the electrons near the surfaces of their atoms, are disturbed. For some reason which we do not at present understand there is a tendency for an excess of electrons to collect on one of the substances thus leaving a lack of them in the atoms of the other material. The first substance is then negatively and the second positively electrified. Notice that it is the negative electricity only which moves from one substance to the other. One can not move a positive charge of electricity unless he carries it with the atoms themselves. 

In the case of insulators the electrons are held to their atoms with considerable force, but with conductors there are certain electrons which are loosely bound to their respective atoms. These electrons easily pass from one atom to another. Suppose some electrons are placed on the end of a neutral copper rod. Being negative electricity they repel each other as well as the electrons of the copper atoms already present. If the other end of the rod is now touched

by the hand there is a readjustment of electrons all along the rod, and the excess of electrons pass off into the hand. 

This movement of electrons from atom to atom along the rod is what constitutes an electric current. Notice that the positive charges do not pass from atom to atom, the negative charges only being the ones which move along the rod. If this rod had been of glass or other insulating substance instead of copper there would not have been any readjustment of electrons as the latter are held too tightly to their atoms. In other words there would be no electric current. 

Any positively charged body has had electrons removed from its atoms. A positively charged conductor, when placed in contact with the earth, draws to itself sufficient electrons to neutralize the charge. Any insulated charged body gradually loses its charge. If the charge is negative, electrons escape from it, mostly by attaching themselves to air molecules; if the charge is positive, electrons are attracted to the body from the air or other materials which come near it. 

Electrostatic lines of force. Attraction and repulsion between charged bodies takes place in a vacuum. Hence we must think of something as extending between the bodies. We suppose that "lines of force" are present and that these are due to some peculiar condition of the ether.

It is assumed that the FIG. direction of these lines

FIG. 123. LINES OF FORCE BETWEEN UN- LIKE CHARGES Ат- TRACTION 

of force is always away

OF 

122. LINES FORCE BETWEEN LIKE CHARGES REPULSION 

from the positive charge and towards the negative charge. Each line of force is thought of as passing through space along that path which would be taken by a small positively charged body were it allowed to be freely repelled or attracted by the charges.

It is also assumed that the number of lines of force passing through any small section of space is directly proportional to the strength of the electric force within that space. 

When these assumptions are made the lines of force between charged bodies take the forms shown in the figures. They act as stretched elastic bands which repel each other yet draw together those objects which they connect. 

The electroscope. This simple but useful instrument consists of an insulated metal rod to the bottom of which are attached two strips of thin metal foil. The top of the rod usually ends in a knob, and it is best to have the leaves within a metal case and to observe them through a window of glass or mica.

When the leaves are charged the repulsion of like kinds of electricity causes them to stand apart. The amount of electricity present is indicated by the extent to which the leaves diverge. 

Charging by induction. If a body charged positively, (e.g., glass rubbed with silk), is brought near the knob of an electroscope but not touching it, the leaves are seen to XXX 

FIG. 124. 

那​听了 ​- 

CHARGING AN ELECTROSCOPE BY INDUCTION 

diverge. The lines of force from the positive charge attract electrons to the knob thus leaving a lack of them in the leaves. These are then charged positively and the knob is charged negatively. When the rod is removed the electrons return to the leaves and the charge disappears. 

But, if while the charge is near the knob, we touch the latter with the finger, the leaves fall together. This is

because electrons are drawn to the knob from our body and there is no shortage of them on the leaves. If the finger is taken away before the rod is removed some extra electrons are now imprisoned on the knob. If the positive charge is now removed the lines of force no longer hold these electrons on the knob. They wander through the metal to the leaves. The whole of the electroscope from knob to leaves is now charged negatively as shown by the diverging leaves. 

A positive charge brought near the knob causes the leaves to fall as the excess of electrons is drawn upward. 

A negative charge brought near causes the leaves to rise even higher as more electrons are repelled to the leaves. 

Notice that the charged glass has not at any time touched the metal. If this had occurred electrons would have been drawn from the knob and the electroscope would have taken on a positive charge.

If a negatively charged body, (e.g., glass rod rubbed with cat's fur), be brought near the knob of an electroscope but not touching it, the leaves diverge. The negative charge on the rod repels electrons from the knob down to the leaves and the latter are charged negatively and the knob positively.

When the rod is removed this charge disappears. But, if while the rod is near, the knob be touched with the finger, the leaves fall as the electrons are driven out into the body. 

There is now a lack of electrons on the knob. If the finger is removed first, and then the rod, some electrons from the leaves pass up to the knob and the elestroscope is charged positively and the leaves diverge. Notice that an induced charge is always of the opposite sign from that of the charge producing it. If a body is charged by contact the charges have the same sign. 

Another example of charging by electrostatic induction is as follows. Let two eggshells covered with tinfoil and hanging by silk threads be in contact. Let a charged body be brought near one of the shells. This shell acquires a charge of opposite sign from that of the original charge and the

other shell acquires a charge of like sign. If the shells are now separated while the original charge is near they will be found to have charges of opposite sign even after the original charge is removed.

FIG. 125. 

In this case some electrons are moved 

EGGSHELLS COVERED BY INDUCTION 

from one shell to the other, being attracted or repelled by the original charge.

We now see why an electric charge attracts bits of hair, straw, pith, and other light objects.

The charge induces electrification of opposite sign on the side of these objects nearest to the charge, and one of like sign on

WITH TINFOIL WHICH IS CHARGED the farther side. The charge of opposite sign being closer than the one of like sign the attraction is greater than the repulsion. This explains the action of the "electric chimes" and numerous other experiments with static electricity. 

Sometimes like charges appear to attract each other. 

This is when one of the charges is very weak in comparison to the other and the stronger charge induces one of the opposite sign on the nearer side of the object having the weaker charge. So the sure test for the sign of an electric charge is not that it is attracted by what appears to be a charge of opposite sign, but that it is repelled by what must then be a charge of like sign.

When a light body is attracted to an electrified object and then touches it, the body acquires a charge of the same kind by contact. It is then repelled. This explains why a charged plate causes light bodies to jump up and down between the plate and the table-top.

The electrophorus. - This consists of a plate of hard rubber, sealing wax, or other insulating material, and a metal plate with an insulating handle. When the plate of insulating material is rubbed with cat's fur or silk it acquires a

negative charge. The metallic plate is now placed on it and touched with the finger. The charges on those points of the insulating material which touch the metal plate are carried off through the body. No matter how smooth this surface appears to be, there are, in reality, many small hills and hollows on it. The charges have been carried from the high spots. 

Those on the low places now induce a charge of opposite sign on the metal plate. If the finger is now removed FIG. 126. - THE ELECTRO

these induced charges remain.

The 

PHORUS 

plate may now be picked up with the insulating handle and will be found to carry a positive charge. This may be removed by touching the metal. The plate may be charged again and again any number of times by replacing on 

FIG. 127. — THE PLATES the plate of insulating material and touching with the finger. The electrical charge in the hollows is not used up in this way, but in time it leaks away into the air.

OF AN ELECTROPHORUS 

As the electric spark which may be obtained from the metal plate of an electrophorus represents an expenditure of energy, and as this charge may be renewed indefinitely by merely placing the plates together and touching one with the finger, one might think that the law of the conservation of energy fails here. But this law holds in this case also. 

Due to the attraction of unlike charges, it requires more work to separate the plates after the one has been touched with the finger than would have been required before. This is the work which becomes the energy of the charged plate. 

Static machines. The first machines for generating static electricity were balls, cylinders, or disks of an insulating substance which were electrified by friction against silk, fur, or like materials. The electricity was collected by means

of sharp metal points. Such of these machines as are in use today operate on the same principle as the electrophorus. 3 

FIG. 128. A WIMSHURST MACHINE 

Pieces of metal foil are attached to rotating disks of glass or ebonite. They are so arranged and connected by means of metal brushes and rods that a small charge on any part induces other and greater charges on the other parts as they rotate, and large sparks may be obtained. The most common forms are the Wimshurst and the Toepler-Holtz machines. For a de- tailed account of these the reader is referred to works on electricity.

Two important facts. There are two facts concerning static electricity that should be kept in mind. The first is that the charge on a closed conductor resides on its outer surface; and the second is that the more pointed any part of the charged conductor, the greater the density of the electricity at that point.

The first of these facts can be demonstrated by placing an electroscope inside a charged metallic can. The instrument will not show a charge when connected to the can. 

Faraday demonstrated this by taking a delicate electroscope with him into a large metal cage which was then charged by a static machine until great sparks came from it. No charge was shown on the electroscope. This is because like charges

repel each other and produce a neutral area within the charged cage.

The second fact is illustrated by holding first a rounded and then a pointed metal rod near a charged object. The pointed rod draws off the charge more quickly because denser charges are induced on more pointed objects. This is why lightning rods end in sharp points. 

The reason why electric charges collect more densely on sharp points is seen from the following. Draw a pointed egg-shaped closed curve

FIG. 129. CHARGE AND CURVATURE 

and indicate electric charges at equal distances apart all around. it. Draw lines of force extending from these charges and perpendicular to the curve. It is seen at once that the lines of force out some distance from the pointed ends are further apart than they are at the less pointed places. But the repulsion acting between these lines of force will arrange them into a more even grouping. 

For this to be the case the charges must be more densely packed on the more pointed parts of the conductor. This action of the lines of force also shows why small charges are not forced off the conductor, but repel with great force any like charge which is brought near the surface. 

The electric whirl consists of pieces of wire balanced on an insulated pivot.

The pointed ends of the wires extend in such a direction that electricity escaping from these points causes the whirl to rotate. Molecules of air are charged from the great accumulation of electricity at the points. These are repelled violently, and the reaction rotates the whirl.

FIG. 130.-ELECTRIC WHIRL

The law of electric force. The force of attraction or repulsion between two electric charges is directly proportional to the product of the amounts of the two charges, and is inversely proportional to the square of the distance between them. Mathematically, this is expressed by, Ke1e2 F = da Here e₁ and e2 are the amounts of the two charges, d the distance between them, F the resulting force, and K a constant which depends on the units used. 

This law was first published by Coulomb, but it was afterwards found among the papers of Cavendish, who had discovered it long before. For experimenting with electrical forces Cavendish used an apparatus quite like the one he used to study gravitational attraction. Notice how similar are the two laws.

The unit of electrical charge. The electrostatic unit of charge is that amount of electricity which, when placed one centimeter from an exactly equal and like charge, repels it with the force of one dyne. If, in the formula above, the e's are in electrostatic units, d is in centimeters, and F is in dynes, the value of K is unity and we write, e1e2 d2 (This is seen at once by making the other quantities unity and solving for K.)

Just as in the case of gravitation where the effect of the mass of a sphere is the same as though it were all concentrated at the center, so, in the case of an electric charge spread out uniformly over a sphere, the effect is the same as though all the charge were concentrated at the center of the sphere. This rule may be proved mathematically and it enables us to work out the following problem. 

In a certain experiment two spherical rubber balloons

after inflation were 20 cm in diameter and each weighed 3 grams. They were hung from a point near the center of the room by threads so that the distances from the point of support to the centers of the balloons were each 100 cm. 

The balloons were then electrified by rubbing carefully with cat's fur, an effort being made to have the electricity distributed evenly over their surfaces. By standing behind them with a meter-stick the distance between their centers 1.09 g was estimated to be 68.2 cm, which, by the theorem of Pythagoras, gives the vertical distance from the point of support to the line of centers as 94 centimeters. 100 cm.

The force holding the balloons apart is found from a parallelogram of forces. The weight of one balloon and the electric force are proportional to two sides of the parallelogram, and we have,

Then e/68.2 V1,070, and e = 2,230 electrostatic units of quantity. Due to the uncertainties of measurement and the inability to secure exactly equal amounts of electricity on each balloon it is not advisable to carry the calculations to more than three significant figures. The coulomb. 

- The amount of electricity used in one

second by an ordinary incandescent lamp is very large indeed when compared with that on the balloons, hence, for commercial purposes, a much larger unit is required. This is called the coulomb and is defined as 3,000,000,000 (three billion) electrostatic units. Now, an ordinary 100-watt light uses about a coulomb of electricity each second, so, dividing three billion by 2,230, we find that it would take the electricity on one and one-third million balloons such as we used in the experiment to supply this light for only one second. This shows what a comparatively small amount of electricity is used in electrostatic experiments. 

The electron. The amount of electricity carried by one electron can be determined with fair accuracy. It is about as much smaller than the electrostatic unit as the coulomb is larger. It is known that about 2,095,000 electrons are equivalent to one electrostatic unit of negative electricity. 

A lack of this number of electrons on any body gives it a charge of one electrostatic unit of positive electricity. 

The ampere. Electric current is measured in amperes. 

An ampere is such a flow of electricity that one coulomb passes each second. Notice that amperes have somewhat the same relation to coulombs as horse power has to foot-pounds. 

Amperes measure the rate at which electricity is used. An ordinary forty-watt light uses about one-third of an ampere, one-third of a coulomb each second. This is one billion electrostatic units, or the amount of electricity represented by 2.095 billion billion electrons.

Electricity and energy. — An electric charge can lift light objects against the force of gravity, rotate the electric whirl, and in other ways demonstrate an ability to do work. We shall now see how this energy is measured. Let A, B, and 

C in the figure be metal spheres of different diameters but each charged with the same amount of electricity. Let D be a smaller sphere charged with one electrostatic unit of the same kind of electricity as the other spheres. It is evident that the lines of force are crowded together more

thickly over C than B, and more thickly over B than over A. 

Hence it will require more work to bring D from infinity, (that is, from very far away), up to C than up to B, and more to bring it up to B than up to A. C is said to have a higher potential than B, and B a higher potential than A. This potential is due to the repulsion of the lines of electrostatic force. It is due to the fact that the electrons are crowded on the body or missing from it. Potential is commonly measured in one of two units, the electrostatic unit of potential or the volt. An electrostatic unit of potential is such a potential that one erg of work must be performed when one electrostatic unit of quantity of like electricity is brought from an infinite distance to the point in question. A volt is such a potential that one joule of work, ten million ergs, must be performed when one coulomb of electricity is brought from an infinite distance to the point in question. From these definitions it follows that one electrostatic unit of potential is equivalent to three hundred volts. 

While potential is due to the crowding of lines of force, i.e., to the "pressure" of the electricity, yet potential is not pressure. It is impossible to reduce 110 volts to pounds per square inch.

FIG. 133. 

POTENTIAL 

Just as a certain definite amount of work must be performed in bringing the charge D up to A, so A will perform this same amount of work as it repels D away from itself. 

Suppose we attach a wire to A and allow the electricity on A to do work as it is repelled by the lines of force and flows away. It is evident that the amount of work the charge at A can do depends on how much electricity is present and also its potential. But when a little charge has left A there will be fewer lines of force and the potential will be less. It is known that the potential of any conductor is directly

Courtesy of General Electric Company. 

FIG. 134. A MILLION VOLTS

proportional to the amount of electricity on it. Hence the average potential of A, as its electricity flows away, is just half what it was when the current began to flow. 

The charge on A in coulombs multiplied by onehalf its potential in volts gives the joules of energy the charge represents. If electrostatic units are used the work is given in ergs.

The difference of potential between two points measures the amount of work each unit quantity of electricity can do as it flows from one of the points to the other.

Thus the difference of po- tential between the terminals of a battery may be six volts, or the difference of potential between two points on a wire may be one-thousandth of a volt. 

The watt. As an ampere is a current wherein one coulomb passes each second and as each coulomb can perform one joule of work for each volt through which it drops, the amperes of current multiplied by the volts of potential gives the number of joules per second of work performed. But work at the rate of one joule each second is called a watt. 

Hence volts multiplied by amperes give the watts of power used. 

This is commonly written, FIG. 135. - LOW VOLTAGE AND STRONG CURRENT 

This "tame electricity" makes an arc but can not harm the girl. This is because the potential is but six volts and only a few thousandths of an ampere pass through her body, which has a re- 

sistance of over a thousand ohms. If the voltage were high enough to send the current of the arc through the body of the girl, she would be killed instantly. W = iV. 

Here W represents watts, i amperes, and V the volts.

Notice the analogy between water power and electric power. The amount of work a stream of water can perform depends on the quantity of water flowing and the vertical distance through which it falls. This determines the footpounds per second or horse power. Two thousand cubic feet of water falling ten feet are equivalent to ten cubic feet falling two thousand feet. Much power may be obtained from a great mass of water falling a short distance, as at the Keokuk plant on the Mississippi, or from a small stream of water dropping through a great height as is the case at the plant in the Rocky Mountains where the head of the water is eighteen-hundred feet. Likewise ten amperes at two thousand volts furnish the same power as two thousand amperes at ten volts.

Capacity. Let us consider the three metallic spheres again. We thought of them as each containing the same quantity of electricity and then their potentials are different as they are of different sizes. We may now think of them as all being at the same potential. It is evident that the larger the sphere the more electricity required to raise it to any required potential. The capacity of any electrical device measures the amount of electricity it holds when at unit potential. For any given conductor the potential is directly proportional to the amount of electricity present. Hence the quantity of electricity divided by the potential gives the quantity the body holds at unit potential, or its capacity. 

Hence we write,

or e = CV.

Here C is the capacity, e the quantity, and V the potential. 

The capacity of a conductor depends not only on its size, but also on its shape and on the nature of the insulating material surrounding it.

Units of capacity. The commercial unit of capacity is the farad. A farad is a capacity which will hold one coulomb

of electricity at one volt of potential. This is a very large capacity. For a metal sphere to have the capacity of one farad it would be necessary to be over five million miles in radius. Hence the microfarad is used, which is onemillionth of a farad. Many condensers have a capacity of only a few thousandths of a microfarad. Capacity quantity potential

Farads coulombs volts

Microfarads microcoulombs volts

The electrostatic unit of capacity has not been given a name. It is such a capacity that one electrostatic unit of quantity raises the potential to one electrostatic unit. It is proved in works on electricity that the capacity of a sphere in electrostatic units is equal to its radius in centimeters. The electrostatic unit of capacity is the capacity of a metal sphere of one cm radius. The capacity of the balloons in our experiment is ten electrostatic units because they have a diameter of 20 cm. Knowing the capacity and the quantity of electricity we can find the potential from the equation C = e/V. 

This gives us, 10V 2,230, or V = 223 electrostatic units of potential. But each electrostatic unit of potential is equivalent to 300 volts. Hence the voltage on the rubber balloons is 66,900 volts. The reader is aware that this is a very dangerous voltage and is inclined to doubt the results of the calculations, as he knows the balloons are harmless.

The reason they can not give a deadly shock is because the quantity of electricity is so small. The work, and hence the harm, that an electric charge can do depends on the product of the quantity of electricity and its potential. Condensers. Though metal spheres have relatively small capacities, yet metal plates may be so arranged as to give comparatively large capacities. A capacity of onethousandth of a microfarad requires a metal sphere nearly thirty feet in radius, or sixty feet in diameter. The same

capacity can be obtained by separating two metal plates, each ten centimeters square, by a sheet of paper one-fifth of a millimeter in thickness. Such an arrangement is called a condenser. To charge a condenser it is necessary that one of the plates be connected to the earth, or else that one plate be charged with positive and the other with negative electricity.

FIG. 136. CONDENSER PLATES 

Suppose one of the plates is connected to earth. A charge of electricity placed on the other plate now induces a charge of opposite sign on this plate. We now have charges of opposite kinds placed very close together. The 

Ground lines of force from the positive plate extend over to the negative charges on the other plate. They are held in place between the plates by the opposite charges and are not free to exercise their force in repelling any small like charges that might be brought near one of the plates. Hence, considering the amount of electricity present, comparatively little work is required to bring a unit charge of like kind up to the plate. In other words, the potential is small; the capacity is large. Hence, the name, condenser, as the electricity acts as though it were packed on the plates. The closer the plates are together the more firmly are the lines of force held between the plates, and the greater the capacity of the device. Experiment shows that the capacity of a condenser varies inversely as the distance between the plates. Condensers often contain a large number of plates, alternate ones being connected by wires. The capacity is then determined by the total area between the oppositely charged plates. 

The dielectric constant. This is also called "Specific 

Inductive Capacity." Let us suppose that a condenser has a certain capacity when there is a vacuum between the plates. With air between the plates the capacity is increased

by about of one per cent. With paper between the plates the capacity is increased from two to two and one-half times, with glass from five to ten times, and so on for other dielectrics (insulators). This ratio is called the dielectric constant of the material. In scientific works it is often represented by the Greek letter mu, written μ. 

The capacity of a condenser in microfarads may be found from the formula:

8.84 με 108 d Here A is the area between plates in square centimeters, and d is the distance between the plates in centimeters. 

Glass is often

Mica is a favorite dielectric for condensers. used. Sometimes the plates are immersed in oil or placed in tanks of compressed air. Not only does this increase the capacity but it helps prevent sparks from passing between the oppositely charged plates. Aside from reference to their capacity, condensers are rated according to the potential they can safely withstand without sparking. Thus, there are 500-volt condensers and 10,000-volt condensers. The 

Leyden jar is a simple condenser, consisting of metal foil pasted on the inside and the outside of a glass jar. A large condenser attached to a static machine stores a comparatively large amount of electricity at a high potential. A shock from this may prove very serious. 

To build a condenser of the capacity of one farad would cost, for materials and labor, about $1,000,000 and, as industry has no need of such a piece of apparatus, this has never been done. Microfarad condensers are, however, not uncommon.

RESISTANCE 

Resistance. When a conductor is connected between two points which are at a difference of potential, electrons pass along it and thus form an electric current. We may think of them as being pushed along or as drawn up by the lines of force. It is evident that the amount of current will depend on the difference of potential, the ease with which electrons can be caused to pass from atom to atom in the conductor, its length, and its area of cross section. The last three of these determine what is known as the resistance

of the conductor.

We think of the resistance of a wire as tending to retard the passage of electrons. Resistance increases with the length of the conductor as the electrons must move over a longer path, and it decreases with the 

FIG. 137. 

BATTERY AND FINE WIRE 

As the larger wire is moved along the smaller one, making the length of the latter which carries current shorter and shorter, the resistance of the circuit becomes less and less and the current gets larger and larger, i V/R.

A point is soon reached where the smaller wire becomes red hot, If the resistance is further reduced the smaller wire "burns out." The larger wire does not get hot. 

The heat generated is proportional to the product of the resistance and the square of the current. area of the cross section of the path since as the path becomes smaller there is less room for the electrons to pass. 

Hence we may write, KL K is a constant depending on the material of the conductor and the units. R is the resistance and it is generally measured in ohms. L is the length of the conductor and A the area of its cross section.

The ohm is defined as such a resistance as will allow one ampere to flow when the difference of potential is one volt. 

The K in the formula above is called the specific resistance

of the substance. It is the resistance in ohms of a block of the material one unit in length and one unit in area of cross section.

Engineer's tables often give the resistance in mil-feet. In this case K is the resistance of a fine wire of the material one foot long and one thousandth of an inch in diameter. The resistance of any wire may be found by multiplying this con- 

FIG. 138. 

RESISTANCES 

The resistance used is The wires wrapped on the spools are resistance standards. the sum of the spools where the plugs are pulled out. stant by the length in feet and then dividing by the square of the diameter measured in thousandths of inches. That is, a thousandth of an inch is considered as one unit. This squaring of the diameter results from the law of similar figures, a wire of ten times the diameter of another has one hundred times the area of cross section. A thousandth of an inch is called a mil. 

If the diameter of a wire is .024 inch it is called 24 mils. 

Ohm's law. The amount of current passing through a conductor is directly proportional to the difference of potential applied and inversely proportional to the resistance. This is known as Ohm's law from the German scientist, George

Simon Ohm, who discovered it. Hence i V/R. This

most important formula is often written, Ri = V. In this form it is easy to remember as it suggests the states Rhode 

Island and Vermont.
