Resistances in parallel. If the electric current, as it flows along its path, comes to a point where it branches and part goes through one resistance and part through another, the current uniting again afterwards, these resistances are said to be in parallel or multiple. We wish to determine the value of the single resistance R which is equivalent to several known resistances in parallel. 

Let A and B be the points where the current branches and comes together again respectively. It is evident that the voltage drop between A and B is the same, no matter which of several resistances are considered. If this were not so it would require less work to force electricity back over one path than over the other.

FIG. 141. GALVANI'S EXPERIMENT 

In 1786 Luigi Galvani, an anatomist in the University of Bologna, announced his famous experiment. He hung the legs of a frog which had recently been killed on an iron railing by means of copper hooks. He noticed that whenever both the copper and the feet touched the iron at the same time the legs jerked as they did when an electric spark was applied to them.

This experiment led Volta to invent the first electric battery. It was the first observation of the effect of current electricity and the father of the electrical science and industry of today.

Notice that the copper and the iron form plates of two metals and the moistened frog flesh forms the electrolyte. Thus there are present all the elements of an electric cell. 

SECTION 19. ELECTRIC BATTERIES 

To the teacher. We advise that the following experiments be shown the class before this section is assigned for study. (1) Place bits of metallic sodium on a solution of copper sulphate in water. The H, ignites. Call attention to the metal, the reaction, and the tendency for Na to go into the liquid. 

Explain in the terms of ions.

(2) Place sodium on water containing phenolphthalein. Explain the purpose of the indicator, the action of the OH ion, and the formation of NaOH. (3) Place hydrochloric acid in this solution and explain the formation of NaCl. (4) Put ammonia hydroxide in this solution and explain the return of the red color. (5) Hold open mouths of bottles of NH4OH and HCl near and explain the formation of the smoke, NH.CI. (6) Pass crystals of NH4Cl about the class for examination, also NaOH and KOH. (7) Define acid, base, and salt. (An acid gives the H ion and a base the OH ion. Combining they give a salt and H₂O.) 

Chemical symbols. Steady currents of electricity were first obtained from batteries. These we shall now study, but it will help matters greatly if we first learn a few chemical symbols that must be used to explain their action.

In a chemical equation Cu stands for one copper atom, S for one sulphur atom; while CuSO4 means one molecule of copper sulphate. Each molecule of this salt contains one copper, one sulphur, and four oxygen atoms.

A chemical equation shows how atoms unite to form compounds, thus,

Zn + H2SO4 = ZnSO4 + H2. 

From this we see that sulphuric acid attacks zinc forming zinc sulphate and hydrogen. Two hydrogen atoms are required to make one hydrogen molecule, hence the H2. 

Ions and dissociation. An ion is an atom or a group of atoms bearing an electric charge. The molecules of many substances, when they are placed in water, break up into ions. When common salt is dissolved in water some of the 

NaCl molecules are broken apart and each forms a positive ion of sodium, Na+, and a negative ion of chlorine, Cl¯. 

This breaking apart of molecules is called dissociation. 

Sulphuric acid dissociates into two positive ions of hydrogen and one negative SO4 ion. The SO4 particle is called a radical, and can not exist except as an ion. It is not a molecule as there is no substance made up of such particles. 

An acid is any substance which forms the positive hydrogen ion when dissolved in water. All positive ions are atoms or groups of atoms that have one or more electrons missing. 

A base or alkali is any substance that forms the negative OH¯ion when placed in water. Ammonia, potash lye, and soda lye are good examples of bases. When an acid is added to a base the ions interact and produce a salt and water. 

The dissociated part of the salt gives neither the H+ nor the OH ion, and hence is said to be neutral. 

The rule for ions. Hydrogen and all metal ions are positive. The reader will find this rule very useful in our further discussion. If a piece of metal is placed in water some of it passes into the liquid in the form of positive ions. 

Ordinarily this action soon ceases as the liquid quickly takes up as many ions as it can hold, and moreover, the negative charge left on the metal prevents further ions from leaving. 

But if some chemical is present to use up the ions as they are formed, and if the charge on the metal is carried off

through a wire, the action continues and the electrons escaping through the wire form an electric current. This is the principle of electric batteries. 

That metal atoms dissolve in water may be illustrated by placing a piece of bright clean iron in a full flask of boiled, distilled water, corking tightly, and leaving it for some hours. 

If some of the clear water is now poured off and shaken with air, it turns a brownish color due to the dissolved iron changing to iron rust.

The electro-chemical series. The force that causes certain metal ions to pass into a liquid is sometimes so much stronger than that which causes the ions of some other metal to dissolve that the first metal can displace the second in the solution. If a piece of iron is dipped into a solution of copper sulphate it soon shows the characteristic color of copper. Copper atoms leave the solution to make room, as it were, for the iron ions which usurp their place. If zinc is placed in this solution the action is much quicker. In an instant the zinc is covered with a black substance which can be shown to be finely divided copper. Here is a list of the more common metals forming an electro-chemical series. Each metal has the ability to replace those metals which come below it in the series. The higher the metal in the list the greater the negative potential acquired when placed in a solution. 

Ordinary tinware is sheet-iron covered with tin, and galvanized iron is the same metal covered with zinc. If tin is scratched

ELECTRO-CHEMI- CAL SERIES 

Magnesium Zinc Aluminum Iron Nickel Lead Copper Tin through to the iron the latter rusts very Antimony rapidly, but when the coating on galvanized iron is broken the iron rusts very slowly. In each case the two metals and water act like an electric battery as described below, electric currents being formed. 

In one case it is the zinc which dissolves, and in the other it is the iron.

The simple cell. This may be made by placing a piece of zinc and a piece of copper in a dilute solution of sulphuric acid and connecting them by a wire. The zinc should be first "amalgamated," that is, covered with mercury. A galvanometer or an ammeter shows a current to be passing through the wire and bubbles of hydrogen collect on the copper plate. 

If the circuit is kept closed the zinc will be entirely eaten away by the acid and zinc sulphate formed. the circuit is broken this action ceases and also no more bubbles form on the copper.

The 

The electric current and the electron current. names positive and negative were given to these two kinds of electricity by Benjamin Franklin because, to him, the spark of the static machine appeared to pass from the positive" to the "negative" terminals. It would have been much better had he changed the names about as electrons pass along wires from the negative to the positive. 

It would be too confusing to reverse Franklin's decision now. 

While the "electric current" is always thought of as passing from the positive to the negative, we must remember that this is a purely fictitious stream. The actual flow consists of electrons which pass from the negative pole to the positive. However, in liquids and gases, positive ions flow in the same direction as the imaginary "electric current." 

Local action. - If the zinc plate had not been coated with mercury the particles of impurities in the zinc would Cu

Zn have acted like small copper plates, thus forming minute cells wherein the action described above would have taken place. The zinc would have been eaten away even though it were not connected to the copper by wire. This action can be observed by placing a piece of zinc in acid and noting how quickly it is destroyed. 

This effect is called local action. Amalgamation prevents this as the mercury covers the particles of impurities, but the zinc atoms, being dissolved in the mercury, are exposed to the solution.

Polarization. The collection of hydrogen bubbles on the copper plate is called polarization. It hinders the action of the cell in two ways. First, the bubbles block the path of the hydrogen ions as they move towards the copper. Second, the plate that is covered with hydrogen acts like hydrogen instead of

The potential drop between the terminals of an electric cell is sometimes called the e.m.f. (electromotive force) of the cell. Unless large currents are being taken from a battery its e.m.f. depends solely on the materials used in its construction, and not on the size of the cell. The Weston normal cell. - The e.m.f. of this cell remains so constant that it is used as a standard by which to compare and measure voltages. By international agreement the volt is defined as 1/1.0183 of the potential difference between the electrodes of this cell if it is made of chemically pure materials and the temperature is 20° Centigrade. The word electrode is used here instead of plate because it is the more general term and there are no plates in this cell. These cells are often made of H-shaped glass tubing with platinum wires sealed in the lower ends. One of these contains mercury covered with a paste of mercurous sulphate which forms the positive electrode. The other contains cadmium amalgam which forms the negative electrode. 

The tube is filled with a saturated solution of cadmium sulphate and hermetically sealed. When properly made these cells do not vary more than one or two hundred-thousandths of a volt, and they remain constant for years. Cadmium

THE WESTON CELL FIG. 144. 

The 

The Daniell cell and the gravity or crowfoot battery. chemistry of these two types of electric cell is identical, though there is a difference in their construction. In the gravity or crowfoot cell a plate of copper is placed on the bottom of a glass or porcelain jar. Over this are placed crystals of copper sulphate and the jar is then half filled with copper sulphate solution. Over this is poured a solution of zinc sulphate, which being less dense, floats above the other solution. In the zinc

sulphate is placed a piece of zinc. Wires connect the two metal plates.

The action of the ions is as follows. The upper solution contains positive Cu++ and negative SO. ions and the lower solution contains positive Zn++ and negative SO. ions. Zinc ions pass from the plate to the zinc sulphate solution leaving an excess of electrons on the zinc plate which therefore acquires a negative Zn++ So-- 

Zn So

Cu S04 As charge. There is now an excess of positive Zn++ ions and these replace some of the positive Cu++ ions in the copper sulphate forming more zinc sulphate. The positive Cu++ ions are repelled to the copper plate where they acquire electrons which have passed through the wire from the zinc plate. They now become atoms of copper and form a mass of copper on the bottom plate. the action continues the zinc is eaten away, zinc sulphate replaces copper sulphate, and a mass of copper collects on the bottom of the jar. 

No gas is formed and there is no polarization. This is a closed circuit" cell and it can be used only where the electric circuit is closed the greater part of the time. If this is not the case the copper sulphate diffuses up to the zinc plate, copper is deposited on this plate, and the cell is ruined.

Cu++ S04- Cu 

FIG. 145. THE GRAVITY OR CROWFOOT CELL 

66 In the Daniell cell the zinc and zinc sulphate are placed in a porous cup. About this is a copper cylinder immersed in a solution of copper sulphate. The cup separates the liquids, yet allows ions to pass through it. If the circuit of this cell is not kept closed, copper is deposited in the pores of the cup and on the zinc plate thus reducing its efficiency. 

The Leclanché and dry cells. The chemistry of these two types is the same though they differ somewhat in construction. The Leclanché cell consists of a carbon rod in a porous cup about which is packed a mixture of manganese

Sealing Wax 

Zinc Shell 

Paper Lining 

Moist Paste

Containing NHCI, 

MnO2, Powdered 

Coke, etc. 

Carbon Rod dioxide and powdered carbon. Outside the porous cup, is a zinc rod. These are placed in a glass jar and covered with a solution of ammonium chloride. The chlorine unites with the zinc to form zinc chloride and hydrogen polarizes on the carbon. The gas is slowly oxidized by the manganese dioxide, one of the products being water. This cell is useful on open circuit work only, as otherwise the gas collects too fast and the current decreases. As the zinc is attacked only when the circuit is closed this cell is very economical where momentary currents are required, as in the ringing of doorbells. The dry cell is similar to the above except that the zinc forms the jar or container. A large carbon rod fills the center of the can and the remaining space is packed with a mixture of manganese dioxide, powdered coke or charcoal, and sometimes plaster of Paris and sawdust, the whole being made into a paste by mixing with ammonium chloride solution. The water formed from the liberated hydrogen and the manganese dioxide helps to keep the cell from becoming too dry. Some water is necessary to form ions. 

There are other forms of electric cells. The materials are always chosen to give as high a difference of potential as possible and their cost is also considered. Almost any two metals can be used to give some current. If the two wires from a sensitive galvanometer are placed on a filling in a tooth and on the tongue respectively the instrument shows the presence of an electric current. The copper in the wire and the filling are the two metals and the saliva forms the electrolyte. 

FIG. 146. 

THE DRY CELL 

Storage batteries. These are called secondary batteries and also accumulators, and are the most common type of electric battery in use today. When "run down" they may be renewed by sending a current of electricity through

them in the direction opposite to that in which it came out of the battery.

Consider the simple cell of zinc, copper, and sulphuric acid. Suppose the zinc has been eaten away and ZnSO4 has replaced the acid. If it were possible to change the sulphate and the liberated hydrogen back to sulphuric acid and a plate of zinc by sending an electric current through the battery, the latter would be renewed. This cannot be done, but if certain other substances are used in the construction of the cell a somewhat similar action takes place. 

The two common types of storage batteries are the lead cell and the Edison cell. The former consists of lead plates immersed in sulphuric acid which has been diluted with water until the specific gravity is 1.280. These plates contain openings filled with "paste." That on the positive plate is made of lead peroxide, PbO2, and that on the negative plate of spongy lead. As the battery discharges, lead sulphate, PbSO4, is formed on each plate at the expense of the lead peroxide and of the spongy lead, and more water is formed from the oxygen of the PbO2 and the hydrogen of the acid. As the sulphuric acid is thus used up the specific gravity of the cell becomes lower.

When discharging, the current inside the cell is from the negative to the positive plate because it is from the positive to the negative outside. Let a current of electricity be now sent through the cell in the opposite direction. Lead peroxide is restored on the positive plate and spongy lead on the negative. If the charging current is continued for a sufficient length of time the cell returns to its original condition. 

The chemical equations are too complicated to take up here. 

This battery should not be allowed to stand idle for any length of time, especially if lead sulphate is on the plates.

This is because the pastes tend to harden and thus impair the cell. Like the soil in a garden they must be kept in a loose condition. The sulphate is especially difficult to keep in good condition and should be thoroughly removed from

99 the plates at intervals by "overcharging." The active materials should be loosened up occasionally by a complete discharge of the battery. 

Each cell should give about 2.1 volts on a normal load, and when the voltage drops below 1.8 there is danger of "sulphating" the battery. Sulphate is then formed which does not disappear upon recharging. 

These batteries may be injured by allowing the liquid to get too low from evaporation, by replenishing this with water which is not distilled, and by charging or discharging so fast that the plates heat, buckle, or drop the "paste," and thus short circuit the battery.

FIG. 147. 

-PLATES OF A LEAD STORAGE CELL 

The darker plate contains the lead peroxide. Notice the thin board used to separate the plates. 

As the plates are large and close together the internal resistance is small and large currents may be taken from the battery. On open circuit each cell gives 2.55 volts. They return about 75% of the energy used in charging. 

The Edison cell consists of iron capsules in a strong solution of sodium hydroxide, NaOH. The positive capsule contains a compound of nickel and the negative one a compound of iron. Each cell gives 1.2 volts. They are less efficient than lead cells. However, for the same capacity they are much lighter. Being built in welded steel containers they are very rugged, and they will also stand almost any amount of neglect and abuse.

plates are connected they will be at the same potential.

2.16 volts. The potential given by any number of cells in series is the sum of the potentials of the separate cells. A battery giving 40 volts must contain arranged in series. boo

It is evident that the potential difference across the terminals of such a battery is the same as that of a single cell.

The resistance of these solutions is called the internal resistance of the cells.

Current given by cells in parallel and series. - By Ohm's law the current passing through any circuit is equal to the potential divided by the resistance, If cells are in series the voltage which they give is equal to the sum of the voltages of the separate cells, if cells are in

parallel the voltage of the battery is the same as that of one cell.

The resistance, in either case, is the sum of the external and the internal resistances. If the cells are in series the internal resistance is the sum of the internal resistances of the several cells; if they are in parallel the internal resistance is that of a single cell divided by the number of cells. This last may be readily shown by using the formula for resistances in parallel, 1/R 1/r+ 1/2 + 1/r3+...

SECTION 20. THE HEATING AND THE CHEMICAL ACTION OF ELECTRICITY 

In these

When an electric current flows through a resistance a certain amount of electrical energy is always wasted in heating the conductor. This is illustrated in the electric toaster, the electric stove, and the electric iron. the resistance coil becomes red hot. A voltmeter connected to the ends of these resistances will show a difference of potential. As electricity flows down through this potential drop electrical energy is used up and reappears as heat. As one coulomb of electricity falling through one volt of potential represents one joule of work, and as an ampere represents one coulomb each second, it is evident that the amperes, the seconds, and the volts multiplied together give the joules of energy liberated. But we have learned that 

41,850,000 ergs of work are equivalent to one calorie of heat.

Hence if the joules are multiplied by ten million and divided by 41,850,000 we arrive at the number of calories of heat

The temperature is controlled by a strip made of two metals. This curls as the temperature rises, and, when a certain point is reached, the contact is thrown wide open by means of a spring. formed. The thermocouple. Though very inefficient, this instrument affords an example wherein heat is converted directly into electrical energy. When two metals are placed in contact there is a momentary flow of electrons due to

"contact potential." If a loop is made of wires of different metals no current flows around the circuit as the action at the two junctions is in opposite directions. But, if a junction is heated, those forces producing the electron flow are altered. 

Hence, if one junction of this loop is at a different tempera- Terminal

The incandescent lamp depends on the fact that any object, if heated to a sufficiently high temperature, gives out light. The hotter the object the more the light in proportion to the heat radiated. Tungsten is used here because of its high melting point. 

The argon tends to keep the tungsten from evaporating and thus blackening the globe. In small lamps the gas conducts heat away too rapidly. As this calls for more power to keep the filament hot a vacuum is here used. ture from the other, an electric current passes around the circuit. 

This is easily illustrated by attaching an iron and a copper wire to a galvanometer and twisting the free ends together. 

When this junction is heated the instrument indicates a current. The different metals in the galvanometer have no effect, though they may form a part of the circuit. This is because the various junctions of these are all at the same

temperature, and the electric forces there balance each other.

The direction of the current depends on the kinds of metals and the temperature of the junctions. Thus, with copper and iron, the cold junction being at room temperature, the electric current is from copper to iron at the hot junction when the latter is below 550° C., and in the opposite direction when the temperature is higher. 

Very sensitive junctions are made by using antimony and bismuth, but where high temperatures are to be encountered it is necessary to select metals which do not melt easily. For this purpose platinum and one of its alloys are used. The pyrometer consists of a thermocouple attached to a galvanometer which is calibrated to read directly in degrees of temperature. These instruments are used to measure temperatures which are too high for a mercury thermometer. They are much used in certain industries, such as in the manufacture of glasses and porcelains. 

Electro-chemistry. If two metallic plates are placed in an electrolyte, (a solution containing ions), and one is kept at a positive potential and the other at a negative potential, the positive ions are attracted to the negative plate, called the cathode, and the negative ions are attracted to the positive plate, called the anode. Upon reaching the plates the ions give up their charges and become atoms. In this way chemical compounds may be separated electrically. We shall give but a few of the many possible illustrations of this phenomenon.

The electrolysis of water. Water may be decomposed into hydrogen and oxygen by the electric current. Platinum plates are immersed in a dilute solution of sulphuric acid and kept at a difference of potential of ten volts or more. 

Hydrogen ions are attracted to the cathode. There they give up their charges and become hydrogen gas. The negative SO4 radicals pass to the anode where they unite with

water molecules to form more acid, and oxygen is liberated. 

The volume of hydrogen given off is twice that of the oxygen as there are twice as many molecules. 

Copper plating. Here the anode is a plate of copper in a bath of copper sulphate solution. The cathode is the metallic object to be plated. Copper atoms are deposited on the cathode and the SO4 radical dissolves away the copper anode to form more copper sulphate. This is seen in a striking manner if a little copper sulphate is placed in the electrolysis of water apparatus and the current changed in direction several times. 

When the copper is plating the platinum electrode, it becomes dark, but when the current is switched it quickly brightens again. Platings of gold, silver and nickel are made by using an electrolyte containing salts of these metals.

FIG. 151. 

O2 

ELECTROLYSIS OF WATER 

Copper is purified in large quantities by means of electrolysis. This is done by keeping the voltage at a certain point and by placing chemicals in the solution which unite with the impurities. The gold and silver in the mud at the bottom of the tank may be quite valuable. Copper may be obtained easily which is 99.95% pure, the greatest impurity being hydrogen dissolved in the metal. 

In electrolysis salts are not always dissolved in water. 

They may be heated to the melting point and the electric potential applied directly. In this way sodium is recovered from sodium hydroxide.

The use of electricity in the manufacture of aluminum has been so successful that today every housewife can afford utensils of this beautiful metal, though formerly it was half as expensive as silver. Other products of the electrochemical industry are carborundum, graphite, phosphorous, soda-lye, chlorine, calcium carbide, and nitrogen compounds used in the manufacture of fertilizers and explosives. 

Iodine turns starch blue and the OH ion turns phenolphthalein red. If potassium iodide and phenolphthalein are mixed with flour paste and the electric current applied, the cathode becomes red and the anode blue. The cathode attracts the potassium which, upon giving up its charge, forms KOH. This produces OH ions. The iodine ion is drawn to the anode. The legal ampere. By international agreement the ampere is defined as such a current as will deposit .001118 gram of silver from a silver nitrate solution each second. It will also deposit .0003295 gram of copper each second.

ILLUSTRATION OF GRAM MOLECULE 

Even though the piles are weighed in pounds and the seeds in milligrams, yet each pile contains the same number of seeds. A gram molecule is weighed in grams, but the number of grams corresponds to the weight of a molecule, the oxygen atom being taken as 16.

The gram atom and the gram molecule or mole. 

The gram atom of any substance is as many grams of the substance as the number of its atomic weight. 

It is 1.008 grams of hydrogen, 16 grams of oxygen, 63.67 grams of copper, and so on.

It is evident that each of these masses contains the same number of atoms, because the atomic weights give the comparative weights of the atoms, as determined from the way The gram molecule, sometimes in which chemicals combine. called a mole, refers to compounds and is as many grams as

the sum of the atomic weights of the atoms in a molecule. A mole of water is 18.016 grams.

Michael Faraday discovered the fact that the amount of any substance deposited by an electric current is directly proportional to the amount of electricity used and has nothing to do with the voltage. We now know that it requires 96,500 coulombs of electricity to deposit one gram atom of any substance if the ions carry a single charge, twice as much if they carry two charges, and so on. This 96,500 coulombs, or 2.89 X 1014 electrostatic units, is called a "faraday." 

By means of the "oil drop experiment," (see Chapter

VIII, page 414), which has been most accurately performed by Professor Millikan, we know that the electric charge carried by a single electron or by an ion carrying a unit charge is 4.774 X 10-10 electrostatic units. As each ion of any substance deposited by the current is an atom or a molecule, then the number of electrostatic units in one faraday divided by 4.774 × 10-10, which gives 6.06 × 1023, is the number of atoms in a gram atom. From this it is a simple matter to find the actual mass of any single atom or molecule. The number of atoms in a gram atom is called 

Avogadro's number." 66

SECTION 21. 

MAGNETISM 

If a magnetic compass is brought near a wire carrying an electric current, it no longer points steadily northward, but is deflected and tends to stand at right angles to the wire. 

This was first noted by the Danish scientist, Oersted. 

This effect is due to the magnetic field which surrounds the electric current. But before studying this most important part of electrical science, we shall first consider permanent magnets.

Magnets. Certain bodies are observed to attract or repel each other and to attract pieces of iron and steel. These are

called magnets. A piece of hard steel becomes a permanent magnet if it is wrapped with insulated wire and an electric current sent through the wire. It can also be made a magnet by stroking it with another magnet. Iron filings cling thickly to the ends of a magnet but hardly any are attracted to the middle part. The end parts are called poles. If a magnet is suspended by a cord it is easy to determine that one of its poles is attracted and one is repelled by any magnetic pole brought near them. 

Simple experiments show that there are two kinds of magnetic poles, every magnet having one of each, and like poles repel and unlike poles attract each other. The poles are designated as north and south or as positive and negative. When suspended so that it is free to move the "north pole" of the magnet points towards the north. 

FIG. 154. 

LODESTONE FIG. 153. - MAGNETIC INDUCTION 

The nails and the steel ball act as magnets due to the flux from the poles of the magnet passing through them.

It is more properly called the "northseeking " pole. Lodestones. 

Certain ores of iron may be magnetized and are often found thus in their natural condition. These are called lodestones or loadstones. 

Man has known of them for ages, and they were used to make the first com- passes. is but a magcompass netized needle free to turn about a pivot. It points to the north because the earth is a great magnet and its poles attract those of the compass,

Magnetic materials. Iron, cobalt, and nickel are the only elements that exhibit decided magnetic properties. Of these, iron, which includes the various kinds of steel, can be most strongly magnetized. Other bodies show a slight attraction or repulsion when in the presence of powerful magnets, but the action is so weak as to require sensitive instruments for its detection. Such materials as iron, which are attracted by magnets, are said to be paramagnetic. 

Other materials are repelled by magnets, and are then said to be diamagnetic. Bismuth is a good example of a diamagnetic substance.

The earth is a great magnet, and its interior contains large quantities of iron, but to what extent this iron has to do with the earth's magnetism we do not know. The interior is extremely hot, and hot iron is not magnetic. It may be that the great pressure neutralizes the effect of the heat, but this is doubtful.

As variations in the compass accompany changes in the number of sun-spots, we know the condition of the sun affects the magnetism of the earth. The rotation of the earth in a cloud of electric charges sent out from the sun may have something to do with the forming of the earth's magnetic field, but much remains to be learned about these matters. 

The north magnetic pole is not at the north end of the earth's axis, but a thousand miles further south in northern Canada. This is the place to which the compass needle points. Hence, for most localities, the compass needle swings to either the east or the west of true north. The amount of this swing is called the declination of the compass. 

It is over twenty degrees in certain parts of the United States. 

Lines drawn on a map through places having the same declination are called isogonic lines. The line of zero declination, the agonic line, passes southeast from Michigan through 

Ohio, Kentucky, and the Carolinas.

The declination at any given place changes from year to year. There is also a smaller daily variation changing with

the hours, a monthly variation due to the position of the moon, and other periodic changes in declination due to the position of the earth in its orbit, the rotation of the sun and the condition of its surface as shown by sun-spots. 

When surveying old farms the engineer must allow for that change in declination which has occurred since the land was first laid out by the magnetic compass. The declination at Paris changed some thirty degrees in about two centuries. 150 120 90 90 180 135 60 30 30 60 90 120 150 60 40 30 10 0 15 40 10 20 30 20 15 20 10 10 5 10 15 20 60 150 120 90 60 30 10 20 30 40 40 10 20 20 60 30 60 90 120 150 

FIG. 155. 

CHART OF ISOGONIC LINES 

If a needle is first balanced on a horizontal axis and then magnetized it is no longer level, but points downward towards the earth. The angle the needle now makes with the horizontal is called the inclination or dip of the needle. The nearer the magnetic pole the greater the dip of the compass. 

There it points directly downward. The magnetic equator is a line about the earth near the true equator along which the compass has no inclination. 

Magnetic lines of force. As magnetic action takes place in a vacuum we are led to think of magnetic lines of force just as in the case of the attraction of gravitation and the

attraction and repulsion of electric charges. Though these three kinds of "lines of force" are all explained as due to some peculiarity of the ether, yet they are not at all alike in their action. The magnetic lines of force affect only the magnetic materials, other substances being relatively transparent to them. 

A magnet attracts iron through plates of glass, copper, etc., almost the same as though these plates were not present. 

Electric lines of force act upon all substances. There are other differences between magnetic and electric lines of 

FIG. 156.- 

MAGNETS AND IRON FILINGS 

Iron filings sprinkled over magnets indicate the directions of the lines of force. On the left unlike poles are facing each other, on the right like poles. force which will be described later. (6 

Magnetic lines of force, commonly called flux" for short, are considered as passing from the north or positive poles of a magnet to the south or negative poles. The direction of any line of force is that path along which a small, free moving north pole would be repelled if placed upon it. The position taken by the lines of force about a magnet can be readily seen by laying a piece of paper over the magnet and sprinkling iron filings on the paper. The filings arrange themselves along the lines of force. When a small compass is brought near a magnet its needle aligns itself along the lines of force. When no other magnet is present a compass points along the lines of force of the earth. The north magnetic pole of the earth is really a negative or south pole as the lines of force of the earth run from south to north.

When magnetic poles are brought near each other the lines of flux arrange themselves in figures similar to those shown by electrostatic lines of force. They act as stretched rubber bands extending from north to south poles, but they repel each other. 

Unit pole. The strength of a magnet may be given as a certain number of unit poles. Suppose we say that a magnet has a strength of 300 unit poles. This means that the amount of flux passing from the north pole of the magnet is the same as that passing out from 300 small unit poles which are defined as follows. A unit magnetic pole is such a pole that, when placed one centimeter from an exactly equal and like pole, the force of repulsion is one dyne. The law of the force exerted between magnetic poles is similar to that for electric charges. It varies as the product of the polestrengths of the two poles and inversely as the square of the distance between them. If we let p₁ and p2 represent the strengths of the two poles in terms of unit poles, then the force of repulsion or attraction is given by

P1P2 d2 where d must be measured in centimeters in order to have F in dynes. This follows at once from the definition of a unit pole. Calculations of the forces acting between two magnets is complicated by the fact that every magnet has two poles, and there is an attraction existing between each of the two pairs of unlike poles and a repulsion between each of the two pairs of like poles. Every magnet has the same number of unit poles at its north pole as at its south pole. Magnets may have poles at other points besides their ends, but in all cases the sum of the north unit poles is the same as that of the south. 

Maxwells. - Lines of flux are not like strings that can be counted. However it has been found convenient to define the number of lines of force coming from a magnetic

pole in the following manner. Let a sphere of one centimeter radius be drawn about a unit pole. Then one line of force is thought of as passing through each square centimeter of this sphere. But the area of the sphere is 4π square centimeters. Then we assume that each unit pole sends out 4π lines of flux. This is also the number of electrostatic lines of force that come from a unit charge.

FIG. 157. 

UNIT SPHERE 

One line of flux, or one maxwell, is the amount of flux coming out of the shaded area, (one square centimeter) when one unit pole is at the center of the sphere of one centimeter radius.

The lines of force about a magnet form a magnetic field. This is said to be of unit strength when one line of flux passes through each Square centimeter of a plane drawn perpendicular to the lines of the field. Magnetic fields are often much stronger than this. Field strengths are measured in "gausses." The field between the poles of a powerful magnet may be 9,000 gausses. Here the lines of flux are 9,000 times as numerous as is the case with a unit field. The field strength of the earth's magnetism is about one-half a gauss, varying greatly from place to place. 

The Heusler alloys. That magnetism is a molecular rather than an atomic phenomenon is indicated by the so-called Heusler alloys. These are composed of copper, manganese and aluminum. Though each of these elements by itself shows little magnetic action, certain alloys of them are almost as magnetic as nickel. 

The molecular theory of magnetism. If a steel knitting needle is magnetized there is a pole on each end.

If the needle is now cut in two, each part is found to have two poles, a north and a south. Each of the four poles has the same strength as that of the original poles. 

These parts may now be cut and still each piece has two

poles. This same thing happens no matter into how many or how short pieces the needle is cut. Again, a glass tube full of iron filings may be made a magnet, but the magnetism 

FIG. 158. 

BREAKING MAGNETIZED NEEDLE 

disappears if the filings are shaken up. Also, a piece of soft iron is more easily magnetized than one of hard steel, but it loses its magnetism much more readily. Magnets are weakened when jarred, and when heated to redness they lose their magnetism entirely. All of these facts are easily explained by the following theory. 

It is assumed that molecules of iron and steel themselves are little magnets with north and south poles. In ordinary iron these are pointing in all directions, and any small area of the sur- Unmagnetized

Magnetized face of the piece of iron FIG. 159. — MOLECULAR THEORY OF MAGNETISM 

has an equal number of north and of south poles exposed. When the piece is magnetized a large number of the molecules are so turned that their north poles all point in one direction and their south poles in the opposite direction. It follows that one end of the bar will have an excess of north poles on its surface and the other end an excess of south poles, thus forming

a magnet. Inside the bar north and south poles are lined up facing each other, the lines of force pass directly from one molecule to the next, and no external magnetic effect results. 

The act of magnetization consists in causing these molecular magnets to align themselves in proper order. The softer the iron the more easily they may be turned about in line, and also the more easily they can return to their original condition. A piece of hard steel may be magnetized by stroking it with another magnet, and the magnetism will be retained for a long time. It may also be magnetized by holding it parallel to the lines of flux of the earth and striking it with a hammer. Pieces of iron become magnetic by induction when placed near the poles of a strong magnet. 

The magnet rearranges the molecular magnets, due to the attraction and repulsion of the unlike and like poles, so that a number of them have their north poles pointing in the same direction. The ease with which a specimen of iron or steel acquires magnetism is called its permeability, and the tenacity with which iron retains its magnetism after it has acquired it is called its retentivity. From what has been said it is evident that strong permeability is associated with weak retentivity. This is especially true of soft iron. 

A magnetic body can be caused to acquire only a certain amount of magnetization. It is then said to be saturated. 

It is supposed that all the molecules possible are now arranged with like poles pointing in the same direction. 

The magnetic properties of any material depend on the mechanical arrangement of its molecules, its chemical composition, and the heat and magnetic treatment to which it previously has been subjected. 

SECTION 22. 

ELECTROMAGNETISM 

Flux about a wire carrying an electric current. - We deduce that there is a magnetic field about a wire carrying a current because the compass is deflected. The presence

of a magnetic field about such a wire may also be determined by passing a current of about ten amperes through a vertical wire which pierces a horizontal sheet of paper. Iron filings sprinkled over the paper arrange themselves in concentric circles about the wire. The direction of the lines of flux about the current is given by the follow- 

Iron filings on cardboard 88 ing important rule.

Think of the wire as 

FIG. 160.-FLUX ABOUT A WIRE 

being grasped by the right hand with the thumb pointing in that direction in which the electric current is flowing. The lines of flux then pass around the wire in the direction which the fingers take. (The current here is the imaginary electric current, not the real electron current.) The truth of this rule is seen by placing a compass near the wire and noting in which direction the north pole is drawn. 

If a current is passing from south to north in a horizontal wire the north pole of a compass placed above the - FIRST RIGHT-HAND wire will be drawn towards the east, and when placed beneath the wire will be deflected towards the west. On the east side of the wire a north pole is drawn downward, and on the west side it is drawn upward.

RULE 

Helix, solenoid, and electromagnet. The geometrical figure illustrated by a spiral spring is called a helix. A helix formed of insulated wire is called a solenoid. However, solenoids may contain several layers of wire. A solenoid with an iron core which increases the amount of flux is called an electromagnet. 

If we apply the above rule to the various parts of the wire

forming a solenoid, we see that lines of flux come out at one end of the solenoid and enter in at the other. Thus, one end acts as the north and the other as the south pole of a magnet. Those lines between the various turns of the wire are passing in oppo- 

FIG. 162. FLUX ABOUT A HELIX 

site directions, and hence neutralize each other. If an iron core is placed in the solenoid the number of lines of flux is multiplied many times, depending on the permeability of the metal. It may be as great as thirty-fivehundred times. 

Rules for the poles of a solenoid. The following rule is of great value when constructing electromagnets. If the coil is thought of as being grasped with the right hand, the fingers pointing in that direction in which the current is flowing in the wire, the thumb then points to the north pole of the solenoid. 

Conversely, if the thumb is known to point to the north pole of the coil, then the fingers show in which direction the current is flowing in the wire. It can be

FIG. 163.- SECOND RIGHT-HAND RULE 

shown that this rule follows from the one given in the first paragraph of this section. 

It follows that a single loop of wire carrying a current acts as a magnet, one side of the loop representing a north pole and the other side a south pole. 

The magnetic circuit. — Electricity moving in a circle produces magnetic lines of flux. In several ways this flux acts like a flowing stream; thus, it draws the north pole of a compass with the flow.

The current flowing in the coils of an electromagnet acts like revolving fans that blow the flux through the core. The "blowing ability " of the fans is measured in gilberts and depends on the number of turns and on the amperes of current. 

Ampere-turns are reduced to gilberts by multiplying by 4π/10. 

The amount of flux depends, not only on the number of fans and how fast they are turning, but also on the size and length of the core.

The longer the path the more friction" and hence the less flux. The greater the area of cross section the more flux. 66 

FIG. 164. 

FANS IN COIL 

The word "flux" means flow. In many ways the magnetic field acts as though some fluid were passing out from one pole and into the other. We can think of this flow as being blown along by fans. Then each fan corresponds to a turn of the wire. 

The size of the fans can be likened to the number of amperes. 

Hence the ampereturns determine that force which is tending to make flux. It is measured in gilberts. 

But if the coil is very narrow or very long there is much resistance to the flow. This resistance to the magnetic flux is called "reactance," and is measured in oersteds. 

It increases with the length of path and decreases with the area of cross section, and depends on the permeability of the materials through which it passes. 

The amount also depends on the nature of the materials through which it flows. An iron path will have thousands of times as much flux as an air path. 

We shall now consider the amounts of flux given by solenoids and electromagnets of various kinds when different strengths of current are used. We begin by using two doughnut-shaped open iron or steel rings about ten centimeters in diameter. They must be made of the same kind of iron and from bars of the same length. Let the diameter of the rod of which one is made be twice as great as that of the other. After being bent into a ring a gap about one centimeter across is left between the ends

of the bars. This opening must be the same width in both rings. 

When insulated wire is wrapped around the bars and a current sent through it, flux passes across the gaps as between the poles of a horseshoe magnet. The amounts of flux passing can be compared and measured by methods which will be described later. We shall here give the results 

FIG. 165. 

THREE RINGS 

of such comparisons. (1) If either ring is wrapped with insulated wire and a current passed through it, the amount of flux is proportional to the number of turns of wire and to the amperes passing through it. Thus, one hundred turns carrying one ampere will produc the same flux as fifty turns carrying two amperes or two hundred turns carrying one-half of an ampere. The product of the number of turns and the amperes is called the ampere-turns. In the above example there are one hundred ampere-turns. 

If N represents the number of turns and i the amperes, then the maxwells of flux produced are proportional to Ni. (2) If the same length of the same kind of wire is wrapped about both rings it is evident that there will be twice as many turns about the bar of smaller diameter. Using the same current, there are twice as many ampereturns. But there is not twice as much flux passing through the smaller bar. In fact there is only half as much. because, though the ampere-turns are double those of the thicker ring, the area of cross section of the ring is but onefourth as large. (From the law of similar figures.) The amount of flux varies with the area of cross section of the path along which it flows. Let A denote this area. 

This is (3) Let a third ring be now taken, one made of the same material as the other rings, of the same diameter as either of them but of a rod twice as long and having an air gap twice as wide.

It is found that the same number of ampere-turns gives but half as much flux as before. The amount of flux varies inversely as the length of the path through which it flows. (4) A slight increase in the width of the air gap will cut down the amount of flux as much as a larger increase in the length of the iron rod. The flux passes through iron with greater ease than it does through air. 

Under certain conditions the amount of flux passing through a path made entirely of soft iron may be two or three thousand times more than when the path is in the air.

This depends on the permeability of the iron. The Greek letter mu, μ, is used to desig-

Electromagnetic units. There is a whole system of electric units based on the magnetic effects of the electric current. The electromagnetic unit of current is ten amperes. It is such a current, that, when passing through a wire in the form of a circle having the radius of one centimeter, it acts on a unit magnetic pole at the center of this circle with a force of one dyne.

Though these units are used in certain studies in electricity it is not necessary that we consider them here.

A general law of electromagnetism. A moving electric charge always produces a magnetic field. So far we have discussed the magnetic effect of electric currents passing through wires, but these phenomena occur whenever electric charges are moved about. If a disk of glass, copper, or other material is charged electrically and rotated at great speed, magnetic flux is found to pass through the disk just as though it were a coil carrying a current. 

Neither copper nor glass show magnetic properties when at rest, no matter how great the electric charge. 

The magnetic effect is due to the moving charges. The 

FIG. 168. THE ELECTRIC BELL 

When the circuit is closed by pressing the flux of an electromagnet is button B the magnet attracts the armature due to the electricity moving through the wire. Moving electric charges always produce magnetic effects. It is believed that the flux of a permanent magnet is due to and the contact at C is broken. As there is now no current and no magnetic flux, the spring pulls the armature back and contact is again made. The armature is again drawn to the magnet, and so on. The period of vibration is determined by the elastic force of the spring, the mass of the moving parts, and by the force of the magnet. This the rotation of electrons in the atoms of the material. causes the molecules to become magnets. The iron or steel is magnetized when a number of these molecules have their poles facing in the same direction as explained before. 

Another general law. A moving magnetic field always produces a difference of electric potential. But electric potential is most easily detected and measured when it is set up in conductors, hence this special case of the law is usually given.

Whenever a conductor is cut by magnetic lines of flux, the cutting being due to the motion of the field, the conductor, or both, a difference of potential is set up in the conductor. If the conductor forms a circuit, an electric current is then produced. This law was first investigated by Faraday, and the understanding of it has been of the greatest practical use. It is the basic principle of the electric generator, without which electricity would have remained but a plaything for students in scientific laboratories. 

An important experiment. - Hundreds of experiments can be devised to illustrate the above laws. The following can be performed with very simple and inexpensive apparatus.

Hang a flat coil of one or two hundred turns of fine insulated copper wire between the poles of a strong horseshoe magnet so that the plane of the coil is parallel with the lines of force of the field. The coil should be suspended by a strand made of one end of its wire, perhaps a meter or more in length. The other end of the wire must dip into a drop of mercury directly beneath the point of suspension. The two ends of another similar coil are connected electrically to the above through the suspension and the mercury. If the pole of a magnet is suddenly thrust into this second coil the first coil is seen to turn slightly. When the pole is quickly pulled out of the coil the suspended one turns in the opposite direction. However, the suspended coil remains at rest as long as there is no moving of the lines of flux of the magnet. Either a permanent or an electromagnet may be used.

FIG. 169. FARADAY'S Ex- 

PERIMENT From rule" we know that, when the the "three-finger magnet is moved into the coil, the current passes in the direction shown by the arrows, and the front of the suspended coil is a south pole.

The cutting of the wire in the coil by lines of flux produces a current in it according to the law given above. This current, while it lasts, makes the suspended coil a magnet. One of its faces becomes a north and the other a south pole according to the right-hand rule given for solenoids. These faces are attracted by the opposite poles of the magnet, and the coil is caused to turn.

The north and south poles of the two magnets may be determined by using a compass. Then,

FIG. 170. -ELECTROMAGNETIC INDUCTION, FARADAY'S EXPERIMENT 

from the direction in which the suspended coil turns, its north and south faces may be determined. Using the right-hand rule for solenoids, the north and south faces being on the side of the thumb and little finger respectively, the direction of the current in the suspended coil is given by the direction of the fingers. From this we have the direction of the electric current in the other coil. It is seen that when a north pole enters this coil the direction of the induced current is such as to make the front of the coil a north pole, and thus tend to prevent the magnet entering it. When a north pole is removed the front of the coil is a south pole, thus tending to prevent the moving of the magnet. Also, when a south pole enters a coil of wire or is removed, the direction of the induced current is always such as to set up a magnetic field which tends to stop the motion. This is another illustration of the operation of the law of the conservation of energy. 

Work must be done to produce the electric current, and follows what is known as Lenz's law.

Lenz's law. Whenever an electric current is produced by the relative motion of a conductor and a magnetic field the direction of the induced current is always such as to set up a field which opposes the motion. The operation of this law can be seen in every kind of electromagnetic phenomena. We can illustrate it by another experiment using the same apparatus as described above. If the second coil is removed and the suspended coil caused to turn it will twist back and forth a number of times before coming to rest. But if the suspension is connected to the mercury drop so that a current can pass through this coil, it comes to rest much more quickly.

This is called "dampening" the swing. The turns of the swinging coil cut lines of flux and a current is induced according to the second general law given above. 

FIG. 171. LENZ'S LAW 

By Lenz's law the flux of the induced currents in the copper disk will tend to stop any motion of the disk. When it is moved by hand it feels as though one were stirring molasses or some other viscous liquid. If allowed to swing as a pendulum when no current flows through the electromagnet, it comes to rest at once when the current is applied.

This current, being electric charges in motion, produces a magnetic field according to the first general law. The action of the two magnetic fields is to tend to stop the motion, according to Lenz's law. The kinetic energy of the swinging coil is transferred to electrical energy of the current and this is dissipated as heat which is formed by the current

as it passes back and forth through the wire. Another experiment is as follows. A piece of copper is swung as a pendulum between the poles of a strong electromagnet.

The copper swings much longer when there is no current through the field of the magnet than when there is. Eddy currents are set up in the swinging copper because differences of potential are formed by the cutting lines of force. These currents form magnetic fields which tend to stop the motion. 

The induced potential. The cutting of a conductor by lines of flux induces a difference of potential in the conductor. The number of amperes of current then depends on the resistance of the circuit according to Ohm's law, the same as though this difference of potential were produced by a battery. It can be shown by both experiment and by theory that the rate at which lines of force are cut must be one hundred million lines per second for each volt of induced potential. When flux cuts through a coil each line of the flux produces a difference in potential in each turn of the coil. As the turns are connected in series these small voltages added together give the potential induced in the coil. 

Let the pole of a horseshoe magnet across the gap of which 

500 maxwells are passing be inserted into a coil of 200 turns in one-tenth of a second. The rate of cutting is 100,000 lines in one-tenth of a second or one million per second. The induced potential is then .01 volt. If the resistance of the circuit is 100 ohms the induced current is .0001 ampere. 

However, this flows only during that small time interval during which the flux is cutting the coil. 

SECTION 23. SIMPLE ELECTROMAGNETIC INSTRUMENTS 

Galvanometers. The suspended coil as described above forms a simple galvanometer, an instrument much used to detect and to measure small currents. In some instruments the coil is suspended by a fine fiber of phosphor-bronze, a

small mirror is attached to the coil, and the amount of turning is measured by reading the reflection of a scale by means of a telescope. It is easy to make these instruments so

A set of resistance coils regulates the amount of current pressing through the galvanometer. Ammeters. There are a number of types of ammeters, but the most common consists of a shunted galvanometer. 

The shunt is a strip of metal connecting the terminals of the

galvanometer. When a current is sent through the instrument most of it passes across the shunt, and only a small part goes through the coil. But this is always the same fraction of the current passing through the shunt. As the deflection of the galvanometer is proportional to the current passing through the coil, and as the current through the coil is proportional to the total current through coil and shunt, the scale of the instrument may be marked to read in total amperes flowing through the instrument. If the resistance of the coil is 200 ohms and that of the shunt 1 ohm, then 20,000 times as much current goes through the shunt as through the coil, and the total current is 20,001 times that through the coil. 

The voltmeter.

A common type of voltmeter consists of a galvanometer in series with a high resistance. This is generally many thousands of ohms. When such an instrument is connected across a difference of potential the amount of current flowing through the coil is directly proportional to the difference of potential. SECTION 24. ELECTROMAGNETIC INDUCTION 

Mutual-induction and self-induction. When one wishes to induce a current in a coil by using the flux of an electromagnet it is not necessary to move the magnet, but simply to change the amount of current passing through it. The way in which the lines of force move as the current is changed is well illustrated by the curving paths of the drops of water of a fountain. As the force of the stream of the latter is increased the water not only rises higher, but the curved paths of the drops spread out further from the center. 

These curves resemble in a way the lines of flux coming from or passing, into the poles of a magnet. As the water is turned into the fountain the drop-curves spread outward 

FIG. 178. -A FOUNTAIN

and cut across any path drawn on the ground near the fountain. As the water is turned off the drop-curves move inward and cut that path again. If the water is first turned on and then off each drop-curve moves outward and then back and cuts each path about the fountain twice. Like- 

FIG. 179. 

MUTUAL-INDUCTION 

The larger coil has about 200 turns and carries about 20 amperes of current of 60 cycles. The smaller coil has 9 turns. The current induced is sufficient to make the small wire white hot and burn in two. wise, when a current is sent through an electromagnet and then turned off lines of flux spread outward from the magnet and then recede. If a coil of wire is wrapped about the electromagnet many of the lines of flux cut each wire of this coil twice. As they move outward they induce a current in the coil in one direction and as they move back they induce a current in the opposite direction. This is called mutual-induction. the moving lines of flux also cut, to a greater or less extent, the turns forming a part of the electromagnet itself. This But induces a voltage in these turns and is called self-induction. 

By Lenz's law this induced voltage is always in such a direction as to tend to produce a magnetic field to oppose the motion of the original lines of flux. The result of this is that the induced voltage always acts to stop the original current when it is increasing and to keep it moving onward when it is decreasing. Thus when the current is growing the induced voltage is always opposed to the voltage producing the

original current. For this reason induced potentials are often called "back e.m.f.'s."

The henry. - The amount of inductance per unit of current change is measured in henries. A henry of mutual-inductance is produced when two circuits are of such size, shape, distance apart, and enclosing such magnetic materials that α current changing at the rate of one ampere per second in one circuit produces a potential difference of one volt in the other circuit. The mutual-inductance of the circuit A on the circuit 

B is exactly the same as that of the circuit B on A.

FIG. 180. MUTUAL-INDUCTION 

The moving lines of flux pass through the hand, cut the smaller coil, and induce sufficient 

The self-inductance of a circuit is one henry when a current changing at the rate of one ampere per second in this circuit produces a "back e.m.f." of one volt. current to light the small bulb. The flux is not felt at all as it passes through the hand. 

Though it is easy to build inductances of several henries, yet, compared with simple coils, this is a large inductance. 

A smaller unit is much used, the millihenry, which is onethousandth of a henry.

Induced potentials. If the inductance of a system is one henry it is not necessary that one ampere grow or die out in the circuit in one second in order to obtain an induced potential of one volt. If but one-thousandth of an ampere grows or dies out in one-thousandth of a second the rate of change

of current is one ampere per second and there will result one volt of induced potential. However, it lasts but a thousandth of a second.

With an alternating current the direction of flow changes a number of times each second. One cycle of the current consists of a growth and a dying out in one direction and then a growth and a dying out in the other direction. Hence, if a line of flux cuts a conductor once during a cycle, it cuts it four times. The number of cycles made by the current per second is called its frequency and is designated by the letter f. The number of times each line cuts each conductor in one second is then 4f. If im is the maximum amount of current flowing at those instants when the alternating current is at its greatest value, then 4fim is the rate in which current is changing. This multiplied by the henries of inductance, L, will give the average voltage induced in the coil throughout the second. Hence, Vaverage = 4fLimaximum.

It is proved in works on electrical engineering that four times the maximum current is equal to 2 times the average current. Hence we can write, V = 2πfLi. 

This equation is true when both voltage and current are average, maximum, or effective. An effective alternating current is measured in terms of the volts and amperes of that direct current which can do the same amount of work. 

By Ohm's law we have 

V = Ri.

This shows that the back voltage in a circuit has the same effect as the insertion of an additional resistance of R = 2πfL ohms. This holding back of an alternating current, due to inductance, is called reactance, and is measured in ohms. It is generally designated by the letter x. Hence, we write, x = 2πfL.

When a coil consists of but a few turns of wire its inductance, L, may be very small. If the frequency of a current sent through this coil is also small, say an ordinary alternating current of 60 cycles per second, the amount of reactance is not large and the current passes through the coil with ease. 

But if the current sent through the coil has a very high frequency, say one or two million cycles per second as is often the case in radio circuits, the reactance may be so great that hardly any current gets through. Currents of low frequency readily pass through coils that block "high frequency" currents almost entirely. Many of the coils used with radio consist of but few turns of wire and do not have iron cores so that the inductance is very small, as otherwise no current could pass. 

Induction and iron cores.

The presence of an iron core in a coil through which a current passes increases the number of lines of flux enormously, and hence it increases the induction. This may be illustrated as follows. A coil is connected to a galvanometer. A second coil is placed close to this one and a momentary current sent through it. The induction may or it may not be great enough to produce sufficient current in the first coil to cause the galvanometer to jump first in one direction and then in the other. However, if an iron core is passed through the two coils, the deflections of the galvanometer will be much more noticeable. 

If a direct current is sent through the second coil the galvanometer is deflected to one side and then comes back to zero even though the current passes steadily through the coil. But when the current is broken the galvanometer then swings to the other side. Currents are induced only when lines of flux move. The flux does not move as long as the current flows steadily. An increase or decrease of current, though slight, causes a shifting of lines of flux, and hence an induced current. 

When a 

Induction at the "make" and the "break." current is sent through a coil it may be several hundredths

of a second from the instant the switch is closed until the current attains its full value. The flow of current is retarded by the back e.m.f. But when the current is broken the flow of electricity stops more suddenly, it may be in a few millionths of a second. In this exceedingly short interval of time the lines of flux drop back, cut the coil and the magnetic field disappears. When a current is broken the rate in which flux cuts conductors is much greater than when the circuit is closed. As the amount of voltage induced is proportional to the rate of cutting of lines of flux, much higher voltages are formed at the "break" than at the "make." When a switch is opened the high induced potential may drive the current across the air gap and form an arc. This arc then continues to burn on the normal voltage of the circuit as the heated air is a fair conductor of electricity. To prevent this, switches are sometimes made so that the break takes place through resistances in steps which extend over several hundredths of a second. For the same reason switches are sometimes immersed in oil, in which an arc does not form as readily as in air. 

We are now able to understand the following. An electric motor, running smoothly, may be on such a voltage that one can touch its brushes with the hand and yet not receive a shock. But if, while he has his hand on the brushes, someone breaks the circuit and thus turns the current off from the motor, he may receive a very severe shock indeed. Men have lost their lives in this way. Notice that the high potential is induced when the circuit is broken. 

The induction coil. This instrument is used to obtain a pulsating current of high potential from a source of low potential. Thus, a battery of but six volts may be used to produce a spark of many thousands of volts. A "primary coil of relatively few turns of wire encircles a core of soft iron, and about this is a "secondary" coil of many turns of fine wire. In front of the core is an armature or vibrator which consists of a piece of iron at the end of a spring. This

spring is attached to a "contact" which opens when the magnetic field of the coil draws the armature towards itself, and closes as the spring flies back. Current from a battery passes through this contact and thence through the primary of the coil. This produces a magnetic field which draws the armature, breaks the circuit, and destroys the magnetic field. As the armature is no longer attracted the spring draws it back to its former position. This closes the contact, current passes, and a magnetic field is again formed. 

As the armature vibrates, flux moves in and out from the core. This cuts the turns of the secondary and, as there are many turns, induces a very high potential in it. As the voltages formed at the break are much higher than at the make, these surges of current have a much higher potential in one direction than in the other. 

When an induction coil is used with an X-ray tube current passes in one direction only, as the voltage made when the contact closes is not high enough to send a current through the tube. A condenser is placed at the base of the coil to absorb the surge of current at the break. This prevents the passage of current as the contact opens.

The three-finger rule for induced potentials. — Experiment gives us the following rule for determining the direction in which an induced potential acts. We assume that the magnetic field is at rest and a conductor moves so as to cut lines of flux. Hold the thumb, the forefinger, and the centerfinger of the right hand so that each is perpendicular to the other two. Let the forefinger point in the direction of the lines of flux, and the thumb in the direction in which the wire moves. 

Then the center-finger points in the direction in which the induced voltage tends to drive an induced current. This

current flows only when the conductor is part of a closed circuit, and its amount is determined by the resistance of this circuit. This rule is often used to determine the direction of the induced current in a loop flux Current Motion of wire turning in a magnetic field as is the case in electric generators. 

To aid the memory notice that t stands for turn and thumb, ƒ for flux and forefinger, and c for current and center-finger. The three-finger rule gives the direction of the induced current in the secondary of an induction coil, but one must be careful to think of the flux as standing still and the coil as moving in a direction opposite to that in which the flux really moves.

leee eeee The electric generator. This machine, which is sometimes called a dynamo, produces electric current by revolving conductors in a magnetic field and thus cutting lines of flux. 

The field is produced by electromagnets and the revolving conductors are coils of insulated wire wrapped about an iron core called an armature. Current is taken from the coils of the armature by means of carbon "brushes" which press against "slip rings," or a 

66 commutator." Slip rings are circular pieces of metal attached to the ends of the coils of the wire on the armature. The current passes from these to the brushes and thence through the external circuit. When these are used the current sent out is alternating. This is seen by applying the three-finger rule to the wires of the armature coil shown in the figure. Only one coil is shown 

FIG. 183. - ALTERNATING CURRENT GENERATOR

for simplicity, though in most generators a number of coils are placed on the armature; in which cases, though the path of the currents in these coils is more complicated, the principle is the same. It is seen that as the wire (a) moves up past N, and (b) down past S the direction of the current in the coil is as shown by the arrow. But after the coil turns past the perpendicular plane through its center and (a) moves down past S and (b) up past N, the induced current is in the opposite direction. When the loop is horizontal it is cutting lines of flux at the greatest rate and the highest voltages are induced. When the loop is perpendicular the wires move parallel to the lines of flux and no voltage is induced. With a single loop and two magnetic poles in the field one cycle of the current is formed by each revolution of the armature. Such a machine is called an alternator. eeee 00000 A direct current generator. A direct current generator has a commutator instead of slip rings. There is but one ring, formed of alternate segments of copper and mica. 

The ends of the loops on the armature are connected to the copper segments.

The diagram shows but one loop and a commutator of but two copper segments. 

In a practical machine there are many loops and many segments. It is seen that as the direction of the current changes in the loop the segments change over to the other brush. Thus, though the current pulsates, it is always coming out of one brush and returning at the other. This forms a direct current. 

With two loops and four copper segments the pulsations are greatly reduced, as, in this case, at every instant current is being induced in one loop or the other. But there is still

Series, shunt, and compound wound generators. 

These are the three types of direct current

The mag- generators. netic fields in commercial machines are formed by electromagnets excited by current from the machine itself or by a smaller generator used for this purpose alone. When all the current generated in the armature passes through the coils of the field and then through the external ... circuit the machine is said to be series wound. If the current from the armature branches and part goes through the field and part through the external circuit it is said to be shunt wound. 

The compound machines have two wrappings of wire on the magnets; one composed of a few turns of heavy wire through which all the current passes as in a series machine, and another winding of many turns of finer wire through 

FIG. 186. 

SERIES WOUND

which a part of the current again passes as in a shunt machine. 

If more current is taken from a series wound machine the voltage rises. The greater the current the stronger the field and the faster lines of flux are cut. If more current is taken from a shunt wound machine the voltage drops. More current can only be taken by reducing the external resistance. 

This allows a greater percent of the total current to flow through the external circuit and less flows through the field, thus reducing the number of lines of flux. Compound wound machines are designed for a certain voltage and this 

FIG. 187.- SHUNT WOUND FIG. 188.- COMPOUND WOUND 

remains constant no matter how much or how little current is used.

The voltage of a generator may be increased by turning it faster, by increasing the strength of the magnetic field, or by having more turns of wire in the coils of the armature, as in either case the rate at which lines of flux are cut is increased. The armature cores and frames are generally built up of thin sheets of soft iron. This is to prevent heating due to "eddy currents" set up in these parts by lines of flux cutting through them. These sheets are sometimes insulated by being varnished, though generally the oxide formed on them is sufficient insulation to prevent eddy currents from becoming too strong. A magneto is a generator which uses a permanent magnet to produce the field.

The motor experiment. - - Moving a conductor across the lines of a magnetic field induces a difference of potential in it. 

FIG. 189. 

-THE MOTOR EXPERIMENT 

If the top of the magnet is a north pole and a current is sent down the wire, the latter revolves about the magnet in the direction the thumb points. 

Conversely, the sending of a current through a conductor which crosses the lines of a magnetic field produces a force that tends to move the conductor.

This is well illustrated by this simple experiment. A bar magnet rises vertically from a cup, and a light wire hangs beside it, as shown in the figure, and dips into mercury or a copper sulphate solution in the cup.

If a direct current of about ten amperes is sent through the wire it revolves about the magnet.

This illustrates the principle of the direct current motor. A direct current generator will also act as a motor. If sufficient current passes through the loops of the armature they turn, Copper Sulphate Needle
