and the action of the commutator keeps them revolving in the same direction.

In such a case there are two magnetic

fields, that from pole to pole across the armature and that about the wires of the armature carrying a current. The turning can be explained by the fact that lines of flux passing in the same direction repel each other, and those passing in opposite directions attract each other. 

The left hand three-finger rule. This is used with motors. The thumb, forefinger, and center-finger of the left hand are held so that each is perpendicular to the other two. If the forefinger points in the direction of the lines of flux of the field and the center-finger in the direction the current is passing, then the thumb shows the direction the wire tends to turn. 

Types of direct current motors. As with generators, motors may be series wound, shunt wound, or compound wound. Series wound motors are used for automobile starters, punches, cranes, and machinery where a strong starting force is desired. Both the field and the armature magnetism are strong at starting as there is little back e.m.f. and a large current flows. This back e.m.f. is caused by the rotating armature as its coils cut the flux and act as do the coils of the armature of a generator. This induced voltage always works against the potential sending current into the motor. Hence the greater the speed of the armature the less current flows through it, and, when starting, this current is much greater than later when full speed is obtained. The speed of an unloaded motor is such as to give a back e.m.f. almost equal to that sending the current through the armature. Then but little current flows, just enough to supply energy to overcome friction. In case of a series motor this shutting off of current weakens the field and the motor must run faster in order to cut enough lines of flux each second to produce the required back e.m.f. 

Hence, the weaker the field becomes the faster the motor must run to prevent more current than needed from passing through the armature; and in turn, the faster it runs the weaker the field becomes. The result is that unloaded series motors run away." They must not be connected to

machines by belts, which might slip off, but by cog wheels, as the motor is apt to destroy itself and do other damage if it acquires too great speed.

Shunt wound motors are used on lathes and other machines where a steady speed is required regardless of whether the 

FIG. 191. A DIRECT CURRENT GENERATOR 

This shows the armature, field coils, and brushes. load is heavy or light. The strength of the magnetic field is fixed by the shunt. If the motor slows down, due to a heavy load, the back e.m.f. becomes less. This allows the armature to take more current which gives it increased strength. Hence, when the load is increased, instead of running slower, the motor takes more power from the line.

Notice that the speed of a motor increases when the field is made weaker. If a motor is given a heavier load than it can handle it slows down, takes a very heavy current from the line, and "burns out." By this we mean that the coils become so hot that the insulation burns and the motor is ruined. If a shunt wound motor has no load it runs at a steady speed and takes only enough power from the line to overcome friction. This is because its speed is such that the back e.m.f. almost balances that sending current into the motor. A compound wound motor starts with greater power than a shunt wound machine, but it can not run "like the series wound machine. Compound wound motors are used where great strength is needed at starting and yet where it is necessary to regulate the speed. A good example of this is in elevators. The speed acquired depends on the strength of the field. This is regulated by a variable resistance or "rheostat " placed in series with the field coils. 66 

These are 

Fuses, circuit breakers, and starting boxes. designed to protect electrical machinery from currents that are too great. A fuse consists of a short strip of lead wire or foil which melts when the current becomes too great and thus breaks the circuit. Circuit breakers consist of one or more electromagnets through which the current passes. 

When the current reaches a certain strength the magnet pulls a trigger which releases a spring and breaks the circuit. 

Starting boxes must be used with the larger motors. They consist of variable resistances by means of which the current can be admitted to the armature a little at a time. If the full voltage is impressed on the armature when it is at rest too much current flows. As its speed increases more voltage may be applied. When at normal speed the back e.m.f. is sufficient to control the admission of current to the armature, and it takes just sufficient power to perform the work required of it. A common example of the use of a starting box is seen in the operation of a street car. 

As the car gains

speed the motorman removes resistance from the circuit containing the armature coils.

The telegraph. Today the principal cities of our country are connected by the printing telegraph. The operator presses keys, similar in appearance to those of a typewriter, and the message appears, printed and ready to deliver, at the other end of the line, it may be a thousand miles away. 

We can not describe this complicated mechanism here, though its operation depends on the fundamental laws of electromagnetism which we have discussed.

Hulle ww Switch 400000Main Battery Relay 

Line between stations Haddond ​Ground

FIG. 192. TELEGRAPHY SYSTEM WITH RELAYS 

In the simpler forms of the telegraph a key is closed at one station thus completing a circuit which includes an electromagnet at the other station. The magnet operates an armature which makes a clicking noise. If the key is released as quickly as possible the noise produced by the armature is called a "dot." If the key is held down momentarily before being released the noise produced is called a "dash."

This is because the first instruments made dots and dashes on a strip of paper. Letters are formed by various combinations of dots and dashes. The instrument forming the letters is called a sounder.

Sometimes the distant station is so far away that sufficient current to operate a sounder can not pass through the circuit. 

In this case the current operates a "relay." The relay is an electromagnet of many turns of very fine wire which operates a light armature attached to a delicate spring. This armature in turn opens and closes a contact in a "local circuit," which in turn operates a sounder. Generally there are a number of stations on the same circuit. All the keys are kept closed except when a station wishes to send a message. The operator at that station then opens his key and gives the call for whichever station he desires. When he finishes he closes his key, or switch, as otherwise no other station could send a message. 541

THE ELECTRIC PHONOGRAPH 

Here we have a most interesting case of electromagnetic induction. The needle is secured to a soft iron armature which is placed inside a small coil. Flux passes through this armature as it is between the poles of a permanent magnet. The needle, following the record on the disk, moves the armature. This, in turn, causes the lines of flux to move slightly. This movement is sufficient to induce currents in the coil, which, upon amplification, reproduce sound.

SECTION 25. ALTERNATING CURRENTS 

We have already discussed all the fundamental principles of electricity and magnetism. But, with this knowledge alone, one would find it difficult to explain the operation of some of the most common electrical machines. This is because they are designed for alternating currents. Their operation, in a general way, is somewhat as follows. An alternating current flows through a conductor A, generally

a coil, and thus generates a fluctuating magnetic field. 

Refer to the fountain analogy on page 275. These moving lines of flux cut another conductor B, and generate an alternating current in it, which, in turn has its own fluctuating magnetic field. The operation of the machine then results from the attraction or repulsion exerted between these two magnetic fields. Thus, the watt-hour-meter, which is found in many homes using electric light, consists of a circular aluminum plate mounted on a shaft. Alternating currents pass through coils placed near this plate and cause it to turn. This then registers the amount of power consumed. 

The plate turns because of eddy currents set up in it by the moving lines of flux from the coils, the magnetic fields of these currents being attracted or repelled by those of the coils. A similar action takes place when an aluminum plate is placed on a pole of a powerful electromagnet and a strong alternating current is sent through the latter. The plate is repelled violently, and may be thrown across the room. 

Before we can explain why the plate is not attracted instead of repelled we must learn something of the nature of alternating currents. 

Sine and cosine curves. Suppose a single coil of wire revolves at a steady speed in a uniform magnetic field. An alternating current may be taken from the coil by means of slip-rings. When the plane of the coil is in a position parallel with the lines of flux it is cutting them most rapidly, and the highest voltage is being generated in the coil. When the plane of the coil is perpendicular to the lines of flux the wire of the coil is moving along the lines of flux and is not cutting them at all. 

Then no current is being generated. 240 

Suppose the coil makes 60 revolutions per second. Beginning with the coil in the horizontal position and the voltage at its greatest value we see that in 10 of a second it has turned through an angle of 90 degrees and the voltage has fallen to zero. 

In of a second the coil has turned through 180 degrees and the voltage is now at its greatest value but in the opposite direction. In of a second the angle is 270 degrees and the 240

voltage is again zero. At the position of 360 degrees the voltage is again a maximum in the original direction. Thus we can plot voltage against time or angular measure, the result being a sine or cosine curve. It is a sine curve when there is zero current at zero time and a cosine curve when the greatest value of voltage or current occurs at zero time. 

If the speed is not steady, or if the magnetic field is not uniform the curve will not be a sine or a cosine curve. 

Cosine curve 120° 150% S180° 1210° 90° 60° 30° 

0° 30° 60 90 120 150 180 210 240 270° 360° 330 

FIG. 193. CURVE SHOWING THE AMOUNT OF INDUCED VOLTAGE FOR VARIOUS POSITIONS OF THE ARMATURE 

Combining alternating currents. The following remarks apply to both currents and voltages. If two such currents as the one described above are sent into the same wire, it is evident that they appear there as a single current. If the original currents have the same frequency, the resulting current will change uniformly, but if the frequencies are different the current will not plot a sine curve. 66 If two currents of the same frequency rise and fall together, both passing through their maximum and their zero values at the same instants, they are said to be in the same phase." 

In this case the resulting current, at each instant, has the value of the sum of the two separate currents. 66 

If one current lags 180° behind the other, it is always rising as the other is falling, and vice versa. It is evident, that, if the two currents are of the same strength, the resulting current is zero, as they neutralize each other. 

If two currents have the same frequency, but one lags behind

the other by any amount not 180°, they sometimes act together and sometimes against each other. The resulting current, in this case, of course, varies, but the maximum values of it are not as great as the sum of the maximum values of the two original currents. The special case, when one lags 90° behind the other, is of importance. Here the value of the resulting current can be found by the theorem of Pythagoras. If a and b are the maximum values of the original currents respectively, and c that of the resulting current, then c² = a² + b². 66 

The current lagging behind the voltage. In the figure the curve A " represents the rise and fall of an alternating current that is passing through a circuit of noticeable self-inductance. 

The curve "B" represents the rise and fall of the induced voltage. It is greatest at those instants when the current is changing the fastest, which moments are when the current is passing 

Later or Behind

FIG. 194. 

GRAPH OF APPLIED VOLTAGE, CURRENT, AND INDUCED VOLTAGE 

through its zero values. Then lines of flux are cutting the wires most rapidly. Also, the induced voltage is zero when the current is passing through its maximum values. This causes the induced voltage to lag 90° behind the current, as shown in the figure. 

But the current, at each instant, is the result of two voltages, the impressed voltage which caused the current and the induced voltage which was caused by the current. As the induced voltage lags behind the current it is necessary that the applied voltage lead the current, so that the resultant of these two will be in phase with the current. In the figure, this applied voltage is represented by the curve C." While we mention this voltage last, it really comes first, as it causes the current, and the current causes the induced voltage. Thus we see, that, with alternating currents, the current flowing in a circuit may lag behind the voltage applied to that circuit. When the inductance is very great this lag may be almost 90°. 66

Resistance, reactance and impedance. - Every circuit has certain amount of resistance to a direct current which we denote by Rohms. On page 278 we observed that a circuit containing inductance offers an additional resistance to an alternating current, which is called its reactance, and is denoted by x ohms. 

Here x = 2πfL, where L is the inductance in henries and ƒ the frequency of the alternations. 

The combined effect of resistance and reactance in an alternating current circuit is called its impedance and is designated as Z ohms. Thus, for alternating currents, Ohm's law is written,

Zi V.

One might think that Z is found by adding R and x together, but this is not the case. A study of the curves for applied voltage, induced voltage, and resulting current shows that, Z2 R2 + x². 

To find the inductance of a coil. An alternating current may be sent through a coil, and the values of current and voltage found by using an alternating current ammeter and voltmeter. Knowing i and V it is a simple matter to find Z from 

Ohm's law. The resistance R may be found by sending a direct current through the coil and measuring the amperes and volts, or it may be found by using the Wheatstone bridge. 

When Z and R are known it is a simple matter to calculate x from the second formula above. Now x 2TfL. The frequency, f, of most lighting circuits is 60 cycles per second, and in any case it can be found from the company furnishing the power. Knowing x and ƒ, the number of henries of inductance, L, may be determined. The The three ways of measuring alternating currents. term alternating current or voltage may mean any one of three things. (1) The maximum current or voltage. This is the value of the direct current which would flow if it were of the same strength as a current of the greatest value attained by the alternating current. (2) The average current or voltage. This is the average value of the alternating current or voltage.

(3) The effective current or voltage. This is the value of that direct current which can do the same amount of work as the given alternating current, the voltages in each case being the The effective voltage is defined in a similar way. 

The relation between these quantities is as follows. same.

Unless stated otherwise effective values are meant. These are measured by alternating current ammeters and voltmeters. 

The power factor. The watts of power furnished by an alternating current depend on the product of the amperes by the volts acting with them. When the current lags behind the voltage the highest values of the current do not occur at those instants when the voltage is greatest. The power is less than it would be if the volts and amperes were in phase, that is rising and falling together. It can be shown that Watts KiV. 

Here K is called the power factor. It is always less than unity when current and voltage are not in phase. 

Importance of a lagging current. The lag of the current must be taken into account when explaining the operation of several types of alternating current instruments. The following example is called "Elihu Thompson's Experiment."

Let an iron rod rise vertically from the pole of a strong electromagnet. About this rod place a light aluminum ring. When a strong alternating curtent is sent through the magnet the ring rises and is suspended in space without any visible support. (It may be necessary to have the rod part of a complete magnetic circuit.)

As the flux rises from the magnet and cuts the ring a current is induced therein. By Lenz's law it is in such a direction as to set up a magnetic field, which opposes the moving flux. As flux from the coil, when it is a north pole, moves upward the flux about the ring is such that the lower side of the ring is a north pole. 

But when this same flux moves downward the bottom side of the ring becomes a south pole as it must then attract the lines of force to keep them from moving. If the induced current in the

ring were exactly 90° out of phase with the motion of the flux the ring would be alternately repelled and attracted by the same amount. But self-induction, acting on the current in the ring, causes it to lag. The polarity of the bottom side of the ring

Condensers in alternating circuits. - The diagram represents two condensers placed in series in an alternating current circuit. 

It is evident that if it were direct voltage impressed on the condensers no current would flow as there is insulating material

between the plates of the condensers. But suppose an alternating voltage is impressed on the plate A. By induction the plate B acquires an opposite charge. This requires that C acquire the same kind of charge as A, while, by induction D acquires a charge of opposite kind. Due to the alternations of the voltage, the sign of the charge on A is continually changing, and so does the charge on all the other plates. In order that this may be accomplished electrons must flow back and forth between B and C. Thus at any point of the circuit, as at x, an alternating current is passing. The amount of the current will depend on the capacity of the condensers, the voltage impressed, and the frequency with which it changes sign. If C is the capacity of a condenser placed in an alternating current circuit, the amount of current flowing is given by, FIG. 196. 

i = 2πfCV. B X

-CONDENSERS IN CIRCUIT 

This may be written (1/2πfС)i V. By Ohm's law Ri V. 

Hence a condenser in an alternating current circuit acts as a resistance of 1/2πfС ohms, and the larger the condenser the smaller the resistance. A capacity so placed will tend to cause the current to lead the impressed voltage, and hence have the opposite effect to an inductance. If capacity and inductance, condenser and coils, are arranged in series in an alternating current circuit, the value of the reactance is given by, 

x=2fL 1/2πfС. 

As all circuits have a certain resistance, R, and as the impedance, Z, is given by, Z = VR2+x², by substituting we get the complete formula,

Z = √R² + (2xƒL — 1/2πƒС)2. 

We shall need this equation to explain the tuning of radio sets which is taken up in the last chapter of this book. 

The oscillatory nature of a condenser discharge. - When a spark passes the terminals of a Leyden jar or other condenser only one report is heard, and it is surprising to know that, in this brief interval of time, the electricity may jump

back and forth between the terminals several hundred times. 

However, the spark is not oscillatory if the resistance of its circuit is above a certain value. 

While there are several reasons for believing the above statement, the conclusive one is that the oscillations of the spark may be photographed by means of suitable apparatus and the frequency of the vibrations calculated. This is determined by the capacity of the condenser and the inductance of the circuit. It is always such that the reactance due to inductance is exactly counterbalanced by that due to capacity, that is 2πfL — 1/2πƒС = 0. If this equation is solved for f we find that the larger the capacity or the inductance the slower the frequency of oscillation. This action can be explained in terms of laws which we have already learned. Let A and B be the condenser terminals between which the spark passes. Let the charge pass from A to B. The passage of the electricity sets up a magnetic field about the path of the current. 

When the charges on the plates have neutralized each other this field still exists. 

As it collapses it induces more current in the same direction as that which has just passed. This piles up a positive charge on the terminal opposite the one on which it first existed. The magnetic field now disappears and an electrostatic field appears in its stead. A spark now returns from B to A. Energy thus changes from electrostatic to magnetic and vice versa until it is used up in heating the circuit and in producing light and noise. 

FIG. 197. SPARK AND FIELD 

Hertzian waves. These are the waves used in wireless and are named after the German scientist, Henrich Hertz, who first experimented with them. Their existence had been predicted from purely mathematical considerations by the great English physicist, James Clerk Maxwell. These waves are formed by the rapidly changing electric and magnetic fields described above. However, they are

radiated much stronger if one side of the condenser is connected to the earth and the other to an "antenna," which is a group of insulated wires comparable in length to the Hertzian wave sent out. The speed of these waves is the same as that of light, 3 X 1010 centimeters per second. The length of a wave is equal to this velocity divided by the frequency and they can be formed of all lengths from a fraction of a millimeter up to many miles. 

If a Leyden jar is caused to discharge through a loop of wire, as shown in the figure, the waves may be detected by their effect on a similar jar and loop placed nearby. When the size of the second loop is so adjusted that the products of capacity and inductance for both jars are equal the frequency of the alternating current set up by the waves is such that there is no reactance to the current in the loop of the second jar. The impedance is due to resistance alone. FIG. 198.—The Leyden Jar 

The current induced in the receiving loop may be strong enough to cause a spark to pass across the gap in it. Again the current may be detected by the use of telephone receivers. 

This is the principle of the simplest wireless telegraph. 

SECTION 26. TRANSMISSION AND UTILIZATION OF POWER 

The sources of power, both waterfalls and steam plants, are often at considerable distances from the places where the energy is used. Thus, the city of Toronto obtains power from Niagara Falls, ninety miles away. As electricity passes through wires some of the electrical energy is used up in heating the conductor, hence, where power is transmitted any distance, it is necessary that this loss be made as small as possible. We have learned that, Hin calories = .24 Ri't.

The energy loss depends on the amount of current flowing but not on the potential. Ten amperes at one hundred volts will lose the same amount of energy in passing through any circuit as would ten amperes at one-hundred-thousand 

Courtesy Utah Power and Light Company.

FIG. 199. A TRANSMISSION LINE 

volts. But in the latter case one thousand times as much power is transmitted. Moreover, to double the amperes increases the power loss four times, to triple the amperes increases the loss nine times, and so on. Hence, to avoid as much loss as possible electrical power must be sent at the highest voltage available. A California company transmits its power at a voltage of 

220,000. A voltage of 

100,000 is quite common and the manufacturers of electrical equipment are experimenting with lines of a million volts. High voltages also permit the use of more resistance, that is, of smaller wires. 

No small part of the cost of electricity is interest on money invested in copper, which is far from being a cheap metal. 

Transformers. The generators furnish the current at medium voltages, quite generally at from 2,300 to 11,000 volts. Before this current is sent over the line it is stepped

up to 50,000 or 120,000 volts by means of transformers. 

We shall now study these marvelously efficient and most useful machines without which it is doubtful if power could be furnished cheaply enough to be used as it is today. 

A simple transformer may be made by building up a square of thin strips of soft iron and then wrapping coils of insulated wire about two of the sides of this square. An alternating current is now passed through one of these coils which is called the primary coil. 

This forms a fluctuating magnetic field through the iron circuit which induces a potential across the other coil, called the secondary coil. If this coil forms part of a circuit a current flows. For practical purposes we may say that the voltage induced is to the original voltage as the number of turns of wire in the secondary are to the number of turns in the primary. Hence, the coils may be so designed as to give either a higher or a lower voltage than that applied for the magnetic flux. to the apparatus. These are called "step-up" and "step-down" transformers respectively. 

FIG. 200. A TRANSFORMER 

The two coils are wound about an iron core which extends beyond the coil to make a complete circuit The core is built up of sheets of iron of high permeability. The sheets form a "laminated "" core which is necessary to prevent eddy currents. 

The action of the magnetic field is such that the number of ampere-turns in the secondary can never exceed those in the primary. But the voltage induced depends on the relative number of turns in the two coils. Hence, if the voltage in the secondary is double that in the primary, the current in amperes is but one-half as large, and so on. The number of watts, volts times amperes, taken out from the secondary is always slightly less than those sent into the primary. 

This is as it should be according to the Law of the Conservation of Energy as some work is expended in heating the coils. 

---

Suppose power is sent to a city at 130,000 volts. Somewhere outside the city limits this is stepped down to about one-tenth this value and sent to the various sub-stations. 

Here the voltage is again lowered. 2,300 volts is a common voltage for these sub-stations. From these stations the power is further distributed to small transformers placed near the houses where the electricity is used. These again reduce the voltage, generally to 220 or 110 volts. These small transformers are often hung on the poles of the power line. They are in iron boxes and are covered with oil. The 

Courtesy Utah Power and Light Company.

FIG. 201.- TRANSFORMERS FOR HIGH VOLTAGES 

oil helps to carry away the heat formed in the coils, improves the insulation, and protects the coils from the weather, insects, and dust. The cores of these transformers are built up of varnished sheets of iron to prevent eddy currents. 

They are shaped so as to give the least possible reluctance to the magnetic flux.

The primary of a transformer contains enough turns of wire to give a large back e.m.f. and thus prevents any but a very minute current from flowing as long as none is taken from the secondary. Suppose a small current, as that due to the turning on of one lamp, is now taken from the secondary.

This current sets up a magnetic field which, to a slight extent, neutralizes that of the primary. This lessens the amount of self-inductance in the primary and a slightly larger current now flows through the latter. If another light is turned on the results described become more pronounced and more power is taken off the line by the primary. Thus the transformer is automatic in its action. If no current is taken from the secondary very little power is used by the primary, and the more current taken by the former the more passes through the latter. Transformers are destroyed when too much current is taken from them. In such cases the coils become very hot and the insulation is destroyed by burning.

By having the secondary a few turns of very heavy wire large currents at small voltages are produced. A common use of such transformers is to give the large currents used in electric welding.

On the other hand very high voltages are produced by having the secondary composed of a great number of turns of exceedingly fine wire. For many purposes these transformers are superior to induction coils. They are used to operate X-ray tubes and in broadcasting. 

Three-phase systems. One notices that the transmission lines of power companies consist of three wires, or some multiple thereof. This is because power can be transmitted most economically over three wires carrying currents 120° out of phase with each other. When this is the case the amount of current flowing out on any one wire at any instant is exactly equal to that flowing back on the other two, and when currents are flowing out on two wires the same amount is flowing back on the third. This may be illustrated and understood by drawing curves representing three equal currents with a phase difference of 120°. The height of any point of one curve is exactly equal to the sum of the heights of the corresponding points of the other two curves on the other side of the x-axis, which proves the statement above.

66 Three-phase currents are formed in three separate coils in the armature of the generator. One end of each coil is brought out of the machine and connected to one wire of the line. The other ends of the coils are connected together, forming, it may be, a star." The transformers in this system must also contain three separate coils and a star for both primary and secondary. The voltage drop between any two of the three wires of this system is always the same as that between any other two. Star

Generator Star

Motor 

FIG. 202. 

THREE-PHASE SYSTEM 

Any two of these wires may be used as the source of a single phase current. Single phase currents also are sometimes carried on three wires, one wire coming from the middle of the secondary coil in the transformer. In this case the voltage drop between an outside wire and the middle wire is but half that between the outside wires. This is quite commonly 110 and 220 volts. 

Alternating current motors. Direct current cannot be carried over such systems as we have described. As the current is steady there is no changing in position of the lines of magnetic flux, and hence no induced current in the secondary of transformers. Alternating currents can be used only with specially designed motors, or such currents may be converted into direct currents by rectifying devices. There are several distinct types of alternating current motors. These we shall describe very briefly.

Induction motors. The simplest induction motor is the three-phase type with "squirrel cage" armature. Let the three coils of the figure represent three electromagnets placed as radii equally spaced about a circle. Let one end of each coil be connected to a wire of a three-phase system, and let the other end go to a "star." The directions of the turns of the coils can be so arranged that as the current comes in on the wire (A) the inner end of the magnet to which it is attached is a north pole. The current returning through the other two coils

66 makes their inner ends south poles. As the currents change in the wires next (B) and then (C) are attached to magnets having their inner ends north poles. At times two inner poles are half north," that is the poles are C "north" but of half strength.

The same applies to south poles. Thus there is the effect B of north and south poles chasing each other about a circle. 

This is called a rotating mag- A netic field. These may also have six, nine, twelve, or more poles. Star

FIG. 203. 

SQUIRREL CAGE MOTOR 

The armature is placed in this field, and the rotating lines of flux induce currents in it. By Lenz's law we know that the fields about these currents tend to stop the motion. Hence forces act which cause the armature to rotate. 

The armature of such a motor has no electrical connection to the power line. The "squirrel cage" armature consists only of parallel copper bars across a core of laminated iron. There are no insulated wires, no commutator or slip rings, and no brushes.

Hence this type of motor is economical in that it seldom requires a visit to 

Courtesy General Electric Company.

FIG. 204.-ROTOR AND STATOR OF INDUCTION MOTOR 

the repair shop. The armature must revolve nearly as fast as the magnetic field, otherwise too much current is induced and the motor gets hot. By using six poles the speed of the motor is

but one revolution for two alternations of the current, nine poles but one revolution for three alternations, and so on. The great disadvantage of these motors is that their speed is fixed between very narrow limits, depending on the load. They are much used where three-phase power is available. Their power range is from one-half to two hundred horse power. The larger types require coils on the armature which are connected to variable resistances or rheostats. These resistances must be "in" when the motor is started, otherwise there would be too much current taken from the line. They may also be used to regulate the speed somewhat. These motors are called "wound rotor induction motors." 

Single-phase induction motors. These motors are used for such light work as the operation of washing-machines, vacuum- 

FIG. 205.- A THREE-PHASE TOY MOTOR 

When a three-phase current is sent into this machine the copper "squirrel cage' revolves. cleaners, and other appliances where three-phase power is not installed. The principle of their action is exactly the same as that of the motors described above. There being but two supply wires and but a single phase current special devices are necessary to secure a rotating magnetic field.

This is done by so making the coils that induction holds the current back more in some coils than in others, thus producing currents out of phase with each other and a rotating magnetic field. 

Toy motors. The small motors used on toys, dentists' drills, barbers' clippers, etc., will run on either direct or alternating current. They are series wound direct current motors. 

When alternating currents are used the direction of the current in the field changes at the same time as it does in the armature, and the motor continues to turn in the same direction. There is an enormous amount of self-induction in the field and much

If the motor is very small this heating due to eddy currents. heat can radiate away, but if the motor is larger it soon becomes too hot to use. Motors of this type have been built large enough to run street cars by employing an alternating current of 25 instead of the usual 60 cycles per second, but with no great success. Notice that the fewer the cycles the less the induction. 

Repulsion motors. This single-phase motor has proved successful for many purposes. The principle of its action is 

​Repulsion Motor 

Polarity of one magnet

N S Wom Polarity of side of coil facing the magnet 

FIG. 206. 

REPULSION MOTOR 

The repulsion exceeds the attraction because the current in the coil on the armature lags as in the Elihu Thompson Experiment. illustrated by the diagram. The armature contains a number of loops of wire, the ends of each loop being attached to diametrically opposite segments of the commutator. The ends of one loop only are connected by two brushes and a short wire. 

When the brushes are in place the only loop in which a current can flow is that one making a certain angle with the perpendicular. 

As the magnetic field moves out from the poles and cuts the wires of the armature, a current flows in this loop. This induced current causes the armature to revolve. The direction

in which this motor turns is determined by the direction in which the line joining the brushes cuts the perpendicular. The speed of the motor can be regulated to a certain extent by adjusting the angle at which these lines cross. This is done by shifting the brushes.

The theory of this motor is not so simple. If we draw a sine curve representing the current supplied to this motor we shall notice that the magnetic field between the poles extends in one direction when this curve is above the x-axis and in the opposite direction when it is below this line. We shall also notice that the cutting of the loop by the lines of flux is always in such a direction that the current induced in the loop flows in one direction from the time the original current is at its lowest negative to its highest positive values, and in the opposite direction when the current is from its highest positive to lowest negative values. The effect would be to push the loop one way half the time and to pull it the other way half the time. It would vibrate back and forth with the alternations of the current instead of turning about the axis of the armature. The reason this does not occur is because the current in the armature, due to self-induction, lags behind the 90° lead which it would otherwise have. The time during which the face of the loop is repelled from the pole in front of it and attracted to the other High resistance Line um Magnet Load

FIG. 207. DIAGRAM OF ALTERNATING-CURRENT WATTMETER 

pole exceeds the time in which this face of the loop is attracted to the pole in front of it; and the armature revolves. The direction it turns depends on the direction in which it is already started, that is, on the angle of the line joining the brushes.

The a.c. watt-hour meter.-This simple instrument is in nearly every home using electric power. The principle of its action is very similar to that of the repulsion motor. A light aluminum

disk is mounted so as to turn freely about a vertical axis. 

The current to be measured passes through two coils of few turns of heavy wire, their poles being near the face of the disk on the lower side. The voltage used is proportional to the current passing through a coil of many turns of fine wire, placed with its pole near the face of the disk on the upper side and between the two ampere coils. The great self-induction in this coil causes the current in it to lag considerably behind that in the other coils. The flux moving in and out from these three coils causes eddy currents in the aluminum disk. The reactions between the mag- 

FIG. 208. 

WILOWATT HOURS 

WATT-HOUR METER 

netic fields of these various currents cause the disk to rotate. Permanent magnets are placed with their poles close to the disk. The effect of their fields, by Lenz's law, is to prevent rotation. These use up the energy imparted by the coils. 

The speed of rotation is proportional to the power supplied by the electromagnets. This varies as the product of the currents in the ampere and the voltage coils. Hence the speed is proportional to the watts of power used. The longer

FIG. 209.--DIAGRAM OF WATTMETER time the disk turns the more the

clockwork attached to it registers. This then indicates the energy consumed in kilowatt hours. 

The synchronous motor. If two alternating current generators are connected, one will furnish current to the other which will then run as a motor. But the armature of the motor must first be brought up to the same speed as the armature of the generator. This is a synchronous motor. "Synchronous " means "keeping in time with."

Suppose a coil on the armature of the motor is attracted to a certain north pole of the field. As soon as this coil is drawn up to this north pole the current reverses in it and it is attracted to the next pole of the field, a south pole. The direction of the current in the coils of the armature must change as the coils pass the poles of the field. In the generator it changes as the coils pass the mid-points between the poles of the field. Hence, to operate, the synchronous motor must make exactly the same number of revolutions per minute as the armature of the generator furnishing the power, or some exact multiple of this depending on the number of poles in the two machines.

Synchronous motors are valuable because they cause the current to lead the voltage. As most other electrical machines cause the current to lag behind the voltage, the insertion of a number of synchronous motors in a power line tends to increase 

FIG. 210. 

ARMATURE DIAGRAM OF ROTOR AND STATOR 

the power factor. Because of this they are much used in certain kinds of engineering projects. Synchronous motors have two disadvantages.

The field must be excited by a direct current and the armature must be brought up to synchronous speed with the generator by some special device. This may be a separate smaller motor or it may be special starting coils on the armature. In spite of these disadvantages, large numbers of these motors are in use, especially where much power is used. A smaller

type is also quite common. 

These have starting coils and the motor then starts as a repulsion motor, but later runs as a synchronous motor. 

Revolving fields. Commercial synchronous motors have a revolving field, the "rotor," and a stationary armature, the "stator" This arrangement is common with many types of motors and generators. There is no change in the electrical principles, only the mechanical design is altered. The magnetic forces acting between armature and field will cause to revolve whichever one is free to rotate. 

Large generators almost always have revolving fields. There are several reasons for this, some purely mechanical. As the amount of power required to excite the field is much less than that supplied by the armature, it is evident that the revolving field. type of machine will have much less trouble with the commutator and brushes. 

Where high voltages are generated, as is the case in large power plants, this becomes an important consideration. Again, large currents passing through an armature generate much heat.

Courtesy General Electric Company. 

FIG. 211. REVOLVING FIELD 

Iron core for revolving field of a 1,200 volt generator.

When the armature is the stator it encircles the rotor and there is a better opportunity for keeping it cool, since there

is more room for ventilating passages, and the insulation can be made more effective.

RECTIFYING DEVICES 

There are many purposes for which alternating currents cannot be used, such as the charging of storage batteries, electroplating, and electrochemical processes. Alternating currents are changed to direct currents by various rectifying devices. In general, these may be classed as mechanical, chemical, or as depending on the action of electrons in vacuum tubes.

Mechanical devices for rectification. The most common of these is the motor-generator, in which an alternating current motor is used to drive a direct current generator. This arrangement needs no further comment. The synchronous converter is used to change large quantities of alternating current power to direct current. For example, street cars usually use direct current. This may be brought to the city from a great distance as alternating current. It is then changed to direct current by using synchronous converters. These are synchronous motors which have commutators on one end of their armatures from which direct current is taken. 

For very small direct currents various types of "vibrators " are used. In this device the alternating current is sent through electromagnets which operate a vibrator. This is connected to contacts in such a way that the points in contact change about as the current reverses its direction, and a pulsating direct current is obtained. 

Electrolytic rectifiers. - We shall describe but one of these. 

If a plate of aluminum and one of carbon are placed in a strong solution of baking soda current will pass through from plate to plate in but one direction. This action is due to the formation of a compound on the aluminum. Iron, lead, or other metals may be used instead of carbon. These rectifiers can be easily and cheaply made. 

Mercury arc rectifiers. 

Here iron electrodes are placed in

ionized mercury vapor in contact with liquid mercury. 

Electrons readily pass from the mercury to the mercury vapor and from the vapor to the iron, but do not pass from the iron to the mercury vapor. Hence this apparatus can be used to rectify alternating currents. The mercury is contained in an exhausted glass tube, and certain accessories are necessary to produce the mercury arc and to prevent its dying out at the reversal of the current. 

Vacuum tube rectification. - Edison placed a metal plate in a vacuum with a hot filament, such as is used in incandescent lamps, and found that an electric current can be made to pass from the plate to the filament but not from the filament to the plate. This is now known to be due to the fact that the positive plate can draw electrons from the hot filament, but the positive filament can not draw electrons from the cold plate. This is called the "Edison effect." 

As current can pass from plate to filament but not in the reverse direction it is seen at once that such vacuum tubes can be used to rectify alternating currents. They are generally used for very small currents at high voltages. A socalled "soft tube contains some gas, a "hard" tube is as nearly a perfect vacuum as is possible. 

HIGH FREQUENCY CURRENTS 

The ordinary vacuum tube of the radio contains a "grid " or metal screen between the filament and the plate. A slight positive potential on the grid greatly increases the flow of electrons. A slight negative potential prevents this flow. Very slight changes of potential on the grid produce considerable changes in the plate current. tube amplification as used in the radio. The plate cur- 

FIG. 212. AN OSCILLATION CIRCUIT 

This is the basis of vacuum

rent may pass through an inductance coupled to one attached to the grid. A variation of grid potential produces a variation of plate current which in turn produces another change in the grid potential, and so on. This gives a pulsating current of high frequency. It is not difficult to produce two million cycles per second. These currents are used in radio telegraphy. Inductance causes them to pass on the surface of conductors only. This is called the "skin effect." Resistance of wires to such currents depends on the amount of surface per unit of length, and not on the area of cross section.

The Tesla coil. This instrument is designed to give a high frequency current at extremely high voltage. It is made of two transformers, a condenser, and a rotary spark gap.

Rotary spark gap. 

20,000 V 110 V AAAAAA 

Million Volts

FIG. 213. 

TESLA COIL 

Its action depends on the oscillatory nature of the condenser discharge as described on page 299. A transformer is used to step up an ordinary 110 volt, 60 cycle current to say 20,000 volts. This is attached to the condenser which therefore is loaded to this voltage 120 times each second. During those instants when the voltage is high the condenser discharges through the primary of the Tesla coil and a spark gap. This primary can consist of only a few turns of heavy wire as the current passing through it oscillates at high frequency. This current produces a magnetic field which also oscillates very rapidly. The lines of flux then cut the secondary so rapidly as to induce potentials of hundreds of thousands or perhaps millions of volts. This high voltage is shown by the large spark, which may be one, two, or more meters long. High frequency currents, sufficiently large to light several incandescent lamps may be

taken through the human body without harm resulting. Due to the "skin effect," the electricity remains on the surface of the body and does not reach the nerves. The Tesla coil is not a safe plaything, however, since the charged condenser and all wires connected to it are very dangerous. 

The practical use of high frequency currents comes from the fact that they send out into space Hertzian or wireless waves. 66 

The telephone. A complete description of a modern telephone system would require too much space to take up here. There must be a means of making a connection with the number wanted and of disconnecting the two numbers when the conversation has ended. These things are all accomplished by means of ingenious devices based on the fundamental principles of electricity and magnetism. Here we will describe only the transmitter and the receiver. The first consists of a diaphragm backed by granules of carbon. 

A direct current passes through these granules and the Diaphragm Carbon 

Granules 

Diaphragm Coils

Mognet 

FIG. 214.-A TELEPHONE TRANSMITTER FIG. 215. -A TELEPHONE RECEIVER 

primary of a transformer. When one speaks into the transmitter the vibrations of the diaphragm alternately squeeze and loosen the carbon granules. This causes the resistance of the circuit to vary. The changing resistance produces a wave in the electric current of the same form as that of the sound which entered the receiver. This fluctuating current induces a current of higher potential and similar wave form in the secondary of the transformer. This current passes through the receiver at the other end of the line.

The receiver consists of an electromagnet of many turns of fine wire placed behind a steel diaphragm or near a bit of steel attached to a diaphragm by means of levers. The varying current causes a fluctuation of the magnetic field which exerts a varying force on the diaphragm. The motions of the diaphragm follow the wave form of the current, and it sends out a sound wave similar to the one which entered the transmitter. When properly adjusted the reproduction is so accurate that one can recognize the voice of an acquaintance. The principle upon which the telephone works is simple enough. The great marvel is that it operates so successfully.

CHAPTER VII SOUND SECTION 27. THE NATURE OF SOUND 

We use the word "sound" with two quite different meanings. It may mean that type of sensation which is carried to the brain by the auditory nerve, or it may mean that motion of the air or other medium which produces that sensation. Thus, the louder of two sounds may be the one which produces the more distinct sensation or, on the other hand, it may be the one which carries the more energy to the ear drum per unit of time, depending upon what we mean by "sound." As the ear perceives sounds of certain pitches more readily than it does those of others, it is evident that the louder sound, as defined in one way, may not be the louder when the other definition is intended.

In physics, the second of the above. definitions is taken. Sound is a wave motion in the air, or other material medium, caused by the vibrations of some body. One can easily verify this definition. Every sound can be traced to some vibrating body as its source. It may be a vibrating solid, such as a violin string or a reed of a harmonica; it may be a vibrating column of air as in a flute or organ pipe; the sound of the bell be- 

FIG. 216. 

As the air is exhausted comes fainter and fainter. Sound cannot pass through a vacuum. or it may be a combination of these, as is the case with a clarinet and the human voice. Sound is always carried by a material medium. 

In general, sounds come to the ear through the air. Fish 319

hear through the water. We may also hear sounds which are carried through a length of steel rail or other elastic solid. Sound cannot pass through a vacuum.

The vibrating body produces alternate compressions and rarefactions of the medium surrounding it. These travel outward as waves and constitute sound. The speed of sound varies slightly with the temperature of the medium. 

The speed of sound in air at 0° C. is about 331.3 meters or 

1,090 feet per second, and the increase is 61 centimeters or about 2 feet per second for each degree rise in temperature. 

In hydrogen the speed of sound is four times as great as in air, but for most gases it is about the same as in air. 

Sound travels faster in liquids than in gases, and faster in solids than in liquids. The speed of sound in water is about 1,400 and in iron and glass about 5,000 meters per second. We must now learn something of the motion of vibrating bodies, as this determines the character of the sounds which they produce. 

Simple harmonic motion. The figure represents a circular track on the west side of a wall. It is about sunset and, as a boy runs around the track at a uniform speed, his shadow moves back and forth along the wall at a varying speed. The shadow stops momentarily at the end points, x and y, and moves the fastest at the center point, 2. The acceleration is sometimes positive and sometimes negative, and it can be shown by mathe- 

FIG. 217. 

ILLUSTRATION OF SIMPLE HARMONIC MOTION 

matics that the acceleration is always directly proportional to the distance the shadow is from the center point z. At x and y, the end points, the acceleration is greatest and it is

SOUND 

321 zero at the middle point z. Any motion of this type is called "simple harmonic."

A weight bobbing up and down at the end of a spiral spring furnishes a good example of simple harmonic motion. 

The vibrations of a rod of wood or metal which has one end clamped in a vise furnish another example. In these cases the number of vibrations per second depends only on the "strength" of the spring and the amount of mass moving. (Review the experiment on the determination of mass by having it bob up and down when attached to a spring, page 37.) The time required for one vibration is the same no matter whether the space over which the mass moves is long or short. This is important as otherwise vibrating strings, reeds, and other materials in vibration would not continue to give out the same note. If the distance through which a sound producing material vibrates is increased, as may be done by striking a stretched string harder, the result is a louder sound, but the tone is the same. This distance through which the body. vibrates is called the amplitude of vibration. If tion is the same no matter whether the amplithe string or the clamped

FIG. 218. -VIBRATIONS IN SIMPLE HARMONIC MOTION 

By applying Hooke's law and Newton's second law of motion we see that the period of vibratude is large or small. rod are shortened, the body vibrates more rapidly and there is a change in the sound which we call a rise in pitch. 

Pitch is determined solely by the number of vibrations per second and not by the amplitude of these vibrations. 

Amplitude of vibration influences the loudness or intensity of a sound. There are also other conditions which affect the intensity.

The human ear can hear sounds when the vibrations are between 20 and 40,000 per second, the exact range varying with individuals. We cannot hear sounds when the vi-

bration frequencies are outside of these limits. It is believed that certain animals, and insects especially, make use of sounds which are inaudible to us. As we grow older we gradually lose the power of hearing the highest notes. The notes used in music extend only between 40 and 4,000 vibrations per second.

Laws of vibrating strings. - Experiment verifies these three laws of vibrating strings: With strings of the same mass per unit of length and stretched with the same tension, the number of vibrations per second varies inversely as the length. With strings of the same mass per unit of length and of the same length, the number of vibrations per second varies 

FIG. 219.-VIBRATING STRINGS 

A photograph of a stretched string vibrating in three parts and thus giving out the second overtone. directly as the square root of the tension. With strings of the same tension and of the same length, the number of vibrations per second varies inversely as the square root of the mass per unit of length. If the strings are of the same material this last law may be stated as "inversely as the diameter." 

Overtones. If we place a finger of one hand lightly on a stretched string at its center, and pluck the string at a point one-fourth the length of the string from either end with the other hand, quickly removing the finger at the same time, the string may be caused to vibrate in two parts. Those points of a vibrating string which divide it into shorter lengths, and where there is comparatively little motion are called "nodes." The vibrations are twice as rapid as they are when the string is plucked at its center, and the note

SOUND 

323 given out is exactly one octave higher. Similarly the string can be caused to vibrate in three, four, five, etc. equal parts. 

Care must be taken that the finger divides the string so that the shorter length is an even fraction of the whole string. 

These higher notes are called overtones. The octave is the first overtone. The note vibrating three times as fast is the second overtone; that one vibrating four times as fast is the third overtone; and so on. The original note is called the fundamental. If the note C is given, then C' is the first overtone and G' the second, C" the third, E" the fourth and G" the fifth. A string may vibrate not only as a whole but at the same time it may vibrate in halves giving the first overtone, in thirds giving the second overtone, and so on. 

If we first strike any of the overtones of middle C on a piano, and then immediately strike this note itself, we can hear the overtones in the note together with the fundamental. When a body is struck other higher rates of vibration may occur besides the true overtones. Thus part of the body may vibrate three times while another part vibrates four times, and so on.

That which we hear in a sound and which is due to these higher rates of vibrations is called its quality. In general the pitch is determined by the frequency of vibration of the fundamental note. In any case it is the loudest component of the sound.

A musical note has a definite intensity, pitch and quality. 

The quality is determined by the number of and the relative intensities of the overtones. When a string is struck in the middle the overtones of odd number are missing and the sound is "hollow." If all the overtones up to the fifth or sixth are present, the tone is richer and more musical than if only the fundamental tone is heard. If the fundamental is strong as compared to the overtones, the sound is described as "full"; if it is weak, the note is said to be "empty." 

A piano wire gives a full note when struck with a soft hammer, an empty note if the hammer is hard. Notes produced by vibrating air columns, as organ pipes, clarinets, and the

Thus two notes of the same human voice, also give out overtones. These also determine the quality of the tone. intensity and of the same pitch, when made by a violin and a cornet respectively, do not sound alike as the quality is different. The variations in quality are almost countless. 

Differences in the qualities of the voice together with other irregularities of speech enable us to distinguish our friends and acquaintances. 

We can extend the term quality to other noises besides musical notes. With these the different vibration rates present may give a sound to which no definite pitch can be ascribed. This is true of many common noises.

The greater the distance above the horizontal axis the greater the condensation, the greater the distance below the line, the greater the rarefaction. 

Wave form. We can plot the distances a vibrating body has moved from its central position and the intervals of time. corresponding to its different positions. Every simple harmonic motion will give a sine curve. In fact, a sine curve is the graph of such a motion. We can thus draw a separate curve for each overtone as well as the curve for the fundamental. These can then be combined into a single curve. 

It will have a form which recurs again and again, but it is not a sine curve. It is seen at once that the quality of the note determines the shape of this curve. Here we show the graphs of a fundamental and three overtones and also the curve resulting from the combination of these.

The condensation is proportional to the distance of the curve from the x-axis. Sounds may be caused to produce their respective curves by the aid of certain mechanical devices.

With the manometric flame apparatus, sound enters one side of a chamber which is divided into two parts by an elastic diaphragm. Illuminating gas flows through the other side to a small, burning jet. The sound waves cause the diaphragm to vibrate. This varies the pressure on the gas and consequently causes variations in the brightness of the flame. 

When this is viewed in a rotating mirror, the flame is drawn out into a band which shows these variations. Another method is to attach a light mirror to a diaphragm and to reflect a beam of light from this to a moving photographic film. The sound waves move the diaphragm and the mirror and the beam of light traces the characteristic curve on the photographic film. There is another arrangement, also using a rotating mirror, by which the beam of light from a small

mirror on a diaphragm traces the curve directly on a screen. 

With this the audience can at once see the form of wave Rotating Mirror Gas curve of any sound impressed on the diaphragm. The three characteristics of a sound. - These are its intensity, its pitch and its 

Diaphragm quality. The intensity or loudness of a sound is influenced by other things besides the amplitude of the vibrations of the source. 

The area of the vi brating surface has much to do with the intensity of the re- 

FIG. 222.- MANOMETRIC FLAMES 

sulting sound. Hence, the use of sounding boards, as in pianos. The larger surface vibrating transfers more energy per second to the surrounding medium. If a tuning fork is struck and the handle pressed against the table, the sound is much louder, but it does not last nearly as long as when the fork is held in the hand. The energy of the fork, being more rapidly carried away, is soon used up. The vibrations of the table are said to be "forced." Their rate is determined by the vibrating fork. The natural rate of vibration of the table top may be quite different. 

The loudness of a sound is also influenced by the nature of the medium through which it passes. It is much fainter in hydrogen, and much louder in water and in elastic solids than it is in air. The intensity of a sound carried to the outer ear through an iron rail, when the latter is struck some distance away, is much greater than when the same sound is carried the same distance entirely through the air. The

SOUND 

327 grinding of the wheels of an approaching train can be heard when it is very far away if the ear is held close to the rails of the track.

The intensity of a sound also depends on the distance from the source. This is, very roughly, inversely proportional to the square of the distance. No exact rule can be given as the intensity is influenced by the wind and by reflection from the ground and other surfaces.

An important relation. Let v be the velocity of sound. 

Then in one second sound waves extend outward this distance from the source. Each wave is due to one vibration of the source. Hence there will be as many waves in the distance v as there are vibrations per second. Let this number be n and the length of one wave λ (lambda). It is evident at once that v = ηλ. 

Doppler's principle. If a person moves towards the source of a sound the waves reach his ear more rapidly than if he were standing still, and hence the pitch appears higher. 

If he moves from the source the pitch appears lower. The same effect is noticed if one stands still while the source of sound moves. If we stand on the street as a fire truck approaches the pitch of the bell sounds higher until the vehicle is abreast of us and then drops as it moves away from us. 

One can sometimes notice the same thing with the whistle of a moving locomotive. This is called the 

Doppler effect.

Sympathetic vibrations. - Under certain circumstances the sound proceeding from one body may cause another to vibrate. This is called sympathetic vibration. We can illustrate this as follows. Suppose we make a pendulum by tying a heavy weight to a rope. If we try to draw the weight aside quickly by means of a thread, the latter will break. 

But if we continue to give slight pulls on the thread in time with the swing of the pendulum, it will soon be moving through a wide arc. In the same way the slight pressure of sound waves will produce motion in a body if the natural period of the latter is the same as that of the waves. This phenomenon also occurs when the frequency of one body is an even multiple of the other. If we hold one key of a piano down and then strike the octave below, upon releasing the second key, the wire of the first can be heard vibrating. 

Transverse and longitudinal vibrations. - Waves may be caused by the particles of the medium moving back and forth in a direction perpendicular to that in which the wave is going, or they may move back and forth in the same direction as that which the wave travels. The first are called transverse (crosswise) vibrations or waves, and the second are longitudinal (lengthwise) waves. Sound waves are longitudinal, water waves are transverse. A good example of transverse vibrations are the waves in a field of grain caused by the wind. Each stalk of wheat rises and falls as the waves pass on, and one gets the impression that the whole field of wheat is moving toward the fence, though he knows that each stalk is firmly rooted in place. 

Interference. Strange to say, under proper conditions, two sounds may produce comparative silence. This is when the compressions of one sound coincide with the rarefactions of the other, their amplitudes being equal. This will happen if the sound from a tuning fork is caused to branch and follow along two tubes, one one-half a wave longer than the other. 

When the two branches of the sound are now brought together again comparative silence results. This is called

SOUND 

329 interference. The two waves sent out by the two prongs of a tuning fork interfere with each other in certain directions. 

If the vibrating fork is revolved about its handle as an axis, the change in sound intensity as the waves strike the ear can be detected easily. of 

Beats. When the fre quency of vibration one body is almost but not quite the same as that of another, the waves alternately assist and work against each other. This causes a rising and falling of sound intensity called beats. The number of beats per second is equal to the difference between the frequencies of the vibrations. Thus, if one string of a sonometer makes 264 and another 274 vibrations per second, there will be ten beats per second. If the number of beats per second is within certain limits the effect is very unpleasant and we call it discord. About thirty beats per second pro-

Notice that the resulting sound is louder at A and B and is fainter at X and Y. duces the most unpleasant discord. When two notes are struck, discord may result from their respective overtones. 

The overtones of strings and pipes are called harmonics. 

They are even multiples of the fundamental frequency and

are more apt to produce harmony than are the notes of bells for instance, which contain overtones which are not harmonics. Chimes of bells are played by striking one at a time. These give such a variety of overtones that it is almost impossible to strike two bells at the same time without producing discord.

Resonance. 

The air enclosed in a vessel may vibrate in sympathy with a sound and thus reinforce it. This is called resonance. It may be illustrated as follows. Let a tube about two inches in diameter and two or three feet long and open at both ends be held vertically in water. 

Hold a vibrating tuning fork over the upper end and raise and lower the tube. When the column of air in the tube has a certain length it will resonate to the note of the fork and a louder and richer tone will be heard.

This length is less when a fork of higher pitch is used. This is a "closed" pipe as the water acts as a bottom of the pipe. A closed pipe resonates when its length is of the wave length of the fork. An "open pipe must have one-half the length of the sound wave, or some multiple of this, to resonate to the tone of the fork. 

FIG. 225. ILLUSTRATING RESONANCE 

When the air column in the tube has the proper length it resonates to the pitch of the fork. 99 When the vibrating fork prong is at A it starts a compression wave down the tube. In a closed tube this wave is reflected from the bottom of the tube and if it gets back to the top at the time the prong arrives at C, this compression will move upward and reinforce the one formed by the prong as it moves

SOUND 

331 A- from C to A. These conditions exist whenever the air in the tube vibrates in sympathy with the fork. The wave travels down and up in the tube during one-half a vibration. Hence the tube is the length of the sound wave, which is the distance the sound travels during a whole vibration. With a tube open at both ends the compression expands into the open air when it reaches the bottom, and returns up the tube as a rarefaction. A rarefaction going up must be followed by B a condensation coming down. Hence, for resonance, the rarefaction must reach the top of the tube at the same instant that the prong gets back to A. The compression goes down and the rarefaction up during one vibration. Hence an open tube which reinforces a sound wave must have a length equal to one-half the length of the wave. As with strings, pipes also may have nodes. These are formed by interference at intervals of half wave lengths as waves meet each other, passing in opposite directions. 

FIG. 226. 

NANCE RESO- 

The closed end of a pipe, where the air has the least motion but undergoes the greatest compression and rarefaction, is a node. The open end is a loop, or point of greatest movement. In an open pipe the node is at the center and the ends are loops. 

A closed tube of a wave in length will resonate also. 

The upper of the tube may be considered as an open tube of the length of the wave, and the bottom of the tube is a closed tube of a wave length. This reasoning also applies to tubes,, etc., times the length of the sound wave. In each case the space divides into a number of open tubes and one closed tube at the bottom just half the length of the others. The middle points of these imaginary open tubes are nodes. Here the air is comparatively quiet due to interference of the ascending and the descending waves. Also,

an open tube can be divided into two, three, four, five, etc., imaginary open tubes. It will resonate when it is 1, 1, 1, 1, etc., times the length of the sound waves.

Overtones in pipes. From the above we see that if a pipe is of proper length to resonate to a note, it will also resonate to certain of the overtones of that note. An open pipe can resonate to all the harmonics of its fundamental 44 43 42 47 ~/7 2/7 217 45 ~15 دالله

Y3 2/3 

FIG. 227. OPEN AND CLOSED ORGAN PIPES 

note. For the first harmonic each half of the pipe acts as an open pipe of one-half a wave length. For the second harmonic each third of the pipe acts as an open pipe, and so on. But with a closed pipe only the even harmonics, of wave lengths,,, etc., of the fundamental wave length will appear. The bottom of the pipe and the top of it act as closed and open pipes respectively to waves the length of the fundamental note of the pipe. In an organ pipe the vibration is started at one end of the pipe by a puff of air. The other end of the pipe may be either open or

SOUND closed. The pipe will send out the fundamental note to which it resonates. If it is an open pipe both even and odd harmonics will be present. With a closed pipe only the even harmonics will appear. The even harmonics are formed by the odd integers, 3, 5, etc., and have wave lengths represented by the fractions,, etc. of the wave length of the fundamental tone. 

Wind instruments. The pitch and quality of the notes. of horns, flutes, clarinets, as well as those of whistles and organ pipes, are determined by the size and shape of the air chambers which they contain, and by the manner in which the puffs of air are formed. The air chamber of the bugle is fixed, but different notes can be produced by changing the way in which the lips are held and by varying the force of the breath. The keys of a clarinet determine the length of the air column, and the note is also influenced by the way the reed is held in the mouth. The cornet can play more notes than the bugle because the length of the air column may be varied by using the keys, but with any given arrangement of the keys several notes may be formed. 

The note given by a flute depends on the length of the air column and the force with which one blows into the instrument. The notes of stringed instruments are determined by the laws of vibrating strings, which have been given. 

The voice. The sounds of the human voice are formed by vibrating membranes, the vocal chords, influenced much by the air cavities of the throat and head. When one holds something in his mouth or closes his nostrils, the quality of his voice changes decidedly. It is through the overtones of the voice as well as by the little irregularities in handling the breath, tongue, and lips, that one distinguishes the voice of one person from that of another. 

The important thing in learning to sing is to hold throat, tongue, and palate so as to give a proper resonance. It is for this, and not the mere learning of tunes, that the aspiring vocal student seeks the help of a competent teacher.

Helmholtz's resonators. The German scientist, Hermann von Helmholtz, first showed that the quality of a sound is determined by the number and relative intensity of its overtones. 

He used a collection of hollow metallic spheres. Each had an opening to admit the sound and a hollow nipple to insert in the ear.

Each sphere resonated to one note. By their use he analyzed sounds and determined just which overtones were present and what were their relative intensities. Then by sounding simultaneously the proper tuning forks, he could imitate the quality of various musical instruments, and even produce the vowel sounds. 

Echoes. The reflection of sound by surfaces, thus forming echoes, is familiar to every one. In auditoriums, the sound returning from the walls may so interfere with that coming directly from the speaker as to make hearing in certain areas very difficult. Some rooms are much worse in this respect than others. Much depends on the shape of the room, the materials of the ceiling, walls, and floors, and the position of balconies. 

This is an important matter for architects to consider. 

SECTION 28. MUSICAL SCALES 

The musical scale. Our music is based on the fact that the human ear considers as pleasing those combinations of notes whose vibration frequencies are proportional to the numbers 4, 5, 6, and 8. Any three notes which, in this way, correspond to the numbers 4, 5, and 6, are called a major triad. With the octave (double) of the lowest note they form a major chord. Minor triads correspond to the numbers 10, 12, and 15. The major diatonic scale is built of three major triads: the tonic, do-mi-sol; the dominant, sol-ti-re; and the subdominant, fa-la-do. These are connected together according to the following scheme. Any number of vibrations we please may be ascribed to some one note, and then all the others follow. mi fa sol la ti do re do re tonic dominant subdominant

SOUND 

335 Notice that the tonic and dominant are connected by having the highest note of one the same as the lowest note of the other. Also the highest note of the subdominant is the same as the octave of the first note of the tonic scale. 

We can have any note we please for do. Then mi has and sol times as many vibrations per second. Ti is in turn § and re is times that of sol. In the second octave each note has double the frequency of the corresponding note in the octave preceding. La has and fa& as many vibrations per second

Sharps and flats. In the key of C, do starts on C and sol falls on G. If C is taken as 256 vibrations per second, G will be 384. We can work out the three major triads, first with do on C and then with do on G, using the same G as before. The result is as follows.

We have the same vibration numbers as before except for A and F, which are higher. In the key of G both A and F sharp." If we were to work out all the keys in this way, starting do on each letter, we would get some six dozen sharps and flats. It is impossible to have so many keys on any musical instrument. However, none of the numbers will be far off if we divide the octave into twelve even steps. This may be done if we make each number the twelfth root of two, 1.05946, times the one preceding it. It is evident that, when we have performed this multiplication twelve times, we double the original number and begin on a new octave. (In the key of G, in music, only F is made sharp. The slightly higher pitch of A is neglected.) CDE

* Musicians recently voted to raise the pitch to 440 vibrations for A.

All instruments with fixed keys, as the piano, cornet, and guitar, must use the even-tempered scale. Hence their music is not as true as that of instruments which can follow the ratios 4, 5, and 6 exactly. Such instruments have no fixed keys. Examples of these are the violin, trombone, and the voice. With these a skilled musician uses a different note for F sharp and G flat, for instance. On a piano he would be compelled to strike the same key for these notes. 

This is why the music of a symphony orchestra is superior to that of a piano or a brass band. But one needs to have a trained and sensitive ear to appreciate it. PROBLEMS 1. The temperature is 18° C. What is the length of a closed pipe that resonates to the G fork?

CHAPTER VIII RADIATION SECTION 29. HISTORICAL OUTLINE CONCERNING RADIATION 

The most apparent form of radiant energy is the light by which we see.

It has long been known that white light, when passed through a glass prism, is broken into a spectrum of colors that run from violet to red. Newton showed that these colors could be again combined to produce white light by passing them through a second prism. This shows white light to be a combination of all colors.
