The ultra-violet radiations are the chief cause of 

FIG. 231.- ULTRA-VIOLET RADIATIONS 

Some remarkable cures have been effected by the use of ultra-violet rays. When it is impractical to use sunlight, as in cloudy weather, the doctor uses light, rich in the ultra-violet radiation, from a mercury arc in a quartz tube. The picture shows a machine designed for use in hospitals, and homes. sunburn. Much of the ultra-violet radiation is absorbed by the air. On high mountains it is very strong and causes severe sunburn, a fact to which those who climb mountain peaks may well testify. This effect is not caused by the light by which we see as may be determined by a number of observations and experiments. We know a certain amount of ultra-violet light is essential for health, especially for growing children.

Probably some of the healthful effects of living out-of-doors are due to this radiation. Ordinary glass is opaque to these rays, hence hospitals, schools, and like buildings are sometimes furnished with windows of a special glass that transmits a portion of them. 

The infra-red radiation is somewhat similar to the radiant heat that passes out from a fire or a hot body. We can make a distinction here however. Heat radiation from the sun passes readily through glass but that from warm objects does not. 

This fact is used to advantage in the greenhouse. Heat from the sun enters more readily than the warmth from the inside passes out.

RADIATION 

339 The highest ultra-violet radiations and the lowest infrared radiations are quite similar. It is generally believed that these radiations are ether waves, the difference between them being merely one of wave length, the ultra-violet being the shortest. The colors violet, indigo, blue, green, yellow, orange, and red are then but waves of gradually increasing length. In the form of the rainbow this spectrum of colors has been known to man since the earliest times. Today everyone is more or less familiar with other forms of radiant energy besides those mentioned above. The doctor uses X-rays to photograph teeth, bones, and other organs. The radio is operated by Hertzian waves sent out by the broadcasting station. We use watch faces that shine in the dark, due to radiations from the radium paint used on them.

LAMP

This lamp gives about the same ultra-violet waves as are found in sunlight. A comparatively low voltage is applied, the potential being lowered by means of a transformer. By the use of a special glass In 1865 James Clerk-Maxwell, one of England's greatest mathematical physicists, showed that there is a very definite relation between light and electricity and magnetism. He developed a theory that light consists of electric and magnetic lines of force vibrating in planes at right angles to each other. He succeeded in calculating the velocity of light from purely electrical measurements. This is the outstanding proof of the correctness of his theory. It is generally accepted today, and is called "Maxwell's Electromagnetic Theory of Light." 

We are reasonably sure that light is a wave motion of lines of electric and magnetic force, but we are not sure that these are waves in the ether just as water waves are a form of about the same waves are in the sunlight which has passed through the attransmitted as are found mosphere.

motion of water particles. Some experiments seem to indicate that this is the case, while others appear to contradict it. 

Maxwell also predicted the existence of radiations of longer waves than those of radiant heat. These were produced experimentally by the German physicist, Henrich Hertz, in 1888, who detected them passing out from the spark of a Leyden jar. Hertz obtained a second spark in 

FIG. 233. 

RADIATION OF SUN THROUGH LENS OF ICE 

Much of the infra-red radiation of the sun, radiant heat of short waves, passes through ice. The light spot on the floor is decidedly warm, though not hot enough to char wood another circuit when it was tuned by having the proper capacity and inductance. Later Marconi used these waves to develop wireless telegraphy, and now they are in everyday use in connection with the radio. The waves Hertz used were but a few feet in length. Wireless stations recently have used waves over seven miles long. By using special apparatus in which the electrical capacity and inductance are very small indeed, Hertzian waves have been formed

RADIATION 

341 that are shorter than the longest waves of radiant heat. 

There is no gap in the sequence of radiations of different lengths.

Before Maxwell had developed his theory many had become interested in the discharge of electricity through tubes of rarefied gases. 

The reader has probably noticed electric signs that are formed of long tubes of helium, neon, and other gases, and which glow brilliantly when electricity of high potential is passed through them. 

The first tubes of this nature were small affairs used to study the types of spectra given out by various gases and vapors. They were invented by the physicist Geissler who,

FIG. 234.-CATHODE RAYS DEFLECTED BY A 

MAGNET Notice that the three-finger rule holds, the right hand being used. Although using the principle of the motor we do not use the left hand because the moving charges are negative. as a boy, had learned the trade of glass-blower. Such tubes are still sometimes called Geissler tubes. This In 1859 Plücker discovered a stream of something passing from the cathode to the anode in exhausted tubes. stream can be caused to cast a shadow, to move paddle wheel affairs, and to produce fluorescence when striking screens. of zinc sulphide and certain other materials. (Fluorescent substances give out light when excited by certain agencies.) 

When a magnet is brought near these "cathode rays," they are deflected as though they were composed of negatively charged particles. The direction they then take follows the three-finger rule. When using this rule remember that a stream of negative particles moving from east to west is

deflected in the same direction as one of positive particles passing from west to east. We now know that these cathode particles are electrons.

One of the interested investigators of the discharge of electricity through rarefied gases was Sir William Crookes, who made many forms of tubes. They came to be known as 

Crookes' tubes.

The discovery of X-rays occurred in 1895. Prof. Wm. 

Röntgen of Munich was experimenting with a Crookes' tube enclosed in a box. He noticed, quite by accident, that some form of radiation came through the opaque walls of the box and affected a fluorescent screen nearby. He found that this radiation affected a photographic plate and he was able to take pictures of coins inside a leather purse, and the bones of his hand. Not knowing the nature of this radiation he called it X-rays because, in algebra, x is often the unknown quantity. We shall discuss them more fully later. 

The next year, 1896, the French physicist Becquerel found that ores of uranium sent out rays which affected a photographic plate after passing through opaque materials. For a while these were called "Uranium rays."

In 1898 Sir J. J. Thompson solved the mystery of the cathode rays. He showed that they are composed of particles of negative electricity, all of the same mass and possessing the same electric charge. Their velocity depends on the voltage used. With high potentials this may reach the enormous speed of 100,000 miles per second. This was the discovery of the electron. Its mass is 16 of that of the hydrogen atom.

The next year, 1899, Sir Ernest Rutherford, then at Montreal, showed that the Becquerel rays consist of three distinct types of radiations which he named the alpha, the beta, and the gamma rays. When passing through electric or magnetic fields the alpha and beta rays are deflected in opposite directions, while the gamma rays pass straight on unaffected. He found that alpha particles are stopped by a

RADIATION 

343 sheet of paper, the beta rays by a thickness of a few millimeters of lead, while some gamma rays can be detected through six inches of lead.

It was soon proved that alpha particles are positively charged atoms of helium, atoms that have lost two electrons. 

These fly out of the active materials with a speed approaching 20,000 miles a second, the swiftest moving atoms known.

The beta particles are electrons. Their speed is sometimes as great as nine-tenths the velocity of light, approaching 

186,000 miles a second. These are the swiftest particles known. The gamma rays are a radiation very similar to the X-rays. 

In 1900 Rutherford discovered the radium emanation or the gas niton. This gas is given off by active materials and does itself send out alpha, beta, and gamma rays, It lasts but a few days, being rapidly transformed to other substances. 

It is found in the waters of certain springs, a trace is detected in the air, and it is similar chemically to helium. 

In 1903 Madame Curie announced the discovery of radium. 

She had noticed that some ores of uranium are much more active than the pure uranium itself, and she determined to search for the substance that caused the more abundant radiation. The story of her efforts furnishes an outstanding example of the romance of science.

She worked in an old shed in the yard of a Paris school. For two years she boiled great iron pots of ore, sometimes containing forty pounds of materials, on an old stove, and then carefully separated the more active substances. The result of her work was the discovery of radium which is a million times more active than uranium. The percentage of radium in an ore is very small indeed. It requires five hundred tons of Colorado ore, and an equal weight of distilled water and chemicals, to produce one gram of radium salt, (radium bromide). So far about one-half pound of radium has been extracted, the cost being about $15,000,000. This makes it many thousands of times more valuable than gold.

In 1912 the German physicist Laue showed that the even spacing of atoms in crystals could be used to determine the nature of X-rays. The next year, 1913, Sir William Bragg and his son, W. L. Bragg, were able to separate a beam of 

X-rays into a spectrum and to calculate the lengths of the waves. They are in all ways similar to the other forms of radiant energy, except that the length of these waves is a thousand times shorter than that of visible light. It was then found that the gamma rays are even shorter waves than X-rays. Thus, after eighteen years the mystery of the X-rays was solved. 

In 1920 Prof. Millikan of California was able to extend the ultra-violet spectrum up until it overlapped the X-rays. 

Since 1913, when experiments were performed in Germany by sending up balloons, it has been suspected that there is another form of radiation coming to the earth from space. 

A series of experiments performed in the high mountains of California in 1925 by Prof. Millikan seem to confirm this suspicion. His electroscopes were discharged by what appeared to be a radiation, when sixty feet deep in the radium-free snow water of mountain lakes. If this were due to a radiation from the sky, then it could pass through six feet of lead without all being absorbed, and suggests to physicists that there are waves perhaps a thousand times shorter than gamma rays. Hence these rays are called the "penetrating radiation" or "cosmic rays." They do not come from the sun.

SECTION 30. THE NATURE OF RADIANT ENERGY 

We see 

Radiant energy readily passes through a vacuum. the sun and stars through the great distances of empty space. (The atmosphere extends upward but a few hundred miles at most.) Also we can see through a tube of glass from which all gas has been removed. It is generally believed that radiant energy consists of waves, but not waves of air or any other material medium as is the case with sound. As there must be something in which these waves can form, we call that something the ether. This is one reason for believing that there is an ether.

We shall now describe an interference experiment which seems to show that light is waves and which enables us to measure their lengths. This experiment cannot be explained satisfactorily by any other theory. We shall later describe a photoelectrical experiment which seems to indicate that light is not waves but particles moving with great speed. At the present time, when we try to reconcile these two experiments, we come to a great mystery, that is, how kinetic energy changes to radiant energy and vice versa. The true theory, when it is developed, will apply equally well to both experiments.

FIG. 235. THE CROOKES RADIO- 

METER 

One side of the vanes is silvered and the other is black. When radiation falls upon it the black sides absorb more heat than the silvered, thus becoming warmer. 

The air molecules then rebound from the heated side with from the other side more reaction than and the vanes revolve. It is necessary that the air be from the bulb. 

Interference. The fact that sound and water waves bend around corners while light does not was long held as an argument against the wave theory of light. We now believe that this apparent difference is due to the extreme smallness of light waves, and that when we consider the relative size of light waves as compared with other waves, they behave in much the same way. 

Let two pieces of plate glass be bound together with a piece of tissue paper or metal foil between them along one edge. Let some common salt be placed in the alcohol of a partially exhausted lamp. The lamp then gives the yellow flame of the metal sodium. The reflection of this flame, when seen in the plates, is crossed by alternate bands of yellow and black. With ordinary plate glass these lines are much curved but if the glass has been ground and polished until nearly plane, the lines are seen to run parallel to the paper strip. The thicker the paper the finer the lines, and with writing paper they are too fine to be seen. 

Light is reflected from the four surfaces of the glass, but

RADIATION 

347 we are concerned with only that from the back of the front plate and the front of the back plate. Referring to the figure, and supposing that this yellow light is composed of waves of uniform length, we see that at certain places, as at a₁, aз, and as, etc., the crest of the wave from the back surface fits in with the crest of the wave from the front, and the reflected light is made brighter. But at a2, a4, α, etc., the crest of one wave comes at the hollow of the other. 

Here the two waves neutralize each other and the result is darkness. 

This is called interference. a3 as 

92 94 96

FIG. 236. -INTERFERENCE 

Suppose the plates are 10 cm long, touching at the top and separated at the bottom by a piece of metal foil .006 cm in thickness. When using the sodium light, as one counts down the glass there will be about ten bright and ten dark strips to the centimeter. The divergence of the plates per centimeter is .006 ÷ 10 or .0006 cm. Then from one bright strip to the next, the divergence is .00006 cm. 

This, as is evident, is the length of one wave of the light. Blue and green light have shorter waves, orange and red longer. 

We say that light is made up of waves because otherwise we have no satisfactory explanation of this phenomenon. 

The interferometer. The method described above gives but a rough estimate of the length of light waves. One instrument that is used to measure them accurately is the interferometer. S is an accurately turned screw that moves back and forth in a metal base when the wheel w is turned. A is an upright plane mirror attached to the screw, and B is a similar mirror attached to the base. C is a mirror placed at the angle of 45° to the axis of the screw. It is so lightly silvered that

half of the monochromatic light from the source, x, is reflected to A and half passes on to B. The lens, L, is used to make the rays of light parallel. Light falling on A and B is reflected back to C where it forms interference bands in the form of light and dark concentric rings. When the eye is at E fringes or alternate Fringes

FIG. 237. THE INTERFEROMETER 

light and dark bands are seen on the nearer side of C. 

A piece of plate glass is placed at D so that both of the rays forming the fringes pass through the same thickness of glass. 

As the screw is turned the circles move. When the distance from A to C is changed one wave length each circle moves into that place occupied by one of those next to it. Wave lengths are determined by counting the moving bands as the distance AC is changed a known amount. By this method results can be secured which are quite accurate. The length of waves of the red light in the spectrum of the metal cadmium was measured by one observer and found to be .000064384702 cm and another observer found them to be .000064384700 cm, an accuracy of

RADIATION 

349 seven significant figures.

66 

Interference methods are used to measure small lengths accurately, such as the amount of give" of a wall when a man leans against it, or the bending of a heavy steel rod when a hand is laid upon it. 

Color is merely a matter of wave length. The longest waves of red light and the shortest of violet are .000077 and .000036 cm respectively. White is the effect on the eye of a mixture of all visible waves. Notice that the range of visible light is about one octave, the longest visible waves being about twice the length of the shortest. 

The speed of light. In 1675 Olaus Römer, a young

Danish astronomer, determined the velocity of light through the study of the eclipses of the moons of Jupiter. The

RÖMER'S DIAGRAM 

wwwwwww Satellite Jupiter instant these pass into the shadow as they move around the planet can be determined accurately, by observation and also by calculation. If the time of an eclipse is calculated from data obtained when Jupiter is nearest the earth, and the eclipse is observed when Jupiter is far from the earth, the observed time is later than the calculated one. But if the time of an eclipse when Jupiter is again nearest the earth is calculated, it is found to agree with the observed time. 

Römer reasoned that this effect is due to the additional time required for light to reach the earth from Jupiter when the planet is furthest away. He found it to take 16 min.

and 36 sec. for light to cross the orbit of the earth. Thus, the velocity of light is 186,000 miles per second. This is the same as 300,000 Km/sec. or 3 X 1010 cm/sec., and is the fastest speed known.

Today the velocity of light is most accurately determined by reflecting a beam of light from a revolving mirror to another mirror many miles away and back again to the revolving mirror. While the beam of light has traveled from the revolving mirror to the fixed mirror and back again, the 

FIG. 239. 

THE VELOCITY OF LIGHT 

A blast of compressed air revolves the octagonal mirror, M, at high speed. Light from a lamp, following the dotted lines, passes to the concave mirror on the distant mountain, thence to its focus and thence back to this mirror. It then returns to the first concave mirror, and finally is reflected from the face A into the telescope. The speed of the mirror is so adjusted that light passes to the distant mountain and returns in the same time that the face B moves over to the position occupied by A. 

The velocity of light can be found from the speed of rotation and the distance to the mountain. revolving mirror has turned through a known angle. Then, since the speed of the revolving mirror and the distance between the mirrors are known, the speed of light may be calculated easily. Using this method at Mt. Wilson, California, Prof. Michelson determined the velocity of light to be 299,796 Km/sec., the error being probably less than 

25 Km/sec. Today the velocity of light is used to determine the diameter of the earth's orbit, this being the most accurate method by which this measurement can be made. 

The velocity of light given above is the velocity in a vacuum. In air it is slightly less, and in some transparent media much less. In water it is but as much as in air.

The rectilinear propagation of light. If the medium is homogeneous, that is, if it is uniform throughout, light moves in straight lines. This fact is well known and one makes use of it when sighting a gun, for instance. This is because light waves are so very short. If we watch waves in water, we see that the smaller waves are stopped by an object and a calm place is behind the obstruction but the larger waves pass around. Sound acts similarly to the larger water waves, light to the small ripples.

The fact that light passes in straight lines accounts for shadows. The darkest part of a shadow from which all the light is cut off by the object is called the umbra. (Latin "umbra," a shadow.) That part from which only part of

Sun Moon Earth 

UMBRA AND PENUMBRA 

The dark mark is the path of the total eclipse on the earth. (Not drawn to scale.) the light is cut off is called penumbra. (Latin "paene,” almost.) When the moon comes between us and the sun we see a total eclipse if we are in the umbra of the moon, and a partial eclipse if we are in the penumbra. 

The light year.

The immense distance light travels in one year is called a light year and is a unit of length used by astronomers. Only four true stars are known to be closer than ten light years. 

The Pole star is nearly 50 light years distant. The beautiful Pleiades are 325 light years away. We see them by means of light that began its journey in the time of Shakespeare. 

The Milky Way, of which our sun and the neighboring stars form a part, is probably at least one-third of a million light years in diameter. The smaller cloud of Magellen, a star

cluster, is at the distance of 100,000 light years and is moving away from us at the rate of 100 miles/sec. It contains some 'half-million suns as bright as our sun, and a few ten thousand times as bright. The diameters of these latter stars are probably as much as one billion miles." 

Beyond the Milky Way are other "universes." The great nebula in Andromeda is one of these. Its distance, 930,000 light years, was announced by Dr. Hubble in 1926. Prof.

Shapley of Harvard announced the discovery of 103 of these nebulae within a sphere of 2,000,000 light years in diameter and at an average distance from us of 10,000,000 light years. 

It is estimated that there are some 2,000,000 of these nebulae about us and within the distance of 150,000,000 light years. 

Many of these contain upwards of a billion suns. We can but wonder if many of these millions of billions of suns have planets upon which intelligent beings live. We also may wonder what lies on beyond the reach of our most powerful telescopes. 

We can form but a very inadequate idea as to how great these distances are. If we represent the distance from the sun to the earth as one centimeter, the earth then being an invisible speck 1 mm in diameter and the sun 1 mm in diameter, then on this scale a million light years is about 400,000 miles. 

An important relation. Suppose a ray of light starts from the point A and goes the distance v in one second. 

Here v is the velocity of light. Suppose the body producing the waves makes one vibration for each wave, as is the case with sound, then the frequency or number of vibrations per second is equal to v divided by the length of one wave. It is customary to represent the wave length of light by the Greek letter lambda, X, and its frequency by nu, v. we have Thus

V = In the case of light v is very large, 3 × 1010 cm/sec., and 

A is very small, less than .00008 cm. This causes the frequency to be very, very large indeed. It would take a middle C tuning fork 5,000 years to make as many vibrations

RADIATION 

353 as a wave of yellow light does in one second. However, we are not yet certain that light waves are caused by something vibrating. 

Intensity of illumination.

Light spreads out uniformly in all directions from the source of illumination if the surrounding medium is everywhere the same. Drawing a number of concentric spheres with the source of light as their common center we see that each sphere receives the same amount of light. In the case of the larger spheres the light is distributed over a greater area. From the Law of 

Similar Figures we know that the areas of the spheres increase as the squares of their radii. Hence the intensity of illumination or the amount of light per unit of area, varies inversely as the square of the distance from the source. A book twelve feet from a lamp is four times as far as one three feet from it and receives but one-sixteenth the illumination. 

The unit of light emission is the candle power. This is that amount of light given by a sperm candle inch in diameter and burning 120 grains of sperm wax per hour. The unit of illumination is the foot candle. It is the amount of illumination given by a one candle power light at the distance of one foot, or a 4 candle power lamp at a distance of 2 ft., or a 9 candle power lamp at 3 ft., and so on. The illumination, in foot candles, is given by the candle power divided by the square of the distance in feet. The photometer. — The instrument used for comparing and measuring light emission and illumination is called a photometer. Suppose the object "O" is so placed between lamps of candle power C₁ and C₂ that it receives equal illumination from each lamp. 

Let the respective C2

FIG. 241. 

A SIMPLE PHOTOMETER 

distances from "O" to the lamps be d₁ and d₂. As the amounts of illumination are equal we have

If the candle power of one lamp is known that of the other may be calculated from the measured distances, d₁ and d₂. 

In the Bunsen photometer the object "O" is a grease spot on a piece of paper. If the side of the paper facing the observer receives less light than the far side the spot appears brighter than the paper, but if it receives more light it appears darker. This is because more light passes through the spot than through the other part of the paper, and the light from the spot is that reflected from the same side plus that which comes through from the far side. To place "O" so as to receive equal illumination from both sides it is moved back and forth until the grease spot appears the same on each side, in which case it nearly disappears. 

The Jolly photometer uses two pieces of wax separated by a sheet of metal. If the illuminations received by the pieces are equal they glow with equal brilliancy when viewed from the side. 

The photo-electric effect. At this point we are interested in a class of experiments that seem to indicate that light is composed of particles rather than of waves spreading out uniformly from the source of illumination. We attach a piece of freshly- Zinc 

FIG. 242. ARC LIGHT AND ELECTROSCOPE 

scraped zinc to the knob of an electroscope and place the instrument about two feet from an open arc light that carries about ten amperes between the the carbons. 

We charge the electroscope positively by induction, using the resinous base of an electrophorus.

The leaves diverge and remain so whether the arc is burning or not. We then charge the electroscope negatively, by induction, using the positively charged metal plate of an electrophorus. We now note that when the arc is burning

RADIATION 

355 the charge on the leaves of the electroscope disappears rapidly but when it is not burning the charge remains as in the first case. If the arc is too close to the electroscope the hot gases discharge it, so care must be exercised to prevent this.

The ultra-violet light from the arc causes electrons to jump out of the zinc atoms. Thus they carry away a negative charge but not a positive one. As ultra-violet light cannot pass through glass, a glass plate placed between the arc and the electroscope prevents the latter from being discharged. Sunlight cannot discharge the instrument because the air absorbs the shorter waves that would produce this effect with copper or zinc. Whether or not the discharge takes place depends on the length of the shortest light waves present and the kind of metal used. Light waves that are too long to produce the effect with copper may do so with zinc and the still longer waves of visible light will produce the effect with potassium, though they cannot do it with zinc. With X-rays of sufficiently short wave lengths, this effect probably can be produced with all substances.

FIG. 243. 

THE PHOTO-ELECTRIC 

CELL

The photo-electric cell depends for its operation on the small electron current sent out when visible light falls on potassium. 

When highly amplified, this stream of electrons is used to operate electrical devices.

The cell is a vacuum tube lined with potassium and containing an electrode. Light enters through a "window" in the potassium lining. The lining is attached to the the window in the potassium lining. negative pole of a battery or other source of current and the electrode to the positive pole. When light enters the cell electrons pass from the lining to the electrode. This very small electron current is then amplified, or it may even be made to operate a sensitive relay. 

The shaded part is

Along the airways and on buoys in harbors are lamps that light when it gets dark, and are extinguished when it becomes light again. These are regulated by photo-electric cells, the minute currents of which cause suitable electrical devices to operate the light mechanism. These cells are much used by astronomers in studying the stars. They have been made so sensitive that they will detect light as faint as that from a single candle when three miles away. 66 99 Another application of photo-electric cells is in the sending of pictures by wire, as by their use variations in light and darkness in a picture can be translated into electrical impulses. In the movietone sounds are recorded on moving picture film as light and dark bands, and in the theater these are translated back to sound again by means of a photo-electric cell and suitable amplifying devices. The most striking of their uses is in television, where even moving pictures can be broadcast by radio apparatus. 

Kinetic energy of photo-electrons. Before we can show that photo-electricity appears to contradict the wave theory X-rays 

FIG. 244.-PHOTO-ELECTRONS 

of light, we must know the kinetic energy with which electrons leave a metal. One method of determining this is as follows. Light of one wave length only enters a chamber from which the air has been exhausted and falls upon the insulated metal plate P. Photo-electrons jump away from the plate and would discharge the positively charged electroscope if they could reach the knob, K. But they cannot reach the knob because they must pass through the negatively charged

RADIATION 

357 screen or grid, G, which, having a like charge, repels them.

The difference in potential between the plate and the screen is varied by means of a sliding resistance until the fastest electrons are just prevented from reaching the knob, and the electroscope remains charged. Suppose e is the charge of an electron and V the difference in potential between the plate and the screen, both being in electrostatic units. Then the work required to bring the charge e from the plate to the screen is eV ergs. (See the definitions of these units in Chapter VI.) As the electrons cannot quite reach the screen, and as it is their kinetic energy that carries them towards it, we see that the kinetic energy of the fastest moving photo-electrons is eV ergs. 

Suppose the photo-electrons are caused by the light from one candle falling on potassium, the light being about ten feet away. This corresponds to about one erg of energy per square centimeter of metal per second. If this energy were spread out uniformly over the wave front, it can be shown that it would require over four hours for enough energy to fall on any one atom to give it that kinetic energy with which the electron leaves the plate. But the very instant the light is turned on the plate, some electrons leave it with this kinetic energy. We cannot understand how this could happen if light were waves, but it is readily understood if light is particles. In the latter case, whichever atom is struck by a "light dart" receives all the energy of that dart, and this energy becomes the kinetic energy of the emerging electron. 

FIG. 245.-PLANK AND SHIP 

The difficulty of explaining photo-electricity in terms of waves, is very cleverly illustrated by Sir William Bragg. plank into the sea from the height of 100 feet, and found "It is as if one dropped a

that the spreading ripple was able, after traveling 1,000 miles and becoming infinitesimal in comparison with its original amount, to act upon a wooden ship in such a way that a plank of that ship flew out of its place to a height of 

100 feet."

Einstein's photo-electric equation and quanta. - Suppose the photo-electric effect to be produced by a beam of a radiation of one wave length only, which we call λ (lambda). The radiation may be visible light, ultra-violet light, X-rays, or gamma rays. 

The frequency of this radiation is then c/λ, where c is the velocity of light in a vacuum. The frequency is designated by v (nu). 

In every case the kinetic energy of the fastest electrons is given by the equation, mv2 2 -ergs hv ergs 

Pergs.

Here m and v are the mass and velocity of the electron, h is called Planck's constant and is 5.547 × 10-27, and P is a constant that depends on the metal used. 

The stronger the light that falls on the metal, the greater the number of photo-electrons that leave it each second, but this has nothing to do with their kinetic energy. The latter is determined by the shortness of the wave length of the light; the shorter the waves, the greater the frequency, and the greater the kinetic energy of the ejected electrons. The quantity of (6 energy hv is called a quantum." The term P in the equation represents the amount of energy required to pull the electron from the metal, and varies with different metals. If the quantum is less than P, no electrons can leave the plate. In the case of potassium, P is much smaller than for zinc. the longer waves of visible light, having smaller frequencies, produce the photo-electric effect with potassium, but it requires the shorter waves of ultra-violet, with their higher frequencies, to produce it with zinc. Hence

Further experiments show that energy always both enters and leaves atoms in quanta. In a Geissler tube atoms and molecules are excited and caused to give out radiation by electrons striking them. Suppose an atom gives out radiation of wave length and frequency v. The kinetic energy of the electron or other

RADIATION 

359 ion that struck this atom and excited it must have had at least hv ergs. If the voltage applied to the tube is not high enough to give electrons or other ions this amount of energy, then radiation of this wave length cannot be produced. 

An atom must either absorb or emit a whole quantum of energy or else none at all. Energy cannot flow into or out of atoms as water flows into or out of a tank through a pipe. It can only enter or go out in quanta, which is as if water could enter or leave a tank only in bucketsful. If an atom is taking up or giving out radiation of frequency v, then determines the size of the "buckets" of energy that is handled.

X-RAYS AND PHOTO-ELECTRONS 

Velocity = v, potential 

V, frequency = V.

Suppose the photo-electric effect is caused by X-rays. Electrons leave the cathode of the tube and strike the anode, their maximum kinetic energy being determined by the voltage applied to the tube. Here electrical energy becomes kinetic energy of moving electrons. When these strike the target, the anode, this energy is taken up by atoms of the target and given out as X-rays. When these X-rays strike a metal plate, the energy of the radiation is absorbed by atoms and given out as the kinetic energy of photo-electrons. The accompanying diagram

illustrates how the rules for quanta hold for each of these changes.

To the teacher. The matters discussed above are fundamental. 

The experiment described first requires only an electroscope, an electrophorus, and an arc light carrying about ten amperes. If time permits there is much interesting material on the practical uses of the photo-electric cell.

SECTION 31. REFLECTION, REFRACTION, AND THE ACTIONS OF MIRRORS AND LENSES 

When light strikes an object some of it, as a rule, is reflected, some is absorbed and, if the object is transparent or translucent, some is transmitted through the material. In the first case light is thrown back as is the case when light strikes a mirror. 

In the second the energy of the light is converted into heat. 

In the third the light or a portion of it passes through the body. Plate glass reflects part of the light that falls upon it as is shown by the fact that one can see his image in it; part is transmitted as one can see through it; and part is absorbed, especially the short ultra-violet and the long heat waves.

If a surface is smooth, so that the various light waves are reflected regularly, it forms a mirror. If the surface is not smooth the many angles at which the light waves strike it cause the returning waves to be broken up and reflected in many directions, or diffused. One does not see an image of those objects from which the waves have come, but only the reflect- FIG. 247. — LIGHT REFLECTED ing surface itself.

FROM ROUGH SURFACE 

If all colors are reflected equally by a polished surface a naturally colored image results. If only certain colors are reflected an image containing only these colors is seen, as in a sheet of polished copper. A surface that reflects and scatters all visible waves appears white, as with a field of snow. 

If certain colors are absorbed the color of the surface is due to the remaining ones that are reflected. Thus, a red cloth appears red in white light or in red light, but in green light it appears black, as no light is scattered. It is by this means that we see nonluminous objects. If only certain colors are present in the light that falls on a white object it appears to have a color represented by a mixture of those colors present. Thus, if red and yellow light only fall upon it, it appears orange. But, if the cloth were yellow, it

would appear yellow, the red light being entirely absorbed. 

A surface that absorbs all colors appears black. 

When a material transmits light uniformly it is transparent. Some substances transmit only certain colors uniformly, absorbing others, as with clear colored glasses. 

When light is transmitted through frosted glass or glass with a very irregular surface, the light of different wave lengths is refracted and scattered in many directions. Such material is said to be translucent. 

When paints of two colors are mixed, the resulting color is due to the absorption of all light except that which passes through thin layers of both pigments. Transparent substances, as a rule, transmit the same colors as they reflect, hence, in reflection, light probably penetrates the surface of the reflecting substance a short distance before being turned back. 

Thus, a mixture of yellow and blue paints produces green as there is some green in each and this is the only color that can pass through both pigments. But the resulting green is not as brilliant as the yellow and blue pigments which make it. A brilliant color must be obtained from natural, unmixed pigment of that shade. Art students are taught to use "raw" colors, unmixed paints, to produce brilliant effects. 

When light of different colors is sent to the eye from the same source, as with rotating disks with segments of various colors, Newton's disks, the effect is quite different from that obtained by mixing pigments of these colors. Here the effect of one color is added to that of the other. Yellow and violet produce white, red and green appear yellow, and green and violet appear light blue. These effects are due to the nature of the eye, which cannot analyze the combination of light waves accurately.

Complementary colors.- Any two colors that produce white when revolved together on Newton's disks are said to be complementary. Some of these are: red and blue-green, orange and green-blue, yellow and blue, green and crimson. A knowledge of complementary colors is of the greatest value

RADIATION 

363 to the artist, the decorator, and the costume designer. 

Thus, crimson appears most brilliant when on a green background. Pigments of complementary colors when mixed produce dull grays, because some light of all wave lengths is absorbed as it passes through the different colored grains. 

For this reason many shades of gray may be produced by the use of only a few brilliant colors in printing pictures. 

In making colored pictures by means of photography first three photographs are taken, one with a red filter, one with a green, and one with a blue-violet. Suppose we were then to print these pictures, the one over the other with these colors of ink. Where there is a white place in the picture it would receive each of the three inks and would appear nearly black. If we print the negative of each of these, the white spot will receive no ink at all and of course will appear white. An effect approaching that of the real coloring is obtained by printing the negative of each picture in ink of the complementary color to that light by which that picture was taken. The inks used then are peacock blue, crimson, and yellow. A green spot is dark in the red and violet pictures. This dark place receives ink when these negatives are printed in peacock blue and yellow ink. As green light passes through both of these pigments the spot. appears green on the picture. It is evident that the beauty of the picture depends on the skill used in selecting the proper filters and the corresponding inks in complementary colors. 

In physics and physiology the three primary colors are red, green, and blue-violet. They are the two ends and the center of the spectrum. They correspond to the three sets of nerves in the eye, which are sensitive to these colors respectively. But in art work the three fundamental colors are red, yellow, and blue, because in mixing pigments these three paints give the greatest variety of colors.

MIRRORS AND LENSES 

Reflection from plane mirrors. There are two important facts concerning the reflection of light from plane mirrors.

The angle of incidence is equal to the angle of reflection. (The angle of incidence is the angle between the ray of light as it strikes the mirror and the perpendicular drawn to the mirror surface at that point.) And the image of an object appears as far behind the mirror as the object itself is in front of it. These laws may be verified as follows. On a sheet of paper draw the line AB with the perpendicular PO and the line DoCO making any convenient angle with PO. Place a piece of plate glass with the front edge along AB, the surface of the glass perpendicular to the plane of the paper. 

Erect pins at C and Do, any points along CDo. With the eye at E place pins at C₁ and 

FIG. 248. 

C₁ D₁ D1, in line with reflections of C and Do as seen in the glass. 

The pins appear double as Peach face of the glass acts as a mirror. Use the images nearer PO which are the reflections from the front surface. It is seen that the angle of incidence, DOOP, equals the angle of reflection, POD₁. 

Pins may now be placed 

— - THE ANGLE OF INCIDENCE AND REFLECTION 

behind the glass at X and Y, the points where the images appear to be no matter how the eye is moved back and forth. These points are seen to be as far behind the glass as C and Do are in front of it. 

Curvature. If we throw a stone into a quiet pool the water waves spread out in circles that grow larger and larger. 

As the diameter of a circle increases the curvature of its wave front grows less. By curvature we mean the amount of bending the circumference undergoes per unit of length. In the same way the waves of light that pass out from any object have curved fronts. These form a system of concentric spheres, and the greater the distance from the source, the less the curvature of the spherical surfaces. Curvature is

RADIATION 

365 measured by the reciprocal of the radius. Ten centimeters from a candle flame the curvature of the light wave is cm, twenty centimeters away it is cm, and so on. In the following paragraphs we designate the distance from the source of a light wave as Do, meaning, distance of object. 

The curvature of the light is then 1/Do. 

Positive and negative curvature. starting from the point Do strikes a plane mirror at Z. (Do may stand for a point or the distance this point is from the wave front. This double meaning of Do need cause no confusion.) By the time the edges of the wave A and B 

Suppose a wave Do

D₂

REVERSING CURVATURE 

1 1 reach the mirror the center has been reflected to Z₁ because the speed of the wave is everywhere the same and AA₁ = BB1 ZZ1. The two arcs AZB and A₁Z₁B₁ have the same length of chords and the same "depths" ZZ1. It is easy to show that these two arcs are parts of circles having equal radii. Hence the curvature of the reflected wave as it leaves the mirror is the same as that of the incident wave when it strikes it. The wave returns in the same form it would have, had it come from the point D; placed as far behind the mirror as Do is in front of it. (D; also may stand for the point where the image is or the distance of the image from the mirror.) Each bit of surface on an object sends out its own wave of light, so when an object is placed in

front of a mirror there are many waves that go out from the object and strike the mirror. These waves are then reflected from the mirror and follow paths which are the same as though they came from a system of points behind the mirror, which correspond to the points on the object sending out the original waves. Hence the image of a material object appears as far behind the mirror as the thing itself is in front of it.

We always assume that the curvature of the wave as it first leaves the object is positive, and when the direction of bending is reversed, as in the above case, the curvature is negative. 

A plane mirror reverses the sign of all light waves that strike it.

FIG. 251. -Two CURVATURES 

image of any object seen in the mirror is very close to it and very small. The small size of the image is due to the fact that, at the mirror, object and image subtend the same angle. This is why one sees a miniature landscape when one looks into the convex driver's mirror on an automobile.

Concave mirrors. We have seen that a convex mirror adds curvature to wave fronts that strike it. We shall now show that a concave mirror subtracts curvature from the curvature of the wave front striking it. The remaining curvature is sometimes larger than that of the front before it struck the mirror. This is because concave mirrors first subtract curvature and then change the sign of the remainder. To illustrate: subtract 22 from 10, which gives 12, then change the sign, and we have 12. This appears as though we had merely added 2. In the same way, concave mirrors at times appear to add curvature to the original wave, but they really subtract. 

Principal focus. The principal focus is that point on the principal axis which, if used as a source of light, would produce a wave having a curvature equal to that which the mirror subtracts and the light reflected then would have a plane front. 

Let AZB be the wave front of light from the principal focus, f. AA₁ = BB₁ = ZZ₁ and hence, when A gets to A1 2f

and B to B₁ the center at Z has returned to Z₁ and the returning wave front is plane. From the diagram we see that the "depth" of the curve subtracted from the front is twice the "depth" of the curved surface of the mirror. The mirror subtracts twice its own curvature from wave fronts that strike it. We let 1/f represent the curvature subtracted. The principal focus, which we also designate by ƒ, is the distance ƒ along the principal axis and measured from the surface of the mirror. The radius of curvature, R, is equal to 2f.

FIG. 256. A REAL IMAGE 

A concave mirror forms a real image on a screen or in space if the object is beyond the principal focus.

If an object is placed farther from a concave mirror than f, light from the various points of the object is brought together at corresponding points, thus forming a picture. 

This is called a real image because it can be thrown on a screen. Light from the upper part of the object is reflected downward and light from the lower part of the object is

reflected upward, which causes the image to be inverted. 

All real images are inverted. 

If Do is at the distance 2f, then the object and the image are at the same distance from the mirror and have the same size. 

This is seen at once from the equation (1/2f-1/f) = 1/2f. If the object is farther from the mirror than 2ƒ, the image is between 2f and ƒ and is smaller than the object. 

If the object is between 2f and f, the image is farther out than 2f and larger than the object.

Spherical aberration. — In the above discussion we have assumed the curvature of the face of the returning waves to be an arc of a circle and all the light from a point to be reflected back to a point. This is only even approximately

RADIATION 

373 true if the mirror has but little curvature, or else if only the central part of the mirror is used. Otherwise it is not at all true. Suppose we have a mirror with much curvature, or, what amounts to the same thing, a large mirror which has an arc of 30° or 40°. A glance at the figure is enough to convince one that, while light that strikes the mirror near the center is reflected to a point, as from x to f, yet light coming from the same point but striking the mirror farther from the center is not reflected to the same point as be- 

FIG. 258. 

SPHERICAL ABERRATION 

fore. Thus, light from far out on the principal axis and striking at y is not reflected to the principal focus, f, but to a point nearer the mirror, f1. 

This defect, which applies to lenses as well as to mirrors, is called spherical aberration. It causes the image to be more or less blurred. If the image is to be clear and sharply defined, lenses and mirrors must have little curvature, or else only the central part of the lens or mirror should be used. This is one reason for the use of "stops" on cameras. If we want a "sharp" picture the opening is made very small, and then of course only the central part of the lens is used.

1/D. We see that D; is always negative and smaller than Do. The image is always virtual, nearer the mirror, and smaller than the object. Refraction.

RADIATION 

375 a small circle. The light that strikes those portions of the glass outside of the center is bent towards the center. This bending of light as it passes through a transparent body is called refraction. It is refraction that often causes objects to appear displaced from their true positions when viewed through glass, water, and other transparent media. Refraction is accounted for by the fact that the velocity of light in these substances is less than it is in air. 

Suppose ab is the front of a light wave that strikes the surface of water, xy, at a. Suppose light travels the distance bd in air in the same time it does the distance ac in water. When the light at b arrives at d that at a has reached c. 

The line cd now determines the wave front. The path of the ray has been bent towards the perpendicular pq. To an eye at e light appears to come from the point o' rather than from o.

FIG. 260.-REFRACTION 

The rod is straight, but it appears bent and broken, because of refraction as the light passes out of the beaker.

FIG. 259. 

REFRACTION DIAGRAM 

In the same way light passing from e towards d is bent from the perpendicular and reaches o rather than o'.

Light is bent towards the perpendicular when it passes from one medium into a medium optically denser, and from the perpendicular when the second medium is optically less dense. In the diagram, consider the light as entering the water from the air. Then the angle odp is called the angle of incidence and qde the angle of refraction.

Index of refraction. The ratio of the speed of light in a 

vacuum to its speed in any substance is called the index of

refraction of that substance. For air this is about 1.0003, varying slightly as its density varies, for water it is 1.33, and for glass about 1.5. For the diamond it is 2.4, which accounts for the beauty and sparkle of this gem.

The depth of water. Everyone who has had experience on a lake or river knows that clear water is always deeper than it appears. Let o be a point on the bottom of a lake and acb the front of a wave from this point, touching the surface of the 

The front passes from c air while the edges pass from a and b to the surface of the water. Now cd is 1.33 times as long as bf. The wave in air becomes more curved than it was before it left the water. It appears to come from o', but as deep as o. The "depth" of the arc of greater curvature must be decreased to give the other arc, hence the radius of one arc is but that of the other.

Lenses. A lens is a symmetrical disk of a transparent material that is either thicker or thinner at the center than about the edge. Both surfaces may be curved or one may be 4859 

FIG. 264. 

THE CRITICAL ANGLE 

The fish sees objects outside the water only if light from them falls within the circle. 

This forms the base of a cone, the angle of the vertex being twice the critical angle for water. Beyond the circle he sees the reflection of objects beneath the water. plane. In any case it is a convex lens if the center is thicker than the edges and a concave lens if the center is thinner. 

A wave of light passing through a convex lens has its center held back and curvature is subtracted from it, while one passing through a concave lens has curvature added to it. Convex lenses act on light as do concave mirrors, subtracting curvature. Concave lenses act as convex mirrors, adding curvature. Convex Concave

LENSES 

The focal length of a lens is the radius of that curvature which the lens adds to or subtracts from light waves.

As with mirrors, we designate this distance by f and the principal focus as the point f.

FIG. 266.- LIGHT THROUGH LENS

The light from any point of the object which passes through the center of the lens goes straight to the corresponding point of the image. The ray of light parallel to the principal axis is refracted through the principal focus. Where these two lines cross is the corresponding point of the image.

381 larger and upright object is called a virtual image. Since it is on the same side of the lens as the object, it can not be thrown on a screen. We see that the following formula gives us the distance from the lens of the image and whether the sign of curvature has been changed or not. If the sign of 1/D; is negative, the image is on the other side of the lens from the object and is real and inverted. 1/D。 — 1/ƒ = 1/D;. 

As concave lenses always add curvature, for them we have, 1/Do + 1/f = 1/D;.

To find the value of ƒ for a convex lens we throw the real image of a lighted candle or other bright object on a screen. 

Do and D; can be measured and ƒ calculated from the formula. 

Any Do with its corresponding D; are called conjugate foci of the lens. In finding the focal length of a lens, ƒ, it is often convenient to use a very distant object, the sun, far mountains, or even a building some distance away. As Do is very large, 1/Do may be called zero, and 1/D; 1/f. 

The focal length of a lens depends on the index of refraction of the glass of which it was made and the amounts of curvature of its two surfaces. It cannot be calculated from the radius or radii of the glass surfaces alone. 

OPTICAL INSTRUMENTS 

The pinhole camera. 

Photographs of still objects may be taken without a lens or mirror. The photographic plate is placed opposite a pinhole in a light-tight box.

From every part of the object a point of light is projected on the photo- 

FIG. 269. 

THE PINHOLE CAMERA 

graphic plate. These, in their varying tones of light and dark, form an inverted image of the object. The light of each of these points acts photo-

electrically on the silver salts of the plate producing chemical changes. These are fixed permanently as a negative by treating the plate with certain chemicals. 

The photographic camera. In the photographic camera the image on the plate or film is a real image formed by a lens. The lens gathers all the light on its larger surface and brings the various waves to points on the plate or film which correspond to the points on the object from which the waves originated. The greater the amount of light collected and focused by the lens, the more rapid is the photo-electric action. Pictures can be taken in a small fraction of a second, depending on the amount of illumination and the light gathering ability of the lens. As a real image must be formed, image and object must be at conjugate foci of the lens. The closer the object the farther from the lens must be the plate. This moving of the lens with respect to the plate is called focusing the camera. 

A "stop" or shutter is used which exposes more or less of the lens to light. With bright illumination only the central part of the lens is used. As there is then less spherical aberration the picture is sharper. Also, objects not in focus will appear clearer when only a small portion of the central part of the lens is used because the camera acts more like the pinhole instrument. Such pictures are said to have more "depth which means that the different parts of the picture are in better perspective than when a large lens is used. 

The eye.

The eye is, in some ways, much like a camera. 

Images of objects seen are formed on the retina by the crystalline lens. This is received by the nerves of sight and a sensation is carried to the brain. Focusing is accomplished, not by moving the lens back and forth in front of the screen where the image is formed, as with a camera, but by changing the curvature of the lens, and hence its focal distance. A small hair-like muscle, the ciliary muscle, passes around the circumference of the lens, which is composed of a transparent, elastic material. The more the muscle contracts,

RADIATION 

383 the more the lens bulges out at the center, and the greater its curvature. This makes the focal length smaller. In the eye the distance D; is fixed. Then for various values of Do in the lens formula, the eye adjusts f to agree with these conjugate foci. The closer an object is to the eye, the more the curvature that must be subtracted from light waves reaching it and the smaller f must be. This is all accomplished by the contraction of the ciliary muscle which thickens the crystalline lens and thus shortens its focal length. If an object is closer than 25 cm, the normal human eye cannot produce a clear picture. Hence 25 cm or 10 in. is the Iris

THE EYE

If the back of the eye is farther from the lens than normal, or if the crystalline lens or other transparent parts of the eye subtract too much curvature from the light waves reaching the eye, the person can see objects distinctly when closer than 25 cm and cannot focus clearly on distant objects at all. Such a one is said to be near-sighted. If the opposite conditions prevail, he cannot see near objects clearly and he is said to be far-sighted. The first condition is remedied by glasses having concave lenses which add curvature to light waves before the lens subtracts it. The second con-

dition is remedied by convex glasses which subtract curvature allowing the crystalline lens to bring. the image forward on

the retina. In astigmatism the eye is not symmetrical. 

The curvature subtracted along one diameter is greater than that subtracted along another. This is corrected by "cylinders" or glasses which are more curved along that diameter where the eye lacks curvature. 

To see an image formed in space by a convex lens or a concave mirror one does not place his eye at the place where the image is, but about 25 cm back of it. It is important to remember this in what follows.

Magnification. In this section we shall use the terms, "lens of long focal length," and "lens of short focal length." 

By this we mean long or short with respect to 25 cm, the distance at which the unaided eye sees most distinctly. 

Magnification results whenever we increase the size of the image formed on the retina by the crystalline lens. 

This is proportional to the angle at the eye subtending the object or the image at which the eye is looking. There are several methods of producing magnification through the use of a single convex lens or concave mirror. The three methods of magnification that explain the principles of the more common optical instruments are as follows.

Case I. This method may be used with any convex lens or concave mirror. The object is placed beyond the focal distance and near the principal focus of the lens. An enlarged real image is then formed in space at some distance

RADIATION 

385 from the lens. This is viewed by the eye at the distance of 25 cm behind this image. If L is the distance from the lens to the image, the magnification is L/f. From the 25 cm Real Image in Space 

FIG. 272. MAGNIFICATION CASE I 

geometry of triangles we can demonstrate that this image is L/f times larger than the object and both are seen most clearly when the eye is 25 cm from them. As the image is made larger and larger, the area over which the light is spread becomes greater, and hence the image is fainter. This places a limit to the amount of magnification possible.

Case II. This method may be used with a lens of short focal length only. The object is placed near but inside the principal focus. The waves that pass through the lens are nearly plane, the same as if they came from an object some distance away.

That they may not be long and unwieldy the light is passed back and forth along the barrel three times by means of total reflecting prisms. These at the same time place the image upright.

The projection lantern. With this apparatus a real image of a brightly illuminated picture is thrown on a large screen. 

If these pictures are made to follow each other at the rate of from 15 or more each second, we have "moving pictures." 

The mind interprets the sensation exactly the same as though the light came from actual moving objects. The illumination of the picture must be very strong. Since the heat accompanying such illumination is necessarily great, some provision must be made to reduce the heat which is permitted to reach the films in case they are stopped to show a "still" picture. 

The search light. To throw a beam of light some distance without its spreading out the waves must be nearly plane.

This is accomplished by placing the source of illumination at the principal focus of a convex lens or by using a parabolic mirror. A section of such a mirror does not give a circular arc but the curve known as 

FIG. 284. PARABOLIC REFLECTOR

a parabola. These mirrors are used in the headlights of automobiles, locomotives, and for search lights. 

Only the more important laws relating to mirrors and lenses have been mentioned. A good lens consists of several simple lenses made of different kinds of glass and combined in such a way as to correct certain defects of more simple lenses. Some of these defects will be mentioned in a later paragraph.

By this formula we can find the position of the image, whether it is real or virtual, upright or inverted, enlarged or diminished, using any lens or mirror. As mirrors tend to reverse the sign of curvature of light waves, when they are used the first sign is negative. Concave mirrors and convex lenses subtract curvature, hence when they are used the second sign is minus. The position of the image is given by the value and the sign of D;. 

When D; is negative the image is on the opposite side of the mirror or lens from the object. With mirrors this indicates a virtual image, and with lenses, a real image. All real images are inverted.

SECTION 32. DISPERSION AND SPECTRA 

The spectrum formed when white light is passed through a glass prism is due to the fact that the velocities of lights of different colors are different when passing through matter, though all light waves have the same velocity in a vacuum.

The result of this is that some colors are refracted through a larger angle than the others. This difference in refraction, due to differences in the wave length of light of different colors, is called dispersion.

Chromatic aberration. The real image of a blue object, as formed by a convex lens, will be closer to the glass than the image of a red one, due to the greater angle of refraction. If the light coming from an object consists of several colors a series of pictures are formed in space, one of each Red

Violet color we might say, the violet picture being the nearest to the lens. The picture thrown on a screen then will be in focus for one color and the other colored images are not in focus but show themselves by blurred ored edges outlining the at the red rays are in focus, and the violet lighter parts of the image. col- 

FIG. 285. 

If the screen is at A, the violet light is in focus and forms the picture, the other colors forming a blur about the edges of objects, If the screen is

CHROMATIC ABERRATION 

red being on the outside. fringes are on the outside.

Achromatic lenses. The amount of dispersion for any particular wave length varies with the kind of glass. Colors

separated by a prism of crown glass may be brought together to form white light by being passed through a prism of flint glass without bringing the line of light completely back to the original line of the light before it was refracted by the first prism. Flint glass has greater dispersion, for the same refraction, than has crown glass. Hence, we can make a convex lens of crown glass and a concave lens of flint glass that neutralizes the dispersion of light but which does not quite neutralize the refraction. Such a compound lens does not show the colored edges about images and is called an achromatic lens. (A, without; chroma, color.) When a single lens is used the image is blurred unless it has a small "stop" to screen off the passage of light near the edges. A combination of 

Crown lenses that corrects this is called an anas- 

Glass tigmatic lens. If one side of a lens is plane the straight lines of the image near 

FIG. 286.— ACHROMATIC the edges are curved. Combinations of lenses that correct this produce a rectilinear lens. The lens in a good camera is achromatic, anastigmatic, and rectilinear. Flint

LENS 

Diffraction gratings. If a number of fine, equally spaced, parallel lines are drawn on a reflecting surface the light reflected forms a spectrum, as with a prism. It can be shown that this effect is due to interference. Such surfaces are called diffraction gratings, or simple "gratings." They generally contain about 14,000 lines to an inch and are made six or more inches in length. Their manufacture is a triumph of mechanical skill. Replicas of good gratings are cheaply made by taking impressions in celluloid.

The explanation of the grating spectrum is quite similar to that of the colors as seen when a thin film of oil is spread over water. If the film of oil is very thin, there will be places where the blue waves are destroyed by interference leaving only the waves at the red end of the spectrum. Then of course the film at this place will appear red. Where the

RADIATION 

395 film is slightly thicker, the red waves interfere and a blue color appears. If the film is of uniform thickness throughout the color appearing depends on the angle at which it is viewed. Each wave length is greatly reënforced when viewed at a certain definite angle and when the film is viewed at an angle which corresponds to the angle of reënforcement for a certain wave length of light that color will predominate. 

By viewing the film at different angles the entire spectrum may be seen. The action of a grating is very similar to this. The beautiful play of color seen in such objects as onion skins, the opal, and mother of pearl is similarly explained. 

The spectroscope. In this instrument light from a narrow slit is caused to pass through a glass prism or to fall on a diffraction grating. Images of the slit are viewed through a telescope or impressed on a photographic plate. 

Suppose the light from the slit is composed of two wave lengths only, red and blue. A red and blue image of the slit are formed, some dis-

Types of spectra. (1) Continuous spectra. An incandescent solid, such as the filament of an incandescent lamp, sends out waves of all lengths visible to the eye. spectroscope gives a continuous band of colors. of a candle does the same, due to the particles of incandescent carbon it contains. An incandescent mass of molten metal or gases under pressure, as in the stars, may do the same. (2) Bright line spectra. An incandescent gas or vapor gives out only certain definite wave lengths of light depending upon the materials in the gas. Such a light gives a spectrum composed of a number of bright lines, one image of the slit for each wave length present. These spectra are often produced by passing an electric discharge through a glass tube containing the gas or vapor to be studied. We have the hydrogen spectrum, the helium spectrum, the mercury spectrum, and so on. The spectrum of the metal sodium contains a yellow line that is so brilliant that it dominates the color of the light. A "sodium light" is much used in the physical laboratory when it is desired to have monochromatic light. (Light of one wave length only.) 

This light may be produced by heating a piece of soft glass tubing in a Bunsen flame. The yellow light is due to sodium in the glass. It may also be produced by burning alcohol in which common salt is dissolved. (3) Band spectra. These consist of numerous bands separated by dark spaces. A powerful spectroscope shows the bands to be composed of many fine lines. These are formed by gas molecules. The line spectra are formed by atoms. The chemical constituents of a body are sometimes determined by means of its spectrum. This is called spectrum analysis. It is especially used by astronomers to determine the materials present in the sun and stars.

(4) Dark line spectra. The spectrum of the sun consists of a continuous spectrum crossed by thousands of fine dark lines called Fraunhofer lines from the astronomer who first studied them. It is known that these dark lines are formed when light from an incandescent solid or liquid or gas under pressure is passed through incandescent vapor. 

The gas absorbs from white light exactly those waves that it gives out when radiating light. In the sun we have light from incandescent gases under great pressure passing through great clouds of incandescent gases and vapors. Hydrogen, calcium, iron and many other elements show their presence there through the Fraunhofer lines they produce. Helium was found there many years before it was discovered on the earth. (Helios is the Greek word for the sun.) 

Flame, arc, and spark spectra. The spectrum of a substance may be obtained by placing it in a gas flame, in an electric arc, or by having a high voltage electric spark pass between terminals containing the substance. It has long been known that the spectrum of any material varies according to which of the above methods is used in producing it. Thus, the arc spectrum of calcium contains a number of lines that are not present in the flame spectrum, and these are greatly enhanced, that is, made stronger, in the spark. For other elements, such as iron, new lines appear in the spark spectrum which were not present in the arc or the flame. These new or strengthened lines are called "enhanced lines."

We now know that the enhanced lines are due to the presence of ionized atoms. If some atoms have lost one electron and are singly ionized they give a different spectrum from that of the neutral atoms. If they are doubly ionized, that is, have lost two electrons, the spectral lines are again different, and so on. 

The arc, being hotter than the flame, ionizes atoms that the flame cannot, or it may ionize a much larger proportion of certain atoms. 

Again the spark may ionize atoms that the flame cannot, or it may doubly ionize those that the flame singly ionizes. The relative intensities of the various enhanced lines is then an indication of the percentage of the atoms present that are ionized.

RADIATION 

399 

The rainbow. The rainbow is a spectrum found in nature. It is formed when sunlight strikes raindrops or the mist from waterfalls or fountains. The light entering the drop is twice refracted and once reflected, as shown in the figure. The resulting dispersion produces the rainbow. Raindrop

Consider the upper figure. An eye at V sees violet at 0, an eye at B sees blue at O, and so on through the spectrum. 

Instead of considering an eye at different points viewing the same drop, let us con Sunlight sider a stationary eye looking at a large number of drops. If the angles at the base of the cone EV1S, EV2S, EV3S, and so on, in the lower figure are the same as the angle 

VOS in the upper figure, it is evident that the eye sees a violet band along the arc V1, V2, V3. In like manner the eye sees a band of red

BUTOR FIG. 289. THE RAINBOW 

along R1, R2 if the angles ERIS and ER2S are equal to the angle ROS in the upper figure. Bands of the other colors come between the violet and the red, and we have a rainbow. 

Sometimes a fainter bow is seen outside the primary bow. 

This is caused by light that has undergone two reflections and two refractions, as illustrated at B in the middle figure. 

The spectra of stars. That the stars show a number of types of spectra has been known for many years, but the true explanation of these differences is quite recent. One would naturally think that two stars show different spectra because they are composed of different materials. This is not the case.

Only relatively minor differences in spectra are supposed to be due to this cause.

It is now believed that stars are composed entirely of gases at extremely high temperatures, and the type of spectrum given by any star is mainly determined by the degree of ionization of these gases. The degree of ionization depends mainly on the temperature at the surface, generally between 2,500° and 25,000° absolute, and to a less extent upon the strength of the gravitational field. It requires a higher temperature to ionize some atoms than others. The sun, whose surface temperature is about 

6,000° absolute, is hot enough to show many lines of ionized calcium, but the iron lines are from neutral iron atoms. Only the very hottest stars can ionize helium. The strong lines from many ionized elements are in the far ultra-violet. As the atmosphere of the earth is opaque to these waves, this light cannot reach us from a star. Such an element is sodium, and hence a very hot star shows no lines of sodium, though this element may be abundant there.

A strong field of gravity counteracts, to some extent, the effect of temperature by increasing the gas density. Ions and electrons are then packed closer and have a better chance to recombine to form neutral atoms, and the percentage of ionization is lowered.

More than half of the 93 elements have been detected in the sun, and there is no good reason for believing any are absent. 

The temperature is such that the strong lines of some would be in the far ultra-violet, the spectra of others have not been studied sufficiently, while the absence of lines of some heavy elements can be explained by supposing these atoms to have settled far below the surface toward the center of the sun. 

Beginning with the hottest and passing to the colder stars, the spectra are designated by the letters "OBAFGK M." 

The O and B stars are blue, as most of the stars in the constellation Orion; the A are white, as Sirius and Vega; F pale yellow, as Procyon; G yellow, as Capella and the Sun; K reddish, as Arcturus; and M red, as Antares.

Hydrogen is the only element that shows its presence in all classes, though its lines are strongest in class A. Ionized helium is found in only the hottest O stars. These also show

THE DOPPLER EFFECT 

The central spectrum is made from light that comes from the eastern edge of the sun, which, because of the sun's rotation on its axis, is moving towards us. The outside spectra are made by light from the western edge of the sun, which is moving away from us.

The shift of the lines is 0.06 Ångström unit, which indicates a difference in relative velocities of 4.2 Km./sec. Some of the lines are marked. Those of iron are due to absorption of light in the sun and show the Doppler effect, those of oxygen are due to absorption by the gas in the earth's atmosphere, and hence show no shift. 

This is a small section of the sun's spectrum in the red. than the enhanced lines. The sun belongs to class G, and there calcium is ionized but iron is not, as noted above. In class K the enhanced lines, in general, are weak and bands due to compounds and molecules appear. The bands of titanium oxide are especially prominent and become more so in class M. 

Doppler's principle with light.-If a source of light is moving towards the observer, the light wave are crowded together and become shorter. This throws all spectrum lines towards the violet end of the spectrum. If the source of light is moving away from the observer, the lines are shifted towards the red. The amount of shifting is determined by the relative speed of the source of light and the observer. By this means the rate of approach of stars towards the earth and their rate of recession from us is determined. This is generally between ten and fifty miles per second, though in some cases it is one or two

hundred miles per second. Two stars revolving about each other may be so close to each other that the telescope shows them as one star, yet their double nature is revealed by the spectroscope. If one of the stars is dark the spectral lines alternately shift towards the red and towards the violet. If both stars are of about equal brightness the lines show double twice during each revolution. There are many of these "spectroscopic binaries." 

Courtesy of 

Mt. Wilson Observatory.

FIG. 291. SPECTRUM OF SPECTROSCOPIC BINARY 

The spectrum of the star is the central one. 

Spectral series. The study of the many thousands of spectral lines would be a hopeless task were it not that the lines are grouped according to definite laws, forming 'spectral series." The first of these was worked out in the case of the visible spectrum of hydrogen by J. J. Balmer, an instructor in a Swiss school. 66 The hydrogen spectrum is made up of three series, the Lyman series in the ultraviolet end of the spectrum, the Balmer in the visible field, and the Paschen in the infra-red.
