Stationary states. Here we caution the reader to distinguish between fact and theory. We do not know what becomes of an electron after it passes inside an atom. It may not exist there as a separate entity at all. However, there are a number of experiments that seem to indicate that radiation is given out

and absorbed as if the electron had certain definite shelves, or steps, or orbits inside the atom. Each shelf, step, or orbit whereon or wherein the electron is placed represents a certain definite amount of potential energy. As an electron passes from one "" stationary state," as these are called, to another of less potential energy the difference in energy is given out as radiation of frequency v, the energy difference being hv ergs. (Here, as before, h is Planck's constant.)

To produce radiation of frequency v from neutral atoms it is first necessary to impart hv ergs of energy to the atom. This may be in the form of the kinetic energy of an electron striking the atom. This amount or quantum of energy is then taken up by the atom and passed out as radiation. It is assumed that the energy raises an electron from its normal place in the atom to another stationary state of greater potential energy, and that the radiation is given out as the electron then returns to its former position. Each line in a spectrum is produced by atoms passing from certain stationary states to certain other stationary states. That is, if we consider any given spectral line, the states responsible for that line are known. For instance, the red line in the hydrogen spectrum is caused by electrons passing from the third to the second stationary state in hydrogen atoms. 

If an electron is removed from its normal position in an atom and is then prepared to send out radiation as it returns to its former position, the atom is said to be "excited." If it is removed completely from the atom, the atom is said to be “ionized."

Atoms of gases and vapors may be excited and ionized by striking them with electrons that are driven through the gas or vapor by an electric field. The kinetic energy of the electron is here determined by the voltage drop through which it falls. The various voltages necessary to so excite an atom as to cause it to give the several lines of its spectrum are called its radiating potentials." Those necessary to remove electrons from their various stationary states and to free them from their atoms are called "ionizing potentials." A study of radiating and ionizing potentials furnishes additional evidence as to the correctness of the theory given above. 66 Another line of evidence has to do with the generation of

RADIATION 

405 X-rays and with the photo-electrons ejected from metals by X-rays. The state within any atom of the least potential energy is called the "K" state. It requires more energy to remove an electron from this state than from any other. This is often called the "K layer" or the "K orbit " of the atom. 

It is supposed to be nearer the center of the atom than the other layers or orbits and, therefore, closest to the nucleus. The other states, in order of increasing potential energy, are called the L, M, N, O. . . states respectively.

The existence of these states is beautifully shown by means of "electronic spectra." If an electron passes through a magnetic field in a direction perpendicular to the lines of flux it is acted on by a force at right angles to the direction in which it is moving and also at right angles to the direction of the lines of the field. This is but another application of the "three-finger rule." If the velocity of the electron is uniform this force causes it to move in a circular path. The greater its velocity, the greater the diameter of this circle. The figure represents a heavy metal box. A rod of some substance, S, lies in the path of a beam of 

X-rays. Photo-electrons are ejected from the various levels of the atoms

Distinct circles or parts of circles are formed. Electrons from the K layer form the smallest circle, those from the L layer the next, and so The velocities of these groups of electrons, and hence their kinetic energies, can be calculated from the diameters of the circles, the strength of the magnetic field, and the mass and charge on an electron. This kinetic energy represents the radiant energy of the X-rays falling on S, and it follows the laws for quanta as in other cases of the photo-electric effect. on.

The Bohr atom. As the reader will see presently, there is

much to recommend this theory of atomic structure. 

It was proposed in 1913 by Professor Niels Bohr of Copenhagen, at that time a student with Sir Ernest Rutherford. Bohr considered the electron in the hydrogen atom to revolve about the nucleus somewhat as the moon does about the earth. In the case of the moon the centrifugal force, mv²/r, is exactly balanced by the attraction of gravity, KM₁M₂/r², as has been explained in Chapter III. 

The radius of the moon's path is about 240,000 miles. Should the moon fall, and revolve in a smaller circle, say one of 120,000 miles radius, it would require a greater speed of rotation to balance the greater gravitational attraction. It can be shown that the increase in speed necessary is but half the speed the moon would acquire in falling from the outer to the inner orbit. 

This would also be the case were the attraction electrostatic instead of gravitational.

Bohr assumed the forces acting on the electron revolving about the nucleus of a hydrogen atom to be balanced, the centrifugal force being equal to the electrostatic force of attraction. The first is given by mv2/r and the second by eE/r². 

Here m is the mass and e the charge on an electron, v its velocity, r the radius of the orbit and E the positive charge on the nucleus. 

He also assumed that the electron can revolve in certain definite orbits only, the stationary states, and that as it falls from an outer to an inner orbit the excess of energy passes out as radiation. This excess is half of the kinetic energy acquired by falling or changing its position, as explained in the case of the moon.

Bohr also assumed that this energy also passed out as a definite amount of energy or a quantum, the amount being hv ergs.

He also assumed that in each orbit the momentum of the electron is some even multiple of h/2″ units. When these suppositions are put into mathematical equations and the proper reductions made, the following interesting result is obtained. 1 2π²е2 E2m hc (1/n,² - 1/n₂²). 

Here c is the velocity of light and n, and n₂ are integers as in Balmer's formula. A very remarkable result appears when

RADIATION 

407 we substitute the numerical values of these quantities as determined by independent means. The formula then becomes, 1 

109,500 (1/n¹² — 1/n½²).

This checks with the formula for the spectral series of hydrogen in a most astonishing manner. It gives assurance, not only of the general truth of Bohr's theory but also of the accuracy of the various constants used. In fact very accurate determinations of some of these constants are now made through a study of spectra. 

Cathode rays and

Fluorescence and phosphorescence. 

X-rays are made visible by being caused to fall on screens of various materials which then give out visible radiations. Such materials are said to be fluorescent. Other materials give out light for some time after being exposed to certain agencies. 

These are said to be phosphorescent. In each case atoms are excited by having electrons removed from their normal states. 

The radiation is emitted as these return to their normal positions. 

These phenomena follow the rules for quanta. Atoms cannot be excited by radiation of a wave length shorter than that of the fluorescent light. Two common substances used to produce fluorescence are zinc sulphide and barium platinocyanide. 

SECTION 33. ELECTRICAL DISCHARGES AND RELATED TOPICS 

When an insulated, charged body is allowed to stand for some time it is seen that the charge gradually disappears. 

Part of it may pass off through the insulation, but generally most of the leak is due to ions in the surrounding air. When an atom or molecule loses one or more of its normal number of electrons it becomes a positive ion, if it gains extra electrons it becomes a negative ion. Electrons alone may be regarded as negative ions.

There are always a few ions present in the atmosphere. 

The air generally contains about a thousand ions per cubic centimeter. This is a very small number indeed in comparison with the number of molecules present.

Ions are formed by collisions and by radiations. When an electron, an atom, or a molecule strikes another atom or molecule it may or may not ionize it. Ionization can occur only when the kinetic energy of impact is equal to or greater than the energy required to remove the lightest bound electron from the atoms involved. Some gases and vapors are therefore much more easily ionized than others. An electron must fall through a difference of potential of 5.13 volts to acquire sufficient energy to ionize a sodium atom, through 

7.54 volts to ionize a silver atom, and through 13.5 volts to ionize a hydrogen atom. 

Ionization sometimes is produced by visible light or by ultra-violet light, but X-rays and gamma rays from radium readily produce ionization. Ionization can occur only when the radiation quantum, hv, is equal to or greater than the energy required to ionize the type of atom present. 

If we turn a beam of X-rays on a charged electroscope the leaves fall. The X-rays ionize the air, thus making it possible for the electric charge to attract ions of opposite sign and so to become neutralized. The gamma rays from radio elements act in the same way. The alpha rays and the beta rays produce ions by collision. Thus the presence of radium and other radio elements is detected and the amount present is measured by noting the rate at which an electroscope is discharged. If we wish to analyze a sample of radium ore, a known amount of the ore is placed within a calibrated electroscope and the amount of radium present is calculated from the rate at which the leaves fall. In this case most of the ionization is due to the collisions of alpha particles. A better way is to treat the ore chemically, boil off the radium emanation and analyze for that by means of an electroscope. The existence of the "penetrating radiation" is inferred from the rate at which electroscopes are discharged when immersed deep in the radium-free water of mountain lakes. The wave length of this radiation is estimated from the great thicknesses of matter through

RADIATION 

409 which it passes with only a small percentage being absorbed.

Ions are also formed by chemical reactions and by high temperatures. The flame of a candle renders the surrounding air conductive. Ions are also formed by the evaporation of water and by its condensation into drops. It is supposed that ions formed in this way are responsible for the electric charge of the lightning. 

The gaseous discharge tube. Suppose we attach high potential terminals to electrodes about a foot apart in a glass tube and also connect the tube to an air pump. At normal atmospheric pressure no current passes through the tube. As exhaustion advances

Neon discharge passes through, first in lightning-like streaks and later as a steady glow. 

This glow exhibits definite colors at certain places with dark spaces between. As

NEON SIGNS 

The use of Geissler tubes for signs was impossible until the inert gases of the atmosphere, argon, neon, and helium were produced in a commercial way. 

This is now done by the distillation of liquid air. 

Other gases are soon destroyed by the electric discharge, but these gases do not combine chemically with any substance.

The pressure used is about 15 millimeters of mercury, or one-fiftieth of an atmosphere. Light is emitted as electrons return to their proper orbits after having been displaced by the electric discharge. the pressure is further reduced it is seen that one dark space, the Faraday dark space, grows at the expense of the bright column in the positive end of the tube. By the time this bright section, the "positive column," disappears greenish patches are seen on the walls of the tube. These finally

fade out also and no current passes through the tube. No discharge takes place when the gas pressure is too high and no current passes when the pressure is too low. The explanation of this is as follows. 

FIG. 295. DISCHARGE PASSING THROUGH TUBE WITH DIFFERENT DEGREES OF EXHAUSTION 

As exhaustion proceeds the Faraday dark space grows towards the anode, thus diminishing the length of the positive column. The names of the parts of the discharge are: the cathode glow, the 

Crookes or Hittorf dark space, the negative glow, the Faraday dark space, the positive column and the anode glow. 99 In all cases the electric current is carried by means of ions. At the first of the experiment, when the pressure in the tube is the same as that of the atmos-. phere, the few ions present are quietly drawn to the electrodes. 

They cannot produce other ions. The air molecules are so thick that before they can move far enough to acquire sufficient kinetic energy to ionize molecules they strike molecules and lose what kinetic energy they may have acquired. 

But, when the pressure is reduced, the ions have sufficient room to get up enough speed to ionize the molecules they strike. Thus more and more ions are produced in the tube. 

Atoms and molecules are 66 excited as well as ionized, and the electrons returning to their various " orbits" send out radiations, some of which are in the visible spectrum. We have here the conditions that prevail in Geissler tubes. 

Various beautiful color effects are obtained by using different gases, vapors and salts. A new type of electric sign consists of very long Geissler tubes. The most common gas used in such signs is neon, which gives a red light. Argon gives a blue light, and other colors are produced through the use of other gases and colored glass.

RADIATION 

411 As the pressure is reduced further, a stream of particles passes from the cathode to the anode. They may be caused to strike the glass walls by means of a magnet. A spot of light, due to fluorescence, is seen where the glass is struck. 

The pressure is now low enough to allow some electrons to pass from the cathode to the anode without meeting molecules. These come from the surface of the cathode or from the gas in that part of the tube. Positive ions, falling the length of the tube, strike the cathode with sufficient energy to liberate electrons from it. If we place a slit in front of the cathode and a fluorescent screen beyond the slit we have the "cathode ray tube." The path of the electron stream is plainly seen where it strikes the screen. 

At this stage, or with further reduction of pressure, if the voltage applied to the tube is high enough, patches of greenish light are seen on the surface of the glass facing the anode. 

These are caused by X-rays from the anode. Electrons now strike the anode with sufficient energy to remove electrons from the deepest "orbits" in the atoms of the metal composing the anode, the K, L, and M orbits. As electrons fall back into these orbits the amount of radiant energy given out for each drop is about a thousand times greater than that in the case of visible light. A plate or button of tungsten is placed in the anode of an X-ray tube because this metal has a very high melting point. This is necessary because of the great heat generated by the striking electrons. 

Also, the heavier the atoms of a metal, the more tightly are the electrons in the inner orbits held in place, and hence the more the energy required to remove them. With tungsten this is large. Therefore there is much energy in the radiation given out, and the X-rays are of very short wave length and very penetrating.

If the gas pressure in the tube is reduced further, the discharge ceases. The few ions present are absorbed by the electrodes without their forming others to take their place. 

Electricity can not pass through a vacuum except when

electrons are furnished by a hot filament or other incandescent metal. 

The Coolidge X-ray tube. The older form of X-ray tube has the disadvantage that the little gas in it is soon driven into the walls of the tube by the discharge. The tube then becomes too hard," that is, the vacuum is too high.

Hard rays are very penetrating, for instance, they will show a bullet imbedded in bone, the bone being somewhat transparent to them. While the rods are touching each other there is little resistance to the passage of the current except at the points where they touch.

THE MERCURY ARC 

This tube is used as a source of ultra violet light. It is made of fused quartz, as glass is opaque to the shorter waves.

The electric spark. If, at ordinary air pressure, the difference of potential between electrodes is 30,000 or more volts per centimeter, ions, in the small distance they move before striking molecules, attain sufficient kinetic energy to produce other ions. These immediately produce other ions, and so on. 

As the number of molecules per unit of volume is large, enough ions are formed to carry a large charge of electricity. This is carried across between the electrodes in a small fraction of a second and we have the electric spark. The potential falls at once but, if it is again built up, another spark follows. 

The charge and mass of an electron. The charge and mass of an electron was first found by Sir J. J. Thompson in 1898. 

If air at low temperature and saturated with water vapor is allowed to expand suddenly, a drop of water condenses about each of the ions in the air. Thompson formed ions in a vessel of air by passing a beam of X-rays through it. This was allowed to expand, and a fog appeared. The amount of water that condenses can be calculated from conditions of pressure and temperature. The size of the particles, which are spheres, was found from the rate at which they fell through the air. From the total mass of water and the size of a particle the number of droplets was calculated. The total electrical charge which they carried was easily found. So from this data it was possible to determine the charge carried by a single ion. 

The most accurate method of finding the unit charge of electricity is the "oil drop method." A single drop of oil, ionized by X-rays, is watched by a microscope as it floats in air between two charged plates. The diameter of the particle can be determined from the rate at which it falls through the air when the plates are not charged. From its diameter and the density of the oil its mass may be calculated. When the plates are charged, the drop is attracted by one and repelled by the other. 

Its motion is governed by its mass, its charge, and the voltage on the plates. As all of these are now known except the charge,

RADIATION 

415 which can be calculated easily. It is always 4.774 x 10-10 electrostatic units, or two, three, four, etc., times this number. This is the charge carried by one electron. Its reciprocal, 2,095,000,000 is the number of electrons in an electrostatic unit. 

In a cathode ray tube the electrons move from the cathode to the anode with great speed, tens of thousands of miles per second, depending on the voltage used. Having a negative charge they are repelled by the cathode and attracted to the anode. If the stream of electrons passes between charged metal plates, they then move in curved paths instead of a straight line. If they pass through a magnetic field, their paths are also curved because they form an electric current and they are acted upon by a force according to the threefinger rule. In applying this rule to find the direction in which the particles are deflected, we would use the left hand, because the action is similar to that of a motor instead of a generator. 

Courtesy of General Electric Company.

FIG. 299. THE COOLIDGE CATHODE RAY TUBE 

This is like an X-ray tube except that the electrons, instead of striking a tungsten target, pass from the tube through a very thin sheet of metal.

But as the particles are charged negatively, the direction is reversed, and we use the right hand. The thumb shows the direction of the deflection when the center finger points in the direction the particles are moving and the forefinger the direction of the lines of the magnetic field. 

Curved paths are formed by electric fields and other paths are formed by magnetic fields. The quantities involved in the equations for these two sets of curved paths are the mass, the charge, and the velocity of the electrons, and the strengths of the electric and the magnetic fields. We assume the charge to be the same as that found by the "oil drop method." All the other quantities can be directly observed except the mass and the velocity of the electrons. We now have two equations and but two unknowns, and hence these can be calculated. 

The velocity of the cathode rays depends on the voltage

applied to the tube. All slow-moving electrons have the same mass, about 9 × 10-28 gram, which is the smallest mass known. 

But when the speed of the electrons approaches that of light, the mass increases very decidedly. This agrees with the theory of relativity, which teaches that mass increases with speed, and that no object can acquire the speed of light because, then, its mass would be infinite.

The velocities and masses of individual atoms and molecules are determined in a manner similar to that described above. 

They are ionized and driven through a tube by a difference of potential. Electric and magnetic fields are applied. The curved paths which they take are determined from the records they leave on photographic plates. Such an instrument is called a "" mass spectroscope." In this way the isotopes of the

Isotopes are atoms of the same element which have different weights. 

For instance, chlorine has two isotopes, of atomic weights 35 and 

37. Yet, whenever we have the gas chlorine, the proportion of these two kinds of atoms, the isotopes, is always such as to give the average atomic weight of 35.46. Some elements, as hydrogen, helium, oxygen, and carbon have but one isotope, others have a half-dozen or more. If we take the mass of an oxygen atom as 16, then the masses of the individual atoms of most of the elements, in the upper part of the periodic table at least, come out whole numbers. The most striking exception being hydrogen, with mass 1.008.

SECTION 34. RADIOACTIVITY 

The discovery of radium and other radio-active elements as well as the nature of alpha, beta, and gamma rays has been mentioned already. When a radium atom loses an alpha particle it drops back four points in atomic weight, (the atomic weight of helium is four), and two points in atomic number. This last is because the nucleus has now lost two positive charges, those on the alpha particle. The substance into which radium transforms is a gas.

It was first called "radium 99 

Current 

Flux 

Charges Deflections 

+Charges emanation.' Chemically FIG. 301. — Alpha, Beta, and Gamma Rays it is quite similar to 

IN A MAGNETIC FIELD 

helium and argon, and is often spoken of as "niton." It is also called "radon."

Radium series. In 3.8 days half of the radon has transformed to radium A, a solid. In another 3.8 days half of the remainder has transformed, and in another 3.8 days half of what is left, and so on. This is called the "half value period" of radon. Radium A has a half value period of only 3 minutes, changing to radium B. Radium B has a half value period of 26.8 minutes, becoming radium C. This element changes to either radium C' or radium C". These become radium D, then radium E, then radium F, and finally radium G which is believed to be an isotope of lead.

Some of these substances can be separated chemically, some are detected by the range of the alpha or beta particles they send out, and the existence of others is inferred from theoretical considerations. Over forty radio-active elements are now listed. They probably all start from uranium, the

last element in the periodic table. This has a half value period of nearly five billion years. There are five products between uranium and radium, three of them changing very fast having half value periods of from 24 days to a little more than one minute. In these changes beta particles, electrons, are lost and the atom advances one step each time in atomic number. In the other two cases alpha particles are lost and the half value periods are thousands or millions of years.

The parent of radium is ionium. The half value period of radium is close to 1,700 years. 

Alpha particles.-When an alpha particle strikes a fluorescent screen it causes a flash of light which can be seen by using a specially arranged magnifying glass called a spinthariscope.

When an alpha particle passes through a gas it produces many ions. A fog may be caused to condense on these ions and a photograph of their paths obtained. This electrical effect may be detected by suitable instruments and a record made on a moving photographic film. Or the effect may be greatly amplified as in the radio and a sound produced in telephone receivers. The rate of transformation of a radioactive element is calculated from the rate of discharge of alpha and beta particles from a known amount of it. 

From the angles through which alpha particles are deflected as they pass through gold leaf Sir Ernest Rutherford was able to show that these atoms contain positively charged nuclei and to calculate the net positive charges on them, which is the atomic number.

It is natural to expect a great disturbance of atoms when alpha and beta particles are ejected to produce radiation of very short wave length. The gamma rays produced are a very short type of X-rays. These changes in radioelements also produce heat. Radium continuously produces enough heat to melt more than its own weight in ice every hour.

RADIATION 

419 

Heating effect. If we could obtain enough radium to heat the water in a heating system there would be no work connected with caring for the furnace and it would still furnish half as much heat after seventeen centuries. But such a furnace would be dangerous. The rays sent out by radium and other radio-elements destroy flesh. If exposed too long to their action living matter receives painful burns that are very slow in healing. With the proper exposure the diseased cells of cancer may be killed without injuring the healthy tissue. For this reason the discovery of radioactivity has been of great value in the field of medicine. 

There is no known means by which radioactivity can be hastened or retarded. It continues at the same rate in the hottest furnace as in liquid air. Physical and chemical changes have no effect upon it. This is supposed to be due to the fact that here we have an explosion as it were, from the nuclei of atoms, while ordinary chemical and physical changes affect or are due to the electrons in the outer shells only.

It is believed that the transformations of the radioelements is accompanied by a slight disappearance of mass. 

It is thought that here mass is transformed into energy. 

The grams of mass lost times the square of the velocity of light in centimeters gives the energy released in ergs. If this source of energy could be utilized a minute bit of matter would supply an enormous amount of energy. And even if mass does not change into energy, the kinetic energy of the rapidly revolving electrons in the orbits inside atoms, if they do thus revolve, represents much energy. We may some day find a store of available energy here, though at the present, we know of no way of obtaining it. 

Age of the earth. An interesting application of our knowledge of the radio-elements is in the determination of the ages of rocks. A certain rock contains radium and helium. The helium came from the radium in the form of alpha particles. 

We know how fast this change takes place. An analysis of the

rock giving the amounts of these elements enables us to tell how old the rock is. This is a minimum age as some of the helium may have escaped. A maximum age is obtained from the ratio of uranium to lead or of thorium to lead. Ages as determined in this way agree among themselves and with the relative ages as determined by geology. The oldest sedimentary rocks were deposited about a thousand million years ago. 

This checks with results from astronomical data concerning the orbits of the planets. When all the evidence we now have from both physics and astronomy is brought together we find the history of the earth to be somewhat as follows. Somewhere between one and one-third thousand and 10 thousand million years ago the sun passed close to another star. The attraction of gravity between these bodies raised great tides and matter was pulled out of the sun which formed the planets. In a short time, probably less than 15,000 years, the earth had cooled sufficiently to form a solid crust. When the sun cooled until it sent not over three times as much energy per day to the earth as it does now, the water vapor condensed and formed the ocean. 

Then the first sedimentary rocks were formed, which was not less than 1,340 million years ago. This is probably a very short time in comparison to the age of the star system to which the sun belongs. Two methods of estimation place this at six million million years. While this figure is not reliable, yet there is other evidence that indicates that the ages of stars are measured in millions of millions of years. 

Counting atoms. An accurate method of determining the number of atoms in a gram atom, Avogadro's number, is by counting the number of alpha particles shot off by a known amount of a radio element. 

Figure 302 represents a tube containing a chamber from which the air has been exhausted. This chamber is separated from the air chamber on the right which has a window at A covered by a very thin sheet of mica. Radium is placed at the point P. 

Alpha particles are shot off in all directions, and a certain fraction of them pass through the window and ionize the air in the chamber, C. Many ions are made by each alpha particle, it may be as many as a quarter of a million. These make the gas conductive, and a minute current passes from the wire, w, to the

RADIATION 

421 sides of the chamber. These minute currents may be amplified and caused to record themselves on a moving photographic film. 

The number of particles passing through the window in a given time can then be counted. Then by knowing the size of the window and the distance to P, the total number of particles shot off in all directions can be calculated. One gram of radium sends out 

3.4 × 1010 alpha particles each second. 

Alpha particles are atoms. of helium, and it is also known C W 

FIG. 302. 

COUNTING ATOMS 

that one gram of helium gives off 39 cubic millimeters of helium each year.

It would then give off one gram atom of helium in 600,000 years. The number of alpha particles shot off by one gram of radium in this time, 6.064 × 1023, is the number of atoms in a gram atom. This is less than two per cent greater than the number as determined by electrolysis. Having this number, it is a simple matter to find the mass of a single atom of any substance.

Polarized light. - Maxwell showed that light consists of waves of electric and magnetic lines of force at right angles to 

Plane of the Electric Lines 

Plane of the Magnetic Lines

Direction Light is Moving FIG. 303. - ELECTROMAGNETIC WAVES 

each other and to the direction the light is moving. 

This is illustrated in the figure. These lines do not remain in any one plane, ordinarily, but shift about in all directions so long as they remain perpendicular to the direction of propagation. Certain crystals, tourmaline is a good example, are transparent to light only when the lines of force vibrate in one direction with respect to the axis of the crystal.

Tourmaline tongs consist of two crystals so mounted that their axes may be made parallel or perpendicular to each other. When the axes are parallel we can see 

FIG. 304. TOURMALINE TONGS 

A wave in a rope can pass through two gratings if they are parallel, but not if the openings run perpendicular to each other. The action of parallel and crossed tourmaline crystals is similar. through the crystals, but when they are at right angles we cannot. In this case a line of force that is parallel to the axis of one crystal is perpendicular to that of the other. If the beam can pass through one it is stopped by the other.

Light that has passed through one such crystal is said to be polarized. There are other means of producing polarized light, such as reflecting it from glass at a certain angle. 

When polarized light is passed through many transparent bodies the planes in which the electric and magnetic lines vibrate are twisted about the axis of propagation. The light is said to be "rotated." The most common application of this phenomenon is in the testing of syrups to determine the amount of sugar they contain. The sugar dextrose is so named because it rotates the planes to the right while levulose rotates them to the left.

Polarization shows that light waves consist of transverse vibrations, like water waves, and not of longitudinal vibrations such as sound waves.

Relativity. This theory, developed mainly by Prof. 

Albert Einstein of Berlin, assumes that the three dimensions of space together with time form a world of four dimensions. 

No velocity can be greater than that of light, and motion through space can never be detected. Space is supposed to be, not infinite, but curved. The surface of a ball is not infinite in extent, yet a fly crawling over it can never find an end to it. So according to the theory of relativity our

RADIATION 

423 space of three dimensions is curved, and if we could go far enough in a straight line we would return to the place of starting. It indicates that mass increases with relative velocity and that it can be transferred into energy, as mentioned before. It helps us to understand otherwise unexplained problems of physics, but it does not tell us what light is, what gravitation is, or whether there is or is not that medium called the ether.

SECTION 35. THE RADIO 

The wonderful possibilities of wireless communication are due to two things in particular: the ability to tune a circuit so as to receive signals of one wave length only, and to the great sensitiveness and marvelous amplifying power of the vacuum tube. The waves of radio are caused by alternating currents of high frequency flowing back and forth in the antenna of the transmitting set. The frequency is, in general, between one hundred thousand and ten million per second. When these waves strike the antenna of a receiving set they tend to set up in it an alternating current of the same frequency. As a rule there is no appreciable current on account of the enormous impedance resulting from the very high frequency of the waves. We have learned that impedance is given by the formula, Vi 

1 2 R² + (2″ƒL − 2 = √C)" · Now Z = R when 2πfL — 1/(2πƒC) = 0.

That is, 4π²f² LC - 1 

0; or, 1 2π VLC Only when this relation is true can the waves cause an appreciable current in the antenna. The impedance is then due to the resistance only, this being very small in comparison to that part of the impedance caused by reactance when

the circuit is not tuned. For each frequency, that is, for each wave length of the radio waves, the product of the inductance and capacity, LC, must have a certain definite value. This may be brought about by changing the value of the inductance, the capacity, or both. One method of tuning is by a slide-wire inductance which places a greater or less number of turns of wire in the circuit and thus changes the inductance. Another is by using a "variometer" in which the inductance is varied by rotating one coil inside another. But the most common method of tuning is by means of variable condensers. By turning the movable element of the condenser the capacity is changed and the receiving set brought into tune.

The diagram in the figure represents a simple crystal receiving set. The antenna, A, and the ground form the two plates of a condenser. Alternating currents set up in the antenna by the radio waves pass back and forth from antenna to ground through the inductance Li and the variable condenser C₁. The circuit is tuned by means of the latter. The inductance L₂ and the condenser C₂ form a second circuit in which alternating currents are induced by the mutual induction between Li and L2. L2 and L₁ are said to be "coupled." This in- 4, Ground

duced current flows from one side of C₂ to the other and back again, the circuit being tuned by varying the capacity of C2.

Whenever a train of waves arrives alternating potentials are impressed on the crystal detector, and due to the manner in which atoms are arranged in this crystal, a current

RADIATION 

425 passes through it in one direction only. Hence each train of waves sends a pulsating current through the crystal which disturbs the diaphragm of the telephone receiver. A single dot of a wireless telegraph message consists of several trains of waves, and each train in turn consists of a number of individual waves. To produce a sound in the receiver the waves must come in this way. If they came in steadily a uniform current would pass through the crystal and the diaphragm of the receiver would be held steadily in one position. The condenser C, acts as a storehouse for electricity and allows it to pass through the telephone in a steady stream. It could not possibly pass through the receiver in high frequency pulses on account of the enormous reactance due to the turns of wire in the receiver. The pulses in the original wave are said to be at "radio frequency," those that pass through the receiver are at "audio frequency.' 

In general, these latter number several hundred per second.

There are several ways of producing these trains of waves at the sending station. The simplest, but not the best by far, is to have a condenser discharge through a "rotary spark gap." The electric spark passes between the knobs of the condenser and the spokes of the wwww mm rotating wheel. While a single dot of a signal is being sent several spokes pass the knobs. A spark passes as each spoke goes by the knobs, and for each spark there are a number of vibrations of the current, each vibration producing one radio wave. The nature of the condenser discharge was discussed in FIG. 306. — ROTARY SPARK Chapter VII. A train of waves is pro- 

GAP duced as each spoke passes a knob. Hence the number of vibrations of the diaphragm per second at the receiving station depends on the number of spokes that pass the knobs per second. This apparatus now is seldom used.

Continuous waves.

There are now in use several means of producing a continuous train of radio waves of uniform length and amplitude. The most common method is by using

Sound vibrations are of so many hundreds per second, but radio waves are of hundreds of thousands per second.

Thus a thousand radio waves are formed in the time one sound wave is formed.

The vacuum tubes of radio. Many types of vacuum tubes are in use today. They vary as much in construction as they do in size, which may be from the size of a peanut to that of a human being. The operation of all types is based upon the "Edison effect," the fact that electrons can be caused to escape from heated metals. 

We shall describe the common tube having a filament, grid, and plate. The figure represents a simple receiving set having but one vacuum tube. The filament is represented by the loop at F. 

Most filaments consist of a tungsten wire impregnated with the element thorium, because this element causes electrons to be given off freely at a much lower temperature than would be the case if pure tungsten were used, thus increasing the FIG. 308. life of the tube and saving power. The earlier tubes used pure tungsten wire.

Sometimes the filament is coated with pastes of various oxides. These also aid in the same way as the thorium.

TELEVISION

RADIATION 

431 from the other parts of the circuit. This is shunted by a high resistance of about a million ohms, the so-called "leak." 

It is now unnecessary to have the grid voltage operate near a bend in the curve shown in Fig. 310. Suppose it is operating near the middle. At those instants when the radio waves make the grid more positive it captures electrons, but at the times when its negative charge is increased it does not lose them readily. A negative charge tends to collect on the grid, but this leaks away gradually through the high resistance. Hence the negative charge on the grid rises and falls with the amplitudes of the radio waves. The plate current then rises and falls with the same wave form. It is an electric current of the same frequency and form as the original sound wave and it then produces the proper sounds in the telephones. 

Amplification. The amount of current passing between the filament and the plate depends on the size and temperature of the filament, the distance from filament and plate, and the potential difference between them. A certain increase in potential causes a definite increase in current, other things remaining the same. But if this same increase in potential is applied to the grid instead of to the plate, the increase in current is much more; it may be only four or five times as much, or, with special screen grid tubes, it may be two or three hundred times as much. This number is called the amplifying factor of the tube. 

Currents of radio or of audio frequency are applied to the grid of one tube. The resulting differences in potential cause variations in the plate current of this tube. This current is then used to affect the grid of a second tube and it produces even greater variations in the plate current of this tube. The second tube may be connected to a third, the third to a fourth, and so on until the amplification is as much as a million times.

There are three ways in which the plate current of one tube can be used to influence the grid potential of a second

tube. These are illustrated in the figures. In the first, two inductances or a transformer are used and is called

When studying radio diagrams the following should be kept in mind: (1) An alternating current can pass through a condenser, but a direct current cannot. (2) A direct current can pass through inductances, "choke coils," and an alternating current cannot.

(3) Currents of audio frequency can pass through inductances that block currents of radio frequency completely. 

Broadcasting. The first step in broadcasting is to transfer the sound vibrations into electrical vibrations by means of a microphone. There are three types of microphones, based on resistance, capacity, and induction respectively. The first type contains carbon, and is but a highly improved form of telephone transmitter. In the second type the vibrating diaphragm varies the capacity of a condenser which, in turn, affects the electric current. In the third type the vibrating diaphragm moves a coil back and forth in a strong magnetic field. This causes currents to be induced in this coil which have the same wave form as that of the sound waves causing the vibration. 

The next step is to greatly magnify these small current variations by amplification through a series of tubes as described previously. The amplified current then passes to a "modulator." In this tube the constant wave formed by the oscillating system has its amplitude so changed that the intensity varies with the vibrations of audio frequency. 

A line drawn through the crests of the graph of the radio waves has the same form as that of the graph of the sound waves.

This modulation may be effected by applying audio frequency variations in potential to the grid of the oscillator and thus influencing the intensity of the radio waves it sends into the antenna. Again, it may be applied directly to the plate circuit, as shown in the diagram. 

The "all-electric" radio. If the power for operating the radio is taken from the 110-volt alternating current of the lighting circuit, special devices are necessary to adapt it for use.

The proper voltages can readily be obtained by means of transformers. The resulting currents must not only be rectified, but the pulsations must be smoothed out, or a loud hum will result.

As electrons pass from filament to plate but not in the

RADIATION 

435 reverse direction, vacuum tubes can be used to obtain currents pulsating in one direction only. These currents are used to charge comparatively large condensers. As current is taken from the condensers it passes through heavy inductances or "choke coils" which, because of the selfinductance, tend to prevent any rise and fall of the currents. 

As the plate currents are of a few milliamperes only, the condensers remain charged to a steady potential, and smooth direct currents pass to the plates. llllllllllll Jewelle

SIMPLE RADIO TELEPHONE WITH ONE OSCILLATOR AND ONE 

MODULATOR

The filaments require about a thousand times as much current as do the plates, but at a much lower voltage. If these heavy currents were taken from condensers, they would not remain at a steady potential. The potential would rise and fall with the alternating currents supplying the condensers unless the condensers were about a thousand times as large as those used for the plates, and the vacuum tubes would need to be correspondingly larger. Such units are not practical, and several schemes have been devised to overcome this disadvantage.

If we wish to heat the filament by using an alternating

current, a common method is to have the heating element electrically separate from the surface that gives off electrons to the plate. The filament passes through a small tube of a nonconducting material. The outside of this tube is coated with an oxide that gives off electrons freely when heated by the filament within the tube. This coating is called the "cathode." It operates in the plate circuit as does the filament in the older type of tube. As it is not electrically connected to the filament, variations in filament voltage do not influence the rate or electron emission. This tube has five prongs or contacts, two for the filament, one for the cathode, one for the grid, and one for the plate. 

Screen grid tubes. - Some tubes have two grids or screens, the regular grid as described previously and another grid which completely surrounds the plate. This second screen is kept at a Epositive potential. It then clears up the "space charge," the accumulation of electrons which forms in the space between the grid and the plate. It also reduces the amount of capacity between the plate and the grid, and it Courtesy of the Radio Corporation of America. prevents small fluctuations in

Loud speakers. The vibrating surface of a loud speaker is generally a cone of stiff material resembling paper. The electrical vibrations of audio frequency pass through coils about the poles of a strong magnet. The magnetic field then varies with the current and this produces movements of a piece of iron or a small magnet placed in this field. The vibrations are then communicated to the cone by rods and levers. The "dynamic" speaker has no permanent magnet. The coil carrying the fluctuating current consists of but a few the cone carries currents of turns of light wire wrapped about 

FIG. 318. THE "DYNAMIC" LOUD SPEAKER 

The coil about the base of audio frequency. the apex of the cone. The turns of this coil pass through the field of a strong electromagnet. As the coil is light and there are no other metal parts to move, it readily vibrates in synchronism with the electrical impulses received and there is excellent reproduction of sound.

Far indeed have we come since Glacial times. The polar explorer flies over great wastes of snow and ice, penetrating the most inaccessible regions of the world. Yet even in this remote country he rests in comfortable quarters and is entertained with music and informed of current events which are broadcast from his homeland, ten thousand miles away.

In spite of all this advancement we should be modest. 

We have indeed yet much to learn. Let us remember the words of the great Newton, who said, "I do not know what I may appear to the world; but to myself I seem to have

been only like a boy playing on the seashore, and diverting myself in now and then finding a smoother pebble or a prettier shell than ordinary, while the great ocean of truth lay all undiscovered before me."
